<meta>Title page of the 1892 edition of Silva Gadelica, a primary collection of medieval Irish literature. This volume contains the English translations and scholarly notes corresponding to the Irish texts.</meta>

# / SILVA GADELICA <note>Latin: "The Gaelic Forest" or "The Irish Wood"</note>

### (I.—XXXI.) /

## A COLLECTION OF TALES IN IRISH

### WITH EXTRACTS ILLUSTRATING PERSONS AND PLACES

### EDITED FROM MANUSCRIPTS <note>original: "MSS."</note> AND TRANSLATED BY

## STANDISH H. O'GRADY
//

## TRANSLATION AND NOTES

> "Others perhaps may offer more noble things; I offer what I can." <note>original: "Nobiliora forsan alii, ego quod possum."</note>—JOHANNES FREDERICUS GRONOVIUS, *in the dedication of his edition of Plautus to Jean-Baptiste Colbert*.

> "When you have understood what you did not know before, either regard me as an interpreter if you are grateful, or as a paraphraser if you are ungrateful." <note>original: "Quum intellexeris quod antea nesciebas, vel interpretem me æstimato si gratus es vel paraphrasten si ingratus."</note>—SAINT JEROME, *in the "Helmeted Prologue" (Prologo Galeato)*.

---

### WILLIAMS AND NORGATE,

### 14 HENRIETTA STREET, COVENT GARDEN, LONDON;
### AND 20 SOUTH FREDERICK STREET, EDINBURGH.

### 1892. <insert>6574</insert>

BOSTON COLLEGE LIBRARY
CHESTNUT HILL, MASSACHUSETTS

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page is the start of the preface to Volume 2 of "Silva Gadelica" (1892). It follows the title and printer's information, introducing the author's intent and target audience.</meta>

<header>PREFACE.</header>

<meta>Handwritten in ink at top: PB 1347 / O 5 / V. 2. Stamped in blue ink at bottom: 2658.</meta>

***

For many—perhaps for most—of the enlightened readers for whom this volume is intended and to whom it owes its existence, both the style and the subject matter will be entirely unfamiliar. Because of this, some might believe that a distributor of such "wares" is obligated to justify his actions. 

However, with all due respect, to attempt such a justification would be to get things backward. Not only is "justification" (whether in a religious sense or otherwise) a strong word—and therefore offensive to the modesty of the Gael <note>original: "Gael"; refers here to the Irish people</note>—but the concept itself comes in many forms. Only one variety of justification is valid here: the kind that happens after the fact rather than before, expressed through actions rather than words, and which comes not from the publisher or the book itself, but from the public. Our reason for publishing this can, however, be explained quite simply.

***Silva Gadelica*** <note>original: Latin for "A Gaelic Forest" or "Gaelic Collection"</note> is, therefore, like a straw tossed into the air to see which way the wind is blowing. In other words, it is meant to test the judgment of certain people who, from time to time and for various reasons, have insisted that an effort like this would be well-received right now. 

The effort, as you can see, has been made; the reception is yet to come. It is now up to the reading public to show whether or not those "experts" (including one or two very famous men) predicted more than they actually knew. Your response will decide whether those of us who made this experiment are guilty of being over-confident in our ability to provide what the public wants.

This work is far from being designed exclusively, or even primarily, for the all-knowing and "flawless" giants <term>original: "leviathans"; sea monsters, used here to describe elite, intimidating scholars</term> of science. Those scholars dive headfirst into the deepest and most difficult parts of the linguistic ocean and—in between attacking one another—lash out at any bold amateurs who dare to swim even on the surface near them.¹ Rather, this book is adapted for the use of those—

***

¹ Thackeray <note>William Makepeace Thackeray (1811–1863), a famous Victorian novelist</note> warns Bob Brown the younger that, since the days of Aesop <note>the ancient Greek storyteller</note>, the desire of frogs to compete with bulls is known to be fatal. So far, no Gaelic...

<summary>The author introduces "Silva Gadelica" as an experimental publication intended to test if there is a modern audience for Irish literature, noting that it is aimed at general readers rather than just elite, combative scholars.</summary>
<keywords>Silva Gadelica, preface, Irish literature, linguistics, academic criticism, Standish O'Grady, Gaelic studies</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface to "Silva Gadelica," where the author categorizes the Irish texts included in the collection and begins providing bibliographic details for each entry.</meta>

# PREFACE.

...less-experienced readers who, instead of staying content in their ignorance, might want to learn a bit about this unusual and interesting branch of literature. Some additional technical details will come later. For now, let us review our Irish selections, which can be grouped into these general categories:

A. **Hagiology** <note>The study of the lives of saints.</note>: I—IV; hagiological matter also appears in XXVIII and XXIX.
B. **Legend**:¹ V—X, XIII—XVI, XXII—XXV, XXVII—XXXI.
C. **Ossianic lore** <note>Stories relating to the legendary hero Oisín and the Fianna.</note>: XI, XII, XXI, XXVI; Finnic matter also appears in XXVIII.
D. **Fiction**:² XVII—XVIII.

<note>Footnotes for the list above:</note>
¹ Here the term includes tales that have either a real historical basis or a slight historical element, as well as those that seem to represent myths and traditions. The precise analysis of these is left to specialists to perform, according to their own specific viewpoints.
² Includes tales from a later date: deliberate inventions, and for the most part (as seen in our current examples) of a humorous character.

Next, the articles shall be taken in numerical order, and a brief description of their sources will be given:

I. This **Life of St. Kieran the Elder**, of Seirkieran in County Offaly <note>original: "King's county"</note>, is taken from <term>Egerton 112</term>: a large and well-organized <term>manuscript</term> in the British Museum (consisting of 1,042 large pages). It was written between 1780 and 1782 by Maurice O'Conor, a shipwright working in the port of Cork, who likely copied it from a version (now held by the Royal Irish Academy) made by his teacher, John Murphy of Raheenagh near Blarney. The text is an example of good modern Irish (from about the 17th century), which is accurate in both grammar and vocabulary. However, it is too close a translation from the Latin¹ to be "purely Irish" <note>original German: "streng irisch"</note> in its style. The <term>Annals of the Four Masters</term> <note>original: "IV M"</note> do not mention this Kieran; the dates of his life are completely uncertain.

¹ That is, from the biography in the <term>Kilkenny Manuscript</term> <note>original: "codex Kilkenniensis"</note>, published by the Irish Franciscan priest John Colgan in his *Lives of the Saints of Ireland* <note>original Latin: "Acta Sanctorum Hiberniæ"</note> (Louvain, 1645).

II. **Life of St. Molasius**, Abbot of Devenish on Lough Erne (after—

***

...small-minded critics <note>original: "batrachian," literally meaning frog-like</note> have tried to puff themselves up until they burst; on the other hand, it would be just as educational and easy to show where famous "whales" <note>original: "cetaceans"; here referring to prominent scholars of other languages</note> of linguistics have waded into shallow waters where the humble student of Irish is perfectly comfortable, only to end up stranded—as the poet John Milton put it—"many a rood in length."

<summary>The author categorizes the collection's contents into saintly biographies, legends, Ossianic lore, and fiction, while providing historical context for the manuscript source of the Life of St. Kieran.</summary>
<keywords>Hagiology, Ossianic lore, St. Kieran, Egerton 112, Maurice O'Conor, John Colgan, Annals of the Four Masters, manuscript history</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the bibliographic descriptions of the Irish hagiological texts included in the collection, specifically entries II, III, and IV.</meta>

<page-num>vii</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

...wards the burial place of the Maguires of Fermanagh) [who died in] 563. He is to be distinguished from his contemporary namesake of Leighlin. This text is from *Additional 18,205*: a well-written, 16th-century small-quarto manuscript on vellum in the British Museum. The remainder of that manuscript consists of a number of metrical pieces regarding the dues, privileges, and rights of Molasius’ successors. These, like all **mnemonic productions** <note>original: "memoria technica"</note> (which is what they really are), have no literary merit. The text appears as though it were somewhat inattentively taken down from dictation [1], and is in places defective or obscure. Formally, it has been altogether modernized, as has the spelling, which is frequently incorrect to boot. The first page of the manuscript is much defaced; the scholar Eugene O’Curry gave up on trying to decipher it. Any errors in the printed pedigree <note>A genealogical table or family tree.</note> are of little consequence since, as far as Molasius is concerned, the lineage is fictitious; see the extracts **in that place** <note>original: "ad loc."</note>.

> [1] A common practice, responsible for much textual imperfection.

III. **Life of St. Magnenn** [1] of Kilmainham, near Dublin, from *Egerton 91*: a small-folio vellum manuscript of the 15th century in the British Museum. It was written by the industrious scribe *Uilliam Mac an Legha* [2] ('William Mac Alea' or 'Lee'). The general character of the text is much like entry II, but the orthography <note>Spelling and conventions of writing.</note> is better. The *Annals of the Four Masters* do not mention this Saint, nor does the *Martyrology of Donegal* give the date of his death.

> [1] The name is feminine in form; its <term>genitive case</term> (the possessive form) *Maigninni* occurs once in this tract (Irish page 38, line 31), but otherwise it does not change its form.
>
> [2] His surname signifies **the son of the physician** <note>original Greek: "ὁ τοῦ ἰατροῦ"</note>, and the Mac Aleas were hereditary physicians. William's transcripts are mostly hagiological <note>Concerning the lives of saints.</note> and medical. A manuscript of his in the King's Inns, Dublin, is dated 1463, and another in the Royal Irish Academy is dated 1467. He had the habit of using in his <term>colophons</term> <note>Brief notes at the end of a manuscript about its production.</note> a peculiar Latin construction of his own, for example: "**William, who wrote [this], so that he may perish by a good death**" <note>original: "Uilliam qui scripsit ut bona morte peribit"</note>. This represents literally the Irish phrase *Uilliam ro scríb degbhás go négaidh sé*, meaning 'it was William that wrote this, may he die a good death.' Sometimes he says **"by a free death"** <note>original: "libera morte"</note>, meaning *saorbhás*, a "free-death"—that is, a death in a state of grace, free or exempt from condemnation. In Irish legal terms, *daor nó saor* means 'guity or not guilty'; *daor ó epscop saor ó bhanríogain* means 'liable to the bishop, exempt from the Queen' regarding dues and taxes: see *Additional Charter 34,938* in the British Museum.

IV. **Life of St. Cellach**, bishop of Killala, from the *Leabhar breac* ('Speckled Book of the Mac Egans'), otherwise known as the *leabhar mór dúna Daighre* ('the Great Book of Duniry'): a large-folio vellum manuscript in the Royal Irish Academy, written at some date not much earlier than 1411. A paper transcript of this life, made in 1629 by Brother Michael O'Clery (of the Order of Saint Francis), is in the Burgundian Library in Brussels.

<summary>This page provides bibliographic details for the Lives of Saint Molasius, Saint Magnenn, and Saint Cellach, noting their manuscript sources, scribal history, and linguistic peculiarities.</summary>
<keywords>St. Molasius, St. Magnenn, St. Cellach, Uilliam Mac an Legha, Michael O'Clery, Leabhar Breac, Irish manuscripts, hagiology, Kilmainham, Killala</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the bibliographic descriptions of Irish manuscripts, focusing on the "Book of Lismore" and the "Book of Sligo," and details the provenance of stories concerning King Dermot and various Irish saints.</meta>

<page-num>viii</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

Brussels. This prelate <note>A high-ranking member of the clergy.</note> is mentioned in neither the *Annals of the Four Masters* <note>original: "IV M"</note> nor the *Martyrology of Donegal* <note>original: "M D"</note>; however, from a literary perspective, our text is perhaps the finest example of Irish hagiography <note>The study of the lives of saints.</note>.<sup>1</sup> Unfortunately, an important episode was missing from the original source used by the scribe of the *Leabhar Breac* <note>The "Speckled Book," a famous 14th-century Irish manuscript.</note>.

> <sup>1</sup> ->The shortest story of this kind is as follows: "Three penitents decided to leave the world for a life of self-denial and sought out the wilderness. After precisely one year of silence, the first one said, 'This is a good life we lead.' At the end of the second year, the second replied, 'It is indeed.' When a third year had passed, the third exclaimed, 'If I cannot find peace and quiet here, I will return to the world!'" The original Irish text is in a paper manuscript in the British Museum, but I have temporarily misplaced the specific reference.<-

V. A story of King Dermot's attendant, Aedh *Baclámh*<sup>1</sup>, taken from the 'Book of Mac Carthy-Riach.' This is a 15th-century folio manuscript on vellum, written (at least in part) for the pleasure of Mac Carthy-Riach (Finghin, son of Dermot 'of the fortress,' son of Donall Riach), who died in 1505. In June 1629, this fine manuscript <note>original: "codex"</note> was kept in the Franciscan abbey<sup>2</sup> known as *Tigh* or *Teach Molaga* ('the House of St. Molaga,' or Timoleague), where Brother Michael O'Clery made a copy of it. That is the last we hear of it until 1814. During some interior renovations at Lismore Castle in County Waterford, the opening of a long-sealed passage or wall-recess revealed a wooden box. Inside was this manuscript in loose sheets <note>original: "staves"</note>, along with a portion of a fine ancient crozier. The manuscript, though much damaged by rats, has been known ever since as 'the Book of Lismore' and is, of course, the property of the Dukes of Devonshire.

> <sup>1</sup> In his 1627 translation of the *Annals of Clonmacnoise*, Conall Mageoghegan of Lismoyny translates this word as 'the king's sergeant.' While its full range of meanings cannot be discussed here, it clearly refers to an official title rather than just a nickname. This is evident on page 75 of the Irish text, three lines from the bottom <note>original: "lin. antepen."</note>, where the plural form *bacláimh* is used. To mean 'cripple-handed,' the *Annals of the Four Masters* <note>original: "IV M"</note> use the adjective *baclámach*.
> 
> <sup>2</sup> The abbey was founded in 1240 by the Mac Carthy-Riach of that time, who was buried in the choir. It also became the burial place for the O'Mahony family of Carbery, the Barrys-More, and the Barons de Courcey.

VI. The story of the death<sup>1</sup> of King Dermot (mentioned above), the son of Fergus 'Wry-mouth' <note>original: "cerrbhél"</note>—otherwise known as Dermot mac Cerbhall, who was slain in 592. This account is from the lost 'Book of Sligo,' preserved in the manuscript *Egerton 1782*. This is a thick, small-quarto vellum manuscript in the British Museum, written at various times between 1419 and 1517. A memorandum in the manuscript records the death <note>original: "obit"</note> of the reigning Mac Murrough-Kavanagh, Art Buie mac Donall Riach, on St. Catherine’s Day in 1517. The scribes were of the O’Mulconry family, and

<page-num>ix</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

Their handwriting is a credit to them; part of the manuscript <note>original: "MS."</note> was written at Enniscorthy, County Wexford, and some of it in Connacht. This tale is similar to story V.

<note>The word used here (*aidedh* or *oidedh*) refers to a tragic death, which in most cases was violent. These "death tales" (*aidedha*) formed one of the categories into which a professional storyteller's repertoire <note>original: "repertory"</note> was divided.</note>

VII. The Birth of Aedh *Sláine*, son of the King Dermot mentioned above, from the *Leabhar na hUidhre* ("The Book of the Dun Cow"). This is a large-format <note>original: "folio"</note> vellum manuscript kept in the Royal Irish Academy and is the oldest non-religious volume <note>original: "codex"</note> in Ireland. We know only one thing about the scribe: his name was Maelmuire, son of Conn "of the Poor" <note>original: "na mbocht"</note> O'Ceilechar, and in 1106 a band of raiders murdered him right in the middle of the great church of Clonmacnoise. Various notes <note>original: "memoranda"</note> recording the book's history over later centuries—showing how it was captured and recaptured in war—testify to how highly it was valued by the O'Donnell clan of Tirconall and the O'Conor-Sligo family.

VIII. The Wooing of Becfola, from the manuscript Egerton 1781. This is a thick, medium-sized <note>original: "small-quarto"</note> vellum manuscript in the British Museum. It was written mostly by *Diarmaid bacach mac Finghin Mheic Pharrtholáin* ("Lame Dermot, son of Fineen Mac Parlane") in M'Gauran’s territory in 1487.

<note>The Scots soften the "P" sound, making the name "Mac Farlane." In addition to trying to spell it phonetically, the Irish have also attempted to "translate" the name, turning it into "Bateman." The process is not obvious: the Irish name *Parrtholán* is always rendered as "Bartholomew" in English, which is often shortened to "Bat." This linguistic connection was too tempting for people to ignore. This practice dates back at least to the time of Duald Mac Firbis (who was murdered in 1670 by an English settler at Dunflin, County Sligo), who lists "Bateman" as the equivalent in his *Great Book of Pedigrees*.</note>

IX. The Disappearance of Caencomrac, Abbot of Inchenagh in Lough Ree, from the *Book of Lismore*. The phrase "son of purity" indicates that he had taken and faithfully kept a vow of celibacy <note>original: "chastity"</note>.

X. A formal speech of praise <note>original: "panegyric"</note> for King Cormac—son of Art, son of Conn of the Hundred Battles (who died <note>original: "†"</note> in 266 AD)—and for Finn, son of Cumall, from the manuscript Egerton 1782.

XI. A list <note>original: "enumeration"</note> of the members of Finn’s household, including the requirements for joining the Fianna <note>An elite band of warriors in Irish mythology</note>, from the manuscript Egerton 1782.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of Irish manuscripts, focusing on the Agallamh na Senórach (The Colloquy of the Ancients) and the scribes of the Book of Lismore.</meta>

<page-num>x</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

### XII. *Agallamh na Senórach* ('The Conversation with the Elders'):¹ from the *Book of Lismore*.² 

In a short introduction <note>original: "exordium"</note>, this text describes Oisín <note>original: "Ossian"</note>, the son of Finn mac Cumhaill, and Caeilte, son of Crunnchu mac Ronan, as the last survivors of the *Fianna of Ireland* <note>original: "fianna Eireann"</note>. It represents them as having survived for more than a hundred and fifty years after the disastrous Battle of Gabhra <note>original: "Gowra"</note> in Meath, where that elite warrior band was destroyed. 

The aging warriors had reached a point well to the north of modern-day Drogheda when they parted ways: Oisín went even further north to search for his mother, a woman of the *Tuatha Dé Danann* <note>the "people of the goddess Danu," a supernatural race in Irish mythology</note> and therefore immortal <note>original: "perennial"</note>; Caeilte moved south until, somewhere near Tara, he met Saint Patrick, who was then in the early stages of his mission. Because the pagan veteran was treated with kindness and respect, he readily accepted the new faith. As a cooperative student <note>original: "docile neophyte"</note>, he joined the Saint on a missionary journey around Ireland. 

They traveled through the south and west; along the way, Caeilte was questioned closely regarding <note>original: "anent"</note> all the lore related to the valleys, hills, lakes, and ringforts <term>raths: ancient circular earthworks or fortified homesteads</term>. Saint Patrick showed as much interest in these subjects as did the regional kings and the chiefs through whose lands the holy men and their famous disciple passed. After traversing Leinster, Munster, Connacht, and Ulster, they reached Tara and found Oisín living at the court of King Diarmait mac Cerbhaill <note>original: "Dermot mac Cerbhaill"</note> (V, VI). During the Feast of Tara, both heroes recited so many of their own and their companions' deeds as their great age allowed them to remember for the benefit of the public. Their stories were recorded by scribes of that time, and copies were distributed so that when the people of Ireland returned to their homes, a copy was taken to every region of the country.

¹ This title is convenient but too literal; standard English usage should be: "The Conversation with the Elders," meaning in modern terms: "the interviewing and questioning of the Elders by Saint Patrick and others," because *agallamh* here is a noun describing the act of speaking with someone. In addition to the loss of certain pages <note>original: "folios"</note> (noted in the text and translation) and damage from mice or rats <note>original: "rodents"</note>, the piece is incomplete at the end. The scribe left it unfinished and apparently had no hope of completing it, as the last line is immediately followed by other unrelated material (which is badly damaged).

² To return briefly to this manuscript: it is the work of three scribes. One is unknown; the other two were a friar named *Ó Buadhacháin* (modernized as "Buchan" or "Buhan"), who copied from the now-lost *Book of Monasterboice* in County Louth; and Aonghus Ó Callanáin <note>original: "Angus O’Callanan"</note>, who in a very beautiful, polished hand wrote two texts: *An Agallamh Bheag* ('The Lesser Colloquy'), and *Suidigud Tellaig Them-* <note>The text cuts off here, likely referring to *Suidigud Tellaig Themra* or "The Settling of the Manor of Tara."</note>

<vocab>Agallamh na Senórach (The Conversation with the Elders), Book of Lismore, Finn mac Cumhaill, Oisín (Ossian), Caeilte, Fianna of Ireland, Tuatha Dé Danann, Saint Patrick, Tara, Diarmait mac Cerbhaill, Ó Buadhacháin, Aonghus Ó Callanáin, An Agallamh Bheag (The Lesser Colloquy)</vocab>

It also details the scribes and physical condition of the Book of Lismore manuscript.</summary>

<page-num>xi</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

...the Settling of the Demesne of Tara; a concluding note <note>original: "colophon"</note> to the latter contains the writer’s name and a short dedication <note>original: "envoi"</note> to Mac Carthy-Riach. Folio 116 shows a curious poem of 44 four-line stanzas <note>original: "quatrains"</note> by Mahon mac Donall mac Eoghan O’Daly, the hereditary poet <note>original: "rhymer"</note> to the Mac Carthys-Riach. In this poem, he justifies the strong measures taken in 1478 by Fineen and his brother Dermot to depose their first cousin, Cormac mac Donough mac Donall Riach, who had seized the leadership <note>original: "usurped the chiefry"</note> from their father, Dermot "of the fortress" <note>original: "an dúna"</note>. This affair is glossed over by the Annals of the Four Masters <note>original: "IV M"</note>, but see the *Annals of Lake Cé* <note>original: "Annals of loch Cé"</note> for that year <note>original: "ad an."</note>. Stanzas 42 and 43 convey a compliment to Mac Carthy’s wife: Lady Kathleen Fitzgerald, daughter of Thomas, eighth Earl of Desmond, who was the Lord Justice of Ireland. In 1467, he was replaced by John Tiptoft, Earl of Worcester, who the next year treacherously beheaded him at Drogheda—which is why the Irish call him "Thomas of Drogheda." In 1470, the Earl of Warwick and the Duke of Clarence had Tiptoft executed in England (see the Annals of the Four Masters for those years). The verses, of which about twenty syllables in total are hopelessly damaged <note>original: "defaced"</note>, are followed (on the reverse of folio 116 <note>original: "f. 116 b"</note>) by a legal opinion on the case, written in prose; the poetic meter is *sédna* <note>a complex Old Irish verse form</note>.

³ This is an example of the freedom <note>original: "latitude"</note> which the original narrator, whoever he was, allowed himself in matters of dates and timing <note>original: "chronology"</note>.

**XIII. The Death of Eochaid, son of Mairid, King of Cashel (that is, of Munster).** He died by the bursting forth of Lake Neagh <note>original: "loch nEchach," anglicized as "Loch Neagh"</note>. The circumstances of this event are related, as well as the story of Liban the mermaid; this is taken from the *Book of the Dun Cow* <note>original: "Book of the Dun"</note>.

**XIV. The Death of Fergus mac Leide, King of Ulster <note>original: "Ulidia"</note>.** He died by the hands of the marine monster called "the Sinech of Lake Rury." This event was brought about by the malice of the King of the *Luchra* or *Lupra* people—otherwise known as *Lupracans* <note>early forms of the word "leprechaun"</note> or "Pigmies"—to avenge certain insults Fergus had directed toward him. These insults, and the reason for the dwarf-monarch's visit to Fergus’s court at Emain Macha <note>original: "Emania"</note>, are told at length. In this tale, there is an element of humor <note>original: "facetiousness"</note>; it is taken from the manuscript Egerton 1782 and is badly damaged <note>original: "defaced"</note> in places. The scene is set before the historical period.

**XV. The Manner of King Cormac’s Birth.** This tale may be considered an introduction <note>original: "prefatory"</note> to the story of the Battle of *Mucramh* (Section XXII). It is taken from the *Book of Ballymote*, a large-format vellum manuscript <note>original: "large-folio vellum MS."</note> in the Royal Irish Academy. It was written around 1400 (some parts earlier, some later) and for more than a century afterward was owned by the Mac Donoughs of Ballymote (County Sligo), a branch <note>original: "sept"</note> of the Mac Donoughs of Tirerrill. Black Hugh O’Donnell (Section XVIII) either bought it from Mac Donough for one hundred and forty dairy cows <note>original: "milch cows"</note>, or rather, while raiding <note>original: "harrying"</note> that chieftain, accepted the book in place of that many cows. The expression used in a note <note>original: "memorandum"</note> on the front of folio 333 <note>original: "f. 333 a"</note>—which needs no correction <note>original: "(sic)"</note>—entirely supports the latter interpretation.

...supposition, as also do the historical accounts between O’Donnell and the Mac Donoughs in 1516 and 1522. After further changes of ownership, it was acquired by the Chevalier Thomas O’Gorman, who in 1785 presented it to the Academy.^1^

^1^ The piece contains a story about a wolf; the medieval Irish are reported to have treated these animals as "gossips"—that is, as godparents—and the Brehon laws <note>The ancient native legal system of Ireland</note> show that they tamed and kept them as pets, as well as cranes, hawks, foxes, and deer (ALI IV, 115).

XVI. *Fiachna’s fairy dwelling* <note>original: "Fiachna's sídh"</note>, a lake-based story of regions beneath the waters: from the Book of Lismore.

XVII. *Flight and Pursuit of the Difficult Servant* <note>original: "Gilla decair"</note> and his wonderful Horse, a favorite tale of a more or less humorous <note>original: "burlesque"</note> nature: from a paper manuscript written by *Labhrás Mac Analla* (Lawrence Mac Nally) of County Meath in 1765; formerly in my own possession, and now Additional Manuscript 34,119 in the British Museum; it is a good manuscript for its type. The piece is not found on vellum <note>high-quality parchment made from calfskin, used for older, more prestigious manuscripts</note>.

XVIII. *The Kern in the Narrow Stripes*^1^ <note>A "kern" was a light-armed Irish foot soldier</note>, otherwise known as "O’Donnell’s Kern," from Additional Manuscript 18,747: a paper manuscript in the British Museum, written in 1800 by Patrick Lynch for Samuel Coulter of Carnbeg, near Dundalk; the tale^2^ does not occur on vellum. There are several versions of it, and I have transcribed a version as good as any I have encountered from a manuscript written in County Cavan in 1847 by Silvester M’Gibney, a country schoolmaster; it is now owned in London by Norman Moore, M.D., who kindly placed it at my disposal. Not only is this version <note>original: "recension"</note> a good one, but for its time the text is remarkably correct.

---

^1^ Literally, "the narrow-striped Kern," referring to his clothing <note>original: "garb"</note>; the conventional translation "slender swarthy Kern" is incorrect. We do not find that he was either slender or swarthy, whereas the new shirt offered to him to replace (and presumably to match) his own was striped (see translation page 313, below). To this, add that the correct reading is *caoilriabhach* (a compound of two adjectives) and not *caol riabhach* (two independent adjectives), and the matter is beyond doubt. The meaning of *riabhach* is "a stripe"; for example, *Lugaidh riabh nderg* means "Lugaid of the red stripes" (Index A); *riabhach* means "striped." When applied to a person or a horse, it means "grizzled" or "iron-grey"; applied to a cow, it means "brindled." In both the Scottish Highlands and Ireland, the pronunciation (except for the sake of the meter <note>original: "metri gratia"</note>) is *riach*; in Ireland, it is commonly used in English speech: everyone knows what a "a *riach* heifer" is, as well as "a *riach* fog" [*ceo riabhach*].

^2^ Two very fragmented <note>original: "abraded"</note> versions of this tale survive orally in the Highlands, where it must have been carried immediately by the Islesmen who, in their thousands, took service as hired soldiers <note>original: "mercenary service"</note> with the great Irish chiefs during the 16th century, especially with O’Donnell and O’Neill. These curious remnants are printed by John F. Campbell in *Tales of the West Highlands*, Volume I, pages 289–319. The Kern (that is...

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the tale "The Kern in the Narrow Stripes," providing historical context for the real-life figures who appear as characters in the story, such as the O'Donnells and the Earls of Desmond.</meta>

<page-num>xiii</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

Manannán mac Lir <note>The Irish sea god</note>, if he is not himself a historical figure, serves as the means of introducing us to characters who are not only historical but relatively modern:

(i) Black Hugh (Young Hugh) <note>original: "Hugh Oge"</note> became the O’Donnell chief upon his father’s death in 1505. In 1522, he fought the bloody Battle of Knockavoe—also known as "the breach of Loch Monann"—near Strabane, where O’Neill was defeated and lost 900 men. For an account of his death, see the extracts at that location. His son and successor, Manus, compiled the *Life of Saint Columba* <note>original: "S. Columbkill"</note> which is now held in the Bodleian Library.

(ii) The contemporary *John, son of the Earl* <note>original: "Seaan mac an iarla"</note> was John of Desmond, the son of the previously mentioned Thomas of Drogheda, the eighth Earl. He was therefore the brother of Kathleen Fitzgerald, who was the wife of MacCarthy Reagh. John is accused of arranging the death of his own brother, James (the ninth Earl). James was beheaded at Rathkeale in 1487 at the age of twenty-nine by *Toothless John, son of Edmond* <note>original: "John manntach mac Edmond"</note>, a member of the MacGibbon branch of the Geraldines. Because of this deed, James’s son Maurice (the tenth Earl) banished his uncle John and had *Toothless Shane* <note>original: "Shane manntach"</note> cut into many pieces. One piece, along with his head, was displayed in the meat market <note>original: "shambles"</note> of Limerick, while the rest were distributed to other cities. In 1516, *John, son of the Earl* was besieged in the castle of Lough Gur (County Limerick) by Earl Maurice’s son James and MacCarthy Reagh (Donall mac Finghin), who were his grand-nephews, along with other members of the MacCarthy family. Since John’s wife was *More*, the daughter of Murrough mac Brian Duff O’Brien, he appealed to her relatives. Before long, the men of Thomond arrived in such strength that the Desmond party ended the siege. This is recorded in the *Annals of the Four Masters* <note>original: "IV M ad ann."</note> for that year, and in *Torna mac Torna O’Mulconry’s* Irish record of the Desmonds, written after the death of the previously mentioned James mac Maurice, the eleventh Earl, who died on June 18, 1529.

(iii) The *Mac Eochaids* (the M’Keoghs) were the hereditary chief poets of Leinster. See several of their 16th-century poems in the *Book of the O’Byrnes* <note>original: "Leabhar branach"</note>, manuscript H. 1. 17 in Trinity College, Dublin. It is not clear who their leader was during the period of our story.

(iv) It is not certain which O’Conor Sligo the storyteller intends to reference: perhaps Felim mac Manus mac Brian, described in the *Annals of the Four Masters* as "a charitable and humane man," who died in 1519. Whether he is the intended person or not, his expedition to Munster (like everything attributed to the other real characters in this tale) is fictitious.

(v) O’Kelly of Hy-Many (Teigue mac Melachlin), head of the entire family name, died in 1513. O’Kelly of the Callow (Teigue Rua mac Melachlin), head of a branch <note>term: "sept"</note> of the *Hy-Many* <note>original: "úi Maine"</note>, died in 1519.

(vi) The actual MacMurrough-Kavanagh, by right of descent from *Diarmaid of the Foreigners* <note>original: "Diarmaid na ngall"</note>—the man who first invited Strongbow to Ireland—was always styled "King of Leinster." Our character must be Art Buie mac Donall Riach, who was mentioned previously and died in 1517.

(vii) The O’Donellans were hereditary bards to the O’Conors of Connacht. There were two other branches of the family, both in Ulster, and all were poets by profession. The Shane O’Donellan from whose house the Kern finally disappears from view was undoubtedly a contemporary of the story's author. 

These identifications are offered merely as starting points for the curious reader. One may also accept that the tale was written very soon after Black Hugh’s death in 1537, if not during his lifetime. Perhaps no one laughed at the story more heartily than his son Manus. Although as a chieftain Manus was a man of the sword who tolerated no nonsense, he was also a great wit and a skilled poet. This is evident from his many epigrams in *strict meter* <note>original: "dán dírech," a complex form of Irish poetry</note> that have survived. He especially loved to occasionally challenge <note>original: "run a-tilt at"</note> his good friends and devoted supporters, the Franciscan brothers of Donegal.

### XIX. *The Churl of the Drab Coat* <note>original: "Bodach an chóta lachtna"</note>

<header>Preface.</header>

...from Egerton 154: a 19th-century paper manuscript in the British Museum, written by Edward O’Reilly. In this, mention is made of the Baron of Inchiquin’s house, but that peer’s identity is hidden. Considering that the first Earl of Thomond and Baron was created in 1543, it would have been offensive <note>original: "invidious"</note> to specify which of them lived at the same time as Finn MacCool <note>original: "Finn mac Cumall"</note>.

XX. **The Healing of Cian’s Leg** <note>original: "Leighes coise Chéin"</note>: from Egerton 1781. The historical figures are Brian Boru <note>original: "Brian of the Tribute"</note>, his sons, and Cian son of Bran. The repetition of the phrase “by your hand” as an oath <note>original: "asseveration"</note> is absurd, but the reality of the matter <note>original: "das ding an sich"</note> is true to life: it was customary to swear by a chieftain’s hand.

XXI. **The Enchanted Cave of Kesh-corran** <note>original: "Bruidhen chéise Chorainn"</note> (County Sligo): from Additional Manuscript 18,747. Here the word *bruidhen* <note>term: A "bruidhen" in Irish mythology is a magical hostel or enchanted dwelling</note> is used in its secondary sense: see section XXVI.

XXII. **The Cause of the Battle of Mucramh** <note>original: "Fotha chatha Mucramha"</note> (County Galway), with details of the battle and its consequences, from the "Book of Glendalough," now better known as the **Book of Leinster**. This is a large-folio vellum manuscript in Trinity College, Dublin. It is not certain who the scribe or scribes were, but marginal notes show that it was in the hands of Finn Mac Gorman, Bishop of Kildare (who died in 1160), while it was still being compiled. The theory held by some that he had a major part in writing its older section is supported by two passages that have been much misinterpreted: the bishop’s letter¹ in the lower margin of page 288; and a closing note <note>original: "colophon"</note> to the *Cattle Raid of Cooley* <note>original: "Táin Bó Cúailnge"</note> on page 104, column 2, the tone of which is that of a serious church official rather than an ordinary lay scribe. In one or two places, the text of our piece is not quite satisfactory.

---

¹ Partly damaged, but not at all unclear; it reads, and should be punctuated, as follows:—

“[Life] and health from Finn, Bishop of Kildare, to Aedh mac Crimthann: to the scholar of the High King of Leth Mogha <note>The southern half of Ireland</note>, and to the successor of Saint Columba, son of Crimthann; and to Leinster’s primary historian for wisdom, skill, and the stewardship of books, knowledge, and learning. Let the end of this little story be written for me. To you certainly, O fierce Aedh, man of holy and great beauty: whether for a long or short time I am away from you, my desire is to be in your company. Let the poem-book of Mac Lonán be brought to me so that we may study the meanings of the poems that are in it. And farewell in Christ <note>original: "et uale in Christo"</note>.”

That is: “Life and health from Finn, Bishop of Kildare, to Aedh mac Crimthann, the professor to the King of the southern half of Ireland (Dermot MacMurrough, known as ‘Dermot of the Foreigners’), the representative of Columba son of Crimthann, and Leinster’s chief antiquary for his wisdom, skill, and cultivation..."

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Book of Leinster" (formerly known as the "Book of Glendalough") and moves on to describe the historical and mythological context of the tales "The Battle of Crinna" and "The Adventure of the Sons of Eochaid."</meta>

<page-num>xv</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

...of books, knowledge, and learning; and let this be the end of this little tale written for me.

[Independent quatrain:]
"O sharp-witted Aedh, let it be a certain thing to you—you man of the fairest and most pleasant qualities—that whether it is a long or short time that any other person stays away from you, it is my desire to have you [ever] conveniently near to myself."

[Postscript:]
"Let the book of Flann mac Lonan’s poems be given to me so that I may see the meaning of the pieces within it; and may you fare well in Christ."

It should be observed that Dr. Todd’s version of this text (Introduction to the facsimile, page 8, column a) violates all natural phrasing <note>original: "idiom"</note>. His version is based, first, on the mistaken assumption that the prose and the poem are a single continuous thought, and second, on the resulting error that because the preposition *do* used with a verbal noun forms a specific construction, it must also do the same with a verb tense—which is not the case. The "little tale" mentioned is our item XXII above. As I understand it, the Bishop was transcribing it when his copy ran out. The verbs *scribthar* and *tucthar* are neither indicative nor subjunctive, but imperatives; they are used here not as commands, but as verbs of request or supplication, as seen in prayers like *pray for us* <note>original: "ora pro nobis"</note> or *give us this day* <note>original: "da nobis hodie"</note>. The brackets in the poem contain my own suggestions, but the general sense is quite obvious.

XXIII. **The Battle of Crinna** <note>original: "Cath Chrinna"</note>: from the Book of Lismore. The central figure of the story is Teigue, the son of Cian (who was the son of Olioll Olom). Teigue was the ancestor of the various tribes known as the *Cianacht*. In stories about this man, there is always an element of humor. Unfortunately, the story telling how he and a deer killed each other at Rosnaree on the River Boyne is lost.

XXIV. **The Adventure of the Sons of Eochaid** <note>original: "Echtra mhac nEchach"</note>: from the Book of Ballymote. Here we are told how and where Niall of the Nine Hostages<sup>1</sup> was born. It also uses an allegory<sup>2</sup> to show how he achieved superiority over his half-brothers.<sup>3</sup>

---

<sup>1</sup> From Niall’s sons Conall (slain 464) and Eoghan (died 465) came the two great tribes known as the *cinél Conaill* ("the family or race of Conall") and the *cinél Eoghain* ("the race of Eoghan"). Their territories were *tír Conaill* ("Conall’s land") and *tír Eoghain* ("Eoghan’s land"), which were later Anglicized as "Tirconnell" and "Tirowen" (Tyrone). Of the various branches or "nations" within these groups, the O'Donnells eventually became the most powerful in Tirconnell, and the O'Neills in Tyrone. Their deadly internal rivalry lasted for nearly a thousand years and ended in the ruin of both families after the Battle of Kinsale in 1602. This rivalry was so famous that later poets imagined Conall and Eoghan as twins born fighting in the womb, each clutching the other by the shoulder and wrist, foreshadowing their descendants' future discord (for example, the poet Teigue Dall O'Higgin wrote about this around 1590). However, the historical annals record that Eoghan actually died of grief following Conall’s death.

<sup>2</sup> This is a standard parable featuring a hideous old woman who is transformed into a miracle of beauty by the approach of a fearless lover. Depending on the context, she represents either the sovereignty of Ireland or the leadership of a clan, signifying that these prizes were won through energy and a strong hand.

<sup>3</sup> The most famous of these was Brian, the ancestor of the tribe called the *Ui Bhriuin* ("descendants of Brian" <note>original: "nepotes Briani"</note>), the branches of which were distinguished by...

<meta>This page continues the catalog of Irish historical and legendary tales, specifically detailing the lineages of the O'Conor clans and the linguistic nuances of the term "bruidhen" in Irish literature.</meta>

<page-num>xvi</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

...their localities, such as the descendants of Brian of the Plain <note>original: "úi Bhriuin seola"</note>, Breifne <note>original: "bréifne"</note>, and so on. After the establishment of surnames, their chief clans <note>original: "septs"</note> were the O’Conors of Connacht, the O’Conors of Sligo, the O’Conors Donn, and the O’Conors Rua (all located in Connacht); the O’Flahertys, O’Reillys, and O’Rourkes (see the *Book of Rights*, page 107, note r). Note that the O’Conors of Faly (in King’s and Queen’s counties <note>modern-day Offaly and Laois</note>) were of Leinster origin, descended from Cahir More’s son, Ros of the Rings <note>original: "Ros failghe"</note>. The O’Conors of Corcomrua (in Clare) and the O’Conors of Kerry were of the Clanna Rudraighe <note>original: "clanna-Rury"</note> in eastern Ulster <note>original: "Ulidia"</note>. Finally, the O’Conors of Glengiven (county Derry), where they have been numerous in our own times, are of the Cianachta <note>original: "Cianacht"</note>, the descendants of Teigue, son of Cian.

XXV.1 **The Death of King Crimthann, son of Fidach, and of Brian, Ailill, and Fiachra** (three of the sons of his predecessor, Eochaid): from the *Book of Ballymote*. Here we encounter the use of poison, which occurs very rarely as an instrument of crime in the massive collection of Irish legends; in recorded Irish history, as far as I can recall, it does not appear at all. Even when describing the Irish people they hated so bitterly, the writers of the Elizabethan era <note>original: "Elizabethans"</note> did not accuse them of such practices. <note>Note 2: They did better than just accuse: they used poison themselves. In 1563, Lord Deputy Sussex—using a man named Thomas Smyth as his tool—attempted to rid "the State" of Shane O’Neill with a gift of poisoned wine, and nearly succeeded. O’Neill sent a formal complaint to Queen Elizabeth, and she expressed great outrage at the attempt (State Papers, Ireland, Elizabeth vol. ix, no. 32).</note> <note>Note 1: The reader should observe that in the Extracts (both the original text and translation), this piece is numbered incorrectly as XXVI.</note>

XXVI. **The Little Brawl at Allen** <note>original: "Bruidhen bheg na hAlmaine"</note>: from British Library manuscript Additional 18,747. This is a good version of a tale which, according to the scholar O’Curry, is not very common.

<note>Note 1: As with item XVIII, this English title is a new interpretation of the traditional translation: "The Little Fort of Allen." In the first place, there is no doubt that the word *bruidhen* (a feminine noun) primarily means not a "fort," but a royal mansion or guest house intended for large-scale hospitality; it is frequently used in this sense in the tales. According to a memorandum in the Book of Lismore (folio 158, verso) and elsewhere, there were five primary or special guest houses <note>original: "bruidhens"</note> in Ireland (some added a sixth), built in this way: each one had seven doors, was divided by seven hallways, and had seven hearths. Every hearth had its own cauldron that could hold an entire beef and a pig in bacon. Secondly, these guest houses are the subjects of many tales that describe, with various times and details, how they were surprised, attacked, and destroyed during banquets, resulting in the total or partial slaughter of the guests. Therefore, in the title of a story, the term *bruidhen* alone is enough to indicate a tale of violence connected to a dwelling. The full formula for such a title is "the destruction of the hostel" <note>original: "togháil bruidhne"</note> of a specific mansion. In the text of other stories, the word *bruidhen* often appears in a secondary sense, implying a trick or a plan for a violent attack. For example: "he set a trap <note>original: "bruidhen"</note> for him," or "he had a fight <note>original: "bruidhen"</note> at sea and a fight on land; he had..."</note>

<summary>This page details the genealogical branches of the O'Conor families across Ireland and provides a scholarly analysis of the term "bruidhen," arguing it refers to hostels where violent "brawls" occurred rather than simple forts.</summary>
<keywords>O'Conors, Irish genealogies, King Crimthann, Shane O'Neill, poison in history, Bruidhen, Irish hospitality, Book of Ballymote, Book of Lismore</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Irish literary terms and begins the catalog of specific historical adventures and chronicles, specifically focusing on the meaning of "bruidhen" and the tales of Teigue and the Boromean Tribute.</meta>

<page-num>xvii</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>
<sig>b</sig>

...one such on sea, and another on land": where clearly there cannot be a question of an actual building. Today, in common speech, the word signifies a "quarrel," "dissension," or "row": *there happened a row between them* <note>original: "tárrla bruidhen eatorra"</note>; *a quarrelsome person* <note>original: "duine bruidhentach"</note>. Such would seem to be the meaning in our title. Consider these points:
(1) Here we have a hall <note>original: "bruidhen"</note> as a dwelling, but it is neither stormed nor destroyed.
(2) If this was a small or lesser "fort" (let us call it), where or what was the greater one? It is not mentioned, nor does the adjective "little" <note>original: "beg"</note> appear except in the title.
(3) The bountiful great hall of Almhain <note>modern-day Hill of Allen, headquarters of the Fianna</note> is famous in Ossianic lore; we do not hear of any secondary dining hall.
(4) The dwelling that seated such a large company cannot have been a small one. This last point suggests: what then does the "little" refer to? Surely the fight <note>original: "broil"</note> in which so many fell was a big one. True; but the adjective is used playfully, to emphasize the fact that not a sword was drawn nor a spear thrown. The affair began with a punch and never proceeded to anything worse than a fight with sledgehammers; it was, in fact, merely "a boxing match."

<margin>95</margin> XXVII. **The Adventure of Teigue, son of Cian** <note>original: "Echtra Thaidg mheic Chéin"</note>: from the *Book of Lismore*. The ancestor of the various Cianachta <note>the clans descended from Cian</note> is presented as true to his reputation for friendliness <note>original: "bonhommie"</note> and shrewdness. When Veniusa, on the island, tells him that after all those years passed together—and they loving each other—she and Connla Rua are still like strangers, Teigue’s comment—"that is delightful and strange" <note>original: "is aebda ocus is ait sin"</note>—amounts exactly to saying "it is magnificent, but it is not war" <note>original: "c'est magnifique mais ce n'est pas la guerre"</note>. Among other localities, I leave "the land of Fresen" to scientific geographers; the name "Fresenius" is not unknown to students of chemistry—is it of Fresenic origin? <note>The author is making a joke referencing the famous German chemist Karl Remigius Fresenius.</note>

XXVIII. **The Boromean Tribute** <note>original: "Boramha"</note>: from the *Book of Leinster*. This is a history, as far as it extends,^1 of the origin of that famous tax <note>original: "impost"</note> and of the difficulties experienced in collecting it during the succeeding ages.^2

***

^1 That is, from King Tuathal the Legitimate <note>original: "techtmar," died 106 AD</note>, who first established the tax, to Saint Moling of Luachair (died 596 AD), who successfully petitioned for its cancellation.

^2 Since the most ancient times, the collection of any kind of dues has been a tricky business in Ireland. The extraordinary tale called "The Siege of the Hill of the Oxen" <note>original: "Cnoc damhgaire"</note>, near Knocklong in County Limerick, is based on King Cormac's attempt to forcibly collect his revenue from Munster. That province appears to have habitually and successfully resisted the High Kings—the kings of all Ireland as distinguished from the five provincial kings. As for the tribute <note>original: "cíos," which Elizabethan writers translated as "rent," and which still means "rent" today</note> which the subordinate chiefs <note>original: "urradha"</note> paid to their paramount chief, it had to be taken by force. In English, a chief's subordinate lords <note>original: "urradha"</note> were called his "gentlemen." For instance, O'Conor Sligo was O'Donnell's "gentleman," and it continually required hundreds of swords and axes (many of whom never saw Tyrconnell <note>original: "Tirconall"</note> again) to persuade him to do his duty. The following, in turn, were O'Conor Sligo's gentlemen:

<meta>This page continues the catalog of Irish manuscripts and historical tales, transitioning from the discussion of the Cianachta clans to the legendary rivalries between the great O'Donnell and O'Neill families, and then moving on to analyze the tone of the Fragmentary Annals found in the Egerton 1782 manuscript.</meta>

<page-num>xviii</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

O'Dowda, O'Gara, O'Hara-Buie, O'Hara-Riach, O'Hart, Mac Donough of the Corann, and Mac Donough of Tirerrill, who all were just as reluctant to part with their wealth. The whole theory of their rivalry is summed up in a still-popular tradition regarding the following correspondence (which was incorrectly recorded in the Abbé Mageoghegan's *History of Ireland* <note>original: "Histoire d'Irlande"</note>): "Send me my rent, or if you do not——" signed, I am O'DONNELL <note>original: "cuir chugam mo chíos nó mara gcuirir——mise O DOMHNAILL"</note>; the answer: "I owe you no rent, and even if I did——" signed, I am O'NEILL <note>original: "ní fhuil cíos agat orm agus dá mbiadh——mise O NEILL"</note>. This exchange may be fictional, but it is typical of their relationship.

XXIX. **Fragmentary Annals**: from the manuscript Egerton 1782. In this piece, as well as in sections I, II, V, VI, and XXVIII, the reader will notice that some outrageously shameful tricks <note>original: "discreditable dodges"</note>—to use the only fitting <note>original: "condign"</note> word—are attributed to eminent saints. The late William Reeves, D.D., and John O'Donovan^1 have commented harshly on passages of this nature. They argued that while these stories are clearly fabricated, the fact that they were created at all reveals the low moral standards of the era in which they were written. I confess that I cannot take the matter quite so seriously. These episodes have all the appearance of broad caricatures drawn to provoke a laugh,^2 and perhaps the worst that can be said of them is that they do not suit the taste of our modern day. Similarly, the political cartoons of James Gillray and Thomas Rowlandson <note>English caricaturists known for their bold and often crude satire in the late 1700s and early 1800s</note> would no longer appeal to a generation accustomed to the more refined style of John Tenniel <note>The principal political cartoonist for Punch magazine and the illustrator of Alice in Wonderland</note>. It is foolish to suppose that the native Irish writers of ancient times—whose general tone is undoubtedly that of gentlemen writing for gentlemen—did not know better than to seriously credit men like Saint Columba <note>original: "S. Columbkill"</note> and Saint Adamnan, for instance, with behavior worthy of Till Eulenspiegel <note>A German folklore figure known for playing crude practical jokes</note>.

---

^1 The former in his *Life of Columba* by Adamnan, the latter in his edition of part of this piece.

^2 Nor were the heroes so sacred that they were exempt from being victims of parody <note>original: "burlesque"</note>. In the tale of *Illann the Multi-Colored* <note>original: "Illann ilchrothach"</note>, the son of the King of Spain, the legendary heroes Finn and Ossian not only act out of cowardice <note>original: "'funk'"</note>, but behave with incredible meanness. The Stowe copy of *The Wooing of Eimer* <note>original: "tochmarc Eimre"</note> is immediately followed by a short story: *The Elopement of Eimer with the Son of the King of Norway* <note>original: "aithed Eimre re mac rígh Lochlann"</note>. In this story, Eimer—Ireland's model of chastity and faithfulness (who was Cuchullin's wife at the time)—is portrayed as shameless and unfaithful. The verses attached to this piece clearly show the writer's humorous spirit. It must be remembered that, as many of their nicknames show, the Irish were (and indeed still are) particularly fond of jokes made through irony <note>original: "per antiphrasin," meaning to call something by its opposite</note>.

---

XXX. **Story of a Wicked Girl of the Greeks**: from the *Book of Leinster*. This story is not of Irish origin, but was selected merely because it was of a suitable length.^1

<sup>1</sup> There are some peculiar grammatical constructions in this tale which can be more accurately translated into Latin. It is true that the style is not Ciceronian <note>refined and eloquent, like the Roman orator Cicero</note>; but there is no knowing what Cicero <note>original: "Tully"</note> might have written had he translated literally from Irish. He would have been no worse off for being able to do so.

XXXI. **Abacuc's Perjury**: from the *Book of Leinster*. How someone bearing a Hebrew name<sup>1</sup> happened to be at the Convention of Taillte <note>an ancient assembly and festival held at Teltown</note> is not explained.

---

<sup>1</sup> This name has undergone a process of folk-etymology; we see the intermediate stage on page 78 of the translation, and the final stage in Maurice O’Conor’s copy of Keating’s *History*:—“A.D. 517: Tuathal the Bald-Rough <note>original: "maelgharb"</note>, son of Cormac the Blind <note>original: "caech"</note>, son of Cairbre, son of Niall of the Nine Hostages <note>original: "Néill naoighiallaig"</note> of the seed of Heremon, held the kingdom of Ireland for thirteen years. It was during this time that a beggar’s head fell off at the Convention of Taillte because he had sworn a lie by Saint Kieran’s hand; and he lived for four years in that condition among the monks, without a head” <note>original: "A.D. 517: do ghab Tuathal maelgharb mac Chormaic chaoich meic Chairbre meic Néill naoighiallaig do shíol Eiremhóin ríogacht Eirenn trí bliadna dég. is fá’n am so do thuit a chenn do bhacach i naonach Thaillten tré láimh Chiaráin do thabairt i néithech . agus do mhair sé cheithre bliadna mar sin idir na manchaib gan chenn"</note> (Egerton manuscript 112, folio 348 b).

Our tales having been organized this way, let us collect a few items of evidence regarding the nature and characteristics of the people from whom they originated. First is Strabo (died approximately 25 A.D.), who says that Ireland lies to the north of Britain, that the inhabitants are wilder than the Britons, are <term>cannibals</term> <note>original: "anthropophagy"</note> with enormous appetites, and consider it quite proper to eat their deceased fathers. He excuses this simple act of eating human flesh by claiming that it is also a Scythian <note>an ancient nomadic people of Central Eurasia</note> habit, and that Gauls, Iberians, and many others have resorted to it when pushed to the limit during sieges. He says they had peculiar notions regarding degrees of <term>consanguinity</term> <note>blood relation or kinship</note>; but the great geographer, like the honest man he was, warns us that he had no credible witnesses to back up these claims.<sup>1</sup> Pomponius Mela, writing in the first century, will not—

---

<sup>1</sup> "There are also other small islands around Britain; but Ireland <note>original: "Ierne"</note> is large, lying parallel to it on the north, being longer than it is wide. Regarding this island, we have nothing clear to say, except that the inhabitants are wilder than the Britons, being cannibals and heavy eaters, and considering it a good thing to eat their deceased fathers... we say these things without having trustworthy witnesses. Indeed, it is said that cannibalism is also a Scythian habit, and during the necessity of a siege, the Celts, Iberians, and many others are said to have done this" <note>original: "Εἰσὶ δὲ καὶ ἄλλαι περὶ τὴν Βρεττανικὴν νῆσι... Ἴβηρες καὶ ἄλλοι πλείους ποιῆσαι τοῦτο λέγονται" (Strabo, Geography, Book IV, chapter v, 4)</note>. The reader will notice this excerpt is slightly shortened. That famous Stoic philosopher, Chrysippus, wrote nearly a thousand lines arguing that survivors are obligated to eat their dead:—"And in his work *On the State*, he says it is permissible to unite with mothers, daughters, and sons; he says the same things in his work *On things not chosen for their own sake*..." <note>original: "ἐν δὲ τῷ Περὶ πολιτείας καὶ μητράσι λέγει συνέρχεσθαι καὶ θυγατράσι καὶ υἱοῖς· τὰ δ’ αυτά φήσι καὶ ἐν τῷ Περὶ τῶν μὴ δι’ ἑαυτὰ αἱρετῶν"</note>

<meta>This page continues the critique of early historians' descriptions of Ireland, moving from ancient geographers like Solinus to the Elizabethan scholar Edmund Campion.</meta>

<page-num>XX</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

...allow that there is any element whatever of good in them: they are ignorant of all virtues and devoid of piety.¹ Lastly (among the ancients), in the second century, Solinus—a pedant and plagiarist—writes that the newborn Irish male child had its first solid nourishment gently administered by the mother on the point of her husband’s sword, while she uttered pagan prayers that by such a weapon her offspring (having lived by it) might eventually and honorably die in war.²

Now let us hear the famous Jesuit Edmund Campion, a Londoner and graduate of Oxford. Under the date of June 9th, 1571, he writes in the preface to his Irish narrative:³—

> (i) Irish chronicles, although they are reported to be full of vile <note>original: "lewde"</note> examples, idle tales, and genealogies, "and whatever lying Greece dares in history" <note>original: "et quicquid Græcia mendax audet in historia"—a quote from the Roman satirist Juvenal</note>; yet concerning the state of that wild people, I am persuaded that with choice and judgment I might have drawn from them some better store of matter. I gladly would have sought them out, had I found an interpreter or understood their tongue: the one [interpreters] is so rare that scarcely five in five hundred have the skill for it; the other [the language] is so hard that it requires a stay in the land of more years than I had months to spare for the business.

Upon the authority, no doubt, of his host James Stanihurst (Recorder of Dublin and Speaker of the Irish Commons, an Englishman), he characterizes the natives:—

> (ii) The people are thus inclined: religious, frank, amorous, full of anger, capable of enduring infinite pain, very boastful <note>original: "glorieux"</note>, many are sorcerers, excellent horsemen...

---

"Immediately at the beginning; and in the third book *On Justice*, near the thousandth line, he commands the eating of the dead" <note>original: "εὐθὺς εν ἀρχῇ. εν δὲ τῷ γ Περὶ δικαίου κατὰ τοὺς χιλίους στίχους καὶ τοὺς ἀποθανόντας κατεσθίειν κελεύων" (Diogenes Laertius in the *Life of Chrysippus*)</note>. In other respects, our heathen Irish were not genuine Stoics, because it was only Bacchus <note>the god of wine; i.e., drunkenness</note> that drove them to do what the Greek philosopher preached, and they were much ashamed afterward. Brantôme and Tallemant des Réaux <note>French memoirists</note> tell us what some perfectly sober Christians did: not occasionally, but habitually and by profession; and there are those who know what goes on nowadays. As for Extract XXIX. xiv, referring to Christian times, it must be believed as an "impossibility" <note>original: "per impossibile"</note> or not at all.

¹ "The inhabitants of it [Ireland] are unrefined, and ignorant of all virtues, devoid of piety" <note>original: "Cultores ejus [Ivernæ] inconditi sunt, et omnium virtutum ignari, pietatis expertes" (Pomponius Mela, III. vi, 65)</note>.

² "Whenever a woman in childbirth has brought forth a male child, she places the first food on the husband's sword, and with the tip of the blade she gently introduces the first taste of nourishment into the mouth of the little one, and with pagan prayers she hopes that he will meet his death no other way than in war and among arms" <note>original: "Puerpera siquando marem edidit primos cibos gladio imponit mariti, inque os parvuli summo mucrone auspicium alimentorum leviter infert, et gentilibus votis optat non aliter quam in bello et inter arma mortem oppetat" (Solinus, chapter 35)</note>. It must be confessed that, for sixteen hundred years at least, the far-reaching effectiveness of those ladies’ pagan prayers was abundantly manifest in their remote descendants.

³ Edition of 1809.

<meta>This page continues the quotations from Edmund Campion’s 1571 description of the Irish people, transitioning from their character traits to their religious practices and social customs, before introducing the historian Richard Stanihurst.</meta>

<page-num>xxi</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

...men, delighted with wars, great almsgivers, exceeding in hospitality; the coarser <note>original: "lewder"</note> sort (both clergy <note>original: "clarkes"</note> and laymen) are sensual and loose beyond measure. They are sharp-witted, lovers of learning, capable of any study to which they apply themselves, constant in labor <note>original: "travaile"</note>, adventurous, stubborn <note>original: "intractable"</note>, kind-hearted, and secretive in their grievances (p. 19).

(iii) In some corners of the land they practiced a damnable superstition: leaving the right arms of their male infants unchristened (as they called it), so that it might deliver a more ungracious and deadly blow (p. 21).

(iv) I found a fragment of a letter <note>original: "epistle"</note> where a virtuous monk declares that while traveling in Ulster, a grave gentleman came to him around Easter desiring to confess and receive communion <note>original: "houseled"</note>, having never received the blessed Sacrament in all his life. When he had spoken his mind, the priest asked him whether he was faultless in the sin of homicide. He answered that he never knew the matter was heinous before; but being instructed in it, he confessed to the murder <note>original: "murther"</note> of five people; the rest he left wounded so that he did not know whether they lived or not. Then he was taught that both cases were execrable, and he very meekly humbled himself to repentance (*ibid.*).

(v) One role in the house of a great man is that of a storyteller, who lulls his lord to sleep with vain and frivolous tales, to which the crowd gives truth <note>original: "sooth"</note> and belief. They are so gullible in believing whatever is affirmed with an air of gravity by their superiors (whom they esteem and honor) that a wicked prelate, needing money a few years ago, was able to persuade his parish that St. Patrick—while fighting with St. Peter to let an Irish soldier <note>original: "galloglass"—an elite mercenary warrior</note> into Heaven—had his head broken by the keys of the kingdom; the prelate then took up a collection for St. Patrick's relief (p. 25).

(vi) Where they take a fancy and favor someone, they are wonderfully kind. They mostly trade by exchanging goods, and have absolutely no coins circulating in any great lords' houses.<sup>1</sup> Some of them have rich silver plates; their ladies are adorned with massive jewels rather than gaudy clothing; it is considered a mark of beauty for women to be tall, round, and plump (p. 28).

So far, this writer [Campion], when dealing with contemporary events in Ireland, reveals great bitterness. It would have been better for him if he had stayed with the "wild men" who never harmed him, or in some of the lands he visited after them. When he returned to England, his own "highly civilized" countrymen rewarded his English chauvinism <note>original: "John-Bullism"</note> with a degree higher than any he had earned at Oxford: in fact, on December 1, 1581, they hanged and quartered him <note>Edmund Campion was executed as a Catholic martyr under Elizabeth I</note>.

A far more fair-minded writer was Richard Stanihurst,<sup>2</sup> son of

---

<sup>1</sup> Writers are fond of remarking either that history repeats itself, or that history does not repeat itself, depending on their needs. It is safe to say that here the former saying is the relevant one.

<sup>2</sup> Born in Dublin 1552, died 1618. Except for the coincidence of his birth, he was an Englishman: he could not speak Irish, a defect which in that day completely shut [him out from certain parts of the culture].

<meta>This page continues the introduction to historical descriptions of Ireland, moving from Edmund Campion's accounts to those of Richard Stanihurst, a contemporary Roman Catholic priest and historian.</meta>

<page-num>xxii</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

...the Recorder and Speaker mentioned above, and (like Campion) a Roman Catholic priest; his *Description of Ireland* is printed in Ralph Holinshed’s *Chronicles*. <note>original footnote 1: Holinshed was also a clergyman, died 1593; these excerpts are from the 1583 edition.</note> Several of his passages are identical to Campion’s, which is not surprising; but the following are his own. Under the heading "The disposition and manners of the mere Irish <note>original: "meere Irish"; referring to the indigenous or "unmixed" Irish people</note>, commonly called the wild Irish," we find:

(vii) The men are clear-skinned and of good complexion <note>original: "hew"</note>, and tall in stature. The women are good-looking <note>original: "well favoured"</note>, of clear complexion, fair-handed, and large of frame; they are permitted from their infancy to grow as they please, and are not at all fussy <note>original footnote 2: meaning they were "careful" not to use artificial aids like corsets to shape their bodies</note> about their features or the proportions of their bodies. Their infants (those of the lower classes <note>original: "meaner sort"</note>) are neither swaddled nor wrapped in linen, but folded up stark naked in a blanket until they can walk <note>original: "go"</note> (p. 44:2).

(viii) They are greedy for praise and fearful of dishonor; to this end, they highly value their poets who write in Irish with great learning and compose heroic sonnets, for which they are bountifully rewarded. If they are not rewarded, they release satirical poems <note>original: "libels"</note> to dishonor their subjects (*ibid.*).

(ix) The Irishman insists so much on his noble status <note>original: "gentilitie"</note> that he calls anyone of the English clan <note>original: "sept"</note> who is born and settled in Ireland a "foreign churl" <note>original: *bodach gallda*; literally "English churl"</note>; but if he is a man born in England, then he names him a "Saxon churl" <note>original: *bodach sacsanach*</note>. Thus, in his eyes, both are peasants <note>original: "churles"</note> and he is the only gentleman.

(x) They observe various social ranks, according to which each man is respected. The lowest class among them are little young rascals <note>original: "wags"</note> called *Daltins* <note>original: *dail-tínedha*; original footnote 3: This word is still in common use; Bishop O’Brien’s dictionary explains it as "a jackanapes, a puppy, or an impertinent, insignificant fellow."</note>. These are lackeys who serve the grooms and horse-boys, who are one rank above the *Daltins*. In the third rank is the *Kerne*, who is an ordinary private soldier using a sword and a shield <note>original: "target"</note> for his weapon. *Kerne* signifies (as noblemen of deep judgment informed me) "a shower of Hell" <note>original footnote 4: This is a play on words, suggesting that the Irish word *ceithern* (a body or regiment of men) was a corruption of *cith ifrinn*, meaning "shower of hell" (Latin: *imber inferni*). There is no direct English equivalent for the individual Irish soldier called a *ceatharnach*, though the word is often used in English to mean a "rowdy"; the Scots call such a person a "cateran," meaning a Highland freebooter.</note>, because they are considered no better than scoundrels <note>original: "rakehels"</note> or the devil's—

---

—[which] kept him off from intercourse with natives; in political feeling he was thoroughly English and was a sufficiently severe critic, yet he had some sense of fair play and wrote without bitterness.

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the descriptions of Irish social classes and customs by Richard Stanihurst and Fynes Moryson, focusing on the military ranks, legal customs, diet, and clothing of the "wild Irish."</meta>

<page-num>xxiii</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

...the black guard <note>original: "blacke gard"; historically the lowest-ranking servants in a noble household or camp followers who handled the dirtiest tasks</note>, by reason of the stinking stir <note>original: "sturre"; meaning a commotion or tumult (Scottish: *stour*)</note> they keep wherever they may be. The fourth degree is a Gallowglass <note>original: "Galloglasse"; from the Irish *gallóglaigh*, elite mercenary warriors</note>, using a kind of poleaxe as his weapon. These men are commonly wayward more by profession than by nature: grim of countenance, tall of stature, big of limb, burly of body, and well and strongly built <note>original: "timbered"</note>, chiefly feeding on beef, pork, and butter. The fifth degree is to be a Horseman, which is the highest rank next to the lord and captain. These horsemen, when they have no estate <note>original: "staie"</note> of their own, wander and range from house to house like knights-errant of the Round Table, and they never dismount until they ride into the hall and as far as the dining table (p. 45:1).

(xi) To rob and plunder <note>original: "spoile"</note> their enemies they deem no offense, nor do they seek any means to recover their losses except to wait for a chance to do the same to them; but if neighbors and friends [blood relatives] send their providers to steal from one another, such actions are judged by the Brehons <note>original: *breitheamhain*; traditional Gaelic judges</note> mentioned before (p. 45:2).

->Their food, dress, and language shall be briefly glanced at:—<-

(xii) No meat do they like <note>original: "fansie"</note> so much as pork, and the fatter the better. One of Shane O'Neill’s <note>original: "John O'Nel's"</note> household asked a fellow whether beef were better than pork; "That," said <note>original: "quoth"</note> the other, "is as intricate a question as to ask whether you are better than O'Neill" (Stanihurst, *op. cit.* p. 45:1).

(xiii) Their abundance of grass allows the Irish to have infinite multitudes of cattle; and in the heat of the late rebellion [1598—1603] even the vagabond rebels had great multitudes of cows which they still (like nomads) drove with them wherever they themselves were driven, and fought for them as they would for their altars and families (Fynes Moryson's *Itinerary*, pt. III. iv, 5: ed. 1617, p. 160). They feed mostly on white meats <note>original: "whitmeates"; a term for dairy products like milk, butter, and cheese</note>, and consider sour curds to be a great delicacy, commonly called by them *bonnyclabber*¹ <note>original: "bonaclabbe"</note>; and for this reason they watchfully keep their cows, and fight for them as they would for religion and life (p. 163).

(xiv) The rich wear linen shirts out of luxury and for show <note>original: "wantonnesse and bravery"</note>, with wide hanging sleeves, pleated; thirty yards are hardly enough for one of them. They have now stopped using saffron <note>original: "saffron"; a yellow dye commonly used in Irish tunics or *léinte*</note>, and are learning to wash their shirts four or five times a year (Campion, *op. cit.* p. 24).

(xv) Ireland yields much flax, which the inhabitants work into yarn and export in great quantity. And of old they had such a plenty of linen cloth that the "wild Irish" used to wear thirty or forty ells <note>original: "elles"; a unit of measurement equal to about 45 inches</note> in a shirt, all gathered and wrinkled [i.e., "kilted"] and washed in saffron, because they never took them off until they were worn out² (Moryson, *above cited*).

---

¹ i.e., *bainne clabair* 'clotted milk' = *bainne reamhar* 'thick milk,' depending on the locality.

² Friend Fynes's truthfulness cannot be dealt with here; but in the same breath he tells us that they slept naked. This had been the custom of Europe:— <note>original French: "C'est que nos aïeux couchaient nus, ainsi que nos aïeules. Cette nudité nocturne était encore usitée au temps de Charles VII. [1403—1461]. Toutes les miniatures de nos vieux manuscrits, même les gravures de nos premiers imprimés gothiques, jusqu'à François Ier [1494—1547], s'accordent à placer..."</note> "The fact is that our male ancestors slept naked, as did our female ancestors. This nightly nudity was still in use in the time of Charles VII [1403–1461]. All the miniatures of our old manuscripts, and even the engravings of our first Gothic prints, until the time of Francis I [1494–1547], agree in depicting..."
