<meta>This page continues the discussion of Irish culture and language, drawing on the accounts of Elizabethan writers like Campion and Stanihurst to explain the linguistic and social environment of the time. It specifically addresses how the Norman-English invaders became assimilated into Irish culture.</meta>

## Preface.

(xvi) The language <note>original: "tongue"</note> is sharp and full of wisdom <note>original: "sententious"</note>, offering great opportunity for clever sayings <note>original: "quick apothegms"</note> and appropriate allusions. Therefore, their common entertainers <note>original: "jesters"</note>, bards, and poets are said to greatly delight those who understand the grace and unique character <note>original: "propriety"</note> of the language. But the true [literary] Irish language actually differs so much from what they commonly speak that barely one person in a hundred <note>original: "five score"</note> can write, read, or understand it; therefore, its use is restricted to certain poets and students of antiquity (Campion, *op. cit.* p. 17).

(xvii) And indeed, the language is so difficult—due to the strangeness of its phrasing and the intricate skill <note>original: "curious featness"</note> required for pronunciation—that very few in the country can reach perfection in it;¹ and much less a foreigner or stranger (Stanihurst, *op. cit.* p. 12: 2).

A possible objection—that these examples (being relatively modern) might not apply to tales from much earlier ages—may be anticipated by noting that until 1600, the traditional Irish way of life had not experienced any break in continuity. English influence was so far from prevailing that the opposite occurred. Within an incredibly short period, many of the Norman arrivals threw off their armor <note>original: "surcoats"</note> and other gear to put on the Irish shirt and trousers <note>original: "trews"</note>. They had a great talent for languages and lost no time in obtaining the best dictionaries available. Strongbow himself chose Dermot Mac Murrough's beautiful sister Eva, while the de Burgos (the 'Burkes') began intermarrying with the O'Briens. So it was with others, whose "most important books were women's looks, which taught them very good Irish." Then they employed bards and Brehons <note>traditional Gaelic judges</note>, and became "more Irish than the Irish themselves" <note>original: *Hibernis ipsis hiberniores*</note> of Henry VIII's time. This power of assimilation was so great that Stanihurst complains:

(xviii) Those who were English by birth, by associating with the "wilder" sort of people, became degenerate and—as if they had tasted from the poisoned cup of Ceres <note>In Roman mythology, Ceres is the goddess of agriculture; this refers to being transformed or corrupted, likely a play on Circe's cup from the Odyssey</note>—are completely changed (op. cit. p. 45: 2).

Perhaps the general reader will not find much of interest in the few remarks that follow; however, the well-being of the book and a justifiable concern for my own reputation make them necessary:

In preparing this collection of Irish tales, I have followed the methods...

---
...shows "all the people they represented in bed in a state of complete nudity" <note>original: "dans un état complet de nudité toutes les personnes qu'elles représentent au lit"</note> (Antony Méray, *Life in the Time of the Courts of Love*: Paris 1876, pp. 229–31–33). This applied to princes, knights, and ladies of high rank; everywhere the people showed themselves to be very traditional <note>original: "strong conservatives"</note> in this regard.

¹ This comment and Campion's are as if someone said the same thing about the English general public in relation to the works of Chaucer, Shelley's *Revolt of Islam*, Tennyson's *In Memoriam*, and Browning's poems <note>The author argues that the "difficult" Irish mentioned by 16th-century writers was simply a literary or archaic form, much like high English literature is to an uneducated person</note>.

<summary>This page discusses the complexity of the Irish language and the historical assimilation of Norman invaders into Gaelic culture, noting how they became "more Irish than the Irish themselves." It also defends the use of 16th-century sources to illustrate much older Irish tales by highlighting the cultural continuity that existed until 1600.</summary>

<keywords>Irish language, Norman conquest, assimilation, Strongbow, Stanihurst, Campion, Brehons, Gaelic culture, cultural continuity</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface, shifting from a discussion of the Irish language's difficulty to the author's specific goals for this volume and their editorial methodology.</meta>

<page-num>xxv</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

of my own, born from a theory that these studies can be popularized only by a division of labor. Accordingly, I aspire to a role no higher than that of the humble quarryman who painfully extracts the raw stone, hauls it to the surface, and there leaves it to be handled as they please by the stonecutter and sculptor, architect and engineer. Here is raw material for the "Celtologist" <note>original: "keltologue"</note> and "philologist" <note>original: "philologue"</note>, for the folklorist, the comparative mythologist, and others. Personally, I cannot boast of being anything that ends in either "-logue" or "-ist"—at least in these countries. Were I back in the United States, I should of course profess at least the arts of "breath-ist," "eat-ist," "sleep-ist," and "walk-ist."

The strategy <note>original: "plan of campaign"</note>—for it is a campaign—demanded that anything outside of Irish matter and its English equivalent should be kept to a minimum. At the same time, it was necessary to give non-experts some sort of foothold in what is otherwise a hopeless swamp of names and events entirely new to them and lacking dates. Rather than providing a body of cheap, second-hand notes stolen <note>original: "pillaged"</note> from the printed works of John O’Donovan <note>A renowned 19th-century Irish scholar and topographer</note>, I have appended the Extracts. For two reasons, the text of these is not (and ought not to be) in the Irish volume: first, the Irish volume was already at the binder’s before it occurred to me to add such an appendix; second, the print run of this English volume is much larger than the other.

This work makes no claim to be a critical edition. Where an editor is denied the opportunity to compare different versions of a text, such a thing is impossible; besides which, the work could not be extended and delayed indefinitely. I hope only to see it occupy the rank which scholars of the East <note>original: "Orientalists"</note> agree in giving to the products of the native presses of Istanbul <note>original: "Stamboul"</note>, Cairo, and Bulaq <note>A district in Cairo known for its historic printing house</note>: that of a good and careful manuscript. Intentionally—or, as some would say, with malice aforethought <note>original: "malice prepense"</note>—I have tampered somewhat with the spelling <note>original: "orthography"</note> (*and that alone*) of my sources for the sake of uniformity, and I have added accents. In this, the student beginning their Celtic studies will find an advantage, and much space has been saved; details of this method will eventually appear elsewhere. It may be well to add that this method is not suitable for all texts, nor for all editors; in the case of these specific pieces and their editor, it appeared to me to be legitimate.

By way of bibliography, it may be mentioned that VII, text

<page-num>xxvi</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

...with a German translation, was printed by Ernst Windisch¹ in 1884. Section VIII, another version from the Book of Lecan <note>An important medieval Irish manuscript compiled between 1397 and 1418</note>, was printed by Brian O'Looney² in 1870. Section XIII is printed in the *Journal of the Kilkenny Archaeological Society* (4th series, volume 1, page 96) by J. O'Beirne-Crowe, whom I also knew. He was a man whose vast amount of genuine knowledge was unfortunately spoiled by eccentric whims in his translations. A Highland tale with the same name as section XX, though sharing no other common elements, appears in *Fire-side Stories* (D. Nutt, London, 1890). Sections XXII and XXVIII are edited by Whitley Stokes in *Revue Celtique*³ volume 13. 

A portion of section XXIX is included in John O'Donovan's *Three Fragments*, edited in 1860 from a paper manuscript written more than a century after *Egerton 1782* <note>original: "Eg. 1782"; a famous 16th-century Irish manuscript now in the British Library</note>, a codex with which O'Donovan does not seem to have been familiar. For section XXX, compare it with item LXXVII in Sir Frederick Madden's *Early English versions of the Gesta Romanorum* <note>original: "Gesta Romanorum"; Latin for "Deeds of the Romans," a popular medieval collection of anecdotes and moral tales</note>, from manuscript *Additional 9066* (re-edited by Sidney J. H. Herrtage in 1879), page 394: "Of the penance of a Woman who had committed three Murders." Also see Méon's *New Collection of Fables and Tales* <note>original: "Nouveau recueil de Fabliaux et Contes"</note> (Paris, 1823), volume 2, page 256: "Of the Queen who killed her Seneschal" <note>original: "de la Roine qui ocist son Senechal"</note>, and Le Grand's *Fables* <note>original: "Fabliaux"</note> (Paris, 1781), volume 5, page 189. I owe this suggestion to Norman Moore. 

Between O'Curry's *Manuscript Materials* and *Manners and Customs*, d'Arbois de Jubainville's *Attempt at a Catalog* <note>original: "Essai d'un Catalogue"</note>, and the Royal Irish Academy (R.I.A.) facsimiles, any researcher should have no trouble finding other manuscript versions.

The pieces referred to as LU, LL, LB, and BB <note>Abbreviations for major Irish manuscripts: Leabhar na hUidhre, Book of Leinster, Leabhar Breac, and Book of Ballymote</note> are derived from those facsimiles. The last of these is a photograph; the first three, which are lithographs, are magnificent monuments of modern Irish calligraphy. They deserved a better fate than what befell the capable and gentle man (the last in a lineage of scribes) who produced them, who...

---

¹ Professor of Sanskrit at the University of Leipzig, one of the few distinguished continental scholars in this field who follow the golden rule: "Use your own property in such a way that you do not harm another's" <note>original: "sic utere tuo ut alienum non lædas"</note>. With his usual kindness, he sent me the offprint <note>original: "separat-abdruck"</note> of his paper read before the Royal Society of Sciences <note>original: "Gesellschaft der Wissenschaften"</note> of Saxony, on July 29th as mentioned above.

² In the Academy's Irish Manuscript series, volume 1, part 1.

³ On January 18th of this year, at which time my text and translation of section XXII were already in the printer's hands, I was offered a loan of this edition. I declined it, however, and never saw it until October 1st (when I requested it at the British Museum), nor have I changed a single tiny bit <note>original: "tittle"</note> of my own work as a result. The same applies to section XXVIII, which I first saw on December 1st, long after mine was printed.

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor's preface, moving from a bibliographic list of sources to a personal account of the difficult conditions under which the book was produced and acknowledging the patrons and friends who assisted him.</meta>

<page-num>xxvii</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

...have had his last years embittered, if not his end hastened, by the outrageous attacks of incompetent critics. I knew Joseph O’Longan <note>Joseph O'Longan (1817–1880) was a renowned Irish scribe and illustrator for the Royal Irish Academy</note> well.

Higher up I spoke of tossing straws; but to me, the person tossing them, this task has felt like the tossing of a caber <note>original: "caber"; a long, heavy wooden pole used in Scottish Highland games. The author uses this as a metaphor for the massive weight of the project</note> as large as they make them. The main body of the work, including the indices, was printed between April 4, 1891, and November 11, 1892. It was started with only enough manuscript <note>original: "copy"; printer's term for the text to be set</note> to furnish 16 pages each of the Irish text and the translation. During that period, therefore, the entire text was prepared for the press. Much of the text and all of the translation were written twice: first as a rough version, and then as the revised one. I confess that, with the benefit of more time, this version might have been made much better than it is. Anyone who has experienced what it is like to work on a difficult project for a long period while trying to stay just ahead of an energetic printer will understand me. At this rate of speed, the "Extracts" were not only written and translated, but tracked down and discovered as well. Index C had to be omitted: it was completed and, besides "matters," contained many words and phrases that seemed worth noting, along with quite a few corrections; but there was simply no room for it.

From first to last, I have worked alone. I have received no textual help whatsoever; and if it is true that "it is more blessed to give than to receive," then native Irish scholars, both past and present, must indeed be considered very blessed. <note>The author is likely making a sarcastic remark about the lack of help or the stinginess of other scholars in his field.</note> I have never even seen one of the several volumes of Irish stories translated into English (without the Irish text) that are so often quoted.

However, I am under serious obligations of another kind, and it is with much gratitude that I acknowledge them:

The late Duke of Devonshire, with his usual generosity, agreed that for my purpose the *Book of Lismore* <note>A famous 15th-century Irish manuscript containing lives of saints and secular tales</note> should be temporarily deposited in the British Museum. Lady Louisa Egerton was kind enough to transport it there herself, after Edward J. L. Scott, the Keeper of Manuscripts, had first kindly agreed to take charge of it. This loan was continued to me in the same spirit by the current Duke.

I am much indebted to my countrymen and friends, Norman Moore (mentioned above) and J. J. Mac Sweeney: to the former for the unlimited use of his excellent Irish library, both as a reader and a borrower; and to the latter for the speed and accuracy of his answers...

<summary>The author describes the intense pressure of preparing this massive work single-handedly over eighteen months and expresses gratitude to the Duke of Devonshire and other friends for providing access to essential manuscripts and libraries.</summary>
<keywords>Standish Hayes O'Grady, Joseph O’Longan, Book of Lismore, publishing history, British Museum, Duke of Devonshire, Norman Moore, J. J. Mac Sweeney, translation process</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page concludes the editor's preface, offering final acknowledgments to his research assistants and printers, explaining technical choices regarding the typography of the Irish language, and clarifying his identity to avoid confusion with his famous cousin.</meta>

<page-num>xxviii</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

...to my queries concerning <note>original: "anent"</note> the classification and details of the manuscripts <note>original: "MSS."</note> in his care as the Assistant Librarian of the Royal Irish Academy.

I must also give sincere thanks for the goodwill and intelligent interest shown by everyone involved in the physical production of this book. The Irish text was printed as easily and correctly as the English, and the entire process went without a hitch.

This leads me to briefly explain why I did not use Irish type <note>traditional Gaelic script</note>. The reason is simply a business one: it was commercially impossible. The old character script is best for the kinds of texts I have printed here, where aspirations <note>original: "aspirations"; a linguistic change in certain Irish consonants, often marked with a dot in traditional script or an "h" in Roman type</note> are common. However, from a scientific perspective, it is not suitable for the oldest texts because they require italics, which are not available in Irish type^1.

I have one deep regret, and it is a sharp one: that Sidney Williams, the head and founder of the publishing house from which *Silva Gadelica* <note>Latin: "The Forest of the Gaels"</note> is published, is no longer alive to see the completion of a project he so readily and kindly undertook at my request.

Let me conclude by mentioning—since I am often frustrated by having a relative's <note>Likely referring to his cousin, Standish James O'Grady (1846–1928), a famous author of the Irish Literary Revival</note> good work attributed to me—that my "trademark" (without which no work is officially mine) is either the name as it appears on the title page, or written out in full as:

->STANDISH HAYES O'GRADY.<-

***

^1 There have been many irrelevant claims about the difficulties of using Irish type and the impossibility of being accurate with it. But what about the work of setting and correcting Arabic, Hebrew, Sanskrit, and mathematical texts? I can personally guarantee that if the demand miraculously increased enough to justify buying an Irish font <note>original: "fount"</note>, not a single complaint would be heard from the office of Messrs. Green and Son.

<summary>Standish Hayes O'Grady concludes his preface by explaining the commercial reasons for using Roman rather than Gaelic type and distinguishing himself from his cousin, Standish James O'Grady.</summary>

->SILVA GADELICA.<-

## Life of *Saint Kieran of Saighir.*

"The most blessed bishop Kieran, the first-born of the saints of Ireland" <note>original: *Beatissimus episcopus Ciaranus sanctorum Hiberniæ primogenitus*</note>—that is, Bishop Kieran of Saighir was the first saint born in Ireland. He was from the eastern portion of Leinster, which is called Ossory. At that time, all the Irish people were non-Christians and pagans <note>original: "gentiles"</note>. Laighne was his father’s name, and he was among the nobles of Ossory; his mother’s name was Liadain, and she was from the southern part of Munster, specifically of the *Corca-laighde* race.

Before she conceived Kieran in her womb, his mother had a dream: it was as if a star fell into her mouth. She told this dream to the magicians and the wise men of the time, and they said to her: "You will bear a son whose fame and virtues shall be great until the end of the world [that is, well-known]." Afterward, that holy son Kieran was born; he was actually brought forth and raised in *Corca-laighde*, on the island called Cléire <note>Modern Cape Clear Island</note>. Truly, God chose him while he was still in his mother’s womb.

When Ireland had first heard Christ’s name—even if only vaguely—the spirit of Christian devotion had its first origin in Kieran. His parents and everyone else marveled at how all his deeds were virtuous. He was mild in nature and sweet in conversation; his qualities were blessed with success, his counsel provided instruction, and he possessed all other traits belonging to a saintly man.

One day when he was in Cléire—being only a young child at the time—he performed his first miracle. A kite <note>A type of bird of prey</note> came soaring in the air right over him and, swooping down before his face, snatched up a little bird that sat upon its nest.

<vocab>Saint Kieran, Saighir, Ossory, Laighne, Liadain, Corca-laighde, Cléire, Munster, Ireland</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces Saint Kieran of Saighir, identified as the first-born saint of Ireland, detailing his noble lineage, his mother's prophetic dream of a falling star, and his first miracle as a child on Cape Clear Island.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Kieran, Saighir, Ossory, Liadain, Corca-laighde, Cléire, hagiography, Irish saints, miracles</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the life of Saint Kieran of Saighir, following the incident where a kite snatched a small bird from his presence.</meta>

# Saint Kieran of Saighir

Kieran felt compassion for the little bird and thought it was a terrible thing to see it in such a state. At that moment, the kite turned back and dropped the bird—which was half-dead and badly injured—in front of Kieran. But Kieran commanded it to rise and be whole. The bird got up and, by God's grace, returned to its nest completely healed.

Kieran spent thirty years <note>original: "a score and ten years"</note> in Ireland living a life of holiness and perfection in both body and soul before he was even baptized. During this time, as we have mentioned, the Irish people were still pagans <note>original: "gentiles"</note>. However, because the Holy Spirit dwelt within His servant Kieran, he lived in devotion and followed perfect paths for that entire duration. Eventually, he heard reports that Christian piety flourished in Rome. He left Ireland and traveled there, where he was instructed in the Catholic faith. 

He remained in Rome for twenty years, reading the Holy Scriptures, collecting books, and learning the rules of the Church. When the Roman people observed Kieran’s wisdom and skill <note>original: "cunning"</note>, his devotion, and his faith, he was ordained into the Church. Afterward, he began his journey back to Ireland. Along the way from Italy, he met Patrick, the primate <note>The chief bishop or highest-ranking church official</note> of Ireland. When these two men of God saw each other, they rejoiced greatly and were filled with happiness. At that time, Patrick was not yet a bishop, but he was ordained as one later on.

It was Celestinus <note>Pope Celestine I, who reigned from 422–432 AD</note> who ordained Patrick as a bishop and then sent him to preach to the Irish. Although there were other saints in Ireland before Patrick, God reserved the leadership and primary authority <note>original: "magistracy and primacy"</note> of the country for Patrick until he arrived. Until his coming, none of the Irish kings or lords were converted to the faith by anyone else’s efforts.

Patrick said to Kieran: "Go before me into Ireland. In the center of the country, where the northern and southern parts meet, you will find a spring. Build a monastery at that spring—the name of which is *uarán* <note>An Irish word for a cold spring or well; this refers to Seir Kieran in County Offaly</note>. There your honor shall remain forever, and there you shall await your resurrection." 

Kieran answered, "Tell me the exact spot where this spring is located." 

Patrick replied, "The Lord will be with you; simply go straight ahead. Take my little bell with you; it will remain silent until you reach the spring I mentioned. But when you arrive there, the little bell will ring out with a clear, melodic voice. In this way, you will recognize the spring. After twenty-nine years <note>original: "nine years and a score"</note>, I will follow you to that place."

They blessed and kissed each other, and Kieran went his way.

<summary>Saint Kieran travels to Rome to study Christianity for twenty years before meeting Saint Patrick, who instructs him to return to Ireland and found a monastery at a spring called Uarán, guided by a miraculous silent bell.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Kieran of Saighir, Saint Patrick, Rome, Irish Christianity, Uarán, monasticism, Pope Celestine, hagiography</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the life of Saint Kieran of Saighir, picking up from his meeting with Saint Patrick in Italy and his return to Ireland to begin his ministry.</meta>

# Saint Kieran of Saighir

<margin>B 2</margin>

Kieran returned to Ireland, but Patrick remained in Italy. Kieran’s bell remained silent <note>original: "without uttering"</note> until he reached the place where the well was located of which Patrick had spoken: namely, Uarán. For when Kieran had arrived in Ireland, God guided him to that well; when he reached it, the little bell immediately spoke with a bright, clear voice. It is called *barcán Ciaráin* <note>meaning "Kieran’s little boat," likely referring to the bell's shape or how it was carried</note>, and as a sign, it is still kept in Kieran’s parish and in his bishop's see. It is carried throughout the surrounding territories to be used for swearing oaths <note>original: "sworn upon [in covenants]"</note> between kings, serving as a legal sanction to ensure they keep their word. Moreover, it is carried among the people generally to gather support for the successors of Kieran’s monastery for everything they might need. The bell was originally made by Bishop Germanus, Patrick’s teacher, who gave it to Patrick.

Regarding the well we mentioned: the very spot where it is located is on the boundary <note>original: "mearing"</note> between two parts of Ireland, with Munster being the southernmost part and... the northern. However, the country is actually in Munster, in the territory men call Ely. In that place, Kieran began to live as a hermit (for at that time, it was entirely surrounded by vast woods). To begin with, he set about building a small cell of simple <note>original: "flimsy"</note> construction; it was there that he later founded a monastery and a main church center <note>original: "metropolis"</note> which everyone now calls *Saighir Chiaráin*. 

When Kieran first arrived there, he sat down under the shade of a tree. From the other side of the trunk, a wild boar of great fury emerged. When the boar saw Kieran, it fled, but then it turned back as a tame servant to him, having been made gentle by God. This boar was the first disciple and the first monk Kieran had there. Furthermore, the boar went to the woods to pull sticks <note>original: "wattles"</note> and thatch with his teeth to help build the cell. At that time, there was no other human being with Kieran, for he had come to this hermitage alone, away from his other disciples. 

From every direction <note>original: "airt"</note> of the wilderness, irrational animals came to Kieran: specifically a fox, a badger <note>original: "brock"</note>, a wolf, and a doe. These were tame toward him, and like monks, they humbled themselves to his teaching and did everything he commanded them.

But one day, the fox—who was greedy <note>original: "gross of appetite"</note>, crafty, and full of malice—came across Kieran’s shoes <note>original: "brogues"</note> and actually stole them. Shunning the community, he headed for his old cave, intending to devour the shoes. This act was revealed <note>original: "shewn"</note>...

<summary>Kieran arrives at the well of Uarán, where his silent bell finally rings, marking the site for his monastery at Saighir. He begins his life as a hermit, miraculously taming wild animals—including a boar and a fox—who serve as his first "monks."</summary>

<keywords>Saint Kieran, Saighir Chiaráin, Saint Patrick, Uarán, barcán Ciaráin, Germanus, Munster, Ely, animal miracles, monastery foundation</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the life of Saint Kieran of Saighir, describing his interactions with the animals of the forest and the growth of his monastic community during the early days of Christianity in Ireland.</meta>

# Saint Kieran of Saighir

Kieran sent another monk from his community <note>original: "familia"</note>—specifically, the badger <note>original: "brock"</note>—to fetch the fox and bring him back to the spot where they all were. The badger went to the fox's den <note>original: "earth"</note> and caught him in the very act of eating the shoes <note>original: "brogues"</note> themselves; the fox had already consumed the leather tabs <note>original: "lugs"</note> and laces. The badger insisted that the fox come with him to the monastery. At evening, they reached Kieran, bringing the shoes with them. Kieran said to the fox: "Brother, why have you committed this theft, which is unbecoming for a monk? You had no need to do such a thing, for we have water in common that is pure <note>original: "void of all offence"</note>, and we have food as well. But if your nature compelled you to think it necessary to eat meat, God would have turned the very bark of these trees around you into flesh for your sake." The fox then begged Kieran for the forgiveness of his sins and asked him to assign a penance. This was done, and the fox did not eat meat again until he had Kieran’s permission; from that time on, he was as righteous as all the rest.

Afterward, Kieran’s own disciples came to him, along with many others. He then began to build a grand monastery. From then on, those animals remained with Kieran in their own natural state, for they amused <note>original: "diverted"</note> him. The Christian faith then grew in Ireland, such that before Patrick’s arrival there, there were three most saintly bishops: Ailbe of Emly <note>original: "Imlech iubhair"</note>, Bishop Ibar <note>original: "Braus"</note>, and Declan in his own land and country, in the Decies of Munster. Meanwhile, in his own territory of Ossory, holy Kieran turned many men to the Catholic faith.

It was after this that the glorious Archbishop Patrick came into Ireland, sent by Pope Celestine <note>original: "Celestinus"</note>; through him, all that land was filled with the Christian faith and baptism.

A young woman once came to Kieran; he made her a Christian and a true servant of God, and near the monastery, he built a small but respectable cell for her. He gathered other saintly maidens around her, and among them was a most beautiful virgin named Bruinnech, the daughter of a noble lord of Munster. She was dearly and devotedly loved by Kieran’s mother, Liadain; she was under Liadain’s special care and was helpful in every way. But when the chieftain of Hy-Fiachrach heard of the beauty of this girl we have mentioned, with great...

<summary>Saint Kieran disciplines a wayward fox and establishes a monastery where wild animals live peacefully alongside him. The text also notes that Kieran and three other bishops began converting Ireland to Christianity even before the arrival of Saint Patrick.</summary>
<keywords>St. Kieran of Saighir, monastery life, Celtic saints and animals, Ailbe of Emly, Bishop Ibar, Declan of Ardmore, St. Patrick, Bruinnech, Liadain, Ossory</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the life of Saint Kieran of Saighir, moving from his interactions with animals to a conflict with a local chieftain named Díma over a woman named Bruinnech.</meta>

# Saint Kieran of Saighir

With bands of Irish foot soldiers <term>original: kerne</term>, he came and carried her away by force; his name was *Díma*, and she remained with him in his castle for a long time. Indeed, she slept with him, and he held her in exceedingly high regard. Kieran came to *Díma* to demand the girl's return, but *Díma* refused to let her go. He further declared that he would by no means allow her to depart unless it were the cry of a stork that woke him the next morning (it was winter at the time and a great snow had fallen; yet on the spot where Kieran and his disciples stayed, not a single flake fell). On the following morning—even though such a thing was against nature—a stork cried out from every rooftop within the castle grounds. When *Díma* heard this, he quickly sought out Kieran, fell on his knees before him, and released the young woman. She was pregnant at the time, which was not pleasing in Kieran’s sight; therefore, he made the sign of the Holy Cross over her body, and her pregnancy <note>original: "burden." In medieval hagiography, a saint miraculously ending a pregnancy was often presented as a way to restore a woman's social status or "purity" after an abduction.</note> completely vanished. Then he led her to her own cell, which is now called *Cill Liadain* <note>Modern Killyon, County Offaly</note>.

However, *Díma* was still greatly entangled by his love for the woman and repented that he had released her. He returned to carry her away once more, but God acted according to the will of three people: Kieran, his mother, and the woman herself. Consequently, when he arrived at the town, *Bruinnech* died. *Díma* took this poorly and said to Kieran: “Why have you slain my wedded wife, who never knew another man before me, for I bound her to myself as a lawful spouse? Therefore, you shall no longer live in this place; I will expel you from it.” 

Kieran answered: “The powers by which you might do that, or any other thing, do not come from yourself. It is God who has given you your authority <note>original: "faculty"</note>, which is but an earthly shadow for as long as it may please Him. Therefore, I will not leave my place for you; whether you like it or not, I will remain here.” 

When *Díma* heard this, he departed in a great rage and uttered threats against Kieran. But in punishment for his injustice, God's distress fell upon him; when he reached his castle, he found it and all the surrounding buildings on fire. Now, a favorite young son of his had been forgotten in the house, asleep in *Díma’s* bed. When his nurse realized that it was impossible for any man to rescue him from the flames, she cried out with a loud voice: “Beloved babe, I entrust you to Kieran of *Saighir* and to his protection!”

<summary>Saint Kieran performs miracles to rescue a woman named Bruinnech from the chieftain Díma, eventually leading to a divine confrontation where Díma's castle is set ablaze.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Kieran, Saighir, Díma, Bruinnech, Cill Liadain, miracles, Irish hagiography, monasticism</keywords>

# Saint Kieran of Saighir

"...I do entrust <term>original: consign</term> you!" At that moment, the flames died down and the area cooled; the child was found unharmed, as if he were merely asleep. When *Díma* saw this, he went to Kieran (who had the bishop named *Aedh* with him). From Kieran, he accepted a severe penance and dedicated his two sons to him: Donough (the son the Saint had saved from the fire) and another son, along with their descendants and future generations. He also granted them the monastery, its income, and a burial place. Then *Díma* returned to his own home with joy and Kieran's blessing. As for Kieran, he grieved that the woman in his care had left the world so quickly, and he knew that from then on *Díma* would no longer act violently toward him. Therefore, he went to where the young woman’s body lay and offered prayers to God on her behalf. She rose from the dead and lived for a long time afterward.

On another day, the steward Kieran had appointed to oversee the monastery's construction came to him and said, "We have no pigs." Kieran answered, "Just as God gives us everything else, He will also provide pigs." Sure enough, the next morning an enormous sow came to the workmen followed by a dozen piglets; eventually, many pigs were bred from them.

On yet another day, the same man asked Kieran for sheep. Kieran said, "The One who gave us pigs will give us sheep." When the steward went outside, he saw twenty-eight <note>original: "score and eight"</note> white sheep grazing on the green. He took them in, and from them came many sheep.

There was a certain powerful man in that country who brought his dead son to Kieran to be brought back to life (the boy's name was *Laeghaire*). After Kieran prayed to his Lord, the lad rose from the dead and lived for a long time afterward. In gratitude, the man granted the land called *Ráth-feráin* to Kieran and his successors <term>original: representative</term> forever.

It was after this that Patrick the Preacher came into Ireland to see the King of Munster: *Aengus* son of *Nadfraech*, who believed in God and in Patrick; and Patrick baptized him.

During that time, a descendant <term>original: seed</term> of *Duach* from the region of Ossory arrived and intentionally killed Patrick's horse. He was seized by the king's men and immediately placed in chains to be put to death. However, his friends pleaded on his behalf—

<meta>This page continues the life of Saint Kieran of Saighir. After Kieran miraculously provided livestock and raised the boy Laeghaire from the dead, he continues his ministry, dealing with King Angus and the recovery of his lost harpers.</meta>

# Saint Kieran of Saighir

Kieran went to the king and, instead of the other, gave him a wealth of gold and silver, thereby arranging for the prisoner to be set free to return to his own country. But after Kieran left, the treasure vanished into nothing. Because of this, the king became angry and summoned Kieran. He asked him why he had given him empty riches (that is to say, fake wealth) in exchange for the culprit he had held. Kieran answered and said:

> "All riches, whatever they may be, come from nothing and must return to nothing."

The king was angry again and threatened Kieran, but God’s vengeance fell upon the king. In an instant, his sight was taken from him, and he fell to the ground in front of everyone present. Then *Carthach* (who was a pupil of Kieran and a relative of the king) came and pleaded with Kieran on his behalf. Eventually, through the prayers of *Carthach* and many others, Kieran restored the king's sight <note>original: "relighted his eyes"</note>, and he stood up healed. To many, it had seemed as though the king were dead and that Kieran had effected his resurrection. Once he had risen, the king gave many gifts <term>original: alms</term> to Kieran and gave thanks to God.

King Angus had some excellent harpers at that time; they were very melodious as they sang poems and played their harps. One day, as they were walking through *Muskerry* in the province of *Munster*, they were killed by some of their enemies. Their bodies were hidden in a lake <term>original: loch</term> next to the open field where they were killed, and their harps were hung in a tree on the lake's shore. 

Angus was troubled by their absence and was upset that he did not know what had happened to them. However, he knew that Kieran was filled with the power of the Holy Spirit, so he went to him to find out what had happened to the harpers. Since the king had embraced the Christian faith, he would not seek answers from his magician <note>In this context, the king's "magician" likely refers to a court Druid.</note>. Kieran said to him:

> "Your harpers have been killed secretly, and their bodies are hidden in a lake near the spot where they were slain; their harps, moreover, are hanging in a tree on the lake's shore."

The king begged Kieran to go with him to the lake so that he could find the bodies and have them recovered. They went to the lake, and Kieran fasted for three days so that it would be possible to recover the bodies. When the three-day fast was finished, the lake's water receded <term>original: ebbed</term> so much that they were no longer hidden at all. They

[The harpers' bodies] were lifted and brought into the presence of Kieran, who prayed to God so that, in front of all the men, the dead rose as though they had only been sleeping. There were eight of them, and they had been in the lake <term>original: loch</term> for an entire month. On Kieran's instructions, they took their harps from the tree and, in the presence of the king, Kieran, and everyone else, played a delightful melody. The music was so beautiful that a great number of the crowd fell asleep. Glory was given to God and to Kieran. As for the lake in which they had been drowned, from that time on, water no longer collects there. However, to commemorate the miracle, it is still called *Loch na gCruitireadh* <note>original: *loch na gcruitiredh*; translation: Lake of the Harpers</note>. Then Kieran, after receiving the blessing of the king and the harpers, returned to his own monastic center <term>original: metropolis</term>.

On another day, as King Angus’s steward walked through the land called Muskerry <note>original: *Múscraighe tíre*</note>, he came across a herd of swine and ordered his people to kill one of the pigs. They killed it and took it into the nearest woods to eat it. <insert>[While they were doing this]</insert>, certain enemies happened upon them and killed the steward and twenty of his men on the bank of the river called the Brosna <note>original: *Brosnach*</note>. When Kieran was informed of this by his pupil Carthach (who was King Angus’s brother, or perhaps his grandson) and by others, he was begged to go and fetch the bodies of that group so that wild beasts would not devour them. 

Having reached the bodies, Kieran saw that the number of people with him was not enough to carry them all to the church. Therefore, with a loud voice, he said: “In the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, rise up, you wretched people! Come with me!” They rose immediately (the pig also rose with them). A certain holy man named Eochaid, who was from that same country, returned to his house; but those who were raised up <insert>[and had not previously been holy men]</insert> became pious monks with Kieran from that time on.

On yet another day, Kieran was walking and happened upon a thicket <term>original: brake</term> where there was a great abundance of blackberries. Through his prophetic vision, he understood that <insert>[for some purpose]</insert> these would be needed later. The king, his queen, and a great multitude of people came to attend the feast, which took place just after Easter. 

At this banquet, the queen fell in love with Concraidh because he was exceptionally handsome. She begged him to return her affection, but Concraidh refused. To stay behind in the town with Concraidh after the king left, the queen feigned illness. She claimed that if she could have blackberries to eat, she would be cured—thinking that it would be impossible to find blackberries at that time of year. 

Because of the king, Concraidh was afraid to let her stay in the town. Therefore, he went to his own patron saint, Kieran, and explained everything that had happened (for every place in Ossory was under Kieran’s spiritual authority). When Kieran heard the matter, he said:

> "She shall have her blackberries."

He went to a thicket <term>original: brake</term> where he had left some blackberries covered up during the previous autumn. He brought back a vessel full of them and sent them to the queen through Concraidh. She ate them and was immediately cured, for she suddenly lost all her desire for him. Furthermore, the queen and everyone else who ate the blackberries found that they tasted like honey. 

She realized then that Kieran had performed a miracle for her. She went to him, humbled herself, and begged for forgiveness. Kieran granted her absolution and his blessing, but said:

> "I cannot save you from the death that has been decreed for you. Both you and Angus must die in battle on the same day, but God will have mercy on your souls."

(This queen was Eithne Uathach <note>original: *Eithne Uathach*</note>, the daughter of Crimthann, son of Enna Cinnselach. Saint Patrick had foretold this for them, and Kieran confirmed it on this occasion. It proved true: for Angus and the queen were killed by Muirchertach mac Erca, Illann (son of Dúnlang, king of Leinster), and the forces of the Northern Half <term>original: "Conn’s Half"; refers to Leth Cuinn, the northern half of Ireland</term> at the Battle of Kellistown <note>original: *Cill-osnadh*</note> on the plain of Moy-Fea in Offaly <note>The original text notes "Offaley (sic)" because Moy-Fea is historically in County Carlow, not Offaly</note>. That battle took place on the eighth day before the Ides of October <note>October 8th</note> in the year of our Lord 493 <note>original: "thirteen years and fourscore and four hundred years"</note>. Saint Patrick died in that same year.)

<header>### Saint Kieran of Saighir</header>

A large crowd came to Saighir (where Kieran was), and eight oxen were slaughtered for them along with other meat provided for the occasion. Someone said to Kieran, “For such a great multitude of people, what is the use of the meat we have here?” Kieran answered, “He who in the wilderness satisfied many thousands with a little bread and fish may well ensure that this small portion of meat satisfies these numbers.” He blessed his own well and turned its water into wine; and by the grace of God and Kieran, it happened that as long as the crowds present desired it, they had plenty of meat and wine.

Another time, the King of Tara <note>The High King of Ireland, representing the northern powers</note> came with a strong force to take hostages <term>original: "pledges," referring to the practice of taking noble hostages to ensure the payment of tribute or loyalty</term> from the men of Munster. Olioll, the King of Cashel, would not submit to him and gathered a large army to oppose him. They met close to Kieran’s main monastery <note>original: "metropolis," referring to Saighir as a major ecclesiastical center</note>. Kieran would have gladly made peace between them, but they would not accept his mediation. Therefore, he obtained from God what he could not get from these proud people: as the men of Munster marched toward the battle, a mighty forest suddenly sprang up in front of them. To block the army of the North <note>original: "Conn’s Half," the traditional division of the northern half of Ireland</note>, the River Brosna <note>original: *Brosnach*</note> flooded over its banks so that no one dared cross it. 

When they saw this miracle, they were seized with fear. The king, seeing the current—which had previously been easy for his army to cross—now rising against them, turned away from the stream and returned to his own country. That night, the Munster army stayed near Kieran’s monastery. He sent the king a beef and a pig, already cooked; the entire army was full from this meat, and they even left leftovers. Through these various miracles, the names of God and Kieran were honored.

On another occasion, large bands of marauders came from other countries into the borders <term>original: "marches"</term> of Munster to plunder and kill. However, a good man of Munster named Lonan overtook them, and the outlaws fled. When the outlaws saw they could not escape, they prayed to Kieran to save them from their desperate situation. Just as Lonan and his people were about to capture and kill them, a thunderbolt fell between them and the robbers. Lonan and his men were so afraid that they stopped their pursuit and returned to their own homes. The bandits recognized that it was Kieran’s miracles that had rescued them. They went to him and told him their story; and...

The course they decided upon was to put on religious habits and serve God and Kieran from then on. They did this, and until they died, they remained under Kieran’s guidance in good works and piety.

Another day, a thief named Cairbre from the province of Leinster stole an exceptionally good cow belonging to Kieran’s monks. But as he headed for Slievebloom, a mist and darkness descended upon him so that he could no longer see the path. He fell into a river and drowned, and the cow returned to Kieran and the monks.

On another day, Kieran sent a team of oxen to his nurse, Cuinche, to plow for her, though they had no driver with them. When the oxen arrived, she knew that Kieran had sent them to plow for her. It was a long distance between Kieran and Cuinche’s monastery at *Ros-bennachoir* <note>Likely modern-day Bangor, though the text places it in the "eastern part of Ireland"</note>, which is near the sea. Those oxen plowed on their own and, when the plowing was finished, returned to Kieran without anyone accompanying them. 

It was Kieran’s custom every Christmas Eve—after administering communion to his own religious community <term>original: *familia*, referring to the monastic household</term> in Saighir with his own hand—to travel to his nurse’s monastery at *Ros-bennachoir* to give her the communion of Christ’s Body with his own hand as well. He would then return to his own convent that same night. We understand that it was God who performed this by considering how He worked with Habakkuk <note>original: *Abacus*; the text refers to the biblical story in "Bel and the Dragon" where an angel carries the prophet to Babylon</note>, bringing him from India to Chaldea and back to India again in only a short part of a day. 

Saint Cuinche’s great stone—on which she used to pray and petition her Lord—stood on the seashore a short distance from the monastery. Its name is now *Carraig Chuinche* <note>Cuinche's Rock</note>, and the sea's waves would often wash over it. One day, Kieran climbed onto this stone and it floated on the sea; then, when Kieran wished it, it returned to its place. This was not surprising, for it is written:

-> "God is wonderful in his saints" <note>original: *mirabilis Deus in sanctis suis* (Psalm 135)</note>. <-

# Saint Kieran of Saighir

They were at their leisure to court and pursued it; but when they were about to embrace, a thunderbolt fell between them so that they barely escaped being consumed by fire. They were seized with great fear, and because of their immense terror, they did not speak a single word. They retreated from their meeting, but the virgin <note>In this context, "virgin" refers to a woman dedicated to a religious life, likely a nun in Kieran's care.</note> was struck blind and remained so until her death. It was not an unfair judgment that the woman who had blinded her mind to the point of committing sin should have her physical eyes blinded as well. *Carthach*, for his part, accepted the penance imposed on him and went on a pilgrimage. This clearly shows Kieran's holiness; for God would not allow those two "virgins" <note>Early Christian texts sometimes referred to both male and female chaste monastics as "virgins."</note> in the saint's care to sin, as Kieran was a most devoted shepherd in protecting his flock.

Two brothers named *Odhran* and *Medhran*, from the territory of *Múscraighe-thíre* and the town called *Letrach*, came to Kieran. When they arrived at *Saighir*, one of the men (*Medhran*) longed to stay with Kieran. But *Odhran* said, "This is not what you promised, my brother," and he told Kieran not to keep his brother from him. Kieran answered, "God shall judge between us to whom he belongs. Let him take this candle <term>original: *taper*</term> in his hand and blow on it; if the candle lights, let him stay with me; if it does not light, let him go with you." The candle was placed in his hand, he blew on it, and it immediately caught fire. Therefore, *Medhran* remained with Kieran in great holiness and good works until the day he died. 

Kieran said to *Odhran*, "I tell you, *Odhran*, that even if you travel the whole world, you will die in your own town of *Letrach*. Return there and spend your time there, for the place shall be named after you forever." Following Kieran’s words, *Odhran* returned to his own town and established a respected monastery. His virtue and holiness were great, and after performing many miracles (as recorded in the account of his own life), he went to Heaven. Thus Kieran’s words were proven true, for the place is called *Letrach Odhrain* <note>Modern Latteragh, County Tipperary</note> to this day.

A servant of the king's people named *Cennfaela* killed Cronan, who was a friend of Kieran. The saint resurrected him in the name of Christ—doing so at the end of the seventh day. When Cronan stood whole again in the presence of everyone else, Kieran said: "The man who killed you (specifically *Cennfaela*) shall be slain, and his body shall be burned in the castle which is called the Fort of Ely <note>original: *Rath... of Ely (?)*</note>."

On another day, the King of Munster, Ailill <note>original: *Olioll*</note>, spoke to Kieran with rude words and left him in a great rage. However, it was not long before the king was struck dumb, so that he was speechless for eight days. He came to Kieran and prostrated himself before him; he confessed his unlawful deeds and begged for forgiveness. When Kieran perceived that the king felt true penitence, he blessed his tongue so that he immediately spoke with plain, clear words. Then, after receiving Kieran’s blessing, the king went home healed and praised the names of God and the saint.

One night, Kieran and a pilgrim named Germanus who was with him entered a stream of cold water. After they had been there for a long time, Germanus said: "Kieran, I can no longer endure this water." Kieran made the sign of the Holy Cross over the water, by which he turned it temperate and as warm as a bath; and there they remained, praising God.

Kieran said: "Tomorrow, Germanus, a beloved guest will come to us: specifically *Carthach*, the King of Munster's son and my own student. I sent him on a pilgrimage because of a sin he desired to commit; if God and I had not stopped him, he would have done it. I stopped him because I did not want him to throw away all his previous devotion and his hard work. Having obtained forgiveness for his sins and being cleansed of his fault, he is returning even now. Therefore, take some of the fish that surround you, so that it will be ready for my beloved son." As Kieran had commanded him, Germanus caught a large fish; and on the next day (just as Kieran had said), Carthach arrived.

<meta>This page continues the life of Saint Kieran of Saighir, describing his interactions with other contemporary Irish saints—specifically Saint Kieran of Clonmacnoise and the two Saint Brendans (of Birr and Clonfert)—as well as the miracles and prophecies associated with their meeting.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>

# Saint Kieran of Saighir

Saint Kieran of Clonmacnoise <note>original: "Clonmacnoise" refers to the great monastery founded by the "other" Kieran</note> was arrested and put in chains. This happened because the saint, who was full of pity, had given a large portion of the king’s treasure (which was in Kieran’s care) to God's poor. While Kieran was in prison, the king came to him one day and said jokingly: "If I received four hornless <note>original: "bald"</note> cows, red-bodied with white heads, I would release you." Kieran answered: "God is capable of doing that very thing; but let me out to look for them, and if I do not find them, I will return myself to be at your disposal." His chains were then removed, and he went to *Saighir* <note>modern Seir Kieran</note>, where the other Kieran was. He told him about the matter. At that time, both the Brendans <note>Brendan of Birr and Brendan of Clonfert</note> were with Kieran, and they were all joyful that Kieran of *Cluain* <note>original: "Cluain," an abbreviation for Clonmacnoise</note> had arrived.

The other Kieran said to his steward: "What shall these saints have to eat tonight?" The steward replied that, except for meat alone, he had no food <note>original: "saving flesh alone, he had no meat." In Early Modern English, "meat" referred to food in general, while "flesh" referred specifically to animal meat</note>. Kieran said: "Quickly prepare what you have." The meat then being boiled, Kieran blessed it and, in the presence of the others, changed it as he wished into oil, fish, porridge, and various other foods. Furthermore, by God’s grace, it happened that for the meal of those saints we have mentioned, all the vessels of the house were filled with fine wine.

There was a monk there (named *Mac Congair*) who found it distasteful to eat meat with the saints, and he said that he would not eat the foods that had been made out of the meat. Kieran declared: "You shall indeed eat meat during Lent, and on the day you eat it, your enemies shall kill you; your head shall be taken from you, and you shall not possess the kingdom of God; and you shall spend your life disastrously, for you shall set aside your monk’s habit." Kieran’s words came true, for he was later killed close to Kieran’s monastery at *Saighir*.

Then those four saints (the two Kierans and the two Brendans) made an alliance <note>This refers to a "union" or "covenant" often established between Irish monastic houses</note> between themselves and between those who would succeed them. Kieran of *Cluain*, after taking his leave of the other saints and receiving their blessings, turned to go on his way, though he had no idea where to find the cattle the king had demanded of him. Kieran of *Saighir* walked part of the way with him to escort him, and each gave the other a farewell blessing. Kieran of *Cluain* said: "By the power of my blessing, may there forever be riches, great treasure, and cattle in your town." Kieran of *Saighir* said: "By virtue of my blessing, may there be in your place forever..."

<page-num>15</page-num>

# Saint Kieran of Saighir

"...wealth of both wisdom and piety." When they had then come to the ford called *Ath-salach* on the river's bank, they found four hornless <note>original: "bald"</note> and white-headed cows. Kieran of *Cluain* said: "Do you see how God has given us the cows that the king required of us?" They parted from each other then, after first giving thanks and praise to God, and having given and received blessings and kisses <note>original: "osculations"</note> as a sign of peace and grace. Kieran the Elder returned to *Saighir*, and the other Kieran went to *Cluain*. He sent the cattle <note>original: "kine"</note> to the king, who wondered how it could be that such cows had been found. But since Kieran was now released from his promise, the cows vanished into nothing, so that from then until now no account of them has ever been found. Because of this, the king realized that what he had done to Kieran was unjust <note>original: "unrighteous"</note>.

In the monastery of Clonmacnoise, there was a child named *Crithid*. In performing good works, he was no better than a fool; but in performing malicious deeds, he was very harmful. He came to *Saighir* and stayed for a while with Kieran the Elder. Kieran had ordered that a certain holy fire, which he had consecrated at the previous Easter <note>original: "Eastertide"</note>, must not be extinguished within the monastery until the end of a year, but should be fueled and protected there. Yet, at the Devil's prompting, the child we mentioned came and deliberately extinguished the fire. 

Kieran said: "Know that the accursed child whom people call *Crithid* of *Cluain* has extinguished our sacred fire; vengeance shall come upon him for this, and he will die tomorrow." This was indeed proven true: for on the next day, wolves killed him in the open fields, and he was left there uneaten. Kieran said: "Until Easter, there shall be no fire in the church unless God puts it there." 

But Kieran of *Cluain* heard that the child had died in that way, and he came quickly to *Saighir*, where he was received with great honor. The monastery lacked all fire, however, because it was from that holy flame that they kindled all other fires every night; and Kieran had declared that (unless God sent fire from Heaven) there should be no fire in it until Easter. But guests came to the settlement <note>original: "town"</note> on that day: Kieran of *Cluain* and his companions, who were very cold because it was snowy weather then. Kieran the Elder went out and, with intense prayer, stretched out his hand to God; then, into his cloak <note>original: "breast"</note> fell...

# Saint Kieran of Saighir

...a fiery mass, around which he wrapped the hem <note>original: "mantle’s skirt"</note> of his mantle and took it into the house where the guests were. Once they were warmed, supper was prepared for them. But when they sat down to eat, Kieran of *Cluain* <note>original: "Cluain Mac Nois," modern Clonmacnoise</note> declared that until he should have the child returned to him, he would not eat food <note>original: "meat"</note>. Kieran the Elder said: “We know that such is the purpose of your journey, and God will grant that he comes back to us alive; eat your food then, for that child is on his way to us.” Just as Kieran spoke these words, the child arrived. When they saw him, they gave thanks to God and to Kieran's holiness. They ate their meal, and Kieran of *Cluain*, having received the blessing of Kieran the Elder, departed and took the child with him.

On another day, one of Kieran’s own brothers came and accidentally—not on purpose—extinguished the fire again. He performed penance and was granted absolution. That same day, *Ruadhan* of *Lothra* <note>modern Lorrha</note> came to the town to visit Kieran, and there was no fire in the monastery during the guests' stay. Kieran therefore went to a large stone that was near him and blessed it; immediately the stone caught fire, and in that condition, he carried it to the house where the guests were staying. When *Ruadhan* and his disciples saw this, they gave glory and praise to God and to Kieran.

The brother whom we mentioned, *Báithín*: he spilled a vessel of milk that he was carrying; however, Kieran made the sign of the cross over the container <note>original: "utensil"</note> and it was full again. A holy fear of their master, Kieran, fell upon the brother who had spilled the milk and upon some of the other brothers, after which many were strengthened <note>original: "confirmed"</note> in the faith and in good works.

Kieran prayed to his Lord one day, and an angel came and showed him that the time of his death was approaching soon <note>original: "[comprised] within but a short space"</note>. In the angel’s presence, he requested three petitions from God, and these were granted to him by the angel just as he desired them, for they had been promised to him by God. The first petition was that whoever should be buried in his monastery <note>original: "metropolis"</note>—that is, in his burial ground—the gates of Hell should not be shut upon them after Judgment Day. The second petition was that whoever should show honor to his feast day would not be afflicted by a lack of worldly wealth, and that in the afterlife <note>original: "yonderside"</note> they should possess Heaven. The third petition was that the tribe to which he belonged and for which he was the patron—namely, the people of Ossory—should never be [oppressed] by any foreign...

<meta>This page concludes the biography of Saint Kieran of Saighir, detailing his lifelong dedication to study, his ascetic lifestyle, his work converting the people of Ossory, and his holy death alongside thirty of his ordained bishops.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>

### Saint Kieran of Saighir

...tribe so that any who might come unlawfully to seize their country would be defeated in battle, nor would they themselves go to make an unjust conquest in any other land.

This holy one of whom we have spoken, Kieran of Saighir, was full of humility in every place; until the date of his death, he loved to hear, read, and study <note>original: "learn"</note> the Scriptures. It is also related that he (along with the contemporary saints of Ireland) lived with Finnian of Clonard <note>original: "Clonirard"</note> and entered that school at an advanced age, where he achieved a great understanding of theology. Because of this, he (like the others) was given the title of "Finnian’s pupil." Although he was now elderly, possessed great wisdom, was a perfect teacher (as we have said), and was an honorable bishop, he nevertheless—out of a love for humility and knowledge—was content to continue learning while others simultaneously received instruction from him. 

Furthermore, from his childhood <note>original: "young age"</note>, Kieran never drank anything that might intoxicate him, never wrapped himself in downy or soft clothing, never participated in a banquet, never slept as much as he desired, nor did he ever rush off anywhere for the sake of carousing or good company. He converted his own tribe, the people of Ossory <note>original: "the tribe of Ossory"</note>, as well as many other men, to the faith. He was visited many times by angels; he ordained a great number of bishops, priests, and members of other Church orders. The angel also told him about a sacred well through which many different diseases and infirmities are healed; its name is *Tobar Ciárain* <note>original: "tobar Ciárain," meaning "Kieran’s Well" or Tubberkieran</note>.

Kieran spent thirty years in zealous service to God before his baptism. Then, as he became frail from age and illness, the days of his death approached. He summoned his people and his parishioners from every quarter where they were and blessed them. He commanded them to keep God’s commandments, and on the fifth of March <note>original: "third of the nones of March"</note>, surrounded by choirs of saints, he received the sacraments of the Church with the peace of Christ. He gave up his spirit and, by God’s permission, thirty <note>original: "a score and ten"</note> bishops whom he himself had ordained also went to the Kingdom of God on that same night.

->Here ends the *Life of Kieran*: written by Maurice O’Conor, a ship-carpenter, in Cork.<-

<sig>C 2</sig>

This concerns Molasius' mother; and as for her, by tracing her genealogy, her birth <note>original: "incarnation"</note> was a noble one: for it was the illustrious *Feidhlim Rechtmar*—son of *Tuathal Techtmar* and High King of Ireland—who was precisely her grandfather and, as all acknowledged, the head of her tribe. These two clear pedigrees (which still exist to be recited) plainly show how the great saint’s ancestry was nobly established in Ireland, originating from her two primary seats of authority: from Cashel and from fair Tara, as the poet said of him:

->Noble is Molasius the miraculous . . .<-

Moreover, thirty years before his birth, the excellent Saint Patrick—known for his melodious prayers <note>original: "paternosters," the Latin name for the Lord's Prayer</note>—when he came as Primate to *Benn-osna*, foretold the coming of that glowing flame of the western world: Molasius, son of *Nadfraech*. In verification of the *Tailchenn's* <note>Tailchenn (meaning "Adze-head") was a name the Irish druids used for Saint Patrick, referring to the shape of his tonsured head.</note> prophecy, the poet said:

->Hail to the guest of many virtues . . .<-

One night, Molasius' mother had a dream: she received seven fragrant apples, and the last apple she took in her hand was so large her grasp could not hold it; to her, gold did not seem more beautiful than that apple. She told this dream to her husband, and he said, "I truly understand it: you shall bear an excellent and famous child, whose name will fill the mouths of all Ireland, and he shall surpass his contemporaries <note>original: "coevals"</note>."

In time, the day came for Molasius' mother to give birth, and her labor pains began. A magician <note>original: "magician," likely referring to a druid in this context</note> said to her: "If you delay the birth so that you do not bear the child until the sun rises tomorrow, then shall that child you bear, woman, be illustrious and held in great honor—miraculous, righteous, and very noble—and he shall be an offspring beneficial for the salvation of the westernmost part of the world;" and he said:

->If tomorrow you shall bear a son . . .<-

God Himself delayed the birth in *Monoa's* womb, so that just as the sun rose the next morning, she gave birth . . . upon a certain flagstone. He was taken to Bishop *Eocho*, baptized, and blessed; the bishop also later conferred holy orders upon him, as someone once said:

->Bishop Eocho the angelic . . .<-

### Saint Molasius of Devenish.

Indeed, it was clear that the Holy Spirit's favor was with Molasius, for only a month after his birth, he spoke and praised the Lord, fulfilling the words of the Psalmist, as it was said:

->Even as the psalm says: Out of the mouths of babes <note>original: *ex ore infantium*</note>...<-

He did not swallow... food, nor anything impure, nor anything obtained through theft; and when they tried to feed him against his nature, he would immediately vomit it up. One thing in particular: every bit of physical growth <note>original: "increment that took place in Molasius' flesh"</note> was matched by his growth in humility, excellence, and purity, just as Christ said:

->Whoever exalts himself... <note>referencing Matthew 23:12: "Whoever exalts himself will be humbled, and whoever humbles himself will be exalted."</note><-

So Molasius began his studies, becoming exceptionally wise and knowledgeable; he was also a leader among monks in performing miracles. He kept nothing for himself, but instead gave everything he received to God's poor and needy out of love for his Maker and Creator. He did this to exemplify the words of the Psalmist: He has scattered abroad; he has given to the poor <note>original: *dispersit dedit pauperibus*, from Psalm 112:9</note>:

->For God's sake, he gave to the poor...<-

Another of Molasius' wonders happened when one of his monks mixed meal and water to knead a cake, but had no fire. When the monk complained to Molasius about this, the saint said, "Bring me two coals." When the coals were brought to him, he breathed on them until they flared up like torches. This was a marvelous sight to the monks and, though it was miraculous, it filled them with joy. "Dear sons," Molasius said, "what is difficult for men is easy for God," as it was said:

->If you believe loyally and dutifully...<-

Now the miraculous Molasius and his monks spent the forty days of Lent without consuming any food or drink <note>original: "bite or sup"</note> except for tree fruits and wild plants and herbs. During another Lent, he and his monks went forty days without any food at all, except for a single handful of barley grain provided by the cellarer <note>term: a cellarer is the person in a monastery responsible for the food and drink</note> to each monk once every twenty-four hours—from one midday to the next—as it was said:

->One Lent, Molasius and his monks were...<-

Once, two lepers looking for hospitality <note>original: "entertainment," meaning food and lodging</note> came to Molasius during a season when he had no food to offer them. He sum—

# Saint Molasius of Devenish.

He summoned his cellarer and said to him: "Give those men enough food and drink." The cellarer answered: "I have no food, either little or much." "Go into the kitchen," Molasius said to the cellarer, "and there you shall find two cakes with butter to go with them, two chunks of fish, and two vessels full of milk." The cellarer went and found exactly as the saint had said, whereupon he gave the poor their fill; as one said:
->Once on a time Molasius whose delight was not in folly . . .<-

At another time, all Ireland lay in the grief of death and destruction, being tormented because the plague called *buidhe chonnaill* <note>original: "yellow plague," a devastating pestilence that struck Ireland in the mid-7th century</note> had by then caused a great slaughter of Ireland's best men. During this time, Dermot and Blathmac, the joint kings of Ireland, perished, along with Saint Fechin of Fore, Saint Aileran the Wise, and a great portion of Ireland's nobles. It reached such a point that those who died surpassed all counting and comparison, all reason and memory. According to some, half of the men in Ireland plus one died; others asserted that two-thirds of them perished. The men of Ireland took counsel on how that sickness might be turned away from them; what they all proposed was to observe a day and a night of fasting for God and for Molasius, seeking their help and relief; as the poet said:
->A three days' fast of zealous abstinence . . .<-

However, the men of Ireland fasted for Molasius, and Molasius fasted for his God, so that they received help and relief from that sickness. It was then that they assigned to Molasius a tribute of one *screpall* <term>an ancient Irish silver coin or unit of weight</term> from every house, provided there were at least three in the family; from every chief of a cantred <term>a medieval Irish land division, roughly equivalent to a barony</term> a "cow of three hands" <note>likely referring to a cow of a certain height or value</note>; a riding horse from every provincial king; and from the High King of Ireland, a horse with its battle gear. All of this was to be honorably paid to Molasius and to his community after him forever at Lammastide <note>the harvest festival on August 1st</note>; as the poet said:
->A lamentable plague of hideous sickness . . .<-

After this, the sons of Declan came to seek out Molasius, and he asked them to write the Gospels <note>original: "Evangile"</note> for him. They wrote all the gospels within the span of two days and one night, during which the light never failed them, but stayed as bright as it is during the day. Because of this wonder, Molasius' miraculous power was greatly praised. He had a hood made of badger skins (from which the *brocainech* <note>original: "badger-skin cloak," one of the saint's famous relics</note> is named: one of the finest of Molasius' relics) and in his

# Saint Molasius of Devenish

...holding a small strip of the same leather, went to Hell for the purpose of calling up a certain entertainer <note>original: "jester," here referring to a professional performer or buffoon</note>, namely Manann the leper. Because of Molasius, God brought him out of Hell along with fifty others who shared his name, as the poet said:

->Molasius brought back three times fifty Mananns . . .<-

On another day, Molasius was bathing himself naked in the water. Even if there were ice on the water, it would not stop him. When he was in this state, no one dared look at him, for his skin clung tightly to his bones; this was because he ate so little that his entire week's worth of food would have been a single dinner for anyone else <note>This highlights the saint's extreme asceticism, or self-denial of physical comforts</note>. One of his monks came to look for him, which displeased Molasius. He said to the monk, "Do not do that again, and perform penance for what you have done."

"I will do so, according to your pleasure," the monk said.

"Then come into this water," said Molasius.

The monk replied, "Truly I will." But he had not been in the water long when he said to Molasius, "Because of its great harshness and cold, I cannot endure this water."

"If that is what you say, then come into this other water," Molasius replied.

The monk entered that water, but after only a short time, he found its heat to be so excessive that he said, "Help me, lord, for I cannot support these pains. It is obvious to me that God’s grace is with you, and we will never do anything that you forbid."

Molasius then blessed the water so that it became mild—somewhere between cold and heat. It is certainly true that the elements were made mild for Molasius according to his will and were obedient to his intentions, as the poet said:

->God’s elements, oh, the elements of God . . .<-

One night, while Molasius and his monks were eating their supper, they saw the roof of the house on fire, with flames bursting from it. The monks intended to abandon the house for fear of being burned.

"By no means," said Molasius, "instead, bow your heads and bend your knees and be prepared for death. Leave the matter between me and the fire; let none of you look up."

The monks did so. It was not long before the roof-tree <note>the main horizontal beam supporting the roof</note> of the house fell to the ground in front of them, but the fire did them no other harm than that.

"Understand, beloved brothers," said Molasius, "that your endurance is clear to God..."

# Saint Molasius of Devenish

...and that you are chosen sons of God. Rise now, for God has saved us from the fire,” as one said:

->When Molasius with his monks was . . .<-

Another time, when Molasius was in the house of a good king among the kings of Ireland, it happened that a fire broke out in the house so that it was impossible to save it. Molasius blessed the house and extended his arms in the *croisfighill* <note>original: "cross-vigil," a prayer posture where the arms are held out to form the shape of a cross</note>, and the fire burned only three wattles <note>interwoven sticks used in traditional wall construction</note> in the house. The name of that place today is *Druim Clethchoir* <note>literally "Ridge of the Sheltering Roof"</note>, and the king offered it to God and to Molasius forever, after his own lifetime.

One day, Molasius encountered a synod of clergy. They had a good "book of ways" <note>an itinerary or guide for travelers</note> from which he would have liked to copy some parts, but neither he nor the company had a pen. However, Molasius spotted a flock of birds hovering over him and stretched out his hand toward them. A quill fell from them to him, so that he was then able to write the book, as one said:

->The bird bestowed his quill . . .<-

Once when Molasius and certain of his clerics were traveling through the land of Carbery, he saw a woman milking. He asked her for a drink for his attendant, and the woman said: "I will give milk not only to the lad, but to all of you." 

"That is even better," said Molasius. 

"Well then, my lord," the woman continued, "until now I have been a barren woman; please help me and pray for me so that I might be blessed with a child." 

"If that is the case," Molasius said, "call your husband to us. Let him take my cup to the well and bring it back to us full of water." 

Then the water was placed in Molasius’s hand; he blessed and consecrated it, and passed it to the woman to drink. "Woman," he said, "be assured that from this time on you shall be pregnant and shall bear a son. He will be good, miraculous, saintly, wonder-working, and righteous. It is to him that God the Creator and all the saints of Ireland will give very great honor and perfect privilege. His first name shall be *Mac na Cretra* <note>original: "son of the consecrated elements," referring to the holy water used to bless the mother</note> because of the sanctifying and consecration I performed on the water. But for us, 'the very noble Bishop Finnacha' shall be his permanent name. I hail him and welcome him before his birth." And he pronounced these words:

->A welcome I utter for the subject of a truthful vision . . .<-
