--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the life of Saint Molasius (also known as Molaise) of Devenish. It transitions from his earlier miracles to a prophecy concerning Bishop Finnacha and Molasius's journey to study under Saint Finnian of Clonard.</meta>

# Saint Molasius of Devenish

Then Molasius blessed Bishop Finnacha in his mother’s womb, and this is what he said: “Ireland’s saints and the Creator of all creatures shall bestow on him exceedingly great honor and privilege, and the right of sanctuary. He himself shall be the fifth high saint to have lived in his place: protecting it, answering every prayer addressed to him, avenging any violation of it, and repaying every evil deed and injustice done to it. Let there be shortness of life and Hell for those who despoil it, but Heaven for his successor, provided that he does not curtail his privileges nor diminish his dues,” as one said:

->“It is a birth of virtue that is in your womb, woman...”<-

In time, Molasius became famous. As he was now advanced in years, and his faith, devotion, wisdom, and guidance were well known, he—like every other apostle—went to *Finnian* of Clonard <note>original: Clonirard; a famous monastic school founded by Saint Finnian in County Meath</note> and studied the Gospel there. Afterward, the apostles asked *Finnian* to visit each of their churches in turn to consecrate them. *Finnian* did not wish to do this (as he was an old man), but said: “I will go with whichever saint among you my dun cow chooses to follow.” 

Thereupon the saints departed; the dun cow followed Molasius and *Nindidh* <note>Saint Ninnidh of Inishmacsaint, another prominent saint of the Lough Erne region</note>, and *Finnian* with his twelve apostles followed them to Devenish <note>an island monastery on Lower Lough Erne</note>, where they stayed with Molasius for a year:

->“Twelve saints who were ordained there...”<-

The chief over that land at the time was Red Conall, son of *Daimhín*, whose wizard said to him: “Unless you go to Molasius at Devenish and quench his fire this very night, it is he who shall be lord over this domain and over the entire lake where it sits; and it is his successor after him who shall prevail in voice, power, and privilege.” 

Then Red Conall’s horses were harnessed, and he set off for Devenish, lashing them hard until he reached the place called *Omna Gabtha* <note>literally "The Sticking Oak" or "The Held Oak"</note>. For there, near an oak tree, the horses’ feet were held fast so that they could not move a single step. To the king and his people, this was a wonder and a marvel, but it was also deeply displeasing to them. A young man among his people said to the king: “Let us turn the horses’ heads eastward and...”

<summary>Saint Molasius blesses the unborn Bishop Finnacha and later travels to study with Saint Finnian of Clonard. Upon returning to Devenish, his spiritual authority is miraculously demonstrated when the local king, Red Conall, is prevented by a miracle from attacking the monastery.</summary>

<keywords>Saint Molasius, Devenish, Saint Finnian of Clonard, Saint Ninnidh, Red Conall, Irish monasticism, miracles, Omna Gabtha, Lough Erne</keywords>

# Saint Molasius of Devenish

“...if they start moving immediately, then Molasius is a man of God.” The horses’ heads were turned toward the east, and they set off at once.

As for Red Conall, he left the horses alone and made his way on foot. He had a wicker boat <note>a traditional coracle</note> launched upon *Linn an Tairbh* <note>meaning "the bull’s pool"</note> with a fully cooked <note>original: "sodden"</note> bull in the bottom of it; however, the bull leaped into the lake <note>loch</note> and the boat sank. Furthermore, the king had two white horses with crimson manes and tails, and they died instantly. Then fear seized Red Conall, and he sent an embassy to Molasius, asking him to raise the horses from the dead. Molasius came and brought the horses back to life, which pleased the king greatly. Molasius said, “Let us now make a bargain: on behalf of my Lord, I will grant this region to you and to your son after you; in return, leave me this spot of land where I am now.” The king replied, “I do not thank you for that! This is my own land, and was my father’s and my grandfather’s before me!” “If that is what you say,” Molasius answered, “may neither your son, nor any man of your bloodline <note>seed</note> ever again rule this land.” Molasius turned his back on him, and at that very moment, the king was struck blind <note>original: "the king’s eyes [i.e. sight] were taken from him"</note>.

To continue the king’s story: people had to lead him by the hand to show him the way until he reached his house. Thereupon, in all haste, he ordered a great feast to be prepared, which he sent as a gift to Molasius. He also transferred ownership of the land to him and established all his legal entitlements <note>dues</note> forever. “On behalf of my Lord,” Molasius said, “I restore your sight completely. As long as you live, neither your own fortune nor your rule shall be challenged; but it is certain that none of your descendants shall ever take up the rule again.” As the saying goes:

->A stubborn, unjust war arose...<-

On another occasion, the holy men <note>original: "apostles," referring to Molasius's monks or fellow saints</note> came to *Inis Cométa* <note>likely an island on Lough Erne</note>. They spent a night without a fire, so they sent a young boy who was with them to *Edardhruim* to fetch some. He took two live coals, but on the return journey, he was drowned—both the boy and his coals. The boy was searched for and pulled out of the water, still holding the blackened coals in his hand. Through the power of his Lord, Molasius called him back to life, and his soul returned to him. Then, the saint breathed upon the black, wet coals until they blazed like a torch. God’s and

<summary>Saint Molasius performs miracles involving the resurrection of horses and a young boy, while establishing his monastery's land rights by overcoming the opposition of King Red Conall.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the life of Saint Molasius of Devenish, following his miraculous interaction with Red Conall and the restoration of the king's sight and horses.</meta>

# Saint Molasius of Devenish

Molasius' fame grew great because of this, and someone composed a song <note>original: lay</note>:
->Ireland’s apostles came . . .<-

On another occasion, Molasius was on Devenish Island <note>an island in Lower Lough Erne, County Fermanagh</note> but had no food with him. A large number of uninvited guests came to visit him, for he was the constant refuge for the sick, for those seeking entertainment, and for strangers. Moreover, he was a sanctuary for the poor and the naked, for orphans, and for those in desperate situations. Indeed, everyone who could find no help anywhere else in Ireland—and all those who were unfit for work, or to whom no respect or kindness was shown—eventually came to Molasius so that he might help them against cold and famine, thirst and hunger. 

In any case, this is what Molasius did for his guests and the poor on that occasion: he ordered all the old, black, broken pots in Devenish to be brought to him. He broke these into pieces, divided them into portions, and gave them to everyone as if he were serving bread. Then, whatever kind of food <note>original: meat</note> any individual desired, that very food was produced from their fragment of the pots. Furthermore, for anyone who wished it, the fragment also turned into clothing, according to their own needs and desires. As the poet said:
->Devenish, the island of oxen . . .<-

It happened once that Molasius went to Moycarne while King *Aedh* <note>modern: Hugh</note> was holding a great feast. Molasius sent his servant with a pitcher to ask for ale and meat, but he was refused. When the servant returned and told him what happened, the saint said, "Well then, let the feast—both the ale and the meat—vanish into nothing."

As for the king, his ale turned to salt water <note>original: brine</note> and his meat turned to rot <note>original: foulness</note>. The king came and asked what had ruined the banquet. "That is easily explained," the house steward answered. "Molasius’ servant came here, and I refused him food and drink." 

"You have done an evil deed," the king said. "I dedicate this place to the saint in compensation for the refusal you gave him, and let it serve him forever." Molasius was then brought to them; the king knelt <note>original: made genuflection</note> before him and offered him the land. Molasius blessed the land and the banquet food, restoring <note>original: renovated</note> them so that they eventually became a feast worthy of both the king and Molasius himself. As the poet said:
->Moycarne, the resort of hundreds . . .<-

<summary>Saint Molasius performs miracles of multiplication and transformation to feed the poor on Devenish and punishes King Aedh's stingy steward by spoiling a royal feast, leading to a land grant for the church.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Molasius, Devenish Island, Moycarne, King Aedh, miracles, hospitality, transformation of objects, Irish hagiography</keywords>

# Saint Molasius of Devenish

After all these miracles that Molasius performed throughout Ireland, he decided to go to Rome. His intention was to write the story of his life there and to bring back some of the city's soil and relics to Ireland. He traveled by way of Ferns, the seat of *Saint Maedóg*; this was immediately revealed to *Maedóg*, who sang a song <note>original: lay</note>:

->Tonight a company comes to us . . .<-

Soon after this, Molasius arrived at Ferns. *Maedóg* went to meet him and welcomed him. Afterward, according to Molasius's wishes and preferences, *Maedóg* served him with food <note>original: meat</note> and drink, provided a bed, and shared private conversation. The two great saints agreed that if either of them secretly asked for a favor from God, their prayers would work together toward that goal. They also agreed that whoever Molasius blessed, *Maedóg* would also bless, and whoever Molasius cursed, *Maedóg* would also curse, and vice versa <note>original: et e contrario</note>. Whatever commands one saint issued, both would work together to fulfill them. Molasius also said, "Pray with me that this journey I am taking will benefit the Church in general and Ireland as a whole." Then, between them, they composed a short song <note>original: lay</note> there:

->I entreat your prayer, O gentle *Maedóg* . . .<-

Then Molasius, traveling toward Rome, crossed the sea and came to Tours, the city of *Saint Martin*. He found the church in Saint Martin’s district locked, with only a single guard appointed by God and Saint Martin to watch over it. Molasius asked for the church to be opened for him. "I will certainly not open it," the guard replied, "but if they think it proper, let God and Martin open it for you." At that, the seven locks on the door opened by themselves for Molasius, and the door swung back so that the entrance was thrown wide open. Molasius then celebrated Mass there in honor of God and *Saint Martin*; afterward, he continued his journey to Rome.

One night, while Molasius and his fellow pilgrims were eating supper, they saw a snake approach him. Everyone was seized with fear and horror at the sight, but Molasius called the snake to him and crumbled some bread for it. The snake ate the bread and then licked his hand without doing him any harm.

On another day, as Molasius traveled through the continent <note>original: eastern world</note>, he came upon masons who were at their work.

<summary>Saint Molasius begins his pilgrimage to Rome, forming a spiritual pact with Saint Maedóg of Ferns and performing miracles at the Church of Saint Martin in Tours.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Molasius, Saint Maedóg, Saint Martin of Tours, Rome, pilgrimage, miracles, Ferns, Devenish, hagiography</keywords>

# Saint Molasius of Devenish

Molasius stopped to criticize some workmen <note>original: "find fault with them"</note>, for the way they were doing their task did not please him. The masons attacked Molasius and laid hands on him violently. This was evil in God’s sight, so He turned the masons back, and their hands and feet failed them (for their feet were stuck to the ground and they lost their ability to speak); eventually, Molasius took pity on them and restored their minds and senses so that they would believe. They then believed fervently in God and in Molasius, and the grace of God came upon them, as someone once said:

->[*The scribe omits this poem.*]<-

However, in the twilight of the evening, Molasius reached Rome and found the city shut against him. He asked for it to be opened, but the gatekeeper would not open it for him. Three times Molasius struck the wooden bar <note>original: "hand-log," likely the heavy beam used to bolt the gate</note> upon the city’s gate. Then, a great noise and a booming roar occurred throughout the entire city, so loud that the people of Rome were seized with great fear, saying that the Day of Judgment had arrived. Rome’s great gate swung open, and every single thing in the city that had a lock or any fastening—whether on the inside or the outside—opened by itself. With the gate thus opened before him, Molasius entered Rome and stayed there that night.

On the following morning, all the people of Rome gathered together in one place where the Pope was. The Pope asked everyone assembled there: “Do you know what that great noise was that happened in Rome, which frightened everyone so much?” The gatekeeper came forward and said: “Yesterday evening, when the gates were closed, a tall and pale-faced Irish <note>original: "Gael"</note> cleric arrived and asked to have them opened. I did not open them, but even though I refused, God opened them for him.” The Pope <note>original: "Abbot of Rome"</note> said: “Bring that Irish cleric to us.”

Then Molasius was led to him and was made welcome. He was asked to say Mass in the presence of the Pope and all the people of Rome. They did everything they could to belittle him, deceive him, and test him: they went with him to Saint Peter’s high altar in Rome, which was prepared for Molasius’s use, but they gave him no missal <note>original: a book containing the texts for Mass</note>, no cruet <note>original: a small vessel for water or wine</note>, and no bell. Molasius put on the vestments, but he said that without those three things, the altar was not properly prepared for the celebration of the Mass.

<summary>Upon arriving in Rome, Molasius performs a miracle by causing all the city's locks to open; he is then summoned by the Pope to perform Mass but is tested by being denied essential liturgical items.</summary>
<keywords>Rome, Pope, Saint Molasius, miracles, masons, Saint Peter's Basilica, Mass, liturgical objects, Irish saints</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the life of Saint Molasius of Devenish during his visit to Rome. After miraculously opening the city gates, he is now challenged by the Romans to provide the necessary items for the Mass.</meta>

### Saint Molasius of Devenish

He was about to celebrate Mass at that place. The Romans said: “Let the God who gives you everything provide you with the three things you require from us as well.” 

“This is a test of my faith,” Molasius said, “and my Lord in Heaven hears it.” Even as he spoke, he lifted both his hands high to his Lord and prayed to the King of Heaven for help in this critical moment. When the Creator of all creatures heard this, He sent down a small missal <note>original: "missal," a book containing the texts used in the Catholic Mass</note> onto the altar; He sent a cruet <note>original: "cruet," a small container for the wine or water used in the Eucharist</note>, and a bell along with it. This pleased Molasius well, and there—in the presence of the Pope and the Romans too—he celebrated Mass and performed the holy sacrifice. Afterward, he preached a sermon and cleansed the hearts of all who heard him of evil, wrongdoing, and malice. After the Mass he had celebrated and the sermon he had preached, the Pope and his twelve cardinals, and all the people gathered there, gave him their blessing and with one accord gave him their souls' affection.

Then Molasius said: “What shall be done with these three things which God has laid on the altar?” 

“Take your choice of them,” the Pope answered, “for today you are the one among us who has performed the greatest labor.” 

“I will take,” Molasius said, “that little Gospel.” 

The Pope replied: “*Little* <note>original: "beg"</note> shall be its name forever.” Therefore, people call it *Molasius’s Little Gospel* <note>original: "soscéla beg Molaise"</note>. 

Molasius continued to excel in gentleness, honor, faith, devotion, wisdom, and knowledge. He remained in Rome for a time, where he copied all that was needed of church law and rules, and all the knowledge that was not previously available in Ireland. With the Pope’s permission, he later returned to Ireland as an illustrious archlegate <term>archlegate: a high-ranking representative or deputy of the Pope</term>. When he reached his home, he found the bell that had been given to him on the altar in Rome hanging on a birch branch; he found the cruet in another place. Three times the cruet was sent back to Rome, and each time it slipped away and returned to Molasius; because of this, it was given the name *the deserter* <note>original: "éloidhech"</note>.

He also brought back a load of soil from Rome, along with relics of Saint Paul, Saint Peter, Saint Lawrence, Saint Clement, and Saint Stephen. He brought some of the Virgin Mary’s hair as well, along with an ankle-bone of Saint Martin, a great many relics of other illustrious saints, and some relics of the holy successors of Peter who were buried in Rome.

<summary>Saint Molasius miraculously receives the items needed for Mass from God, receives the Pope's blessing, and returns to Ireland as a papal legate with sacred relics and a miraculous "Little Gospel."</summary>
<keywords>Saint Molasius, Devenish, Rome, Pope, miracle, Little Gospel, relics, Ireland, church law, archlegate</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the life of Saint Molasius of Devenish. Following his time in Rome, where he received a miraculous Gospel book, bell, and cruet from God, he is now returning to Ireland to reunite with his contemporary, Saint Maedóg of Ferns.</meta>

## Saint Molasius of Devenish

Molasius has now arrived in Ireland; this was revealed to *Maedóg* <note>Saint Maedóg (also known as Aidan) was the founder and first bishop of Ferns in County Wexford</note>, and he spoke these words:—

->I hail miraculous Molasius . . .<-

After this, *Maedóg* was not there long when Molasius arrived at Ferns. *Maedóg* went to meet him and bid him welcome. *Maedóg* asked Molasius about all his travels, from the day he departed from Ireland until he had returned again. Molasius told him how he had fared both in Rome and in every other place. “Give me my share of the gifts you bring from Rome,” said *Maedóg*. “I will indeed,” Molasius answered, “and open the front of your robe so that I may lay them in it for you.” 

Then *Maedóg* opened the fold of his garment, and into it Molasius placed some of Mary’s hair and of Martin’s ankle-bone; some of Paul’s relics and of Peter’s, a portion of Laurence’s relics and of Clement’s, and of the relics of Stephen the Martyr. *Maedóg* rejoiced to see the sacred relics in his robe and said to Molasius: “Now I am well provided for by you.” 

Molasius answered: “*Brec Maedhóig* <note>original: "brec Maedhóig," meaning "The Speckled/Variegated One of Maedóg." This refers to a famous decorated reliquary box intended to hold these items</note> shall be its name forever; its privilege shall be complete and its miracles many. No one shall dare to violate it. To the descendants of *Fergna* <note>original: "seed of Fergna," the ruling dynasty of Breifne (O'Rourkes and O'Reillys)</note>, failing to obey it when it happens to be among them shall be a fatal wound of death; to all the children of Brian <note>the O'Briens or Dál gCais of Munster</note>, both east and west, it shall be a venomous fire; and to the children of Niall <note>the Uí Néill dynasties of the north and midlands</note> and to the people of Oriel <note>an ancient kingdom in north-central Ireland</note>, it shall bring destruction and slaughter. Let it be enshrined well; and it is not lawful for anyone but a member of the clergy to carry it, or else one who is free from all defilement whatsoever.” And someone has pronounced a song:—

->By us Molasius’ tale is told . . .*<-

As he was coming from Rome, Molasius happened upon a certain holy man—one who was a namesake of his own, called Molasius the Hebrew—who was floating in the middle of the open sea on a flagstone. Then Molasius and that man swapped places, and it was upon that stone that Molasius reached Ireland. As proof of this miracle and to commemorate this story, that same stone remains in Devenish to this day.

At all events, Molasius, having now returned from Rome, reached—

<meta>This page continues the life of Saint Molasius. Having returned from Rome with sacred relics and gifts for Saint Maedóg, he now establishes his monastery at Devenish and is called to mediate a major political and spiritual conflict at Tara, the seat of the High King of Ireland.</meta>

## Saint Molasius of Devenish

At Devenish <note>An island in Lower Lough Erne, County Fermanagh, where Molasius founded a monastery in the 6th century</note>, he deposited the relics of Paul, Peter, Laurence the martyr, Saint Clement, the martyr Stephen, Mary, and other Roman saints. The reason he brought these relics and samples of soil back with him was so that the Irish people <note>original: "Gael"</note> would not be required to travel to Rome unless they were going for a very significant reason, or unless a saint went there to write an account of his life.

->Well-gotten is the land that we have gained . . .<-

After Molasius had placed those holy relics in the little sanctuary as we have described, he had not been in Devenish long when "the apostles" sent a message from Tara <note>The inaugural site and seat of the High Kings of Ireland</note> to fetch him. This was the time when a great war and conflict had broken out between Dermot <note>Diarmait mac Cerbhaill, High King of Ireland (reigned c. 544–565)</note>, son of *Cerbhall* and King of Ireland, and *Ruadhan of Lothra* <note>Saint Ruadhán, founder of Lorrha monastery</note> along with all the other "apostles." This conflict arose because the King had violated the saint’s right of sanctuary in the case of *Aedh Guaire*, the King of Connacht. King Dermot had forcibly seized *Aedh Guaire* from the protection of *Ruadhan* and the other saints of Ireland. This was the same *Aedh Guaire*, King of Connacht, who shortly before had killed *Aedh Baclamh* because he had offended him.

Molasius arrived at the green of Tara where the "apostles" were staying in their tents. They all rose to receive him and bowed their heads to him; then, Molasius’s tent was pitched in the middle of all the other saints' tents. The nature of the contest they held against the King of Ireland was a spiritual one: the saints, relying on their holiness, their prayers, and their miracles, fasted for one night. King Dermot, on the other hand—convinced of the truth of his cause, his royal prerogative, and his princely rights—fasted against them the following night. 

Until that moment, there had been eleven saints fasting, but with the arrival of Molasius, they became twelve. These "apostles" were the primary saints of Ireland: *Ruadhan* of Lorrha, *Maedóg* of Ferns, *Féichín* of Fore, Columba <note>Saint Columba or Colum Cille</note>, *Cainnech* the Pious, *Tighernach* of *Cluain-eois*, *Enan* the angelic, the priest *Fraech*, *Becan* son of *Culu*, the bishop *mac Carthainn*, the elder *Mochta* of *Lughba*, *Mochuda* the devout, and Molasius of Devenish. It was nightfall and snow was falling heavily when Molasius reached Tara, yet it was the saints who were fasting...

<summary>Saint Molasius establishes his monastery at Devenish with Roman relics and is summoned to Tara to join the "Twelve Apostles of Ireland" in a ritual fasting protest against King Dermot, who had violated the right of sanctuary.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Molasius, Devenish, Relics, Tara, King Dermot (Diarmait mac Cerbhaill), Ruadhan of Lothra, Aedh Guaire, Twelve Apostles of Ireland, Fasting, Sanctuary rights</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the account of the conflict at Tara. Saint Molasius has just arrived to join the "apostles" (the collective of Irish saints) in their fasting protest against High King Dermot, who had violated the right of sanctuary.</meta>

# Saint Molasius of Devenish

That night, Molasius (having just arrived from his journey) joined them in their fast. During that night, the King of Ireland was not permitted to settle himself comfortably nor to rest; he had neither a doze nor a nap of sleep. Instead, as if in a waking dream, it was shown to him that the men of God, fasting against him on the green of Tara, were treating him unfairly. Dermot felt the night was far too long and waited anxiously for daybreak. When morning finally came, they had to use great force to open the doors because of the thickness of the fallen snow. 

The King of Ireland rose and looked out upon the tents. The entire area was pure white with snow, except for Molasius's tent. No snow had stuck to it at all, and for seven feet on every side of the tent, the earth was clear of snow. 

"Who is in that tent which the snow has not touched?" asked the King. 

"Molasius of Devenish," the others answered, "who arrived yesterday around the hour of <term>nones</term>." <note>The traditional time for the ninth hour of prayer, roughly 3:00 PM.</note>

"It is because of him that this oppression has been cast upon me," said Dermot, "and the 'pale-face of Lough Erne' <note>A reference to Molasius’s ascetic appearance; Lough Erne was the location of his monastery.</note> affected me heavily last night. He is indeed a living, blazing fire; but I believe he should not have been so hard on me, for my burden was already very great. Now I place myself under his protection and under that of the King of Heaven and Earth, in whom we both believe."

The situation was explained to Molasius, and it moved him to pity. It was also arranged for the King of Ireland to confer with the saints that day. Molasius struggled to make peace between the King and Ruadhan <note>Saint Ruadhan of Lorrha, the leader of the protest against the King.</note> along with the others, but he did not succeed. 

When he saw that he could not achieve peace, Molasius gave the King of Ireland a choice: he could have his life cut short and his body tormented in this world, but with Heaven for his soul and the high kingship for his descendants <note>original: "seed"</note> forever; or, he could have a long life now, but coupled with Hell for himself and none of his descendants ever attaining the kingship again.

The choice the King made was to have his body suffer pain in exchange for the continued dominion of his descendants. And so God brought it to pass. Because of this, the Clan-Colman <note>The Clann Cholmáin, the dominant branch of the Southern Uí Néill dynasty.</note> and the descendants of Dermot's son, *Aedh Sláine*, are bound to pay a tribute to Molasius every year during the winter, as the poem says:

->The twelve apostles of Ireland <note>original: "Innisfail", a poetic name for Ireland</note> . . .<-

As for Molasius, after this he stayed no longer at this contest with the saints at Tara. In his eyes, it was a tragic thing that Tara must be abandoned <note>The "Cursing of Tara" is a legendary event where the saints' protest led to the eventual abandonment of the site as the royal seat.</note> and the seat of...

<summary>High King Dermot submits to Saint Molasius after a miraculous snowfall and chooses a legacy of royal power for his descendants over his own earthly comfort. This establishes a lasting tribute from the King’s lineage to Molasius’s monastery.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Molasius, High King Dermot (Diarmait mac Cerbhaill), Tara, Devenish, Ruadhan of Lothra, Irish Saints, Snow Miracle, Clan-Colman, Aedh Sláine</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Saint Molasius's conflict with High King Dermot at Tara. Having arrived and fasted against the King, Molasius's spiritual authority is now being established over the secular power of the monarchy.</meta>

# Saint Molasius of Devenish

Ireland's royal sovereignty was stripped of its strength. Molasius knew well that, in the end, the saints would prove stronger than the King of Ireland. Because of this, both the saints of Ireland and the laymen all granted Molasius the highest rank in performing miracles and the precedence in working wonders. Indeed, he never ceased performing miracles: uprooting the wicked, lifting up the righteous, and blessing the <term>tuatha</term> and the <term>triucha</term> throughout all of Ireland. <note>original: *tuatha* and *triucha*; these refer to traditional Irish territorial units, ranging from local tribal lands to larger districts similar to baronies.</note>

Next, he traveled toward Devenish. Along his way, he happened upon a company of young clerics who were clearing away a thicket of briars and blackthorn. They began to complain to him about the state of their hands and feet, for the thorns were piercing them. Immediately, Molasius tore his own mantle for them. From one portion of the cloth, gloves of great value were miraculously created, as soft as if they had been made of well-suppled glover's leather. From the other part, thick shoes were produced, as sturdy as bark-soaked leather from a tannery.

It was during this time that the <term>Dartraighe</term> <note>The Dartry, a tribe from the border regions of modern-day Sligo, Leitrim, and Monaghan.</note> were reaching the end of a long and vengeful banishment. The people of Munster had inflicted this exile upon them because the Dartry's rule had been so destructive to Cashel, and because they had slain so many of the <term>Ui Chonaill Ghabra</term>. <note>A powerful ruling dynasty in southwest Ireland (modern County Limerick).</note> Furthermore, they had frequently aided foreigners and heathens against the native Irish, showing the invaders the secret paths and ways where their enemies used to hide. For these reasons, the Dartry were exiled from their original lands and from their own natural <term>leasc láimhe</term> <note>original: *leasc láimhe*; likely meaning their "hand-rest" or home territory.</note>—namely, a <term>triucha céd</term> <note>A "thirty-hundred" land division, roughly equivalent to a barony.</note> of their ancient inheritance in the Southern Half of Ireland. This territory stretched from the assembly hill of the O'Carbery in the south to the "Cave of the Giant" on the borders of the <term>Cechtraighe</term> to the west, and from the "River of the Horses" to...

For forty years, they lived in exile across the length and breadth of Ireland. Their strength consisted of five hundred armed men. During this entire time, they never stayed on any one piece of land for more than three years. The regional kings would take them in under specific conditions and offer them protection, but eventually, the Dartry’s own misdeeds would catch up with them. This was due to the excessive amount of land they would seize and absorb for themselves, as well as their general rowdiness.

<summary>Saint Molasius is recognized as the most powerful miracle-worker in Ireland and provides miraculous clothing for young clerics. The text also details the history and 40-year exile of the Dartry tribe, who were banished from Munster for their betrayals and violence.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Molasius, Devenish, miracles, Dartraighe (Dartry), Munster, Irish tribes, exile, Cashel, land disputes, medieval law</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of the Dartraighe tribe during their period of exile and conflict, concluding the narrative of Saint Molasius's life and the tribal movements associated with his era.</meta>

# Saint Molasius of Devenish

Because of their rudeness and their frequent brawls and fights in formal assemblies, conventions, and every other meeting of any kind, the provincial kings grew weary of them. Moreover, they used to assault and commit violence against Ireland’s various chieftains, attempting to forcibly occupy their lands. Consequently, these chieftains were forced to complain about them to their allies <note>original: "friends"; referring to allies by bond of blood</note> and neighbors.

During this entire period, they stayed longest in Connacht, until—while attacking western Connacht—they killed both the King of *Umhall* and the King of *Partraighe* <note>the baronies of the Owles and Partry in County Mayo</note> on the same day. As a result, the people of Connacht drove them across *Luan’s* Ford <note>Athlone</note> westward into Meath; there they settled in the center of Delvin. The people of Delvin and Westmeath attacked and harassed them, but they had barely managed to set up temporary huts <note>original: "bothies"</note> in one place when the *Dartraighe* caught them in the middle of the plain of Durrow <note>in County Offaly, formerly King's County</note>. There they fought a stubborn and difficult battle, until the men of Delvin and Westmeath were routed and a massive "red slaughter" was carried out against them. The *Dartraighe* then returned and made peace with Connacht; having done so, they forcibly seized both Delvin territories <note>two baronies in Westmeath</note> for a year and a half.

From there, they went to the land of the *Fir Ceall* <note>the barony of Fircall, County Offaly</note>, and for a period of time, they fought them for their land. Consequently, the men of Fircall, the Delvins, and the men of Meath gathered against the *Dartraighe* and almost completely devastated them. The *Dartraighe* overtook them from the rear of their cattle herds, and they fought a battle at *fán na neach*, which is called *fán an ghribaigh* today. The Delvins and Fircall were defeated there; they suffered great carnage and were forced to abandon the *Dartraighe’s* cattle <note>original: "kine"</note>. The *Dartraighe* had a poet with them, and he composed a poem:

->A victorious fight you have fought . . .<-

## *Life of Saint Magnenn of Kilmainham.*

Magnenn, Toa, Librén, and Cobthach were the four sons of Aedh, son of Colgan, son of Tuathal, son of Felim, son of *Colla fó chrích* <note>original: "Colla of the territory"; likely referring to one of the Three Collas, legendary ancestors of the Airgíalla</note>. This bishop, Magnenn, was a tower of piety from the Shannon River to *Benn Edair* <note>the Hill of Howth</note>. In his own time, he was a chosen vessel <note>original: "vessel of selection"; a biblical term for a saint</note> of sanctity: he was one who, from the time he was seven years old, had never told a lie. Furthermore, he never looked a woman in the face, for fear that he might see her guardian devil.

It happened once upon a time that Magnenn went to visit his companion and friend, Loman of *Loch Uair* <note>Lough Owel</note> in Meath. In that town was a man who was also his friend and had been his follower. When Magnenn saw him, the man’s eyes were in a terrible state, running with a constant discharge. The holy cleric was startled to see his friend and spoke thus: "Thanks be to God <note>original: "Deo gratias"</note>! Pitiable, O my friend, is the plight of your eyes; they seem to mock the world, while the world mocks them! It is thirty years to this day since I last saw you; had you only followed the advice I gave you then until today, you would have secured your share of the Heavenly City's beauty <note>original: "amenity"</note>, which is great indeed. For the bird in the Heavenly City that has the smallest and harshest voice yields more delight than all the good things of the Earth." 

The other answered: "Friend, I place myself under your protection!" 

Magnenn undertook to protect him for God's sake and said to him: "Do not do to another what you would hate to have done to yourself; and though you are in your final days, yet God will accept you." Bishop Magnenn and Loman of *Loch Uair* then made a pact together. Each bestowed his blessing on the other, and they took their leave.

At that time, Magnenn also preached to Diarmait <note>original: "Dermot"</note>, son of Fergus, the King of Ireland. When Loman of *Loch Uair* heard the signs of Judgment Day <note>original: "Day of Doom"</note> and the rigorous judgments—

...the praise of the Triune God, and in the presence of the king and the people, he broke out and wept aloud. When the king's people heard the holy cleric's harsh judgments and stern declarations, thirty of them renounced the secular world <note>original: "severed themselves from the false world"; meaning they entered the religious life</note> right there in the king's presence. From that time on, the king himself, Dermot son of Fergus, also sought his own peace with God. He granted Magnenn great tributes and endowments <note>original: "alms"</note>, such as a *screpall* <term>an ancient Irish silver coin or unit of weight</term> for every person <note>original: "on every nose"</note>. He also decreed that for every chieftain’s daughter who married, Magnenn would receive an ounce of gold or—should the saint's stewards prefer it—the wedding clothes the bride had worn. From the gold tribute that the king received from foreigners, he also commissioned the making of a pastoral staff and a crozier for the saint. During this period, Magnenn’s preaching at *Loch Uair* <note>Lough Owel, County Westmeath</note> was highly celebrated, as was his influence with the King of Ireland. Magnenn pronounced a blessing upon Dermot, saying to him: "May the Lord’s mercy be upon your sons <note>original Latin: "misericordia domini super filios vestros"</note> (that is, may God’s mercy be on you and your sons)."

Once, when Magnenn went to the monastery of Finnian of *Magh Bile* <note>Movilla Abbey, County Down</note>, they greeted one another. When they heard the bell for the evening service, they went outside on Sunday evening. On the way, they opened their hearts to God, and right where they stood, they witnessed a linen altar-cloth fluttering down from the heavens. Bishop Magnenn said, "Pick that up, Finnian." "Never say such a thing, holy bishop," Finnian answered. "You are the one whom such a gift best suits, and it is not right for me to have it." Bishop Magnenn replied, "I swear by the angels that I will not lift it until God gives me another one just like it." They looked up to God a second time and prayed together for another altar-cloth, and it was granted to them. This was a miracle by which God's name was magnified, and the two saints, because their devotion was so powerful, shouted for joy. Those same linen cloths still exist today.

It happened once that the King of Ireland’s steward came to collect rent from Magnenn’s foster-mother. At the time, Magnenn was only three years old and was resting in her arms. The steward refused to take what was legally owed to him; instead, he demanded something he had no right to take at all. Magnenn’s foster-mother...

While he was in her arms, as mentioned before, he wept with a loud cry, and immediately the power of one leg, one arm, and one eye left the steward. He shouted, saying: “I had a dream recently; it seemed I was guilty in the matter of a ‘lamb of compassion.’ I now believe that child you hold is that lamb; if you would obtain God's help for me in his name, I will never again collect rent from you.” The nurse looked at the little boy and said: “Dear son, those in misery should rightly have comfort.” When the child heard his nurse’s words, he raised his eyes and both his hands toward God on high; then, immediately and in an instant, the steward was healed. There was a great clamor indoors, and among everyone there, it was reported that Magnenn was a holy child. These, then, were the first miracles of Magnenn.

Once, Magnenn had a ram that accompanied him, and when they traveled, the ram would carry Magnenn’s book of prayers. However, a certain wicked man came to Magnenn and stole the ram. Magnenn, with his twenty-seven <note>original: "thrice nine"</note> clerics, followed the trail to the robber’s house. Swearing by various relics and by Magnenn’s hand, the thief denied that he was guilty of taking the ram—even though <insert>[at that very instant]</insert> one part of it was in a hole in the earth beneath the robber’s house, cut up, while another portion of it had been eaten and was in his stomach. For the holy cleric, God performed a clear miracle then, so that the ram spoke to them from the hole where he was. Magnenn and his twenty-seven followers looked up to God and thanked Him for multiplying His miracles. As for the thief: his eyesight was taken from him, his legs and arms lost their strength, and his entire body was thrown into a great physical distress <note>original: "perturbation"</note>. With a loud voice he cried: “Woe is me, for I am a sinner! O Magnenn, I beg you by God that you do not let me lose Heaven as well!” When Magnenn heard the sinner perform an act of penance, he felt affection and compassion for him. He made a fervent prayer to God, and through that prayer, he won from Him the return of the blind man’s sight <insert>[that is, his vision]</insert>, and the man was restored to health <insert>[that is, restored as he was before]</insert>. By this miracle, God’s name and Magnenn’s name were glorified, and so forth <note>original Latin: *et reliqua*</note>.

# Saint Magnenn of Kilmainham

...son of Muiredach Redneck: now Molasius was in such a state that his body was afflicted with thirty diseases, and for the sake of devotion, he lived confined in a narrow hut. Moreover, he remained in this position: spread out in the form of a cross, with his face to the ground, weeping bitterly, the earth beneath him wet with his tears of penance. 

Magnenn said, "I command you by God to tell me why you asked Him that your body should suffer seventy <note>original: "three score and ten"</note> diseases." 

Molasius answered, "I will explain it, holy bishop. My spiritual condition has been revealed to me, showing that my sinfulness pervades my body like a flame. Therefore, I am eager to have my purgatory here, so that 'on the other side' <note>beyond the grave</note> I may find eternal life. Do you know, Magnenn, how a grain of wheat is treated before it is sown in the earth? It must be threshed and beaten. In the same way, before I am laid in the grave, I wish for my body to be threshed by these illnesses; and thanks be to God that, however near death may be to me now, you have come my way before I die. For the love of God, lay me out properly and perform the rites of my funeral and burial." 

Accordingly, when the time came, Magnenn carried out those funeral rites, which became the third most exalted burial performed in Ireland: first, Patrick in *Downpatrick* <note>original: dún dá leth nglas</note>; second, Mochuda in *Raheen* near Tullamore <note>original: Ráithín of O Suanaigh</note>; and third, Molasius, who was buried by the holy bishop Magnenn at *Leighlin*.

It happened once that Bishop Magnenn went to the place where Finnchua of *Brí Gobhann* <note>near Mitchelstown</note> lived. Magnenn asked Finnchua to go with him on a visit to the island of Arran, where Enda of Arran lived. This was a place frequented by the saints of Ireland and indeed all of Europe, and where the "Great Pope of Popes" <note>original: mórphopa pápa</note> had been. 

So Magnenn proceeded to Arran and made friends with the saints there. Then, after achieving a victory of penance and pilgrimage, he departed the island with the twenty-seven <note>original: "thrice nine"</note> holy clerics who were his companions. One night during their travels, near *Garmna*, they were without food. Magnenn’s people said to him, "Holy cleric, it is a pity that we are not in Tallaght tonight where we might have enough food for the evening, for we are very hungry <note>original: "sharp set"</note>." 

Magnenn answered, "Young men, never say such a thing! For God helps both the poor and..."

# Saint Magnenn of Kilmainham

...rich, and that His ability to relieve us is no greater in any other place than it is to help us right where we are.” They had not been there long when they heard the baying and cry of a hound chasing a deer. When the deer came close to the holy clerics, it took a sudden, desperate leap and, right before them, broke its neck. Magnenn said: “Thanks be to God <note>original: *Deo gratias*</note>; eat moderately, and give thanks to your Maker that you are so well-provided for.” His followers did so, and once the meal was finished, they carried off their various leftovers of the meat. In this way, they traveled on until the time for fasting arrived. A man of his religious community <note>original: *familia*; the monks or followers attached to a saint</note> then said to Magnenn: “I urge you to share with us the doctrine and instructions on fasting.” 

He answered: “Fasting is of no benefit when meat is withheld from you against your will, so that you cannot have it; nor is there virtue in a fast based on vanity and pride, should those be your motives for abstaining. Neither is one required to observe a fast from meat any more than a ‘fast of the lips’ <note>meaning temperance or restraint in speech</note> and general abstinence from all faults. I tell you also, miserable beings, that God does not judge a man more strictly for the evil he actually commits than He indicts him for the good which he neglected to do when he had the chance. Woe to him, too, who unconcernedly watches evil being done and does not fear Him who is Lord forever and ever!”

On another occasion, Magnenn went to visit the place where Maelruain of Tallaght lived. He found Maelruain just climbing out of a well of water after chanting all one hundred and fifty psalms <note>original: the psalter’s three times fifty psalms</note> while standing in it. Through humility, Maelruain saluted the sacred bishop, gave him a great welcome, and offered him the kiss of peace, saying: “My friend, pay attention to me.” 

He reached across himself and, from the hem of the hair garment <note>original: hair integument</note> that he wore next to his skin, he plucked a sturdy brooch pin <note>original: *fibula*</note>. With this, he struck himself a blow in the breast on the left side <note>original: the gospel side; the side of the altar where the Gospel is read</note>. From where the pin struck, no blood flowed, but only a little pinkish fluid. The purpose of this ordeal was to show Bishop Magnenn that no pride existed in Maelruain’s body. 

Magnenn replied: “I see that; and the reason I have come is to receive an exhortation from you, and to beg that I may...”

# Saint Magnenn of Kilmainham

"...make a confession and be purged of all my sins and guilt."

Maelruain said, "In God’s name, I command <note>original: adjure</note> you to make your confession immediately." 

Magnenn began: "Three times I say to you, 'Have mercy on me!' I tell you," he continued, "that from the day I took holy orders, I have never allowed the canonical hours <note>the specific times set aside for prayer throughout the day</note> to overlap one into another [unobserved]. And I tell you that from the day I was baptized, I have never violated my purity or my chastity. Nor, from the time I was ordained a priest, have I gone even a single day without celebrating Mass." 

Maelruain then asked, "Holy bishop, in terms of physical labor <note>original: corporal labour</note>, do you perform any manual work at all?" 

Magnenn answered, "I perform neither work nor labor; nor, indeed (considering my age and position), is it required of me to do so." 

Maelruain cried out, "Alas for that! I have never heard the confession of any man who did not labor with his own hands for his physical needs <note>meaning to supply his own bodily requirements</note>." 

Magnenn replied, "Then, holy cleric, grant me your respect <note>original: yield me reverence</note>." 

Maelruain agreed, "I will indeed." 

"I tell you further," Magnenn said, "that I never laid a penance—no matter how severe—on any man who came to me for confession without inflicting a more severe one upon my own body. Thus, once upon a time, the son of the King of the Saxons <note>the English</note> came to me to confess and to seek spiritual instruction <note>original: devotional tuition</note>. I asked him, 'Do you perform any manual work?' He said that he did not. But I maintained that I would not violate God’s law and the command He gave to Adam, when He ordered him to feed himself through the labor of his hands and body, and by the sweat of his brow. Alas, then, that my pilgrimage <note>original: peregrination</note> and my visit here must be just like his!" 

But Maelruain replied, "By no means! Instead, sages and ancient books shall preserve the memory of your journey here and the miracles yet to come from you until the end of the world, for both are truly excellent." 

Bishop Magnenn pleaded, "Instruct me, for God's sake!" 

Maelruain said to him: "In His name, I tell you: weep for the sins of friends and neighbors as though they were your own. Set all your thoughts on God. Do not dwell on friends or companions, on gold or silver, or on the deceptive appearances <note>original: specious show</note> of this world. Instead, place all your heart and your confessions in God. Meditate on Mary, the Mother of Glory. Ponder the great prophets (the twelve major prophets), along with John the Baptist, just as you ponder the lesser prophets..."

Then the two sealed their friendship. Their souls <gloss>[along with their bodies]</gloss> were to be lodged in the lowest level of Hell’s pit.

Bishop Magnenn also had three requests <gloss>[granted to him]</gloss> by God: abundance, honor, and worldly wealth for those who should favor his clergy and his representative after him. However, for those who should persecute his sacred grounds and his own specific seat of authority, he left three legacies: a short and fleeting life, the erasing of their descendants, and the Earth not yielding them her fruit. For those who are under Magnenn’s protection but lose hope in his help, woe to them! For he obtains from God any rightful request they ask of him; on this side <gloss>[of the grave]</gloss>, he secures length of life and a fruitful land, and on the other side, the presence and enjoyment <gloss>[original: fruition]</gloss> of eternal glory. He also obtained the blessing that whoever held Bishop Magnenn dear would also be beloved by other men.

<meta>This page continues the life of Saint Magnenn, focusing on his spiritual practices—specifically his five pre-meal meditations—and his encounter with a mysterious grave at Wind-gap. It highlights his extreme devotion to the symbol of the cross.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>

### Saint Magnenn of Kilmainham

Before he tasted his meal or whatever he was to consume, he would perform five meditations. The first was on how he was originally born and in what a humble state <note>original: "mean estate"</note> he came from his mother’s womb. The second was how, in time, he would escape from the finality of death <note>original: "death-extremity"</note>. The third was how the soul is carried away to witness Hell. The fourth was how the soul goes to contemplate the Heavenly City so that it may avoid being pulled back <gloss>[into sin or the world]</gloss>, which increases its humility and spiritual concern <note>original: "self-distrust"</note>. The fifth was how the sinners' monument <note>original: "cairn"; a pile of stones serving as a memorial or landmark</note>—the structure of their ambition, no matter how high it is piled—is brought low <note>original: "abased"</note> in just a short time. 

He used to tell his monks that they ought to leave a path free within their inner selves for the Holy Spirit: a passage where they should not allow worldly <note>original: "secular"</note> nourishment to enter. He used to say three times at once that the world is nothing but a mass of deception. "Pay attention, my beloved people," he said, "and take notice: if you reject <note>original: "spurn"</note> God’s commandments, how can you look up to Him when making your requests? Or how will God listen to your cry and earnest prayer?"

At one time, Bishop Magnenn went to the place where Saint Moling lived. A meal of food <note>original: "victual"</note> was served to them and, according to tradition <note>original: "precept"</note>, it was blessed. A member of his monastic community <note>original: "familia"; Latin for a household or religious family</note> said, "Today, as we came here, we noticed a cross and a fresh grave, but we do not know what is buried there." 

Magnenn asked, "In what place did you see that?" 

The other answered, "On a slope <note>original: "acclivity"</note> on the side of *Berna na Gaoithe* <gloss>[Wind-gap]</gloss>." 

The bishop said, "I have never seen a cross without kneeling <note>original: "genuflection"</note> three times before it." Therefore, even though his meal had already been blessed, he left it behind. Accompanied by his twenty-seven <gloss>[thrice nine]</gloss> holy clerics, he traveled until he came to *Berna na Gaoithe*. There, he spent a long time contemplating the cross and the grave. He spoke to no one, but knelt three times before the cross. 

His people asked him why he was silent, but he did not answer them. He remained this way for three hours; then, in a mild and gentle voice, he said: "I command you to tell me who is buried in that grave, and why I never saw the cross despite having passed close by it." 

The miserable soul <gloss>[the occupant of the tomb]</gloss> answered him, saying: "I will tell you that, holy bishop, even though I gain no relief from your questioning <note>original: "interpellation"</note>. I am a pagan <note>original: "heathen"</note>, and it was never possible..."

<summary>Saint Magnenn practices five daily meditations on mortality and humility and discovers a mysterious grave at Wind-gap, where he commands the spirit of a buried pagan to speak.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Magnenn, Saint Moling, Kilmainham, Berna na Gaoithe, Wind-gap, meditations, monastic life, paganism, genuflection, Irish saints</keywords>

<page-num>43</page-num>

# Saint Magnenn of Kilmainham

"...to do evil but I did it. I harassed the weak and sought to gain favor with the strong. I persecuted the vulnerable churches and was excommunicated with bell, book, and candle <note>A formal ritual of excommunication involving ringing a bell, closing a book, and extinguishing a candle to symbolize spiritual darkness</note> by the curse <note>original: "malediction"</note> of the righteous. I died without repentance <note>original: "penitence"</note>, and all the scholars <note>original: "philosophers [i.e. learned]"</note> in the world could not describe even half of my suffering—which indeed cannot even be shown—unless Almighty God Himself should tell it. This is why, holy bishop, the guardian angel who was with you did not allow you to see me <gloss>[referring to his cross and grave]</gloss>. And now, by God, I beg <note>original: "adjure"</note> you, holy bishop, pray for me and show me your mercy!" 

At that, Magnenn looked up to God, but his guardian angel said to him: "Do not provoke <note>original: "rouse"</note> God’s anger, and do not waste your time any longer." Magnenn knelt <note>original: "made a genuflection"</note> and returned by the same path to where Moling was. Neither Moling nor his congregation had tasted the meal that Magnenn had blessed until he returned. 

Magnenn said, "This is strange, holy cleric. Why has this food <note>original: "meat"; used here for food in general</note> not been eaten?" 

Moling answered, "We were not worthy to eat it after it had been blessed by you." 

"Holy one, never say that!" Magnenn replied. "For even if all the saints of Ireland had blessed it, you still would have been worthy of it, and it would have been right for you to eat it."

They confirmed their friendship and agreement <note>original: "ratify their concord and their amity"</note>, and Magnenn departed with his twenty-seven <note>original: "thrice nine"</note> bishops. However, that night he happened to lose his way. He began to pray urgently <note>original: "supplicate instantly"</note> so that he might be delivered from his wandering, and very soon he found himself at a large house <note>original: "mansion"</note> where there was a large group of rowdy <note>original: "riotous"</note> people. 

He said, "Alas for this! As bad as it was to be lost, this crowd is worse because of its filthiness and its vulgar <note>original: "ribald"</note> language." 

He asked if there was any respectable place nearby, and he was told that close by lived a poor widow of little importance. He went to where the widow lived, and she showed her joy at the company of saints she saw coming toward her. The clerics greeted her and set up camp <note>original: "make a pitch"</note> on the property, with Magnenn highly praising the decency and quietness of the place. 

"It is well for one who lives in poverty as you do," he said, "as long as it is not a poverty suffered against your will <note>original: "against the grain"; literally a poverty of 'unwillingness' or 'disinclination'</note>. In the Church, such involuntary poverty is not praised, because it leads the person practicing it into sin and eventual regret <note>original: "lamentation"</note>."

<summary>Saint Magnenn hears a confession from a tormented spirit, returns to Saint Moling to share a meal, and later finds shelter with a poor widow, where he teaches on the spiritual nature of voluntary poverty.</summary>

<page-num>44</page-num>

# Saint Magnenn of Kilmainham

The next morning, Magnenn rose. He and his twenty-seven companions <note>original: "thrice nine"</note> walked all through Saturday. When the start of Sunday arrived <note>original: "Sunday’s [anterior] limit"; likely referring to Saturday evening when the liturgical Sunday begins</note>, the holy bishop happened to be on an open plain, and they camped there for the night. Throughout that cold and wet night, they endured heavy rain and harsh, shifting winds. However, Bishop Magnenn planted his four-sided pastoral staff <note>A staff carried by a bishop symbolizing his role as a shepherd</note> in the ground to stand over them; around it, each of his men also planted their own hooked staves. The holy bishop then spread his four-cornered hood over his company of clerics and performed a clear miracle for the group: despite the night’s great storm and foul weather—with every pool and hollow overflowing—not a single drop of the storm fell upon the saints. They rose on Monday, and the wonders he had performed for the saints were obvious; news of these miracles spread throughout all of Ireland.

One of Magnenn’s notable characteristics was his attitude toward wealth, for he never accepted gold, silver, or any metal designated as money <note>original: *moneta*</note>. A Culdee <term>A member of an ascetic Christian community in Ireland and Scotland known for their strict devotion</term> in Kilmainham gave this great testimony about him: “Magnenn the miracle-worker, who never sinned with a woman; Magnenn the wise, whose habit it was to weep.” Furthermore, when preaching, he never repeated a single word in the same discourse. He never finished a sermon without converting someone to the faith. He never sat beside a king or a chief (aiming thus to avoid developing a prideful spirit <note>original: "high mind"</note>), and he greatly despised the honors given by kings and mighty lords, saying, “Woe to the person who, after renouncing the world, finds satisfaction in honors granted by the powerful.”

After this, a robber came from Howth <note>original: *benn Edair*</note> and stole a cow belonging to a woman with leprosy in Kilmainham. This woman’s cow was always in milk and provided enough to support the poor, the needy, and those with paralysis. She recognized the robber and went, crying aloud as she went, to the place where Bishop Magnenn was with his gathering of saints and clerics. She bitterly told him how she had been robbed of her only cow, which had caused her to become thin and weak. At this story, the holy Bishop Magnenn and his group of clerics were angered...

<summary>Bishop Magnenn performs a miracle by sheltering his followers from a violent storm using only his staff and hood. The text also details his ascetic virtues, including his refusal of money and his defense of a woman with leprosy whose cow was stolen.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Magnenn, Kilmainham, miracles, Culdee, asceticism, Howth, hagiography, pastoral staff, charity</keywords>

# Saint Magnenn of Kilmainham

...exceedingly: they ring the bells of the place, both great and small. Against the robber, they pronounce excommunication with the ringing of bells, curses, and maledictions. After this, the holy bishop remained silent for a long time. He did not move a foot, a hand, or any part of his body <note>original: "organs"</note>. Then he spoke softly, saying that although he had tried to pronounce a blessing <note>original: "benediction"</note> on the robber, the depth of his anger was so great that he could not manage it. Neither the other saints nor any righteous man could persuade Magnenn to offer the thief a prayer or even a single sigh of compassion. They asked, "O righteous one, why do you do this?" He answered, "I will tell you: it is because of the greatness of my anger <note>original: "incensement"</note> and the weight of my severity. I wish to rouse God’s anger to increase the thief's everlasting torment in the afterlife—in that place where no friend can give help. It is the place where, once a soul falls into the clutches of Malemantus <note>A name appearing in medieval Irish texts, likely referring to a demonic figure or the personification of evil</note> in the deepest pit of Hell, neither saint nor just man can ever again succeed in a petition for mercy."

> "On those who violate my privileges <note>original: "prerogatives"</note> and the rights of my monks, I lay three heavy sentences: that their eyes be closed to the world they have loved (meaning, may they be blinded); that the Heavenly City be shut against them so they cannot enter it; to the actual violators, I leave death by the point of a weapon; and to their successors after them, a meager harvest <note>original: "niggard yield of fruits"</note>. As David says in the Psalms: *the offspring of the ungodly shall perish* <note>original Latin: "semen impiorum peribit"</note>.

> "I beg of God that on the day when the twelve royal thrones are set on Mount Zion—the day when the four streams of fire surround the mountain and the three groups of people are there (the people of Heaven, Earth, and Hell, meaning angels, men, and devils)—those who have outraged me will be found guilty and sentenced to death in Hell. But as for those who have honored and supported me and my successors, may it be—with Christ’s permission—that I myself, sitting beside Saint Patrick, shall judge them."

Bishop Magnenn also said: "Woe to him who is not found faithful on that day—according to the sacred records and writings that describe the signs of the fifteen days preceding Judgment Day <note>original: "Doom"</note>..."

->**Saint Magnenn of Kilmainham**<-

...faithful, steadfast, mild, gentle, and well-regarded. God’s Son will show no frown or sternness toward such a person as they come joyfully to meet and rejoin their body. But for the followers of Lucifer who come that day to meet their false bodies only to increase their torment—misery! For the scriptures say this: such people shall then be bald, dark in complexion, hairless, and toothless. Even if one’s father, mother, or wedded wife were on either side of them, they would never look at them; instead, they will tremble all over, with their attention fixed only on the sins laid out before them. No individual from that crew of Lucifer’s may sneak in among Jesus’ people; they must all be huddled together as a filthy group apart.

A prophecy of Bishop Magnenn’s was this: a time should come when daughters would be disrespectful and sharp-tongued, lacking obedience to their mothers; when those of low social standing <note>original: "low estate"</note> would grumble much, and elders would lack respectful care; when there would be both unholy laymen and corrupt prelates <note>High-ranking church officials</note>, wicked and perverted judges, and a lack of respect for elders. The soil would be barren of fruit, the weather disordered, and the seasons unpredictable <note>original: "intemperate"</note>. Women would be given to witchcraft, churches would be left empty, and deceitful hearts and treachery would increase. It would be a time when God’s commandments would be violated, and the signs of Doomsday would occur every year.

Once, when Bishop Magnenn went on a journey to Athlone, he sat on the riverbank <note>original: "strand"</note>. When a certain leper saw the holy bishop from a distance <note>original: "from him"</note>, he cried out with an exceedingly great cry and said to him, "Hear my complaint, and pray to the mighty Lord for me!" Hearing this, the holy bishop opened his heart to God and looked up toward heaven. His compassion was moved for the afflicted man <note>original: "the unclean"</note>; he asked for water, washed the leper’s hands and feet, and the man was made whole.

Another example of the holy bishop’s perfection was this: he never entered any place where there was war or conflict without mercy and pity effectively following his words. Before he departed, the opposing parties would be at peace. He would say to them lovingly: "What you have spent, you have used; what you have given away, you still possess; what you have hoarded up, you have lost; and the things you have selfishly denied others have already brought judgment <note>original: "avenged"</note> upon you." As soon as the *tuatha* <term>Irish: clans or tribes</term> and the tribes heard that, straight—

<header>Saint Magnenn of Kilmainham</header>

...This was the way they used to make peace. He would go on to say that this was the third thing with which God was best pleased in the world: love toward Him, the giving of generous alms, and the maintenance of peace.

A young boy of his monastic household <note>original: *familia*</note>—one who was just seven years old—said to him, "Holy bishop, how must we practice piety?" He answered: "Early morning prayer <note>original: "tierce," the service for the third hour, around 9:00 AM</note> and long afternoon prayer <note>original: "none," the service for the ninth hour, around 3:00 PM</note>; only as much food <note>original: "meat"</note> as may suffice for a little boy; sleep as if you were a prisoner condemned to death; frequent meditation on God; do not allow one prayer hour <note>original: "canonical hour"</note> to pass into the other without having meditated upon it. Pray much every night, as though that night should be your last, and your own final end—to be determined by your state at that moment—would lead to a state without limit or end in the eternal life hereafter, enjoying endless existence and free of all care. Whoever now, by a wicked course of life, shall make these commands of no effect shall lose <note>original: "abandon"</note> three things: a memorial <note>original: "monument"</note>, a son <note>meaning male heirs to carry on the name</note>, and posthumous renown <note>original: "praise"</note>."

Bishop Magnenn had a habit: no one was ever in his company for three hours but that he would reveal what kind of spirit was in them. He would understand quickly whether it was a good angel or a bad one that accompanied a person <note>original: "any man’s body"</note>.

He studied fervently with Ireland’s twelve apostles, whose names were these: two *Finnians*, two *Colmans*, *Kieran*, *Cainnech* (Saint Canice), *Comghall*, two *Brendans*, *Ruadhán*, *Nindidh*, and *Mobhí* son of *Nadfraech*. These are the twelve chief saints who, together with Patrick, were in Ireland; they were also (along with Bishop Magnenn) teachers <note>original: "preceptors"</note> in devotion and in spiritual encouragement. They all blessed him with every spiritual growth <note>original: "increment of piety"</note> they could think of.

It was another time that, while on a devotional tour, Magnenn went to the place where Mochuta of Raheen was. Mochuta enquired, "How are you, my friend?"

"I am not as I have been; I shall not remain as I am, and I shall yet come to nothing. I tell you, Mochuta, that I have seen an old man requiring his sons to be virtuous, while surely he never disciplined his own limbs or his senses against the world’s evil ways."

Immediately after this, Mochuta questioned him: "In the case of...

<page-num>48</page-num>

# Saint Magnenn of Kilmainham

"For those who, being in holy orders, break their vows, what should we do?"

Magnenn answered: "With the permission of God’s angel, I will tell you. I affirm that whatever priest violates his orders or his chastity is, in the eyes of God, guilty of death because of it. And whatever woman indulges the desires of even one ordained man, I consider it the same as if she had not refused a single man in all three parts of the world. The reason for this is that it is proper for a priest <note>meaning one of his essential attributes</note> to walk in the honor of his orders throughout all three parts of the world—that is, to remain pure in all his travels <note>original: "peregrinations"</note>, no matter how distant.

"Or again, I consider her guilt as though she had ten thousand husbands, and a thousand more <note>original: *supra mille*</note>. The reason for this is that ten thousand legions of angels accompany the body of every priest who remains chaste; and this is because he, just like Jesus, is in everlasting intercession <note>original: "supplication"</note> at the angelic altar. 

"Woe also to him to whom such a woman becomes a partner <note>original: "prize"</note> after she has been with a priest; for to be intimate with her is like thrusting one's head into the mire. It is a renunciation of baptism, of faith, and of piety; it is a pact with Lucifer, with Dathan and Abiron <note>Biblical figures who rebelled against Moses and were swallowed by the earth</note>; with Pluto and Beelzebub; with Malemantus, with the black <note>original: "swart"</note> sow, and with the chief captains of Hell’s host." These were Bishop Magnenn’s testimonies regarding the concubinage of women and priests.

Mochuta said: "Tell us, holy bishop, how should a pilgrimage be performed?"

"There are three ways <note>original: "species"</note> in which a person, when leaving their country, enters upon a journey of pilgrimage; but there is only one way by which they win the Heavenly Kingdom from God, which is as follows: When a person breaks with the world’s vices with their whole heart, mind, and true zeal and becomes a pilgrim, then in that way they unerringly reach God. 

"But when someone goes on a pilgrimage in body, while their mind remains at home with their children, their wife, or their land, and they prefer these things to God, then their journey is in vain. Except for the physical movement <note>original: "displacement of body"</note> and the useless labor, they gain no profit from it. For to have traveled abroad from one’s own natural inheritance is of little benefit to anyone unless they are found to have performed the pilgrimage effectively. Also, when the faithful Abraham went out from his own particular fatherland, the Lord gave him this counsel: 'From now on...'"

<summary>Saint Magnenn delivers a stern warning against priests breaking their vows of chastity, equating the act to a demonic pact, and explains that a true pilgrimage requires a total mental and spiritual break from worldly attachments.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the dialogue between Saint Magnenn and Saint Mochuta. After discussing the severity of broken vows, the conversation moves to the spiritual value of pilgrimage—emphasizing intent over physical travel—and ends with a chilling prophecy concerning the coming of the Antichrist to Ireland.</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>

# Saint Magnenn of Kilmainham

"Care no more for your land and soil, nor let your mind be set on returning to it again." And this is the guardian angel’s advice to every man who goes on a pilgrimage: do not repeat, by the actions of your hands or body, the behaviors <note>original: "ethics"</note> you had in the land where you lived before [and for which you are now a wanderer seeking atonement]; for God judges every human being by their progress in morality and virtuous practice. 

Furthermore, a person performs a pilgrimage [in spirit] when—though staying physically with their family—their heart strongly desires to go on a pilgrimage, yet weakness, poverty, or the burden of family responsibilities prevents them from actually doing it. This [inner intention] is then the same as if [in person] they had visited the tombs of Peter and Paul, or Christ’s sepulcher—assuming that was where they intended to go and that the body’s weaknesses prevented them. In such a case, the body takes the soul’s responsibility for the unmade pilgrimage. Finally, every Christian is bound to follow the rules of the Church, for the Lord, who judges fairly, credits contrition as devotion. This, then, is the problem you proposed to me in conversation and for friendship’s sake."

Magnenn said: "Mochuta, do you know when the *roth rámhach* <note>The "Rowing Wheel," a famous prophecy in Irish tradition involving a flying wheel or ship that brings destruction</note> will come, foretelling the arrival of the Perverter <note>Antichrist</note> in Ireland?" 

"This is how Antichrist shall come: as one who is powerful and wise, yet foolish—foolish toward God, but wise in working toward his own destruction. He will be born of incest (for he is the child of a daughter and her own father), making his mother also his sister. His entire face will be a single flat surface, and he will have six toes on each foot. Furthermore, he is a violent and pitiless judge with a light gray tuft of hair on his forehead. He transmutes all metals into gold and raises the dead. In his time, there will be no mercy until Elijah <note>original: "Eli"</note> and Enoch return... [the rest is missing <note>original Latin: *cætera desiderantur*</note>]."
