# Saint Cellach of Killala

## *The Life of Saint Cellach of Killala*

There was once a king who ruled over Connacht: Eoghan Bél, son of Cellach, son of Ailill Molt, son of Dathí, son of Fiachra, son of Eochaidh Moyvane. He used to ravage every province in Ireland and would always return victorious, bringing his plunder with him. No one was ever successful in taking back what he had stolen, for he always defeated his enemies in battle. Even if he could not catch those who attempted to raid him before they left his territory, he would raid that very same country on that very same day in retaliation. Indeed, even the people of Munster and Leinster obeyed him; because their cattle <note>original: "kine"</note> had been forcibly driven away so many times, they were eager <note>original: "fain"</note> to win his favor.

In any event, a great feud broke out between this Eoghan and the descendants of Niall <note>The Uí Néill dynasty</note>. Eventually, it was not just these families but the entire two provinces that stood opposed to one another: Connacht against Ulster. Their situations were unequal, however, because Eoghan Bél had never lost a battle, and no one had ever recovered stolen property from him. On the other hand, the number of times he took plunder and won triumphs over the people of Conall, Eoghan, and Oriel <note>The kingdoms of Cenél Conaill, Cenél Eoghain, and Airgíalla in the north</note> was beyond counting. As long as Eoghan Bél lived, there was never a day of peace with them; every quarter of a year—even every month—he raided them and put them to the sword. The descendants of Niall thought it a hard and grievous thing to constantly endure the violence of Eoghan Bél and the descendants of Fiachra, while the rest of Connacht was also against them. Therefore, the people of Ulster in general, debating what they should do, resolved to gather and prepare for a full-scale invasion, and so they fell upon the land of Connacht.

Two kings ruled Ulster at this time: Fergus and Donall, the two sons of Muirchertach mac Erca. They now took great spoils from Connacht and completely ravaged everything before them as far as the River Moy. They were a gathering "five battles" <term>a "battle" (cath) was a traditional unit of several hundred to a thousand soldiers</term> strong while driving away this plunder. The territory of the Clan Fiachra...

<summary>This page introduces King Eoghan Bél of Connacht, a powerful and undefeated ruler whose constant raiding of Ulster leads to a massive retaliatory invasion by the sons of Muirchertach mac Erca.</summary>
<keywords>Eoghan Bél, Connacht, Ulster, Uí Néill, Clan Fiachra, medieval warfare, cattle raiding, Fergus and Donall, River Moy</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the conflict between Eoghan Bél, King of Connacht, and the Northern dynasties (the Uí Néill). Following a raid by the Northerners, Eoghan Bél pursues them, leading to the pivotal Battle of Sligo.</meta>

<page-num>51</page-num>

# Saint Cellach of Killala

The warriors set out to pursue them, but not a single cow was recovered nor a sword blunted against the enemy until, at the bridge of *Martra*, Eoghan’s family and household overtook them and pressed them hard in a fierce fight. At *Sceichín na Gaoithe* <note>original: "sceichín na gaoithe," meaning "the little hawthorn of the wind"</note>, Eoghan himself caught up with them. Seeing such a vast army, he sent ambassadors—men of learning and the arts <note>likely poets and scholars who served as diplomats</note>—to Fergus, Donall, and the nobles of Ulster. They were to command them to abandon the stolen cattle <note>original: "prey"</note> in full and depart in peace, or else face an immediate challenge to battle. 

The envoys found Fergus and Donall and delivered Eoghan's message. But because they had the stolen cattle right in front of them, they were confident and eager for a fight, and they refused to return anything. The Northern forces consisted of five battalions from the Uí Néill <note>original: "clan-Neill"</note> and Ulster, along with the men of Oriel. Opposing them was one massive battalion of the Uí Fiachrach <note>original: "clan-Fiachrach"</note> and the warriors of Connacht in their own separate companies, all under the command of Eoghan Bél.

When Eoghan heard the response his poets brought back from the Uí Néill, he dismounted; they told him that war was now his only choice, and the Ulstermen declared that even if he fought, he would never win back a single cow. Then the men of Connacht armed themselves and launched a sudden, swift, and unsparing charge upon the Uí Néill. At the sight of Eoghan’s standard and the banners that had triumphed in so many raids, the Ulstermen wavered. Both sides, quivering with hatred, clashed together, and the Battle of Sligo began. 

The North of Ireland was defeated; their plunder was recaptured, and a great number of their people were slaughtered. Fergus and Donall also died there. However, Eoghan Bél was severely wounded and had to be carried away on the shafts of spears. Some say he lived for three more days; others say he lasted a week. The nobles came and went to see him, mourning him deeply during that time.

Surgeons treated King Eoghan Bél, but in the end, it was certain that he would die. The children of Fiachra sought his advice on who should be made chief in his place. Eoghan Bél said: "Your situation is difficult. I have two sons: Cellach, who is a disciple of Kieran of Cluain <note>Saint Ciarán of Clonmacnoise</note>, and Muiredach, the younger son, who is not yet old enough to be inaugurated as king. My advice to you is..."

# Saint Cellach of Killala

"Therefore, go to Clonmacnoise <note>original: Cluain</note>, to where Kieran is, and entreat him, begging for his consent so that Cellach may be released to go with you and be made chief, seeing as you have no one else who is fit. In this matter, be careful to beseech him urgently <note>original: instantly</note>."

This having been done, Eoghan prescribed the manner of his burial: in the open field on the borders of the Uí Fiachrach <note>original: clan-Fiachrach</note>, with his red spear in his hand and his face toward the North. "For," he said, "as long as my grave faces them—with my face also turned toward them—they shall not withstand Connacht in battle."

He was buried accordingly, and the rest of what he prophesied was truly accomplished; for wherever the Uí Néill <note>original: clan-Neill</note> and the men of Connacht happened to meet afterward, it was defeat that fell upon the Uí Néill and the North in general. Therefore, the descendants of Niall and the North made a plan: they would come with a great host to the fort of the Uí Fiachrach <note>original: ráth ua Fiachrach</note>, exhume Eoghan, and carry him off northwards over Sligo <note>original: Sligeach</note>. They did so, and away in the flat land of Lough Gill <note>original: loch Gile</note>, he was buried with his face downwards.

But just as Eoghan Bél had instructed them to go, the Uí Fiachrach went to Clonmacnoise and to the place where Kieran was in prayer. When they came to him, he welcomed them and settled them in a room <note>original: cubicle</note>. That night they were well provided for, and they explained their errand to Kieran afterward; but he utterly refused to give up his disciple.

Nevertheless, even though he refused their request, they stayed in Clonmacnoise <note>original: Cluain</note> a second night until Cellach came to visit them. They conferred with him and begged him to go with them; in the end, he yielded and agreed to accompany them. He departed the next morning without taking any farewell of his spiritual master at all.

Kieran was told how his disciple had been stolen away without his advice or permission. Kieran said: "If he is indeed gone, then may the choice he has made not thrive with him, but let him have a curse <note>original: malison</note> on whatever he undertakes. May it be that, at the last, a destructive grief comes upon him, and 'death by a sharp point' <note>likely referring to death by a spear or sword</note> be what removes him. I, acting for my Lord who is King of Heaven and Earth, further decree that for all time, such a 'death by point' shall be that which—beyond all help and without fail—takes whoever deserts their life as a student of the church."

As for Cellach: the children of Fiachrach led him away and con—

<meta>This page continues the story of Cellach, the son of Eoghan Bél. Despite his father's wish for him to leave the monastery and lead the Clan Fiachrach, Cellach's transition from monk to chieftain is fraught with conflict and spiritual regret.</meta>

# Saint Cellach of Killala

[They] conferred on him the chieftainship <note>original: chiefry</note> of the Clan Fiachrach <note>The descendants of Fiachra</note> from the River Robe <note>original: Rodhba</note> to the Codnach. He held it for a while, but when he heard that his teacher <note>Saint Kieran</note> had cursed him, he grew to dislike that way of life. At that same time, Guaire, the son of Colman, was so renowned that his fame and honor excelled throughout Ireland; the territory of the Clan Fiachrach of Aidhne <note>A region in modern County Galway</note> belonged entirely to him. 

Thus, without delay, matters concerning land ownership went poorly between the two of them, and it soon became common knowledge that they hated one another. Even so, they arranged a meeting <note>original: trysted</note> and made peace. However, Guaire acted with guile during this peace-making and treated Cellach treacherously: he killed as many of Cellach's people as he could catch, while Cellach himself escaped secretly from the camp with twenty-seven <note>original: thrice nine</note> of his followers.

For a full year, he was "under wood" <note>an idiom meaning he lived as a fugitive and outlaw in the forest</note>. He was filled with weariness and remorse for ever forsaking his life as a student, as well as for all the kindness that Kieran had shown him. He rebuked himself continually, so grieved was he by what had happened to him. 

"Woe is me!" he cried. "Why did it ever enter my head, for the sake of the material things <note>original: grossness</note> of this wretched, fleeting world, to abandon my studies and my master?" Then he said:

> "Alas for the man who forsakes the religious life <note>original: clerkly life</note> for any of this vile, crude world’s estates—woe to him who gives up the great love of a faithful God for the royalty of a passing world! 
> 
> Alas for him who takes up arms in this life, unless he performs penance for it; better for a person are the white-paged books from which the canonical psalms are chanted. 
> 
> As grand as the art of war may be, it brings little profit and is filled with heavy toil; from it, one gains only a very short life, which must in the end be exchanged for Hell. 
> 
> But of all professions, theft <note>original: stealth</note> is the worst: sneaking, perjured, and nimble thieving. He who commits it, even if he were once a good man, is from then on regarded only as a wicked person. 
> 
> A large portion of these evils has now fallen upon Cellach, son of Eoghan. As he wanders from table to table with a gang of villains, let him beware of death. 
> 
> Alas for the one who abandons Heaven, the blessed home of saints, to suffer the dark, murky slavery of Hell; O Christ, Ruler of Battles, woe to him who deserts his mighty Lord!"

After this great fit of repentance <note>original: penitence</note> came over Cellach, he decided on a plan: the nine companions who had been with him in the recent war against Guaire should seek out his former tutor, Kieran of Clonmacnoise <note>original: Cluain</note>. Cellach himself was hesitant to trust Kieran because he had previously disobeyed him. He waited outside of Clonmacnoise until he met some of his former classmates <note>original: whilom condisciples</note>.

# Saint Cellach of Killala

...and fellow clerics. They bade him welcome and kissed him; he entered the town with them and, unknown to Kieran, stayed there that night. On the following morning, the leaders of the community went with him to the place where Kieran was to beg for peace and mercy; and there he knelt before his master. Then, although his initial anger had been so great, Kieran repented of the curse which he had laid on Cellach and granted him peace: "My son," he said, "if I were able to do it, I would revoke your curse; but since I cannot, may God never be less favorable to you because of it, nor may your place in Heaven be lost because I spoke such words."

The grace of the Holy Spirit and the love of the Trinity then entered into Cellach. He commanded his people to return to the place where his brother Muiredach was (at that time, the youth happened to be in the house of the King of Luighne <note>An ancient kingdom in the west of Ireland, in parts of modern Sligo and Mayo</note>): "Stay with him," Cellach said, "and remain loyal to him always." As Cellach commanded them, so they went their way and from then on became Muiredach’s people.

As for Cellach, he devoted himself zealously to study, pursuing it strenuously and with careful attention. For every degree of progress in his learning, his almsgiving, charity, and all his other good works increased threefold. The fame of his piety spread throughout Ireland; men loved him with a universal love, and because Cellach acted in all things according to his teacher's <note>original: preceptor</note> word, Kieran was well pleased with him. He was then ordained as a priest, a role in which he remained for a long time; but then the clergy of his tribe came and elected him to a bishopric. He was consecrated in episcopal orders, and he took Killala as his bishop’s see. From then on, he administered this greater bishopric, though he remained mostly at Clonmacnoise rather than in his own diocese. In all of Ireland, there was no one of greater renown for honor, piety, and scholarly <note>original: clerkly</note> bearing; no one whom the learned cherished more dearly, and people of all ranks and backgrounds remained loyal to him.

Once upon a time, while on a bishop's official tour <note>original: visitation</note>, he came to Kilmore of the Moy <note>Cill Mhór Muaidhe, near the River Moy in County Mayo</note> with a great company of mounted clerics. It so happened that Guaire, son of Colman, was at Dúrlas Guaire that day with many of his trusted companions <note>original: confidentials</note>. In his immediate company were his own son, Nar mac Guaire, and Nemedh, the son of Ferchoga, who was an untainted <note>original: uncontaminated</note> [i.e., utterly...

# Saint Cellach of Killala

...and was a devoted fosterling to Guaire. To Guaire, a man named Nemedh said: "Looking unfriendly and with an ill-tempered expression, Bishop Cellach has ignored us." Guaire answered: "It does not matter; I will send messengers after him to ask him to come speak with me." He then sent one of his trusted advisors to Cellach (at that time, it was exactly noon on a Saturday). 

The envoy said to the bishop: "Because you passed him by a little while ago, Guaire is displeased with you; yet he asks that you come even now and speak with him." 

"I will not go," Cellach replied. "It is the time for evening prayers <note>original: vesper-time</note>, and I will not break the Sabbath. I will say my prayers <note>original: hours</note> and celebrate Mass here tomorrow. If it pleases him, let him come to hear it and then speak with me afterward; he does not have far to travel. But, if he does not wish to do that, then—with his consent—I will go to him on Monday."

The messenger returned to Guaire and repeated everything the King had said; in addition, he claimed that Cellach had flatly refused to come and accused the bishop of having no love for Guaire at all. Because of this, great anger entered into Guaire, and he said to his messengers: "Return to Cellach. Warn him that he must leave this country tonight. If he does not go, then the church he is in shall be burned down around him—the building and all his people with it." 

The same messenger found Cellach again and delivered Guaire's message in full. "May God stand between me and the unrighteous," Cellach replied, and he did not leave that spot until Monday morning. 

He departed then and came to the borders of Lough Conn <note>original: loch Con</note>, where he spent the night. Next, he reached the lake that people today call Claenloch <note>Likely Cloonagh Lake in County Mayo</note>. He gazed upon it until he saw an island—named *Etgar's Island* <note>original: oilén Etgair</note>—where it was revealed to him that many angels were at work <note>original: angelic ministration</note>. 

As he drew near, he asked whether any saint had ever blessed the island; but the local people said that no saint had ever conferred a blessing upon it. Then Cellach said: "Even so; it is here that I am ordained to be a hermit." 

When his followers mocked him and tried to talk him out of his plan to stay on the island, he replied: "It is decreed that I must stay here. But you must leave, for there are many various places for you to serve within my bishopric."

Reluctant as they were, they did as he asked, leaving only four clerics in his...

# Saint Cellach of Killala

The company left Cellach all alone; these four were *Maelcróin*, *Maeldálua*, *Maelsenaigh*, and *Macdeoraidh*, who were once Cellach’s fellow students <note>original: condisciples</note>. From Shrovetide until Easter, they continued performing their duties, serving God zealously. News spread throughout Ireland that the holy bishop Cellach (having abandoned his bishopric) was living the life of a hermit. When Easter arrived, his brother Muiredach (the son of Eoghan Bél) visited him often and did nothing without his advice.

When Guaire heard all of this, he was possessed by rage and felt great enmity toward Cellach. As bad as things had been between them before, they now became much worse. Guaire feared that Muiredach—prompted by his brother Cellach, and because of his own natural abilities and his awareness that he was fit to be a leader <note>original: apt matter of a chief</note>—would seize total power.

Furthermore, Guaire’s son Nar and Nemedh, the son of Ferchoga, constantly plagued Guaire with fabricated and wicked stories about the bishop. They kept insisting to Guaire that he must kill the holy bishop Cellach. Together, they devised a plan of treason: they would invite Cellach to visit them and have poison ready for him. Although Guaire hated him, the king did not want weapons to be used against Cellach in his own presence.

This is what they did: intending to kill Cellach, they prepared poison. Then they sent messengers to the island in the lake <note>original: loch</note> where he lived. They were instructed that if Cellach refused to come, they should invite his fellow students to return to Guaire instead, so they could serve as friendly messengers between the two men.

On his island, these messengers found Cellach just as he was reading his daily prayers <note>original: hours</note> and saluted him. He greeted them, and they told him they had come from Guaire to bring him to a great feast the king was holding for him, and to speak with him.

“I will go there no more,” Cellach said, “nor will I neglect my prayers for the sake of a feast or the favor of this perishing, poor world.”

“Never do what they ask,” his fellow students cried. “It is foolishness for Guaire to imagine that you could be drawn to love him through such things.”

The messengers then said, “Then allow your fellow students to come with us; Guaire will be pleased with you for that, and they can carry whatever private messages <note>original: privy errand</note> he has for you.”

Cellach said, “I will not stop them, nor will I force them to go.” When Maelcróin and the others heard this, all four of them went together...

# Saint Cellach of Killala

They accompanied the messengers on their return to Guaire, where he was staying in *Dúrlas*. He welcomed them and rejoiced at their arrival; they were diligently provided with food and drink.

Then a separate banqueting house was prepared for them, and the fort's best liquor was brought there for their use. Two of them were seated on either side of Guaire and, hoping to persuade them to abandon Cellach, he promised them great gifts: all the territory of Tirawley; four single women <note>original: "spinster women"</note> of their own choosing from the province, along with a full complement of horses and cattle for these wives (these gifts were to be secured to them by a legal covenant); and a complete set of weapons for each man. They stayed there that night, and at the morning meal, they all agreed with one accord to kill Cellach. They left for Loch Conn; where they had left the boat, they found it, and after rowing out, they reached Cellach. He was in this state: his psalter <term>A book containing the Psalms</term> was spread before him as he recited the psalms; he never spoke to them; he finished his prayers and, looking at them, noticed their eyes were shifting and clouded with the look of a murderer <note>original: "parricide," the act of killing a parent or a person in a similar position of authority</note>.

"Young men," said Cellach, "you have a wicked look; since you left me your natures have changed, and I see that for King Guaire’s sake you have agreed to murder me." They did not deny it for a moment, and he continued: "It is an evil plan; but do not pursue your own ruin any longer, for I can give you gifts that will benefit you far more than Guaire’s promises." They replied: "By no means, Cellach, will we do as you wish, for if we did, we could not find shelter anywhere in all Ireland;" and even as they spoke, they plunged their spears into Cellach all at once; yet he managed to thrust his psalter between himself and his robe. They placed him in the middle of the boat, with two of them in the bow, and reached the shore; then they carried him into the great forest and into the dark depths of the woods. Cellach said: "This deed you wish to perform I consider a truly wicked work; if you would renounce it even now, you might find safety forever in Clonmacnoise; or if you would rather go to Bláthmac and Dermot (the sons of Aedh Sláine) who now rule Ireland, you would be secure with them." Then he composed these words:—

# Saint Cellach of Killala

> “O you young men who terrify me, pride is an abomination to Heaven’s high King; as twisted as your eyes are, the secrets of your hearts are even more perverted. Since you have conspired against me—a cruel decision foretelling violence—the shame of it shall last a long time for you, and this murder of your master <note>original: "parricide"; while technically the killing of a parent, here it refers to the killing of a spiritual father or mentor</note> will yet bring you to repentance. Though it is you who are [visibly] killing me, I believe you are not my true slayers; rather, it is the curse of Kieran, my teacher <note>Saint Ciarán of Clonmacnoise, who supposedly cursed Cellach for leaving the monastery to pursue his claim to the throne</note>, [that strikes me]—the sting of a burn is hottest in the lingering pain. This curse is very bad for me, yet I do not seek to avoid my butchery; but to you, it shall be a plague and a horror that you ever laid a bloody hand on me. I have a certain One on my side, the like of whom does not exist: my cause is bound up closely with Christ, and the angels’ Heaven shall be my dwelling place. It was treason when you decided to attack me unrighteously; but in the end, death by the sword’s point shall work your destruction and, O young men, Hell awaits you!”

“Giving us further advice on this matter is useless,” they retorted; “we will not do [your bidding].”

“Well then,” he pleaded, “grant me this one night’s respite for God’s sake.”

“Reluctant as we are to grant it, we will give you that,” they said. Then they raised the swords which they had carried hidden in their clothes, and at the sight of them, a mighty fear took hold of Cellach. They searched the wood until they found a hollow oak with one narrow entrance, and Cellach was committed to this; they sat at the hole to watch him until the morning. They stayed there until the night was nearly over, when a longing for sleep came to them and a deep slumber fell upon them there. 

Cellach, in distress over his impending violent death, did not sleep at all. At that time, it was in his power to have fled (had he wished to do so), but in his heart he said that it would be a lack of faith for him to attempt to evade the designs of the living God. Moreover, he reflected that even if he were to flee, they would surely overtake him, as he was weak and feeble after the season of Lent [which had just passed]. 

Morning now shone upon them, and he (for fear of seeing the light and in terror of his death) shut the door. Yet he said: “To shirk God’s judgment is a lack of faith in me, since Kieran my teacher promised that I must meet this end.” As he spoke, he flung open the tree's door. Then the raven called, and the hooded crow <note>original: "scallcrow"</note>, the wren, and all the other birds. The kite of the yew tree of *Cluain-eo* came, as did the ‘red hound’ [a wolf] of *Druim mic Dair* (called the *Brécaire*, or ‘the deceiver’) whose lair was near the island’s landing place. 

“My dream from last Wednesday night was true,” said Cellach: “that four ‘wild dogs’ tore me apart and dragged me through the ferns; that I then fell down a precipice and never came up again.” Then he recited this poem <note>original: "lay"</note>:

# Saint Cellach of Killala

> “Hail to the fair Morning that falls like a flame upon the ground—hail to Him also who sends her—the Morning of many virtues, ever new! O fair Morning, so full of pride—O sister of the brilliant Sun—hail to you, beauteous Morning, who lights my little book for me! You see the guest in every dwelling—you shine on every tribe and family—hail, O white-necked and beautiful one, here with us now—O golden-fair and wonderful! My little book with its checkered pages tells me my life has not been right; Maelcróin—it is he whom I am right to fear: he is the one who comes to strike me down at last. O hooded crow <note>original: "scallcrow"; a regional name for the hooded crow (Corvus cornix)</note>, O hooded crow, gray-coated, sharp-beaked, pathetic bird! The intent of your desire is clear to me; you are no friend to Cellach. O raven, you who croak! If you are hungry now, O bird, do not depart from this fortress <term>rath: a circular earthen fortress or homestead</term> until you have gorged yourself on my flesh. Fiercely the kite <note>a bird of prey</note> from the yew tree of *Cluain-eo* will take part in the scramble; he will carry off his fill in his horn-colored talons, and he will not leave me in kindness. To the blow that fells me, the fox in the dark woods will respond with speed; he too, in cold and trackless boundaries, shall devour a portion of my flesh and blood. The wolf in the fortress on the eastern side of *Druim mic Dair*: he comes to me on a passing visit, so that he may lead the lowlier pack. Last Wednesday night I had a dream: wild dogs dragged me as one toward the east and west through the reddish-brown ferns. I had a dream: that men took me into a green glen; there were four who carried me there, but (so it seemed to me) they never brought me back again. I had a dream: that my fellow students <term>condisciples: fellow students or disciples</term> led me to their house; they poured out a drink for me, and they also drank a toast to me. O tiny wren with such a short tail! You have piped a prophetic song mournfully; surely you have come to betray me and to shorten my life. Why should Macdeoraidh seek to hurt me through treason? It is a monstrous act, for our fathers were brothers. Why should Maeldálua go about to injure me—he who has truly shown me treachery? For our mothers were sisters. Why should Maelsenaig lust to harm me—he who has used me deceitfully in this conspiracy? For I know well that he is the son of a pure man—Maelsenaig, son of Maelibair. O Maelcróin, O Maelcróin, you are resolved on a deed that is wicked! For a thousand golden ingots, Eoghan’s son [Cellach himself] would never have consented to your death. O Maelcróin, O Maelcróin, it is wealth <term>pelf: money or wealth, often gained dishonestly</term> you have taken to betray me! You have accepted it for the sake of this world, and accepted it for the sake of Hell. All the precious things I ever had—all the sleek-coated young horses—I would have given them to Maelcróin so that he would not commit this treason against me. But Mary’s great Son above speaks to me like this: ‘You must have the earth [for burial], but you shall have Heaven; a welcome awaits you, Cellach.’ ”

Cellach was then lifted out of the tree by them, and Macdeoraidh struck him first; afterward, Maeldálua, Maelsenaig, and Maelcróin struck him in turn. In this fashion, they put the holy bishop to death—Cellach, son of Eoghan Bél. # Saint Cellach of Killala

...met them with great joy. The ravens, the hooded crows <note>original: "scallcrows"; a common name for the hooded crow (Corvus cornix)</note>, and the forest’s various predatory animals flocked together (just as he himself had predicted). They consumed some of his flesh and blood; however, every predator that ate even a small amount of him died instantly.

Regarding the holy Bishop Cellach’s brother, Muiredach, son of Eoghan Bél: on that same day, he came looking for his brother. He had often come to speak with him and seek his counsel, for he did nothing without Cellach’s guidance (since Cellach was his teacher, his brother, and his spiritual father all in one). When he arrived at the island's ferry port as usual, he heard neither Cellach's voice nor his chanting from across the water. He and his men found a boat at the port, but when they reached the island, they found it completely empty; Cellach was not there at all.

They returned in haste. As soon as Muiredach learned through questioning that the young clerics had been at King Guaire’s house, he knew that Cellach had been marked for death there. He then traveled toward the place where the *Congheilt* <note>The name of the location, likely meaning "The Wood-Grazier"</note> lived, between Loch Cuilinn and Loch Con. A raging beast opposed them there, guarding the *Congheilt*; it immediately killed nine of his people before his eyes. Conall, the son of Eochaidh and Muiredach's fellow student <term>condisciple: a classmate or fellow student</term>, scolded him for this. He said that a king's son who could stand by while his people were slaughtered by a beast was nothing but a coward. 

Muiredach went out in search of the monster and dived into the lake, but he did not find it the first time. He went a second time, and on the third attempt, he found its track. He followed it up out of the lake until he found the creature sleeping, gorged with food. He thrust his sword through her and into the earth; with the weapon still stuck in her, the beast fled and sprang back into the lake. Muiredach followed the track and fought her. Though he was severely wounded in the fight, he eventually killed the beast, took its head, and carried it back to shore to Conall and his people. 

Conall said, “That was a gallant fight you fought, my son, to slay the monster of the *Congheilt*. Because of this, your name shall be ‘Cuchongeilt’” <note>literally "The Hound of the Congheilt"</note>. From then on, the practice grew of calling him Cuchongeilt. They left that place and zealously followed a trail of five people through the wild woods, until they found the clubs...

# Saint Cellach of Killala.

...where those killers had left them. “Even so,” said Muiredach: “as a sign to slay Cellach, these clubs were brought from Guaire. Let them lie there, and let us follow the tracks of the band.” Again they followed the trail, and so they found the tree with Cellach’s body: partly eaten by the wild creatures. The gruesome deed lay heavy upon Muiredach, and he said:

> “Dear was he whose body this is: I liken his death to my own; I see the corpse of Cellach, son of Eoghan Bél, drenched in its own blood. I have no sister, alas, in either Ireland or Scotland! My father is dead, my mother is dead, and now God has left me brotherless. Unless it be with pure Gelghéis, or else with Conall, son of Eochaidh, I do not know if there is any kindness or dear love left for me now. O Loch Claen, and O Loch Claen, from now on you shall prosper no more! For you did not save from slaughter that which is now only the corpse of Eoghan’s son, Cellach. You renounced your bands of <term>kerns</term> <note>original: "kerne"; Irish Gaelic "ceithern", meaning light-armed foot soldiers</note>, Cellach, to follow the singing of psalms with light; you gave up deeds of valor for books full of all purity. You deserted the feasting-house to frequent the altar; you forgave tributes, O man! and placed your love in Jesus the Beloved. In vengeance for high Eoghan’s son, Macdeoraidh is as good as slain by me; Macdeoraidh shall lie lapped in his own blood, he who butchered dear Eoghan’s son in this way. His pious clerical life was good in his beautiful yew-shaded church; dear was his head of fair hair, dear is his corpse and well-beloved. In vengeance for the pale-skinned Cellach, Maeldálua is as good as fallen by my hand; if Maelsenaigh had a part in this foul treason, he too has fallen. As for Maelcróin—as rare as gold is, I would give it to have the ruthless slaying of him.”

When this was done, they carried Cellach’s body to Dromore, which is now called Turlach; but for fear of Guaire <note>King Guaire Aidne mac Colmáin, who ordered the murder</note>, the people of Turlach would not allow him to be laid to rest with them. They came to Liscallan; but the <term>familia</term> <note>original: "familia"; Latin for "family," here referring to the monastic community</note> of Killcallan, also dreading Guaire, did not endure to have him buried with them. Cuchongeilt, being vexed at this, said that he would be avenged on them for their refusal. They had not gone far from the church when they saw it ablaze with fire—fire fallen from heaven—that flamed on high, burning because they did not agree to take in Cellach’s body. Since that time, there has been no human habitation in that spot.

While they were still there, they saw two wild deer coming toward them with a <term>wain</term> <note>original: "wain"; a large wagon or cart</note>, which they drew between them with great effort until they came alongside the body. Amidst that company, the stags laid their bier upon the ground, and to all those who saw it happen, it seemed most strange; but at the miracle...

# Saint Cellach of Killala.

When they saw the miracle God had performed for the sake of holy Cellach, they rejoiced exceedingly. On the bier which the two stags had carried, they laid the corpse; then they moved onward until they reached the Eskers <note>The 'Eskers' (Eiscir Riada) are a series of glacial ridges that stretch across the center of Ireland.</note> in the west. There they saw a church with an adjoining monastic cell; at the door of that cell, the deer laid the body down, and the church bells began to ring by themselves. The clergy, having come out and standing over the body, asked who it might be; and when they learned the truth, they zealously sang psalms for the rest of his soul. A company of angels also descended from Heaven to honor his soul and his place of burial on earth. Furthermore, the same deer came daily and ploughed the land just like oxen. When their ploughing was finished at noon, they would go to Cellach’s tomb to lick it. Then Cuchongeilt came and, standing at his brother’s grave, said:

> “Sorrowful and wretched, I stand here after the loss of my brother who cherished me; from the day the son of Eoghan ceased to live, I seek his home no longer. Evil shall come to the one who committed this treason, and his noble home shall become a desert after him; he who butchered you in the east shall lie upon the Devil’s black flagstones. Woe to anyone who trusts them or enters their house, or who confides in the children of Colman, son of Cobthach; the deed brought about by Guaire shall subject him to the misery of eternal woe.”

From every <term>airt</term> <note>original: "airt"; a word meaning a point of the compass or a specific direction.</note> in Connacht, those who had loved Cellach and had been his friends gathered to Cuchongeilt, so that three hundred armed men were assembled in one spot. Seeing that he could not yet find a successful way to wage war against Guaire, Cuchongeilt resolved to go to Marcan, king of Hy-Many and of *Medraighe*; from him, he received a guarantee of protection against all of Ireland. Cuchongeilt struck his hand in Marcan's to seal the pact, and for twelve months Marcan provided quarters for his men. Cuchongeilt himself stayed in Marcan’s house for that time and was shown great honor. But now, when the year had passed, Marcan said to him: “Cuchongeilt, depart tomorrow; it is not out of any lack of hospitality that I say this to you, but because we cannot risk offending Guaire by keeping you with us any longer.” And Marcan said:

> “Your visit to my house, Cuchongeilt son of Eoghan, has been good; O yellow-haired son of Eoghan, your strength grows like a flood! At early morning tomorrow, go bravely on your way, and stay for a year with them—with the noble sons of Aedh Sláine. May the path you take be prosperous, O...

### Saint Cellach of Killala

"Son of Eoghan, generous one! May you have a favorable journey from Marcan's house, so that your travels may end well."

They traveled eastward across the Shannon River—three hundred men in total—on to Tara, where Dermot and Bláthmac (the sons of Aedh Sláine) <note>Aedh Sláine was a High King of Ireland; his sons Dermot and Bláthmac ruled as joint High Kings in the 7th century.</note> were staying. There, they were welcomed. Cuchongeilt's people were lodged across the *tuatha* <note>original: "tuatha"; an Irish term for a kingdom, territory, or the people living within it.</note> of Bregia, while he and a portion of his inner circle joined Bláthmac’s personal company and were held in high honor. 

Now Bláthmac had a proud, unmarried daughter named Aife. A romance began between her and Cuchongeilt; they fell deeply in love, and very few people at the time had any hint of their relationship. One day, while Cuchongeilt was playing chess with Bláthmac and losing the game, Aife came and stood over her father, prompting Cuchongeilt to make a move that would disadvantage her father. 

Looking at her closely, Bláthmac said, "You are very eager to give advice against me, daughter, and you have cost me the game. Truly, there must be a friendship between you and Cuchongeilt." 

She replied, "I do not seek to hide it." 

"Why then," he asked, "since you admit to it, did you not seek my permission?" 

Cuchongeilt said, "We have done nothing wrong so far, and we will not act further without your consent." 

"In that case, I will not stand between you and your love," Bláthmac said. "Though many seek her hand, I will give her to you; I consider you a worthy son-in-law." 

The wedding feast was held that night, and they lived together happily for a time. This continued until one night, while Aife and Cuchongeilt were talking softly, she said to him, "Though you have a brave appearance and a great reputation, it is a major flaw in you that your courage is so weak and your resilience so small." 

"Where did that idea come from?" he asked. 

"From your failure to take revenge on those who killed your brother," she replied. 

"Your words are right, young woman," he replied, feeling ashamed of what his wife had said to him. 

Rising early the next morning, Cuchongeilt sent a secret message to his people. They flocked to him from all directions, and surrounded by them, they marched out of the town. Hoping to stop him, Bláthmac and all the nobles of the fort gathered there; yet Cuchongeilt would not stop, even to please his father-in-law. This caused Aife terrible grief, and she commanded everyone to prevent him from leaving, for—

"If the women of Connacht see him, they will love him, and I shall never see him again." Then, when she could not hold him back, her heart grew heavy, and she composed a poem:

> "What I have spoken is a matter of grief: I have reproached a man who committed no crime; it is not God’s Son [but my own petulance] that has sent Eoghan’s son to wander. Immediately then, sorrow filled me; my strength will increase no more; rather than remain in Bregia, I would depart to follow Cuchongeilt. The man of challenges—the winner of prizes in every gathering—I fear for him; [I fear] that, even if he reaches his country by a circular route, he will still be vulnerable <note>original: "obnoxious"; in Early Modern English, this meant being exposed to harm or subject to someone's power.</note> to Guaire’s traps. I will no longer seek pleasure—from now on, sorrow holds all my heart; my death is undoubtedly closer to me than a simple illness is to another. Alas that he ever came to Tara: he who is gentle and kind to young women; and as quickly as he sets out now for Guaire’s country, the time will come when he shall feel regret."

Concerning Cuchongeilt and his men: they traveled westwards across the <term>tuatha</term> <note>original: "tuatha"; an Irish term for a kingdom, territory, or the people living within it.</note> of Bregia and Meath, over the Shannon River, through Connacht, and so into Tirawley. These were his own ancestral lands, where they immediately faced hardship; for their numbers were so great that they could not easily hide, and they had no food at hand. Cuchongeilt headed for a house he had known long ago in *Glenn Mac Ú-Arann* in the west, and that night they all fit inside it. Cuchongeilt left them there while he went out alone to scout the country. 

He had not gone far when he saw a massive herd of pigs and watched them until he spotted a strong, heavy hog. He threw a javelin at it and killed it. Then a swineherd came running to him and asked, "Man, why have you killed a pig that was not yours?" 

"A desire to kill it came over me, for I am hungry," Cuchongeilt answered. 

But the swineherd said, "The deed you have done will cause you regret yet." 

"Come this way for a moment and let us speak together," said Cuchongeilt. The young man tried to avoid him but could not manage it. Having the swineherd in his power, Cuchongeilt questioned him: "Whose are these pigs? Does the owner live in this country?" 

The swineherd answered: "If you are indeed from the province of Connacht, it seems strange to me that you do not know the four men who own this land: Maelcróin, Maeldálua, Maelsenaigh, and Macdeoraidh. These four were <term>condisciples</term> <note>original: "condisciples"; fellow students or disciples who studied together under the same teacher.</note> of Cellach, the son of Eoghan Bél; for everyone has heard how he gave this country over to them."

# Saint Cellach of Killala

“Your words are true,” Cuchongeilt said; but the swineherd stood and scrutinized him closely. “Why do you stare at me so?” “If I am right—and though it is long since I last looked upon you—you are Cuchongeilt, the son of Eoghan Bél.” “Your recognition is certain,” Cuchongeilt assented. The young man threw his arms around Cuchongeilt’s neck and kissed him three times, then asked: “And do you know me?” “Not yet.” “I am that little boy whom you used to see with your own brother Cellach; and I thank God that He has guided you to me before any other man in this country. But do you have a company? Do you have people with you?” “I do; I have come here in search of meat for them.” “How many are there?” “Three hundred who are skilled in arms and valiant as one man.” 

“Then a single hog is far too little for them,” said the herd. “But lead them here to me, so that they may take enough of the swine for a night’s supply for everyone. From now on, I am on your side; I am the one who will guide you through this land in the time to come. I will deliver it into your hand and instruct you how to reach the four men who slew your brother Cellach. They are currently in *Dún Fidhne* <note>A fort, likely located in modern County Mayo.</note>, where they have recently built a fort with four doors—a door for each of them: Maeldálua, Maelsenaigh, Maelcróin, and Mac-deoraidh. Up to this day, their *Irachts* <note>original: *oireachtas*; a district assembly or the people of a territory</note> have opposed them. For the inauguration of their fort, I will bring the swine to them and also take a supply of rushes <note>Used for floor covering in ancient Irish halls.</note>; nevertheless, kill as many of the porkers as you need.” Cuchongeilt answered: “I will go with you; and as for your load of rushes, I will be the one to carry it.” “I am well pleased with that,” said the herd.

They went away then, but first Cuchongeilt ordered his people to follow after him once they had finished their meal. They were to wait until the night grew dark and then travel by lonely and tangled paths toward *Dún Fidhne*. The swineherd made for the *dún* <note>term: a fortified residence or earthwork</note> with his hogs, accompanied by Cuchongeilt, who had the load of rushes on his back and his weapons girded about him, well hidden beneath his clothes. To those who questioned him, asking, “Who is that under the load?” the herd would answer, “It is a fellow-herder of mine.”

# Saint Cellach of Killala

The four men who occupied the fort each had a private door. Cuchongeilt, wearing the clothes of a swineherd and accompanied by the actual swineherd, entered the *dún* <note>original: dún; a fortified residence or fort</note> and cast down his bundle on the floor. They sat down in the middle of the building among the servants of the feast. The swineherd thrust a golden drinking-horn into Cuchongeilt's hand; he took a drink from it and then carefully studied his enemies throughout the house. He said to the swineherd: "I will not leave this house tonight. You must go and fetch our people; bring them here, for these men are now all foolish and merely drunk." 

Just as Cuchongeilt commanded, the swineherd went away and led the others back to the fort. As they arrived, they were never noticed until they stormed into the fort through all four doors at once. The four men who had previously slain Cellach, son of Eoghan, were captured on the spot: Maelcróin, Maeldálua, Maelsenaigh, and Macdeoraidh. All of their trusted guards were killed around them, but a proclamation was made to the general guests that they should remain in their seats and continue drinking, as they were all friends to Cuchongeilt. He and his people sat among them after their enemies were destroyed, and they drank and made merry together until morning. 

At early dawn, they rose from the banquet and carried the four captives westward through the country in bonds. They passed near *Lec Turscair* <note>A local landmark, likely meaning "the stone of the sea-wrack"</note> with the sea-resounding River Moy on their right. Four long, thick posts were brought to that place; the four captives were laid upon them and, while they were still alive, their limbs were cut off. Their torsos were then hung up and they were strangled to death. Because of this, *Ard na Riagh* <note>original: Ard na Riagh; modern Ardnarea, meaning "the Hill of Executions"</note> has been the name of that place ever since, as a poet once said:

> "These executions are timely, O Cuchongeilt, son of Eoghan! The deaths of Maeldálua, Maelcróin, Maelsenaigh, and Macdeoraidh. A violent, mutilating, and untimely death, followed by the hanging of their carcasses—my God, it is a joy to speak of it, for torment was their rightful due. Their shame shall endure for a long time, even until the arrival of the world-striking Judgment Day; their souls are with the Devil, and it was right to strangle them."

After the four were hanged, Cuchongeilt entered the territory of Hy-Fiachrach <note>The land of the descendants of Fiachra, in modern County Mayo and Sligo</note>. Although many of his people were killed in the process, he assumed power over the inhabitants and took their hostages as a sign of submission. From then on, the fame of his generosity and bravery increased greatly. As great a king as Guaire was, Cuchongeilt became even dearer than him to all the poets, scholars, and craftsmen throughout Ireland. Over...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the conflict between Cuchongeilt and Guaire following the death of Saint Cellach.</meta>

# Saint Cellach of Killala

In the territories of Tirawley and Northern Hy-Fiachrach <note>A tribal territory in modern County Mayo</note>, Cuchongeilt then and there established himself as supreme ruler. Meanwhile, in the south, Guaire was lord of Hy-Fiachrach Aidhne <note>A territory in modern County Galway</note>. A violent war immediately broke out between the two, and it would be impossible to describe all the violence and evil that Cuchongeilt inflicted upon Guaire. In short, the conflict between them nearly resulted in the total destruction of both their clans <note>original: "Irachts"</note>, and even threatened to bring the entire province to ruin.

Now, Guaire's daughter Gelghéis was deeply in love with Cuchongeilt; for the sake of this love, she had always refused to marry any other man. As the two kings grew weary of the war, Cuchongeilt pressured Guaire for his daughter’s hand in marriage. Guaire, however, absolutely refused to give her to him. Nevertheless, his people—desperate for the war to end—pleaded with Guaire so urgently that he finally consented, but only on these conditions: he himself would host the wedding feast, and Cuchongeilt must come to his house. Cuchongeilt would not agree to this under any circumstances, so they continued to wage war for a long time, until Guaire (to satisfy the majority of his people) was forced to seek peace. Exhausted by the struggle, he finally handed Gelghéis over to Cuchongeilt, and they lived together affectionately; at this time, Cuchongeilt’s reputation for generosity was highly regarded throughout Ireland.

However, although Cuchongeilt was now peaceful toward Guaire's people, he continued to desolate the churches in his own land, which Guaire considered a great evil. Therefore, Guaire practiced this treachery: he sought out the place where Kieran of Clonmacnoise <note>Saint Ciarán, founder of the famous monastery</note> was staying with his clergy. Guaire urged the saint to go and bring Cuchongeilt back with him to ensure their mutual peace and friendship. Guaire also had Kieran deliver a promise to Cuchongeilt: that if he would only come to Guaire's house, he would be allowed to rule his own country without opposition.

Kieran found Cuchongeilt and strongly encouraged him not to let the lordship of Connacht slip away, even if it meant risking a meeting with Guaire. Gelghéis also persuaded him to go, for she knew that Kieran harbored no treachery, and she could not imagine that Guaire would deal deceitfully with the saint. Against his own natural instincts, Cuchongeilt finally consented to accompany Kieran (many others also urged him to do so), and he remarked:

> "Even though I am escorted by a hundred men, I am loath to set out on this journey; but whether I return or not, it is more honorable that I go."

Here Cuchongeilt’s death is not recorded, but that is not intentional on our part <note>original: scribe’s note</note>:—

With a company of which Cuchongeilt was also a member, Saint Kieran came to *Dúrlas Guaire* <note>The fortified residence of King Guaire</note>, where for three nights they were served and cared for; and then, in Kieran’s presence, a peace treaty between Guaire and Cuchongeilt was established. But once Kieran had left the town, Guaire plotted to commit a murderous deed against his kinsman, his son-in-law, and a leading saint of Ireland. In *Dúrlas Guaire*, therefore, by Guaire (son of Colman, son of Eochaidh), a plan was carried out by which Eoghan Bél’s son, Cuchongeilt, was then and there put to death . . . . . as one poet said:—

> God having permitted it, Eoghan’s dwelling-place is empty tonight; whether built of timber or of stone, no house has risen there; it shall remain a lonely wilderness forever. A protector of women and of children the unconquered hero-warrior was—a leader of armed bands and of companies of poets—well might all men obey him. He was good at serving his friends’ needs—he was generous with gifts to the poets—and he was no drinker of ale in low-quality houses. At all times he desired the music of stringed instruments—the cry of hounds was melody to him—he delighted in a great company drinking mead, and he never agreed to hold a feast on islands <note>Likely referring to a preference for mainland hospitality over isolated retreats</note>. When his mother first happily gave birth to Olioll’s grandson, Eoghan Bél, the voice <gloss>that is, the acclamation</gloss> of every surrounding country welcomed the little blue-eyed child. Therefore it was that (as I now proclaim) the name of Eoghan Bél stayed with him; to the favorite of Connacht, and to Fiachra’s grandson of the flowing hair, the support of all chiefs was given. By the time he was sixteen years old, the youth’s physical stature was sufficient for him; and from then on, no man dared to attempt a raid or robbery upon the territory of the Hy-Fiachrach. His mind was inclined toward Meath, the territory of Flann’s son—his right hand was toward Saint Brendan’s fertile fort—his ‘smooth side’ <gloss>that is, his friendship</gloss> was toward Cruachan of the poetic companies—and his ‘rough side’ <gloss>that is, his hostility</gloss> was turned toward the people of Oriel. He delighted in the attacks made to enforce his tribute upon the noble men of Oriel—in plundering the descendants of Eoghan, and in preventing their alliance. He was never the man to go a single month—nor was he ever actually so long—without a journey, whether by land or by sea, to plunder the descendants of Conall. On the other side of Assaroe at one time (and a gallant charge it was), Eoghan did not leave the people of Ulster a single cow, from east to west, that he did not bring back into Connacht. Fury fell on the noble children of Niall; grief and martial rage possessed them; from the dark River Drowes to Kesh-corann of the hazel woods, they laid everything waste. At that time, Eoghan’s strength was only a small portion of his people: there where he was (with horses...

# Saint Cellach of Killala

And with hounds, and with sorrowful women, in the high fortress of Olioll's grandson. He—seeing his country's plundered goods driven past him—went into the men of Ulster like a mighty and raging bull and encountered them. He tore the prey away from the children of Niall, but he, the king of Hy-Fiachrach <note>A tribal territory in ancient Connacht</note>, was himself wounded. Having reached his own house, he died; and tonight, the home of Eoghan is deserted.

The dwelling of gentle Cellach is also deserted—the home of him who lies mangled by the point of a weapon. Since Eoghan's son is undeniably dead, the churches of Connacht have withered away. Gentle Cellach's dwelling-place is a desert, he having been torn by weapon points... The habitation of Cuchongeilt <note>Cellach's brother</note> is a desert, the home of one to whom whole countries gave great love... He whom the River Moy most favored: alas, that he should have fallen by Guaire's violence! Alas for her whom it was his fate to love; woe that he ever listened to Gelghéis!

Had Saint Kieran <note>Saint Kieran of Clonmacnoise</note> but known everything, Cuchongeilt would never have met the death he did—had Brendan of pure piety known it, or Mac Duach... Until he had first denied them for a time, he did not go to Guaire's house with the company; he did not go until they had forced a fast upon him and three days of abstinence from food.

To Eoghan's most handsome son, Gelghéis of the blooming cheek said: "And would you then deal treasonably with the honor of Ireland's exalted saints? Had you only given up <term>Dúrlas</term> <note>The royal seat of King Guaire</note> and the level marshland of the Moy to Guaire, you would not have needed to enter his house under the guarantee of a saint or a <term>nemhed</term> <note>A privileged person or sacred site under Old Irish law</note>. Wit and wisdom are not equal—age and hardihood are not equal—gentleness and affection are not equal between you and the Clan Colman."

Cuchongeilt of the conventions answered: "Since you desire it, and to please you, I will go to the house of Colman's son, which is filled with many followers."

The clergy and Cuchongeilt equipped themselves in haste and, with Cuchongeilt leading, held a straight course to <term>Dúrlas</term>. Though Kieran of Cluain <note>Clonmacnoise</note> was a man prone to righteous anger, and though Brendan's miracles were potent, Guaire (Colman's son, the destroyer) never cast a single look of respect toward that perfect band of clerics. Then, in both low places and high, the people of <term>Dúrlas</term> carried out the slaughter; so it was that at the hands of the long-haired Clan Colman, Cuchongeilt perished as was ordained. Without reprieve, the saints then banned and cursed the murderous Guaire: they blighted both his life and his death, so that this spot remains empty and a desert.

<summary>This page recounts the tragic death of Cuchongeilt, brother of Saint Cellach, who is lured to King Guaire's stronghold at Dúrlas despite the protection of prominent Irish saints, resulting in a massacre and a divine curse upon Guaire's lineage.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Cellach, Cuchongeilt, King Guaire, Gelghéis, Dúrlas, River Moy, Saint Kieran, Saint Brendan, Clan Colman, Irish hagiography, martyrdom, blood feud</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the historical narratives of early Irish kings, specifically focusing on the reign of Diarmait mac Cerbaill.</meta>

### *A Story of Aedh Baclamh*

Aedh Baclamh <note>The name "Baclamh" likely refers to a physical disability or a specific way of carrying a spear; literally "lame-arm."</note>, the spear-bearer of Dermot, son of Cerbhall (the King): a fit of heavy sickness took him, and for a year he suffered from a wasting, continuous illness. However, he recovered his health and went to speak with Dermot, to whom he said: "During this past year that I have been lying down, how has the order of your discipline and peace fared?" Dermot said: "I do not perceive any decline <note>original: "imminution"</note> that it has suffered." "There is a way I will test that," said Aedh Baclamh. "I will carry your spear laid crosswise in the bend of both my arms and traverse Ireland diagonally, traveling west and south, until I reach the door of every fortified homestead <note>original: "liss," a ringfort or circular earthen dwelling</note> in Ireland. I will carry the spear transversely over their thresholds; in this way, the governance and peace of Ireland shall be proven."

From Tara, therefore, Aedh Baclamh (accompanied by the King of Ireland's herald to proclaim the peace of the land) arrived in the province of Connacht. There, he made his way to the mansion of Aedh Guaire of Kinelfechin in Hy-Many. At that time, Aedh Guaire had a stockade of red oak around his fortress <note>original: "fortalice"</note>, and a new house that had just been built for his wife's marriage feast. Now, a week before Aedh Baclamh's arrival, Aedh Guaire had heard he was coming and ordered an opening to be made for him in the outer palisade, but not in the dwelling itself.

Aedh Baclamh arrived accordingly, and Aedh Guaire welcomed him. Aedh Baclamh insisted that the house itself must be hacked open <note>to the width of the spear held crosswise</note> before him. "Give your own orders according to how you wish it to be cut," Aedh Guaire said, and even as he spoke, he struck him with a sword and cut off his head.

In those days, the law <note>original: "discipline"</note> of Ireland was such that whoever killed an innocent man could not offer cattle or any other valuable payment as compensation <note>original: "consideration"</note> for the slain. Unless the King of Ireland himself ordained or permitted a fine to be accepted, the killer had to be put to death.

<summary>Aedh Baclamh, King Dermot's spear-bearer, attempts to test the King's authority by carrying a spear horizontally through every door in Ireland, but he is killed by Aedh Guaire for demanding the destruction of his new home.</summary>
<keywords>Aedh Baclamh, King Dermot (Diarmait mac Cerbaill), Aedh Guaire, Tara, Connacht, Irish Law, blood money, sovereignty, spear-bearer</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the conflict between King Dermot and the saint Ruadhan of Lorrha regarding the sanctuary of Aedh Guaire.</meta>

# Aedh Baclamh

When Dermot had heard of the killing, he sent his young men and his executive officers to lay waste and plunder the lands of Aedh Guaire. Aedh fled to Bishop Senan, for they were born of the same mother. Bishop Senan then went with him to Ruadhan of Lorrha, because two of Ruadhan’s sisters—named Cael and Ruadhnait—had been the ones to nurse Bishop Senan. However, Aedh Guaire found no permanent sanctuary with Ruadhan and was banished away into Britain, where he remained for a year. Dermot’s people traveled there to demand his surrender, so he was sent back to Ruadhan once more. 

Dermot came in person to Ruadhan to demand him, but the saint had him hidden in a hole in the earth which today is called *Poll Ruadháin*, that is, "Ruadhan’s Pit." Dermot sent his servant to search Ruadhan’s kitchen to see if Aedh Guaire was there; but as soon as the servant entered the kitchen, he was immediately struck blind. When Dermot saw what had happened, he entered the kitchen himself. He did not find Aedh Guaire there and asked Ruadhan where he was (for he believed that Ruadhan would not tell a lie). Ruadhan answered: "I do not know, unless he is under that thatch over there."

Dermot began to return home; however, on the way, he remembered the cleric’s specific words and turned back again. He entered the <term>reclusorium</term> <note>original: reclusorium; a place of prayer or a cell for a hermit or monk</note> and saw a candle being carried to the place where Aedh Guaire was hidden. He sent another confidential servant named Donnan Donn to fetch him. Donnan excavated the hiding place, but the arm he reached out to grab Aedh withered up to the shoulder. At this, the servant performed an act of submission to Ruadhan. Both he and his companion who had been blinded remained with the saint, and from that time to this, they are said to be in Pollruane. Despite these miracles, Dermot seized Aedh Guaire and carried him off to Tara <note>The ancient seat of the High Kings of Ireland</note>.

Ruadhan went to the elder Brendan of Birr and to the twelve apostles of Ireland <note>A group of influential 6th-century Irish monastic saints</note>. Together, they followed Dermot to Tara and fasted against him that night <note>A traditional Irish legal custom where a person of lower status fasts at the door of a superior to compel them to settle a grievance</note>. Dermot, relying on his royal status and the perceived justice of his cause, fasted against them in return. During that night, the sons of "Tara’s Twelve Pillars" who were with the king’s steward died; but the next morning, after the steward begged him in God’s name, Ruadhan brought them back to life.

In this manner, they remained for an entire year outside Tara under Ruadhan’s tent, exposed to the wind and the rain.

<summary>King Dermot attempts to seize Aedh Guaire from the protection of Saint Ruadhan, leading to a supernatural standoff at Tara involving miracles, blindness, and ritual fasting.</summary>
<keywords>Aedh Guaire, King Dermot, Ruadhan of Lorrha, Tara, Poll Ruadháin, Brendan of Birr, Twelve Apostles of Ireland, sanctuary, fasting, Irish hagiography</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the conflict between King Dermot and the Irish clergy, specifically focusing on the miraculous intervention of Saint Brendan during the siege of Tara.</meta>

# Aedh Baclamh

Both parties went without food every second night: Dermot and the clergy, who were fasting against each other <note>A traditional Irish legal practice called 'troscad', where a person fasts at the door of someone who has wronged them to compel a settlement</note>.

At that time, the other Brendan (son of Finnlogh) <note>Saint Brendan the Navigator</note> was exploring the sea in search of the Promised Land. An angel appeared and showed him that Ireland's twelve apostles were gathered before Tara, contending with the King of Ireland, who had just committed an act of violence against Ruadhan. Brendan returned from the sea and landed at *Dun Rosarach* <note>original: dún Rosarach; a fortified residence</note>, where he stayed that night and blessed the fort. However, when Dermot heard of Brendan's arrival and that he had come to help the saints and clergy, he was seized by great fear. He said to the saints, "If you were to give me fifty horses, blue-eyed and with golden bridles, I would hand Aedh Guaire over to you."

When Brendan heard this, he summoned fifty seals, transformed them into the shapes of horses, and drove them before him to the green of Tara. Then the clergy and all the people of Tara welcomed Brendan. He began to tell them of all the hardships he had faced at sea, and the people of Tara found Brendan’s speech very sweet. He asked Dermot if he would accept cattle or some other payment in exchange for Aedh Guaire. 

"I will accept those fifty blue-eyed horses," Dermot said, "but only on the condition that someone guarantees their health and service to me for a year and a quarter." 

So the horses were handed over to Dermot, and the cleric acted as security for them for that duration. When that time had passed, the horses were racing one day on the green of Tara. The riders, thinking they were not going fast enough, struck them with their riding crops. At this, the horses became frantic. No one could pull them back or control them before they—carrying their riders with them—dived into the sea. Both the men and the horses were turned into seals. 

Dermot was furious at this. He went into Tara and ordered the seven forts <note>original: lisses; enclosed farmsteads or courtyards</note> of Tara to be shut against the clergy so they could not enter, fearing they would leave behind curses or evil legacies.

Then meat and ale were given to the clergy, and people were assigned to wait on them, but also to keep a close watch over them. These guards were to stay until they had seen the clergy truly and effectively finish eating and drinking <note>to ensure they broke their fast and lost their spiritual leverage</note>. But that night, Brendan gave them this advice: they should pull their hoods over their heads and let their meat and drink pass by their lips <note>implying they should pretend to eat while continuing their fast in secret</note>.

<summary>Saint Brendan the Navigator returns from his voyage to assist the clergy in their standoff against King Dermot, using a miracle involving seals transformed into horses to trick the king. Dermot retaliates by attempting to force the clergy to break their ritual fast under guard.</summary>

<keywords>Saint Brendan, King Dermot, Tara, Aedh Guaire, Irish hagiography, fasting, miracles, seals, Dun Rosarach</keywords>

It was reported to the king that the clergy partook heartily of meat and liquor; he therefore ate meat that night, while in the same the clergy fasted on him by stratagem <note>The clergy pretended to eat so the king would break his own fast, while they actually continued their ritual fasting to bring divine judgment upon him.</note>.

Now Dermot’s wife (Mughain was the woman's name) saw a dream, and the dream was this: that upon Tara’s <note>The ancient seat of the High Kings of Ireland.</note> green was a vast and wide-foliaged tree, and eleven slaves were hewing at it; but every chip that they knocked from it would return into its place again and there adhere instantly, until at last there came one man who dealt the tree but a single stroke, and with that single cut laid it low. And the poet pronounced a song:

->The wife of Tara’s king of the heavy torques <note>term: large metal neck-rings worn by Gaelic nobility</note> beheld an evil dream . . .<-

As for Dermot, son of Cerbhall: after that dream he rose early, so that he heard the clergy chant their psalms, and he entered into the house in which they were. “Alas,” he said, “for the iniquitous contest that you have waged against me. I see it is Ireland’s good that I pursue, to preserve her discipline and royal right; but it is Ireland’s ‘unpeace’ <note>original: "unpeace" (discord or lawlessness)</note> and murderousness that you endeavor after. For God himself it is that confers the orders of prince, of righteous ruler, and of equitable judgment on a man, to the end that he shall maintain his truthfulness, his princely quality, and his governance. 

Now, that to which a king is bound is to have mercy coupled with the stringency of law, and peace maintained in the *tuatha* <note>term: the traditional Gaelic territories or tribal kingdoms</note>, and hostages kept in fetters; to succour the wretched, but to overwhelm enemies; and to banish falsehood. For unless one does the King of Heaven’s will on this side of eternity, no excuse is accepted from him in the hereafter. 

And you, Ruadhan,” said Dermot: “it is through you that injury and the tearing of my sway, and of my integrity toward God, has come about. I pray God that your diocese be the first in Ireland that shall be renounced, and your church-lands the first that shall be attacked.” 

Ruadhan retorted: “Rather may your dynasty come to nothing, and may no son or grandson of yours establish himself in Tara forever.” 

Dermot said: “May your church be desolate continually.” 

Ruadhan said: “Desolate be Tara forever and forever.” 

Dermot said: “May one of your limbs be missing from you so that it does not accompany you under the ground, and may you, moreover, lose an eye.” 

“May you have a repulsive face in the sight of all before you die,” said Ruadhan...

# Aedh Baclamh

All; may your enemies prevail over you mightily; and may the thigh that you did not lift to stand up before me be mangled into pieces.” Dermot said: “Take away with you the person who is the subject of our dispute <note>original: Aedh Guaire</note>; but in your church, Ruadhán, may the alarm-cry always sound at the hour of nones <note>The ninth hour of the day, or 3:00 PM</note>, and even though all Ireland be at peace, may your church’s precinct be a continuous scene of war.” And from that time to this, that curse has been fulfilled.

Then a great repentance came upon Dermot for having pitted his wrath against the clergy, and he recited the poem below:

->Woe to him who joins in battle with the clergy of the churches . . .<-

Dermot, son of Cerbhall, lived once upon a time, and the official poets praised the king, his peace, and all his excellent ways.

Black Aedh, son of Araidhe, was there in the presence of Beg mac Dé <note>A famous Irish prophet or seer</note>. (It was Dermot who had slain Araidhe of Ulster, but he had taken in his son, Black Aedh, to be raised in his household). Beg said <note>original: dixit</note>: “I see the valiant wolf-dog that shall destroy the brilliant mansion.” “Beg,” said Aedh, “what hound is that?” “It might happen to be yourself.” “Why, how could that be?” asked Dermot. “Easily enough: it is this hand of Black Aedh’s that shall administer a poisonous drink to your lips in the house of Banbhan and his wife Bainbhsech. At that time, you will be wearing a shirt woven of flax grown from a single seed and a mantle made from the wool of a single sheep; in your horn will be ale brewed from a single grain of corn; and on your dish will be the bacon of a pig that was never born <note>likely referring to a piglet taken from a slaughtered sow</note>.” Dermot said: “As long as I am alive, he [Black Aedh] shall not stay in Ireland.” All the people cried out: “Kill him!” “No,” said Dermot, “but he shall be expelled from Ireland.” So Black Aedh was banished to the land of Scotland.

One day, Dermot saw a warrior enter the house to see him. “Where have you come from?” he asked. “Not from any great distance,” the newcomer answered; “come and spend a night with me as my guest.” “Good,” said Dermot, “tell that to Mughain <note>Dermot's queen</note>.” She replied: “As long as I am alive, I will go on no such invitation.” Nevertheless, the rest of them accompanied Banbhan (for it was he) to *Ráth Bhig*. After they had sat down there, they saw on the floor of the house, set apart, a...

# Aedh Baclamh 75

...a gentle and beautiful young woman carrying a bundle of excellent apparel. "Where does this woman come from?" enquired Dermot. "She is a daughter of mine," said Banbhan. "Now then, woman," he continued to his daughter, "do you have clothing there for Dermot?" "I do," replied the woman; and out of the bag she carried, she drew a shirt and a mantle <note>original: "mantle"; a loose sleeveless cloak</note>, which he placed around himself. 

"It is a good shirt," everyone said. "It is a good shirt, made from a single grain of flax-seed," said Banbhan. "That young woman is a daughter of ours with a curious imagination; it was she who managed to plant a single flax-seed from which she produced a handful, and so on, until eventually her sowing filled a whole ridge of land." 

"It is a good mantle," everyone said. "It is good," Banbhan answered, "and it was made from the wool of a single sheep." After this, food and drink were given to them. "This is good bacon," everyone said. "It is good," returned Banbhan, "being as it is the bacon of a pig that was never born." "How can that be?" they asked. "That is easily explained," he answered. "There were certain swine that were pregnant, and men took knives to them so that the piglets were extracted from them while still alive, and then they were fattened." 

"Good ale," everyone said. "It is good," said Banbhan, "though it is but a sample of ale from a single grain of wheat. One day when I went out to inspect the ploughing, I killed a wood-pigeon; in its crop was found a grain, though what kind of corn it was remained unknown. It was planted in a ridge, and from it in due course there sprang a sickle-full, so that this is its grain and this is its ale here."

Dermot looked up after that. "The lower part of this house is new," he said, "but its upper part is not fresh." "There was a time," Banbhan said, "when we went in currachs <note>original: "currachs"; traditional Irish boats made of a wicker frame covered in hide or canvas</note> to catch fish, and we saw the ridge-beam of a house floating toward us on the sea; and I built a house using that beam in the roof." 

"It is true," said Dermot. "That is the ridge-beam of my own house, which I ordered to be thrown into the sea. What Ireland's saints prophesied for me was that I would not face death until all these specific signs should coincide; for that reason, I cast the beam into the sea." 

Also, with the same glance that Dermot threw at the beam, he saw a small herd of red-headed cattle with white stars grazing; and seeing them was a matter of prohibition <note>original: "geis"; a supernatural taboo or prohibition in Irish mythology</note> to him. "Come, let us go on our way out," said Dermot. "By no means," said Suibhne's son, Black Aedh, meeting him...

The house is now seized while Dermot is inside and burned over his head. He performs earnest penance and dies. Because he received his punishment in this life—just as Saint Brendan had prophesied to Flann of the Monastery—he went to heaven. As a poet said:

> “Black Aedh of the taxes, son of Suibhne, was the honorable King of Ulster. It was he—and this is no obscure mystery—who slew Dermot, son of Cerbhall.”

### *This is the Death of Dermot, son of Fergus Cerrbeoil, as the Book of Sligo tells it.*

It happened at the time when Dermot, son of Fergus Cerrbeoil, was driven into exile on Lough Ree and the River Shannon by Tuathal Maelgarb. At that very same time, *Kieran, son of the carpenter* <note>original: Ciarán mac an tsaoir</note>, came to *Druim Tibrat* (the place where Clonmacnoise stands today) to found his monastery. Kieran traveled with eight companions on the lake, but with twelve hundred followers on land. A fire was kindled by the clergy.

Dermot was then staying in his exile at *the swimming-place of the two birds* <note>original: snámh dá én</note>. This place was named for two birds that Nar—son of Finncha, who was the son of Conall Cernach—killed there on the shoulder of Eistine the Amazon. Dermot’s wizards said to him, “The purpose for which that fire is kindled tonight is such that it will never be extinguished.” “Truly, it shall be extinguished right now,” Dermot said, as the boats arrived at *Port-grencha*, where *Finghin’s Well* <note>original: Tipra Finghin</note> is located today.

There, the cleric was in the middle of planting a church. “What is the work you are doing?” Dermot asked. “Building a little church,” Kieran answered. “That might as well be its name: *the little church* <note>original: eglais bheg</note>.” “Thrust in the foundation post with me,” Kieran said to Dermot, “and as we do it, allow my hand to be placed over yours. If you do, your hand and your royal rule shall be imposed over the men of Ireland by this time tomorrow.”

<summary>This page recounts the death of King Dermot at the hands of Black Aedh and begins a flashback to Dermot's first encounter with Saint Kieran, the founder of Clonmacnoise.</summary>
<keywords>King Dermot, Black Aedh, Saint Kieran, Clonmacnoise, Irish hagiography, Book of Sligo, Lough Ree, Druim Tibrat</keywords>
