<meta>This page continues the narrative of Dermot mac Cerbaill's rise to the High Kingship of Ireland and his interactions with Saint Kieran of Clonmacnoise.</meta>

# Death of King Dermot

Ireland.” “How will that be achieved, for Tuathal rules over Ireland and I am driven out?” Kieran replied: “That is a matter for God.”

Dermot's foster-brother, *Maelmór son of Argat*, went [at the predicted time] to the place where Tuathal was, at *Grellach-eilte* <note>Grellach-eilte: likely located in modern County Meath</note> southeast of *Ros-ech*. He drove a spear into Tuathal’s breast so that he left him lifeless. For this deed, Maelmór himself was killed immediately afterward; hence the tale called *The Adventure of Maelmór* <note>original: "echtra Mhaeilmhóir"</note>. (Now, Maelmór was of the Hy-Conall of Murthemny, and the third foster-brother to Dermot; *Luchta* of *Ath-ferna* and *Enna son of Laighse* were the others). Consequently, before a week had ended, the men of Ireland inaugurated Dermot as king.

By Dermot and the men of Ireland, the great congregation of Usnach <note>Usnach: Uisneach, the traditional geographical center of Ireland</note> is held now at Beltane <note>Beltane: May 1st, a major Gaelic festival marking the beginning of summer</note>. At that time, Ireland’s three high gatherings were these:
*   The congregation of Usnach, at Beltane.
*   The convention of *Taillte* <note>Taillte: Teltown</note>, at Lammas <note>Lammas: August 1st, the harvest festival</note>.
*   The feast of Tara, at *Samhain* <note>Samhain: November 1st, the beginning of the dark half of the year; modern All-Hallows</note>.

Whosoever among the men of Ireland should have transgressed these—that is, whoever violated the laws of these assemblies—was guilty of death.

A message comes from Dermot to Kieran, requesting him to join the gathering. The king himself proceeds to *Cnoc-brecáin* to receive him; there he halted to wait, which is why the place is called the *Hill of Halting* <note>original: "tulach na comnaidhe"</note>. Kieran went to him accordingly. “Why, how now,” Dermot said, “since this is the first time we have met since I attained the kingdom through your blessing, let this stretch of land (with its oxen and its cattle) be made over to you as an ‘altar-sod’ <note>altar-sod: land granted to the church for its support</note>.” 

But in this same plain was a man who was an enemy to the king: Flann, son of Dima (from whom *Dima’s Hill* or *Flann’s Hill* is named). The king, finding himself in the neighborhood, has Dima’s house burned, and the owner is severely wounded within it. This warrior, seeking to escape the flames, gets into a bathing-vat inside the dwelling and there expires. 

“You have broken your covenant very quickly,” Kieran said to Dermot, “seeing as you have already committed violence upon the land you granted to us. Yet in any case,” he went on, “I will not take either Heaven or Earth from you or your children <note>meaning: I will not revoke your spiritual or temporal authority</note>...”

<summary>Dermot becomes High King after his foster-brother assassinates King Tuathal, but his reign begins with a conflict of conscience when he commits an act of violence on land he had just granted to Saint Kieran.</summary>
<keywords>Dermot mac Cerbaill, Saint Kieran, Tuathal Maelgarb, Maelmór, Uisneach, Beltane, Samhain, Irish Kingship, Church-State relations</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of King Dermot (Diarmait mac Cerbaill) and his interactions with the Irish clergy, specifically focusing on the prophecies regarding his death and his relationship with Saint Kieran.</meta>

# Death of King Dermot

[The joys of the one, and the temporal possessions of the other]; but the violent death which he has received because of you, that shall be the very same one which you too shall have: to be wounded, and drowned, and burnt." <note>This refers to the "threefold death," a common motif in Irish mythology where a king dies by three simultaneous means.</note> "Cleric," said Dermot, "I am terrified. I award you whatever compensation you assess for the deed." "Nay," the cleric answered, "the missile that I have delivered—by that same I may myself be hurt to death if it does not happen exactly so." And hence it was that Dermot's death was indeed brought about as had been promised.

The two of them, king and cleric, repaired to Usnach <note>Uisneach, the symbolic center of Ireland</note> and joined the congregation of the men of Ireland <note>original: "Erin"</note>, and there they remained for a fortnight. During this meeting, a mighty drought afflicted them, so that the people were in dire peril and their livestock perished in large numbers. Then they had recourse to Kieran <note>Saint Ciarán of Clonmacnoise</note> to find them relief. Kieran prayed, and there came a rain that, as a sign of the miracle, left twelve main streams in Ireland; because of this, Kieran is entitled to a general tax <note>original: "cess"</note> throughout Ireland. In the presence of the men of Ireland there, Dermot made obeisance to Kieran and settled on him his own service and his children's service forever.

Following this, at Lammastide <note>The harvest festival of Lughnasadh, around August 1st</note>, Kieran was at the assembly of *Taillte* <note>Teltown, the site of the ancient royal games</note>, where he worked many wonders and exceeding great miracles. There also this prodigy occurred: a man took a perjured oath, and in consequence, a running ulcer developed in his neck, causing his head to fall off. Thus, in the presence of the men of Ireland, he walked about the gathering without a head. This man was the <term>bacuc</term> <note>A "bacach" or "bacuc" usually refers to a person with a disability or a beggar; here it refers specifically to the headless man</note> whom the monks kept in Cluain <note>Clonmacnoise</note> for a long period—for seven years, that is to say.

After this, Dermot reigned in Ireland for a long period. There never came in those times a king who was grander, more revered, or more excellent in figure, face, wisdom, speech, and royal rule than he.

It happened once upon a time that Dermot held a feast. Mughain—daughter of Concraidh, son of Duach of the Eoganacht of Cashel—was at his side. She was the mother of Dermot's son Aedh, and she was pregnant with that same Aedh Slaine at the time. Then, as many as had been at the carouse stepped outside onto the green to cool themselves; and as they were there, they saw drawing near...

# Death of King Dermot

Near them on the sward stood Dermot’s nephew, Suibne, the son of Colman More. He was accompanied by a hundred riders: dark grey mantles with silver clasps wrapped one half of the troop, and the other half wore crimson cloaks with fringes of gold and silver. Under one half of the band were dark grey horses, and white horses under the other. They had fifty greyhounds with bronze chains, and every man had a bossed shield slung over his shoulder. Even as Suibne entered the assembly, the woman (namely Mughain <note>Mughain was the wife of King Dermot</note>) uttered a loud, wordless cry that was heard throughout the entire company. 

“Woman, what is the meaning of this?” Dermot asked. “Is your mind set on the young man who has just arrived?” 

Beg mac Dé <note>A famous 6th-century Irish prophet or seer</note> said: “You are indeed no prophet, but you have a seer here.” 

“Explain the matter then, since you are a prophet.” 

“I know it,” said Beg. “The son that the woman is carrying is the one who shall slay that youth over there.” 

That proved true: Aedh Slaine did afterwards kill Suibne. Suibne left a son, Conall mac Suibne, and he in turn slew Aedh Slaine. It was concerning this that a four-line verse was spoken:

> “Some of the young men do not calculate their accounts correctly: it was Conall who slew Aedh Slaine, because Aedh Slaine had slain Suibne.”

That is to say: Conall son of Suibne killed Aedh Slaine at Loch Sewdy. Aedh Gustan, on that same day, slew Aedh Buie, King of Teffia, and Aedh Róin, King of Offaly, in the *Hostel of Dáchoga* <note>original: bruidhen Dáchoga; a famous site in Irish literature</note>. This was the first instance of kin-slaying <term>fratricide</term> between the Clan Colman and the descendants of Aedh Slaine—namely, Aedh Slaine killing his kinsman Suibne, son of Colman, and Suibne’s son Conall killing him in retaliation.

Now, this same Beg mac Dé was the best seer of his time. It was he who said a clever thing to three men who had just come out of Tara. 

“Look now,” the three had said, “here comes Beg toward us; let us say something to him. Beg, greetings!” 

“Greetings,” said Beg. 

“How long will there be inhabitants in the fort we just left?” asked the first man. 

“What is the river’s depth?” asked the second. 

“How thick is the bacon fat this year?” asked the third man. 

“A step to the bottom tomorrow,” <note>original: Pas go tóin amárach. This is a cryptic prophetic pun suggesting the imminent abandonment of Tara and the shallow nature of worldly things.</note> answered Beg. 

He was the one who could speak with nine people at once, delivering a single speech that satisfied and resolved all nine of their different questions. Furthermore, it was he who, in Tara, declared to Dermot son of Cerbhall (at the time when the official poets...

# Death of King Dermot

<margin>The prophecy of Beg mac Dé</margin>
They praised the king, his peace, and his good ways. At that time, Black Aedh, son of Suibne (the King of Dalaradia), was in the presence of Beg mac Dé. <note>Beg mac Dé was a famous Irish prophet of the 6th century.</note> It was King Dermot who had slain that Suibne and had taken his son, Aedh mac Suibne, to be raised in his own household. Beg said, "I see the gallant wolfdog that shall destroy the brilliant mansion." 

"What hound is that, Beg?" asked Aedh. 

"A *red hound* <note>original: *cú ruadh*, meaning a wolf</note>—some 'hound' or other—it might well be yourself," Beg replied. 

"How could that be?" Dermot asked. 

"It is easily said: it is truly the hand of Black Aedh that, in the house of Banbhan the hospitaller, shall cause a poisoned drink to enter your mouth. At that time, you will be wearing a shirt made from a single flax seed and a mantle produced from a single sheep. In your horn will be ale brewed from a single grain of corn; on your plate will be the bacon of a pig that was never born. Finally, it is the main beam of the house—the ridgepole—that shall fall on your head after your enemies have nearly beaten you to death." 

"Put Black Aedh to death!" everyone cried out. 

"Not so," said Dermot. "Instead, let him be removed from Ireland, and as long as I live, he shall not return." Consequently, Dermot exiled Black Aedh to Scotland, and he was not allowed back into Ireland as long as Dermot lived.

Dermot’s tribute, discipline, and law prevailed throughout Ireland. His stewards, managers, and regular soldiers in their quarters were stationed up and down the country. At this particular time, the king’s stewards and sergeants accompanied him into Connacht. They were joined by the king’s herald, who used to go before them to make a proclamation to any house where they arrived seeking hospitality. The crier’s announcement was this: that the gate of any town or castle they entered must be demolished before them so that Dermot’s spear could be carried in *sideways* <note>original: *athwartwise*</note>. Because of their fear of the king, no one dared to do anything but obey. But the Devil <note>original: *Diabolus*</note>—it was he who violently possessed the crier to urge this evil demand upon him, so that an even greater evil would result from it.

For they came once to the house of Aedh Guaire in the land of Hy-Many in Connacht. His castle had to be breached to make way for them and the king’s spear. Then anger seized Aedh; he killed "the lad of the spear" (the herald), and immediately fled to escape Dermot...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the conflict between King Dermot (Diarmait mac Cerbaill) and the nobleman Aedh Guaire, focusing on the sanctuary provided by Saint Ruadhan of Lorrha.</meta>

# Death of King Dermot

Aedh Guaire fled into the land of Muskerry and placed himself under the protection of Bishop Senach, for the bishop’s mother and Aedh Guaire’s mother were sisters. Subsequently, Bishop Senach brought him to Ruadhan of Lorrha and committed him to his safeguard; this was because Ruadhan had two sisters, Cael and Ruadhnait, who had raised Bishop Senach. However, Ruadhan sent Aedh Guaire away to live among the Britons <note>The people of Wales or Cornwall</note>, for because of King Dermot’s wrath, he could not safely remain anywhere in Ireland. Yet such was Dermot’s influence and power over others that, because of him, Aedh ultimately could not remain in either Scotland or with the Britons. Consequently, he returned to Ireland to Ruadhan, who hid him in a chamber underground. The place where Ruadhan was then is the same spot known as *poll Ruadháin* <note>original: "Ruadhan's Pit"</note> today. 

It was told to the king that Aedh Guaire had come to Ireland again and that Ruadhan held him concealed in the earth. Then Dermot traveled to Ruadhan and dispatched his charioteer to seize Aedh Guaire from him by force. The young man entered the sanctuary, but he was instantly struck blind. The king, now furious at this, went to Ruadhan and asked him where Aedh Guaire was (for he knew that Ruadhan would not tell a lie). Ruadhan answered: "Truly, I do not know where he is, unless he is under you, right where you are standing." 

The king then left the sanctuary and at first paid no more attention to what the cleric had said. Afterward, however, he recalled Ruadhan’s words and realized that Aedh Guaire was in the ground directly beneath where he had stood. He ordered one of his people (named Donnan) to go down to Aedh; as he began to dig away the earth over Aedh's head, his arms were suddenly paralyzed. Thereupon, he went to Ruadhan and performed an act of submission to him. The man who had previously been blinded also performed an act of submission, and from then on, they remained with Ruadhan. These are the two men who are today honored as saints at Pollruane <note>original: Poll Ruadháin</note>. 

Then Dermot himself entered the church and pulled Aedh Guaire out of the hole in the ground, which today is called Pollruane. Aedh was brought in chains by the king to Tara, where Dermot intended to have him hanged as punishment for all his schemes.

In the meantime, Ruadhan had sought out Brendan of Birr to...

# Death of King Dermot

With the purpose of taking him with him to retrieve his protégé <note>original: "protégé," referring to Aed Guaire, who had sought sanctuary with the saint</note>, the pair went on to Tara. There they demanded that the king release the man whose safety Ruadhan had guaranteed. However, Dermot answered that the Church had no right to extend immunity to someone who had broken royal law; he argued that doing so was an inherent violation of both human and divine justice.

The clerics then chanted psalms of commination <note>psalms intended to invoke divine vengeance or cursing</note> and rang their bells against the king. That night, at the exact same moment, twelve sons of chiefs who were being raised as wards of the king died in Tara. Their respective guardians came to the clergy and persistently urged them to bring the youths back to life. The saints prayed, and the boys were restored to life.

For a full year after this, they excommunicated <note>original: "anathematised"</note> Dermot and directed miracles against him, while he matched them wonder for wonder. But in the end, they gained no advantage over him until they promised Heaven to the king's house-steward in exchange for a deception. They convinced the steward to tell the king that the clergy had finally broken their fast and shared a meal. The house-steward went to Dermot and told him the clergy were eating, so that in this way—though it was not actually true—they gained a spiritual advantage over him in their fasting contest. 

That night, Dermot had a dream: he saw a great tree in Tara, the top of which reached the clouds of heaven and whose shade covered all of Ireland. He saw fifty foreigners (including two leading strangers) chopping down the tree, but every chip they cut from it was immediately restored. Eventually, they drove him away from the tree and leveled it; it was the crash of the falling tree that woke him. "Even so," Dermot said, "I am the tree. The foreigners chopping it are the clergy cutting short my life, and it is by them that I have fallen."

The next morning, the king rose and went to the place where the clergy were. "You have done an evil thing," he said, "to destroy my kingdom simply because I upheld a righteous cause. At any rate," he continued, "may your diocese be the first one ruined in Ireland, and Ruadhan, may your monks desert you!" 

The saint retorted: "May your kingdom wither quickly!" 

Dermot said: "Your seat of power shall be empty, and swine shall root up your churchyards." 

"Tara shall be desolate," Ruadhan replied, "and no one shall dwell there forever." 

Dermot said: "May a shameful blemish affect your...

person," and straightway one of Ruadhan's eyes burst. Ruadhan said: "May your body be mangled by enemies, and your limbs torn apart so that they are not found in the same place." Dermot said: "May a wild boar come to dig up the hill where you shall be buried, and may your relics <note>sacred remains of a saint</note> be scattered. Also, at the hour of nones <note>original: *nones*; the ninth hour of the day, or 3:00 PM</note> let there be a continual howling of 'wild hounds' (that is, wolves) in your churchyard, and the alarm-cry every evening; and let it not be your own monks who dwell there." Ruadhan said: "The knee that was not lifted in reverence before me, let it not be buried with your body." Then, upon the royal hearth, Ruadhan called down the blackness of darkness: that never again in Tara <note>the ancient seat of the High Kings of Ireland</note> should smoke rise from a roof-tree.

Just then, Dermot looked up at the ridgebeam. "That beam is hostile to you," Ruadhan said. "That very roof-tree shall be hurled upon your face as you look up at it, after you have been struck down by those from over the sea." "Cleric, take all you desire!" the king cried. Then their prisoner was released to them, and both parties made peace; whereupon Dermot said this:

> "Alas for him who shows fight to the clergy of the churches; woe to him who would contend, giving blow for blow. Because of this—through the dissension between me and Ruadhan—Tara shall be desolate and swept clean."

He continued: "Evil is the work you have done, clerics—the ruin of my kingdom; for in future times Ireland shall be no better off than she has been until now. But in any case, let it be so that bad chiefs, their heirs, and their warriors shall lodge themselves in your churches then; and let it be the inhabitants themselves who, in your own houses, must pull off the shoes <note>original: *brogues*</note> of such people, while you are powerless to rid yourselves of them."

The clergy (with their prisoner) started for home toward Pollruane; but first they saw thirty dark-grey horses, of excellent shape, which came out of the sea toward them. These they presented to the king. Their speed was tested against his other horses and they proved to be faster; but the horses then returned to the same form they had held in the sea and went back to the place from which they first came. After this, Dermot and the clergy were at peace.

<meta>This page continues the account of the reign of King Dermot (Diarmait mac Cerbaill), focusing on the prophetic visions of his downfall and the rising conflict with Saint Columbkill (Columba).</meta>

# Death of King Dermot

One night, while Dermot was sleeping, he saw two men draw near him. One man, he believed, had the appearance of a clergyman; the other appeared to be a layman. They came up to him, took off his royal crown, and made two crowns out of it—each man taking one half, for that is how they divided it between them—and then they departed from him. Dermot started out of his sleep and told his vision.

"Just so," said Beg mac Dé <note>A famous Irish prophet and seer</note> and Cairidh son of Finnchaemh (Dermot’s poet). "We have the interpretation of your dream for you: Your kingdom is decided, your reign has reached its end, and from now on your princely grip on Ireland is cast off. Specifically, there shall now be a division between the Church and the Laity. The partitioning of your royal crown foretells a similar dividing of Ireland’s sovereignty between the Church and the State."

He continued: "A time will come when the Church shall be enslaved by the State, and when the privileges of church lands shall no longer exist; instead, they shall be subject to 'free quartering' <note>The forced housing and feeding of soldiers</note> by everyone. In exchange for this, however, evil shall overtake the State: so that son, father, and kinsman—of whatever degree—shall kill each other, and every man’s weapon shall be red with another’s blood. Because of the treachery of all men, the fruits of the earth shall perish, as well as the nuts of the trees and the produce of the waters."

The Festival of Tara was then held by Dermot. During the actual banquet, Curnan—the son of Aedh, son of Eochaid Tirmcharna, from whom the *descendants of Maeilruain* <note>original: síol Maeilruain</note> in Connacht are descended—killed a man. Curnan placed himself under the protection of Muirchertach mac Erca’s two sons, Fergus and Donall, who in turn placed him under the guarantee of Columbkill <note>Saint Columba</note>. 

The King had Curnan executed to pay for his crime, and the people of Connacht turned against Dermot, accusing him of the wrongful slaughter of their king’s son, Curnan. Dermot proceeded to ravage Connacht and reached *Cúil Sibrinne* near *Cúil Dreimne*. In order to avenge the violation of his guarantee, Columbkill gathered the northern O'Neill clan <note>original: clan-Neill</note>. Along with him, Fergus and Donall (the two sons of Muirchertach mac Erca), Ainmire son of Sedna (King of Kinel-connell), Muiredach mac Duach, and Aedh (son of Eochaid Tirmcharna) marched into Connacht. 

Between the two armies, Frechan son of Tenesan (Dermot’s wizard) set up a "magic barrier." It was then that Columbkill spoke:

> "Why, O God, do You not ward off from us . . . ."

# Death of King Dermot

Tuatán (son of Dímán, son of Sarán, son of Cormac, son of Eoghan, son of Niall) then arrived, capsized the barrier, and cleared it in a single jump. However, on the other side, a spear met him and pierced him, and he was killed. Now, of all of Columbkill’s <note>Saint Columba</note> people, he was the only man whom death reached. Then Dermot was defeated. “It is *against a fierce warrior* <note>original: "fri féinnidh ndremain"</note>—that is, a case of a barrier opposed to a warrior who would not be denied,” said Columbkill. From this, the name *Cúil Dreimne* <note>The site of the battle, traditionally translated as "The Back of the Fierce Ones"</note> (otherwise known as *Cúil Dreimfhéinne*) has prevailed.

Dermot went to Tara <note>The ancient seat of the High Kings of Ireland</note> and again said to Beg <note>Beg mac Dé, a famous Irish prophet or seer</note>: “Let me have certain knowledge of what manner of death shall carry me off.” Beg said: “That is not a matter of doubt:

> In Beg’s rath <note>term: a circular earthen fort or homestead</note> you shall drink a malt-drink made from a single grain; and it is there that you shall be laid to rest, Dermot.”

“My kingdom after me—in what fashion shall it be?” asked Dermot. It was then that Beg spoke this prophecy:

> “An evil world is now at hand: one in which men shall be in bondage and women free; forest nuts <note>original: "mast"</note> shall be wanting, the woods shall be smooth, and the blossoms bad. There will be many winds, a wet summer, and green corn; much cattle but scant milk. Dependants will be burdensome in every country, hogs will be lean, and chiefs will be wicked. There will be bad faith and chronic killing; a withered world with many abandoned forts.
> 
> These are the princes that shall succeed you:
> 
> The kingdom shall revolve from Niall to Niall, from land to land: a Niall by sea; a Niall in slaying; a Niall in fire; a Niall to hew down every night, after the wrecking of Ailech <note>The ancient stone palace of the Northern Uí Néill kings</note>.”

“Bring our magicians to us,” Dermot said, “so that we may ascertain whether they and Beg forebode the same thing for us.” 

“He doubts me, does he?” said Beg. Thereupon, in great anger and a vindictive huff, he went out from Dermot’s presence. A great crowd followed him, begging for a prophecy, and so it continued until he saw Columbkill waiting for him. He saluted him, and Columbkill said: “It is a marvelous prophecy; this great foreknowledge granted to you comes from God.” 

“We thank God for the same,” Beg answered. 

Columbkill then inquired: “Do you know the day of your own death?” 

“Cleric, I know it well,” said Beg. “There are yet seven years of my life remaining.” 

“That is a grand thing for a man to know...”

"To whom it is so done; if indeed it be true," said Columbkill. 

"It is not true," Beg said: "there are but seven months of my life remaining." 

"Good again, if it be true," said Columbkill. 

"It is not true," Beg said: "there are of my life but seven hours of the day—speedily let me have communion and the sacrifice <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion</note>!" 

Then the cleric tonsured him <note>The ritual shaving of the head for a monk or priest</note>, gave him communion and sacrifice, and he went presently to Heaven. Now it had been prophesied for Beg that before he reached death he must utter three falsehoods <gloss>as shown above</gloss>; for up to that hour he never had told a lie. For the same reason also, Columbkill sought him out, for he knew that on that day Beg was destined to die immediately.

His magicians <gloss>as mentioned before</gloss> were brought to Dermot, and he inquired of them what manner of death he should encounter. 

"Slaughter," said the first magician: "and it is a shirt grown from a single flax-seed, with a mantle of a single sheep's wool, that shall be around you on the night of your death." 

"It is a light matter for me to evade that," Dermot said. 

"Drowning," said the second magician: "and it is ale brewed from a single grain of corn that you shall drink that night." 

"Burning," said the third wizard: "and bacon from a pig that was never farrowed <note>original: "never was farrowed"—likely referring to a pig taken from its mother's womb before natural birth</note>—that is what shall be on your dish." 

Dermot said: "All of this is unlikely."

Then on his royal circuit, Dermot <gloss>set out and</gloss> traveled clockwise <gloss>that is, south and west about</gloss> around Ireland: from Tara into Leinster; from there into Munster; then into Connacht, and across the province of Ulster. At the end of a year's progress, he would by <term>Samhain</term> <note>An ancient Gaelic festival marking the end of the harvest and the beginning of winter, roughly November 1st</note> reach Tara again in time to perform his <term>Samhain</term>-tide duties and to meet the men of Ireland at the Festival of Tara.

One day, as Dermot was on this circuit, he saw a warrior enter the house to meet him. "Where do you come from?" the King asked. 

"Not from any great distance," the warrior replied. "Come along and spend a night of guestly entertainment with me." 

"Good," said Dermot. "Tell Mughain <note>Dermot's queen</note>." 

"Not so," she answered. "As long as I live, I will never go on such an invitation; and if you eat with him, it is against my will. For to go upon an invitation will <gloss>as it is prophesied</gloss> have a bad ending for you."

<summary>The prophet Beg mac Dé dies after fulfilling a prophecy to tell three lies, and King Dermot receives a cryptic threefold prophecy regarding his own death by slaughter, drowning, and burning.</summary>

# Death of King Dermot

Dermot went with Banbhan (who had invited him) to Rathbeg. When they had sat down in the house, they saw a graceful young woman enter, wearing exceptionally fine clothing. "Where does this woman come from?" Dermot asked. Banbhan answered: "She is a daughter of mine; and to spite Mughain <note>Dermot's queen</note> because she did not come with me, the girl shall be your wife tonight." "I am well pleased," said the king.

While the food was being prepared, a bed was made for them. When the meal was ready, Banbhan said: "Well, girl, have you brought clothing for the king?" "I have," she said, and handed him a shirt and a mantle, which the king took and put on. "It is a good shirt," everyone remarked. "It is one worthy of you," said Banbhan, "being a shirt made from a single flax-seed. That girl there is quite imaginative; she sowed a single seed of flax and harvested a handful from it, which then grew to fill an entire ridge of land." "It is a good mantle," everyone cried. "It is indeed," said Banbhan, "it is made from the wool of a single sheep."

Then food and drink were served to them, and Banbhan said: "This bacon from a pig that was never farrowed <note>born naturally</note> is good." "How can that be?" asked Dermot. "There were sows with young; they were cut open with knives so that their piglets were extracted while still alive, and then fattened afterward." "It is good ale!" everyone said. "It is indeed," said Banbhan, "ale brewed from a single grain of corn. One day I went out to inspect my plowed land and killed a ringdove; in its crop was found one grain, though we did not know what kind of cereal it was. It was planted in a ridge, however, and its yield was a sickle-full. This was sown again, and this ale is the result of its harvest."

After this, Dermot looked upward and said: "The lower part of the house is new, but its upper-work is not recent." Banbhan answered: "Once upon a time, we went out in <term>currachs</term> <note>traditional Irish boats made of a wicker frame covered in hide</note> to catch fish, and we saw the ridgebeam of a house floating toward us on the sea. Because it was such a curiosity, I had a house built using it." Dermot then said: "Beg’s prophecy was truthfully spoken!" <note>Beg mac Dé was a prophet who predicted Dermot would die in a house roofed with sea-borne wood, wearing a shirt of one flax-seed, and eating food never born.</note> With that, he sprang up to escape. "This is your way out!" said Black Aedh at the doorway, striking him in the breast with a spear that pierced him through and so broke his—

# Aedh Sláine

Then Dermot turned back into the house; on the outside, the men of Ulster surrounded the dwelling, and it was burned down upon those inside. Dermot himself, seeking refuge from the flames, entered a large vat of ale. Soon after, the mansion's roof-beam fell on his head, killing him.

Thus the king perished. His body was consumed by the fire except for his head, which was carried with his relics to Clonmacnoise and buried in the "sloping trenches" <note>original: *claen ferta*</note>, otherwise known as the "assembly hill" <note>original: *céite*</note>. He had chosen to be laid there because of a time when he fasted at the "little church" <note>original: *eglais bheg*</note>. Through that fast, he was healed of a disease in his head after he had previously fasted against the saints of Ireland and been denied a cure. Concerning his death, this poem was recited:

> "The spell of shelter in Rathbeg—the loss of Dermot that was... the extinction of a prince—an abundance of battles—alas for him who shall plot his utter destruction."

And this is the account of the death of Dermot, son of *Cerbhall* (a name which signifies "crooked-mouth" <note>original: *cerrbhall*, i.e. *ceirrbheol*, i.e. *bél cerr*</note>).

->The End.<-

### Birth of Aedh Sláine

Tara of the Kings: she was the private estate belonging to all the kings who successively ruled Ireland. It was a universal custom that all of Ireland's taxes, prescribed dues, and rents had to be brought to the kings there. It was also common for the men of Ireland to travel from all directions <note>original: "airts"</note> to Tara to hold the Feast of Tara at the time of *Samhain* <note>The festival marking the beginning of winter and the new year in the Gaelic calendar.</note>. These were the two principal gatherings they held: the Feast of Tara at every *Samhain* (which was the pagans' equivalent of Easter); and at every *Lughnasa* <note>The harvest festival beginning in August.</note>, or "Lammas-tide," the Convention of *Taillte*. No person was allowed to break any laws or enactments ordained by the men of Ireland during either of these festivals for the entire following year.

Once upon a time in *Taillte*, the Gaels held an extra-

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the reign of Dermot, son of Fergus Cerrbeol, focusing on the domestic and miraculous events surrounding his wives and the birth of his heirs.</meta>

# Aedh Sláine

At a regular great convention, the man who was King of Ireland at that time was Dermot, son of Fergus Cerrbeol. The men of Ireland were arranged along the benches of the assembly ground, all of them according to their precedence of rank, their profession, and their legitimate claims—essentially following the established customs and traditions of the past.

Now the women, including the king’s two wives, had a separate seating area. The queens accompanying Dermot on this occasion were Mairenn (surnamed *Mael*, meaning "the bald") and Mughain, daughter of Conchraid, son of Duach (of the men of Munster). Mughain felt a great jealousy toward Mairenn. She told a certain female jester that she could name her own price if she would remove the golden headgear from the other queen’s head. This was because Mairenn was completely bald, and she habitually wore a queen’s headdress to conceal this defect.

The jester approached Mairenn and began to beg her for some gift or favor. The queen declared she had nothing to give. "You shall have this, at any rate!" the jester cried, as she tore the golden helmet from the queen’s head. Mairenn cried out, "May God and Saint Kieran <note>Saint Kieran of Clonmacnoise, a major Irish saint</note> help me in this moment of need!" Before a single person in the crowd could even turn their eyes toward her, a mass of flossy, wavy, golden-shining hair—which grew through Kieran’s miraculous power—fell down to her very shoulders. The entire host was astonished by the miracle and pleased that the queen was not shamed. "I pray to God," cried Mairenn, "that you will be disgraced for this in the presence of the men of Ireland!"—which later came true.

After this, Dermot continued to visit Mughain, but she was barren. This made her unhappy, as the king considered abandoning her entirely. The king's other wives were also a source of grief to her because they bore children: specifically Eithne, daughter of Brenann Dall of the *Conmaicne Cúile Talad* <note>A tribal territory in County Mayo</note> and mother of Colman the Great; and Breo, daughter of Colman mac Neman from *Dún* Suane, mother of Colman the Small. Mughain was deeply saddened by this—both by her lack of a son or daughter and by the king's intention to dismiss her.

Finnian of *Magh Bile* <note>Modern Moville, County Down</note> and Bishop Aedh, son of Bri, arrived in Bregia <note>An ancient territory in the east of Ireland, including the Hill of Tara</note>. The queen came to visit them and began to implore the clerics to help her. Finnian...

# Aedh Sláine

And Bishop Aedh blessed water and gave it to her to drink, and she became pregnant; but what she eventually produced from this promise was—a lamb. She cried, "Woe is me that I should have borne a four-footed creature! After this, I shall never be acceptable to anyone!" 

"That is not what shall happen," said Finnian. "Instead, this creature—a likeness of the sinless Lamb <note>A reference to Jesus Christ as the Agnus Dei or Lamb of God.</note> who was offered up for the human race—shall serve as a consecration for your womb."

Again the cleric blessed water for her, and she conceived from it; then she gave birth to a silver salmon. "Woe is me because of this!" she said. "For all you do on my behalf, I am only worse off, cleric. Among the men of Ireland, these two births will become a matter of public scandal; no good awaits me from any of this." 

"That is not what shall take place," said the cleric. "Instead, I will take the silver salmon and make use of it; <note>original: *on the head of him*. The concluding poem of this text explains that Finnian used the silver from this miraculous fish to create a reliquary and other sacred objects.</note> by virtue of it, you shall bring forth a son and bear brothers to him as well. From him, more kings of Ireland shall descend than from any of the others." 

Mughain answered, "I am well pleased, if what you say is fulfilled for me!" 

"It shall be fulfilled," said the cleric.

Then Finnian and Bishop Aedh pronounced a blessing upon the queen and upon the descendants who would come from her. One of them put water into his cup and gave it to the queen, who both drank it and washed herself with it. Through this process, she found herself with child and, this time, had a son: who was **Aedh Sláine**. <note>Aedh Sláine (d. 604 AD) was a High King of Ireland and ancestor of the Síl nÁedo Sláine dynasty.</note> 

Truly, that was a good child who was born then: **Aedh Sláine**. His clan in Bregia <note>Magh Breagh, a fertile plain in modern-day County Meath and North County Dublin.</note> are good as well: good in their generosity, renown, and honor; in their hardiness, their ability to collect tribute, and their dominance; in their righteousness, heroic deeds, and brilliance; in their dealings with the church, their roles as hospitable hosts, and their compassion; in their ethics, wisdom, and pride; in their fame, affection, and sincerity; in their physical form, good sense, and intelligence; in their nobility, excellence, and splendor. For "a golden wand laid across a plate of white bronze"—that is what the descendants of Aedh Sláine are across the plain of Bregia.

## *The Wooing of Becfola.*

It was once upon a time, when Dermot, the son of Aedh Sláine, enjoyed the royal rule of Ireland, that his foster-son Crimthann, son of Aedh, was with him as a hostage from the people of Leinster. One day, he and Crimthann his student, taking with them their various weapons and a single servant boy, went to *Ath Truim* <note>modern: Trim, County Meath</note>. They saw a lone woman in a chariot coming out of the west and across the ford. She was fairer than any other woman in the whole world. Dermot inquired: "Where have you come from, woman?" "Not from far," she answered. "What causes you to be alone?" "I am in search of wheaten grain," she said. "You shall find such with me," said Dermot. "We do not refuse it," said the woman. Thereupon he escorted her to Tara, and she shared his comfortable bed. Everyone in general inquired: "Where is this woman from, Dermot?" "I will not tell," he replied. Everyone said then: "*Bec a fhola*" <note>original: "bec a fhola"; meaning "small is her bride-gift" or "little value"</note>, that is, his bride-gift to her is but small." "Let 그 that be her name," said the magicians, "namely, *Becfola*."

Subsequently, the woman set her love on Crimthann, son of Aedh, the king's pupil, instead of Dermot, and for a long time she persisted in soliciting him. The young man indeed said that at the hour of <term>tierce</term> <note>the third hour of the day, approximately 9:00 AM</note> on Sunday, he would go to *Cluain Dá Chaillech* <note>The Meadow of the Two Nuns</note> to meet her for the purpose of carrying her off secretly; however, his people dissuaded him from eloping with the wife of the King of Ireland.

Then, at early morning on Sunday, she rises from Dermot's side. "In what direction is this early rising, woman?" he asked. "To *Cluain Dá Chaillech*," she answered. "What is the meaning of that?" The lady said: "Eight smocks with embroidery of gold, eight...

# Becfola

"I have left brooches fully set with jewels and three gold diadems in safekeeping there." 

Dermot said, "Do not go on Sunday to look for them; a journey taken on Sunday does not bring good fortune." 

She replied, "Let some men come with me, for it is certain that I will go." 

"It shall not be from me <note>meaning: Dermot refuses to provide an escort from his own retinue</note> that any will bear you company," said Dermot.

She and her handmaid then went out of Tara southwards into the Duffry of Leinster <note>original: *Dubhthar*, a forested district in County Wexford</note>. There they lost their way and wandered until nightfall, when they noticed a pack of wolves approaching them on the hillside. To escape them, she climbed into a tree, but the wolves devoured her maid. She had not been in the tree long when she discerned a fire in the heart of the forest. She approached it and saw a young man by the fireside who had the most handsome appearance in the world, both in his physique and his clothing. She sat down close to him. The young man glanced at her but neither spoke nor turned his face toward her until he had finished cooking a wild boar that he was preparing. As soon as he had finished roasting the pig, he washed his hands and went down from the fire to the lake. 

She followed after him. The young man got into his boat, and she went with him. They rowed until they reached a high, pleasant island, and there they entered a vast and beautiful palace where they found no one else. Next, they ate various meats and drank delicious mead. The two of them retired to the same bed, but until the next morning, he never turned toward her nor bothered her in any way.

When morning came, they heard a shout: "Come out, Flann!" a voice cried, and several men appeared. The young man rose, put on his armor, and went out. She went to the door of the dwelling to watch him and saw three men there who were of equal age, stature, and bravery; in another direction, she saw four others fully armed. Then the eight fought a manly and vigorous fight, four on each side. He and his three companions defeated the other four, but all of them—except for him alone—fell side by side, lifeless and dead. He then returned to the fort. 

"May you have good luck from your valor," she said. "It is a gallant deed that you have done." 

"It would have been a good deed, if only it had been against enemies that I performed it," he replied. 

She asked, "Where did these young men come from?" 

"Those four who opposed me were my nephews <note>original: "brother's sons"</note>, and the other three were my own brothers."

"What was it that they fought you for?" she asked. He replied: "The island of Fedach, son of the Blind <note>original: *inis Fedaigh mhic an daill*</note>." "And why did you not take me for your own?" she asked. "Because I am such a poor match for you after you abandoned the King of Ireland, and because the island is not yet mine. However, should it fall to my lot, I will come to fetch you; and if it seems good to you, you shall be my only wife. But for now, return to the King. Your handmaid is safe and sound at the foot of that same tree, free from all harm or danger, and I will personally escort you both back to Tara."

Then they made their way to Tara. When she reached King Dermot’s dwelling, the King was just rising from his bed on that same Sunday morning. "Truly," he said, "it was not right for you to violate God’s law by traveling on the Sabbath." She replied, "I have not done so at all." <note>Becfola implies that her supernatural journey took no time in the mortal world.</note>

Just as they were speaking, they saw four young clerics <term>ecclesiastics</term> enter. The King asked, "What has caused you to break the Sabbath?" They answered, "It is the command of our superior, Molasius of Devenish <note>Saint Molaise, founder of the monastery on Devenish Island</note>, that has sent us to you." Then they explained the reason as follows: "One of the monks <term>familia</term> of Devenish rose early to let out his cattle, and he saw eight handsome young men, well-equipped with armor and weapons, fighting one another. In that battle, they all killed their opponents; every man was slain except for one. Molasius buried the seven who died. They left behind a treasure of gold and silver—enough to fill two men's loads—which had adorned their necks, arms, and weapons. The reason we have come to you is so that you may determine your own royal share of this treasure."

The King said, "Not at all. I will not interfere with the treasure that God has given to him to claim a share. Instead, let that gold and silver be used to fashion a reliquary <term>shrine for holy relics</term> and sacred emblems with expert craftsmanship." This was exactly what was done; for from that gold, the Shrine of Molasius and his pastoral staff <term>crosier</term> were made.

The young clerics then told the King that the Queen had been present at the battle and the slaying of the warriors. By this time, the King had finished dressing, and he commanded Becfola to return to Flann, the descendant of Fedach. She rose eagerly and traveled back to Flann, and after that, the two were never parted again.

### *The Disappearance of Caenchomrac*

There was a certain noble bishop in Clonmacnoise named Caenchomrac, whose original name had been Mochta. He was a man of pure character and a <term>coarb</term> <note>original: "coarb"; a Gaelic term for a spiritual successor to a founding saint</note> of God. He came to Cluain <note>Clonmacnoise</note> on a pilgrimage, where he was held in great reverence and consideration. This was because he could learn from God whether those who died from time to time would receive a heavenly reward or suffer torment. Furthermore, he could announce to anyone who desired to know, during the final quarter of the preceding year, the exact year in which they would die. However, he eventually felt that the deference shown to him in Cluain was excessive. Therefore, he went to *Inis Aendaimh* <note>modern: Devenish Island</note> in Lough Ree to continue his pilgrimage, for he considered it a suitably lonely place for performing the canonical hours, the Mass, and his prayers.

A prayerful community of monks lived with him on the island. They used to travel across the mainland to gather alms and firstfruits in Teffia, for the men of Teffia were very devoted to him. They gave him one hundred piglets, a hundred calves, a hundred lambs, a cake of bread from every kneading trough, and a <term>screpall</term> <note>an ancient Irish silver coin or unit of value</note> from every <term>cathair</term> <note>a stone fortress or settlement</note>. They yielded these gifts on the condition that—provided they paid him this tribute—the number of their men killed in any single battle would never exceed nine. As he once said after a battle:

> "I thank my King <note>meaning God</note> that the men of Teffia remain in their land; not one of them has been killed. I declare to you—and this is no false promise of friendship—that if you only call upon my name, your losses in battle shall be no more than nine men."

He added: "Furthermore, even if those who attack you are many and you are but few, if you only think of me, you shall return home safely:

> "Though nine men in the land of Teffia face a hundred thousand thousands, let them only meditate on Caenchomrac, and they shall return to their own countries safe and sound. The armies of this world shall not achieve victory over them—provided they render me their service, just as my service is directed toward God."

For a while, then, he remained in both Cluain and the island...

# Caenchomrac

*Inis Aendaimh* <note>original: "inis aendaimh"; likely meaning "Island of the Lone Ox" or "Island of One House"</note> [that is, first in one, then in the other], and during a time when he was on the island, his monks went forth as mentioned above. Eoghan and Ecertach—two sons of Aedhacan of Hy-Many <note>A large kingdom in medieval Ireland, located in parts of modern County Galway and Roscommon</note> and both close disciples of the cleric—traveled to Slieveleitrim in Hy-Many. The Fannan clan was there hunting on the mountain; they had killed a good number of wild swine, and they gave a piglet to the clerics. These men carried it back to their house and, having placed it on a forked stick, put it over the fire. 

But as the cleric <note>Caenchomrac</note> chanted his psalms, he saw a tall man coming toward him who emerged out of the lake—that is to say, from the bottom of the water. He greeted the cleric, and the cleric greeted him in return. The man said, "He who is on that forked stick at the fire would have well performed the responses and sung psalms with you." 

"What is the meaning of this?" Caenchomrac asked. 

The other answered, "It is easily told. We have a monastery down under this lake (for God finds it no harder for men to live under the water than to dwell in any other place), and the monastery's young men mutinied. For this, they were expelled in the form of swine. These are the ones being slaughtered today in Slieveleitrim, and the one on the forked stick at the fire is one of them. I am his father according to the flesh; here in my hand is his psalter <note>A book containing the Psalms</note>, and I bestow it upon you." 

(It was called "The Swine's Psalter," and for a long time it remained at Clonmacnoise <note>One of the most famous ancient monastic sites in Ireland</note>; but the name given to Eoghan was *An Banbh*, or "The Piglet," which was a nickname applied to him because he had a mouth like a boar). 

Caenchomrac gave the father permission to take the body away and bury him, and the man said to the bishop, "What hinders you, cleric, from coming with me to inspect the monastery that is under this lake?" 

Caenchomrac answered, "I will go." 

They both dove into the lake and entered the monastery, where Caenchomrac stayed from one canonical hour <note>The specific times of day set aside for prayer</note> until the same time the following day. On the next morning, he returned to his house, completely covered in lake-weed <note>original: "lacustrine wrack"</note>. He made a frequent practice of visiting the areas beneath the lake; from that time forward, as long as he lived, the monastery was never hidden from him.

On every Holy Thursday <note>original: "Easter Thursday"; the Thursday before Easter</note>, various clerics used to travel to *Inis Aendaimh* to visit Caenchomrac so that he might consecrate holy oil for them. He, in turn, would perform the canonical service...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the holy man Caenchomrac and transitions into a historical panegyric (a speech of high praise) for King Cormac mac Airt.</meta>

for them, give them Mass, consecrate their oil, and preach to them. After the service and Mass, on which day it was customary to have a banquet, ale and meat were served out to the clerics as was the habit. <insert>[On this particular occasion of ours]</insert> Caenchomrac left them, went out, and spent the greater part of that day away from them. Later he came back to the house where they dined and saluted them, and they greeted him in the same fashion. He saw that their dishes were full of fat pork and began to scold them for eating such meat during Lent <note>The forty-day period of fasting and penitence before Easter</note>. He gave them a great reprimand—anger and prodigious indignation seized him to the extent that, because of the godliness flashing in his countenance, they could not look him in the face. The clerics fled before him. Caenchomrac rushed away from them into the open, and from that time to this he has not been seen. It is not known whether he went to dwell in a monastery under the lake to serve God, or whether it was angels that carried his soul to Heaven. After this, the sages of the Gael <note>The Gaelic people of Ireland and Scotland</note> have never eaten meat on Maundy Thursday <note>The Thursday before Easter, commemorating the Last Supper</note>.

### *Here is the Panegyric of Conn's son Cormac and the Death of Finn son of Cumhall.*

There was a monarch, noble and honorable, who attained the rule of Ireland: Cormac, son of Art, son of Conn of the Hundred Battles. Subsequently, he reigned over her for forty years, excepting the two years during which Ulster usurped the throne: that is to say, Fergus Black-knee for one year, and Eochaid Gonnat for another. Twice, in fact, the Ulidians <note>The people of Ulster</note> deposed Cormac. This same Cormac was also missing from among his people for four months; until he himself came back and told of his adventures, it was not known in what direction he had gone.

To proceed: except for David's son Solomon, there never was a king in the world who could be compared to Cormac for the brilliance of his intellect or the wealth of his reign. For he never gave a judgment unless he had the three judicial requisites: a mind gifted with sagacity, judicial precedent, and...

# Cormac and Finn

As a result of the wisdom and knowledge of these judgments, it was said that in Cormac's time, calves were commonly born after only three months' gestation. In his day, a sack of wheat was produced from every ridge of land; in his day, the *colpach*-heifers <note>original: "colpach"; a young heifer or bullock, usually in its second year</note> were already fully grown, calved cows. In any river that was only knee-deep, a salmon was caught in every single mesh of the net. In his time, every cow had an udder full of colostrum <note>original: "biestings"; the first milk produced after giving birth, rich in nutrients</note>. In his time, men could gather honey with their fingertips <insert>as they walked</insert>, because it rained down from Heaven due to the righteousness of Cormac's governance. In his time, enough vessels could not be found to hold all the milk, for the cattle produced milk without ceasing.

That king was also comparable to Octavius Augustus: for just as everyone paid the Caesarian (that is, imperial) tribute to Augustus for their paternal inheritance, so too did all men pay the royal rent to Cormac from their own districts; for Cormac never deprived anyone of what rightfully belonged to them.

In all the world, there was no king like Cormac. He excelled in form, figure, and clothing; in stature, justice, and equity. This was true even of his eyes, in each of which were seven pupils, as Senuath the poet tells us when he says:

> "Beautiful was the difference between that variegated pair of eyes: for in the man's head, fourteen pupils existed."

He was the most vigorous man of his time in terms of sagacity, wisdom, eloquence, action, and valor; in royal influence, domination, splendor, ambition, ethics, and lineage. He made Ireland into a "land of promise." At that time, the country was free from theft, robbery, and violence. It was exempt from the need for constant watching or herding, and no man suffered any anxiety regarding food or clothing.

But <insert>what we have said</insert> in praise of Cormac is far too little; for unless an angel were to instruct him, a man could not declare it all. Great were his power and control over the men of Ireland, seeing that—unless they were performing military service for Cormac—none of them dared to remain idle.

Now, the man whom Cormac appointed as his chief of the household and his paid master of the hounds was Finn, son of Cumhall <note>Finn mac Cumhaill, the legendary leader of the Fianna</note>; for the finest leader the King of Ireland had was always his master of the hounds.

<sig>H</sig>

# Cormac and Finn

A warrior better than Finn never struck his hand into a chief's. In terms of service, he was a soldier; in hospitality, he was a host; and in heroism, he was a hero. In his duties in battle, he was a fighting man, and in strength, he was a champion worthy of a king. Ever since that time, and until this day, every such great man is compared to Finn. Besides all this, Finn and the king's special bands <note>The Fianna, the elite standing army of ancient Ireland</note> enjoyed the general right to hunt and pursue game throughout all of Ireland.

Finn lived mostly in *Almha* <note>modern: Allen</note> in Leinster. However, when the weakness of old age weighed on him (King Cormac also being dead by then), he lived in *Almha* permanently, unless he had a reason to make a brief trip away from it. Finn's wife was Smirgat, the daughter of Fatha Canann. She was a prophetess and a wise woman, and she had told him that whenever he should drink from a horn, that act would end his life. Because of this, he never again drank from a horn, but used *cuachs* <note>original: "cuachs" [Scottish: "quaighs"]; shallow, two-handled drinking bowls</note> instead.

One day, Finn set out from *Almha* and eventually found himself in the place called *Adharca Iuchbadh* in Offaly. There, on a hillside, he came upon a well and took a drink from it. He then put his thumb under his "knowledge-tooth" <note>A legendary ritual where Finn gained prophetic insight by sucking his thumb</note> and performed the incantation of *teinm laeghda* <note>original: "teinm laeghda"; a traditional Irish mantic or prophetic ritual</note>, through which it was revealed to him that the end of his time and his life had come. He then sang these following verses:

->The prophecy has befallen Finn . . .<-

Then he traveled on until he reached *Druim Bregh* <note>original: "druim Bregh"; the Ridge of Bregia in County Meath</note>. In that country, there were many reasons for people to hate Finn and the Fianna; for it was by Finn's hand that Uirgrenn, of the tribe called the *Luaighne* of Tara, had once fallen. These people now gathered together, including Uirgrenn's three sons and Aichlech the Great—the son of Duibrenn, who was the third of Uirgrenn's sons. Between them, an extraordinary and ruthless battle was fought—manly, fierce, and violent—in which every person remembered their grievances against one another, whether they were old grudges or more recent injuries. This battle took place at *Brea* upon the River Boyne. They fought hand-to-hand for a long time, until both sides had suffered many casualties. The battle was won against Finn, and he perished in it. It was by Aichlech, the son of Duibrenn, that—

# Finn's People

Finn fell, and it was he <note>referring to a specific warrior mentioned in the preceding text</note> who beheaded him. Therefore, to commemorate the deed and to lead the unlearned toward knowledge, the <term>sennachie</term> <note>original: "sennachie"; a traditional Gaelic historian or storyteller</note> sang these verses:

> *Brea's great battle of bright exploits . . .*

This, then, according to archaeological truth and as the experts relate it, is the manner of Finn's death. However, they give various accounts of his origin. Some say that he was of the *Corca-Oiche* in *Ua Fidhgeinte*; others assert (and this is the truth of the matter) that he was of the *Ui Tairrsigh* of Offaly, who were of the Attacotti, as Maelmura said in the chronicle: "There are six stocks that shall have territorial settlements but are not of Breogan's people, namely: the *Garbraighe* of the *Suca*; the *Ui Tairrsigh*; the *Galeoin* of Leinster [and others]."

The people of Leinster, however, state that Finn was the great-grandson of Nuada Necht, and that his pedigree is as follows: Finn, son of Cumhall, son of Sualtach, son of Baeiscne, son of Nuada Necht.

->The above is Cormac's Panegyric and Finn's Death.<-
->The End.<-

## The Enumeration of Finn's People

This is the enumeration and description of Finn's people. Their strength consisted of seven score and ten officers <note>150 officers</note>, each of whom commanded twenty-seven warriors. Every one of these was bound to certain conditions of service (just as Cuchulainn was in his time). These conditions were:
*   That they must not accept material compensation <note>original: "eric" or blood-money</note> for the violation of their honor or guarantees.
*   That they must never deny any person a request for valuables or food.
*   That no single individual among them should flee before nine enemy warriors.

No man was accepted into the <term>Fianna</term> <note>original: "Fianna"; the semi-independent warrior bands of ancient Ireland</note>, nor admitted to the great Gathering of Usnach, the Convention of Taillte, or the Feast of Tara, until both his paternal and maternal relatives, his <term>tuatha</term> <note>original: "tuatha"; tribes or people-groups</note> and kindreds, had given legal securities for him. These securities guaranteed that even if the man were killed, his family would urge no claim for compensation. This was required so that the warriors would look to no one for protection or redress but to themselves alone.

<summary>This page concludes the account of Finn mac Cumhaill's death and ancestry before detailing the rigorous moral and legal requirements for joining the Fianna, including the renunciation of family protection.</summary>

# Finn's People

<margin>The laws of the Fianna</margin>
The community should look to avenge them. On the other hand, if it were they who inflicted great mischief upon others, reprisals were not to be made upon their respective people.

Of all these candidates, not a man was accepted until he was a prime poet, well-versed in the twelve books of poesy <note>original: "poesy"; refers to the traditional Irish poetic arts required of a warrior-elite</note>. No man was accepted until a large hole had been made in the ground (deep enough to reach the waist-fold of his belt) and he was placed into it with his shield and a hazel stick the length of a forearm. Then, nine warriors with nine spears, standing ten furrows' width away from him, would assail him and let fly their spears at him all at once. If he were wounded despite his guard, he was not received into <term>Fianship</term> <note>The status or profession of being a member of the Fianna</note>.

Not a man of them was accepted until his hair had been interwoven into braids and he started at a run through the woods of Ireland. While he ran, the others followed in his wake seeking to wound him, with only the distance of a single forest bough between them at the start. Should he be overtaken, he was wounded and not received into the <term>Fianna</term> <note>The legendary semi-independent bands of warriors in Irish mythology</note> afterward. If his weapons had quivered in his hand, he was not accepted. Should a branch in the wood have disturbed any of his hair out of its braiding, he was not accepted. If he had cracked a dry stick under his foot as he ran, he was not accepted. Unless, at his full speed, he had both jumped over a stick level with his brow and stooped to pass under one level with his knee, he was not accepted. Also, unless he could extract a thorn from his foot with his fingernail without slackening his pace, he was not taken into <term>Fianship</term>. But if he performed all this, he was one of Finn's people.

He who led those <term>Fianna</term> was a good man indeed, for he was the seventh king ruling Ireland. That is to say, there were five kings of the provinces and the High King of Ireland; he himself was the seventh, ruling in conjunction with the High King of all Ireland.

Finn's two head-guards <note>original: "poll-wards"; guardians of the head or perhaps personal bodyguards</note> were Noenalach and Raer, grandson of Garb. The two stewards of his hounds were Crimthann and Connla Cas. His dispenser was Cathluan, son of Crimthann. His master of the banquet was Corc, son of Suan. His three cupbearers were Dermot, grandson of Duibhne; Faillin; and Colla, son of Caeilte. The two overseers of his hearth were Caeilte and Glanna. His two bed-makers were Admoll and Mac Neri. His twelve musicians were Fergus True-mouth, Fianu, Bran, the two Reidhes, Nuada, Aithirne Aghmar, and... Flann, Aedh, and Cobthach of...

## The Colloquy with the Ancients.
<note>original: "Acallam na Senórach." This is a famous Middle Irish narrative where the last survivors of the Fianna meet Saint Patrick and recount the legends of Ireland's landscape.</note>

When the battle of Comar, the battle of Gowra, and the battle of Ollarba had been fought, and after the <term>Fianna</term><note>The Fianna were semi-independent bands of warriors in Irish mythology, led most famously by Finn mac Cumhaill.</note> were for the most part extinguished, the survivors had dispersed in small bands and companies throughout all Ireland. At the point in time which concerns us, there remained only two good warriors of the last of the Fianna: Ossian son of Finn, and Caeilte son of Crunnchu son of Ronan (whose youthful vigor and power of spear-throwing were now diminished), along with enough fighting men to make eighteen in total. These eighteen came out of the flowery-soiled, wooded borders of Slievefuad <margin>County Armagh</margin> and into the *white gardens*, at this present called Louth. There, at the falling of the evening clouds that night, they were melancholy and dispirited.

Caeilte then said to Ossian: “Well now, Ossian, before the day ends, what path shall we take to find lodging for the night?” 

Ossian answered: “I do not know, seeing that of the elders of the Fianna and of Finn’s people, only three survive: I and yourself, Caeilte, along with *Cámha*, the female chief and guardian. From the time he was a boy until the day he died, she kept Finn son of Cumall safe.” 

Caeilte said: “We are entitled to this night’s lodging and food from her; for it is impossible to describe or show the great amount of wealth and goods she received from Finn and the Fianna.”

<summary>This page concludes a list of Finn mac Cumhaill's retinue and begins the "Colloquy with the Ancients," describing the twilight of the Fianna as the last survivors, Ossian and Caeilte, seek shelter in Louth.</summary>
<keywords>Fianna, Ossian, Caeilte, Finn mac Cumhaill, Cámha, Irish Mythology, Louth, Slievefuad, Acallam na Senórach</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the surviving members of the Fianna, specifically the meeting between the aging warriors Ossian and Caeilte and the female chief Camha.</meta>

# The Colloquy

Finn, captain of the Fianna <note>The legendary band of Irish warriors</note>, bestowed on her many precious things and treasures, including the third best valuable item that Finn ever acquired: namely, the *Anghalach*, a drinking-horn which Moriath, daughter of the King of Greece, gave to Finn, and Finn in turn gave to Camha.

With Camha, therefore, they received hospitality for that night. She asked them for their names and, at the sound of them, wept vehement showers of tears; then she and they sought news from one another. Next, they entered the guest house prepared for them, and Camha, the female chief, ordered their meal: she commanded that the freshest of all kinds of meat and the oldest of all sorts of drink be given to them, for she knew the manner in which men like them were accustomed to be fed. She also knew exactly how much had, many times before, constituted a sufficient portion for Ossian and for Caeilte. Languidly and feebly she arose and spoke at length about the Fianna and about Finn mac Cumall; she also spoke of Ossian’s son, Oscar, of *Mac Lugach*, of the Battle of Gowra <note>The catastrophic final battle of the Fianna</note>, and other matters. Because of this, a great silence eventually settled over them all.

Then Caeilte said: "We find such memories no more painful than the fact that we—the eighteen of us who remain of that great and noble fellowship—must now by necessity part and go our separate ways." Ossian answered him: "Now that they are gone, I swear by my word that there is truly no more fight or strength left in me." Valiant as these warriors were, they nevertheless wept in gloom, sadness, and dejection there with the female chief, Camha. Their full allowance of food and drink was given to them; they stayed there for three days and three nights, then bade Camha farewell, and Ossian said:

> "Camha today is sorrowful: she has reached the point where she must struggle to stay afloat; Camha is without either son or grandson: it has befallen her to be old and withered."

They came out of the town then and onto the green. There they made a resolution, which was this: to separate. This parting of theirs felt like the sundering of soul from body. And so they did: Ossian went to the *sidhe* <note>original: *sídh*; a fairy mound or otherworldly dwelling</note> of *Ucht Cleitigh*, where his mother lived: Blái, daughter of Derc, surnamed *Dianscothach* <note>original: *dianscothach*; meaning 'of the forcible language' or 'eloquent'</note>; while Caeilte took his way to *Inbher Bic Loingsigh*, which is currently called *Mainistir Droichid* <note>The Monastery of the Bridge, likely Mellifont Abbey</note>.

<summary>Ossian and Caeilte receive hospitality from the elderly female chief Camha, sharing a mournful reunion before the final remnants of the Fianna decide to part ways forever.</summary>
<keywords>Finn mac Cumall, Ossian, Caeilte, Camha, Fianna, Battle of Gowra, Irish Mythology, parting, hospitality</keywords>

# The Colloquy

*Átha* <note>original: "the Monastery of Drogheda"</note> was named after *Beg Loingsech*, the son of Arist, who was drowned there. He was the son of the King of the Romans who came to invade Ireland, but a tidal wave drowned him there in his *inbher* <term>inver: an Old Irish term for an estuary or river mouth</term>. Caeilte then went on to *Linn Féic* <note>Fiac’s Pool</note> on the bright-streaming River Boyne, southwards over the Old Plain of Bregia, and finally to the earthen fort of Drumderg where Patrick, son of Calpurn, was staying.

Just then, Patrick chanted the Lord’s order of the canon <note>the Mass</note>, praised the Creator, and pronounced a blessing on the fort where Finn mac Cumall <note>the legendary leader of the Fianna</note> had once lived: the fort of Drumderg. The clerics saw Caeilte and his band drawing near to them. Fear fell upon the priests at the sight of these tall men accompanied by their huge wolf-dogs, for they were not people of the same era or time as the clergy.

Then Heaven’s distinguished one, that pillar of dignity and angel on earth—Patrick, son of Calpurn and apostle of the Irish—rose and took the *aspergillum* <term>a brush or branch used for sprinkling holy water</term> to sprinkle holy water on the great men. Floating over them until that day there had been a thousand legions of demons. Into the hills and "skalps" <note>rocky clefts or caves</note>, and into the outer borders of the region and the country, the demons immediately fled in all directions. After this, the enormous men sat down.

"Tell me now," Patrick said to Caeilte, "what is your name?" 

"I am Caeilte, son of Crunnchu, son of Ronan." 

For a long while, the clergy marveled greatly as they gazed at them; for the tallest man among the clergy reached only to the waist, or perhaps the shoulder, of the visitors even while the giants were sitting down. 

Patrick spoke again: "Caeilte, I desire to ask a favor of you." 

He answered: "If I have the strength or power, you shall have it; in any case, please name it." 

"I wish to have a well of pure water nearby, from which we might baptize the *tuatha* <term>tribes or people-groups</term> of Bregia, Meath, and Usnach." 

"Noble and righteous one," said Caeilte, "I have that for you." 

Crossing the fort's ramparts, they came outside. Caeilte took Patrick’s hand, and in a short time, they saw a spring-well right in front of them, sparkling and clear. The size and thickness of the watercress and the *fothlacht* <note>brooklime, a medicinal water plant</note> growing in it was a wonder to them. Then Caeilte began to describe its fame and qualities, saying:

<summary>Caeilte and his giant companions encounter Saint Patrick at Drumderg, where the Saint exorcises demons from them and Caeilte miraculously reveals a hidden well for baptisms.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Saint Patrick and Caeilte, a survivor of the ancient Fianna warriors, as they bridge the gap between Ireland's pagan past and its Christian present.</meta>

<header>The Colloquy</header>

> "O Well of *Tráigh Dá Bhan* <note>original: 'two women's strand'</note>, beautiful are your luxurious, branching cresses! Since your produce is neglected, your *fothlacht* <note>an edible aquatic plant, likely brooklime</note> is not permitted to grow. Forth from your banks your trout are visible, and your wild swine roam in your neighboring wilderness; the deer of your fair hunting crags, and your dappled and red-chested fawns! Your acorns hang from the branches of your trees; your fish swim in the estuaries of your rivers. Lovely is the color of your purling streams, O you who are yourself azure-hued, and again green with the reflection of the surrounding woods! . . ."

"It is well," Patrick said. "Has our dinner and our provisions reached us yet?" "It has indeed," answered Bishop Sechnall. "Distribute it," said Patrick, "and give one half to those nine tall warriors who are the survivors of the Fianna <note>The legendary band of warriors led by Finn mac Cumhaill</note>." Then his bishops, his priests, and his psalm-singers arose and blessed the meat; and they consumed their full share of both meat and drink, yet in a manner that served the well-being of their souls.

<margin>✓</margin> Patrick then asked: "Was he not a good lord with whom you served—that is to say, Finn mac Cumhaill?" Upon which Caeilte spoke this little tribute of praise:

> "If only the brown leaf shed by the wood were gold—if only the white wave were silver—Finn would have given it all away."

<margin>✓</margin> "Who or what was it that sustained you so in your life?" Patrick inquired. Caeilte answered: "The truth that was in our hearts, the strength in our arms, and the integrity of our tongues."

"Tell me, Caeilte," Patrick continued, "in the houses you frequented before our time, were there drinking-horns, or cups, or goblets of crystal and pale gold?" Caeilte answered: "The number of horns in my lord's house was as follows:

> "Finn had three hundred and twelve drinking-horns made of gold; whenever they were filled for the pouring, the quantity of liquor they held was immense."

"If it were not a distraction from the devout life, an occasion for neglecting prayer, and for abandoning our conversation with God, we would feel the time pass quickly as we talked with you, warrior." Then Caeilte began to list the drinking-horns, along with the names of the chiefs and lords to whom they had belonged:

> "The horns that were in Finn's house, I bear their names in mind . . ."

"Success and blessings attend you, Caeilte," Patrick said. "This is a lightening of spirit and mind to me; and now tell us another tale." "I will indeed; but tell me what story you would prefer—"

# The Colloquy

"What would you be pleased to have?" <note>The speaker is Saint Patrick, addressing Caeilte.</note> "In the Fianna, did you have horses or cavalry?" Caeilte answered: "We did indeed; we had thrice fifty foals from a single mare and a single sire." "From where were they obtained?" "I will tell you the truth of the matter:

"There was a young man who served with Finn: Arthur, son of *Béine Brit* <note>Béine the Briton</note>, and his troop consisted of twenty-seven men <note>original: "thrice nine men"</note>. Finn organized a hunt at Ben-Edar <note>Modern Howth</note>, which turned out to be a bountiful and fruitful hunt. They released their hounds accordingly, while Finn took his seat on the *Cairn of the Fian-warrior* <note>original: "carn an fhéinneda"</note> between the heights of Edar and the sea. There, his spirit was joyful as he listened to the bellowing of the maddened stags as they were rapidly killed by the hounds of the Fianna.

"Arthur, the son of Béine Brit, was stationed between the main body of the hunt and the sea to prevent the deer from taking to the water and escaping by swimming. But Arthur, being thus on the outskirts and close to the shore, noticed three of Finn's hounds: *Bran*, *Sceolaing*, and *Adnuall*. He devised a plan: he and his twenty-seven men would depart across the sea, carrying those three hounds away with him to his own land. This plot was then put into action; for I know well that they, taking those three hounds with them, crossed the surface of the sea and reached harbor and haven at the *Estuary of the Stormy Sea* <note>original: "inbher mara gaimiach"</note> in the land of the Britons. They landed there, proceeded to the mountain of Lodan, son of Lir, and hunted there.

"Regarding the Fianna: after this event, they finished their hunting and their slaughter of forest game. They then camped at the height of Edar, son of Edgaeth, and—as was the custom then—Finn's household hounds were counted. Now, his hounds were many in number, as the poet said:

> "It was like counting branches on a tree to number Finn's full-grown hounds and his sleek, melodious pack of young dogs: there were three hundred of the first, and two hundred puppies."

"There must have been many men who owned those," said Patrick. "That is true indeed," Caeilte answered, "for the report that used to be told in Finn's house was this:

> "Those who lived in the house of Finn were one hundred and fifty joyous leaders of the Fianna; there were three hundred confidential servants as well, and two hundred foster-sons who were worthy of their chiefs."

"But when the hounds were counted, a great deficiency was dis—" <note>The text breaks off here, likely continuing with "discovered."</note>
