covered in them: Bran, Sceolaing, and Adnuall <note>Finn’s three most famous and prized hounds</note> were missing, and it was told to Finn. "Have all three battalions of the Fianna searched for them," he said; yet though the search was made, the hounds were not found.

To Finn then was brought an elongated basin of pale gold; he washed his kingly face and put his thumb under his "tooth of knowledge" <note>original: *fios*; a supernatural method of divination where Finn gained prophetic insight by biting his thumb</note>. The truth was revealed to him, and he said: "The son of the King of the Britons has stolen your hounds from you. Therefore, choose nine men to go in search of them!" They were chosen, and these were their names: Dermot, son of Donn, son of Donough, son of *Dubhán*, of the *Erna* <note>An ancient tribal group</note> of Munster in the south; and Goll mac Morna—"

"Was Goll the son of a chief, or a simple warrior?" Patrick inquired.

"A chief's son," answered Caeilte:

"He was the son of Teigue, son of Morna of the plain <note>original: *magh*</note>, who was the son of Faelan, son of Feradach, son of Fiacha, son of Art of the plain, son of Muiredach, son of Eochaid."

"There was also Cael the Valiant <note>original: *cródha*</note>, the hundred-slayer, grandson of Nemhnann. He was a champion of Finn’s who possessed a deadly quality: the property attached to him was that his arm never threw a spear that missed its mark, and if his hand drew blood from a man, that man would be dead before nine days had passed. There was Finn’s son, Ossian: a man who never refused anyone, provided they had a head to eat with and legs to walk upon to carry away his gifts."

"Caeilte," said Patrick, "that is a great reputation."

"And though it is a great one, it is true," Caeilte answered, and said:

"In matters of gold, of silver, or concerning food, Ossian never denied any man; nor, even if another man’s generosity was great enough for a chief, did Ossian ever ask anything of him."

"There was Ossian’s son, Oscar: the chief’s son who was the best in all Ireland for spear-throwing and for vigorous activity; also Ferdoman, son of Bodhb Derg, son of the Daghda <note>A primary deity of the Tuatha Dé Danann</note>; Finn’s son Raighne Wide-eye, and his son Cainche the Crimson-red; Glas, son of the Smith of Beare <note>original: *Encherd Béra*</note>, Mac Lughach, and myself. Now, saintly Patrick, we nine felt confident within ourselves that from Taprobane <note>An ancient name for Sri Lanka, representing the far East</note> in the east to the Garden of the Hesperides <note>A legendary garden at the western edge of the world in Greek mythology</note> in the westernmost part of the world, there were no four hundred warriors who were a match for us in hand-to-hand combat on the battlefield. We did not have a head without a helmet, nor a shoulder with—

# The Colloquy

<margin>The capture of Arthur and the hounds.</margin>
...without a whitened shield, nor a right fist that did not grasp two great and lengthy spears. On this expedition we went our ways then, until we reached the mountain of Lodan son of Lir <note>Lodan mac Lir: a figure from Irish mythology, likely associated with the sea god Lir</note>. We had been there only a short time before we heard the conversation of men hunting in the field.

As for Arthur, son of Beine Brit: he was sitting on his hunting-mound with his people. We charged them in a lively fashion and killed all of Arthur’s people; but Oscar <note>Finn's grandson and a renowned warrior</note> threw both arms around Arthur, granted him quarter <note>mercy/sparing of life</note>, and we recovered our three hounds. At that moment, Goll son of Morna <note>Goll mac Morna: a leader of the Connacht Fianna and sometimes a rival to Finn</note>, looking about him, saw an iron-grey horse, flecked with spots and wearing a bridle decorated with wrought gold. With another glance to his left, he spotted a bay horse—one not easy to catch—which had a woven bridle of double-refined silver and a golden bit. Goll son of Morna seized this second horse as well and placed it in the hand of Ossian <note>Finn's son and a famous poet-warrior</note>, who passed it on to Dermot *grandson of Duibhne* <note>Dermot ua Duibhne: the famous lover and warrior of the Fianna</note>. After successfully carrying out our slaughter and celebrating it, we departed. We brought with us the heads of those twenty-seven men, our hounds, and the horses, with Arthur himself as our prisoner. We returned to where Finn was: in the old *Plain of the Flocks* <note>original: magh nelta; modern: Moynalty</note> at Edar. We reached his tent, and Caeilte said, "We have brought Arthur." Arthur entered into a bond of service with Finn and remained Finn’s follower until the day he died. 

<margin>The origin of horses in the Fianna.</margin>
We gave the two horses to Finn: a stallion and a mare. From their offspring came all the horses of the Fianna, who until that time had not used any. The mare gave birth eight times, producing eight foals at each birth. These were distributed among the various detachments and noblemen of the Fianna, who subsequently had chariots built for them.

"Success and a blessing be upon you, Caeilte," said Patrick, "now tell us the names of the chiefs and mighty men who owned those horses." Then Caeilte, telling the story, said:

-> "The horses of the Fianna are known to me..." <-

"Success and a blessing, Caeilte! All of this is a recreation for our spirits and minds—if only it were not a distraction from our devotion and a neglect of our prayers."

They remained there until the following morning, when Patrick dressed in his robes and went out onto the green, accompanied by his sixty priests, sixty psalm-singers, and holy bishops.

# The Colloquy

<page-num>108</page-num>

There were sixty others as well who, along with him, disseminated faith and piety throughout Ireland. Patrick’s two guardian angels came to him now: *Aibellán* and *Solusbrethach*. He asked them whether, in God’s sight, it was appropriate for him to be listening to the stories of the Fianna <note>The Fianna were a legendary band of warriors in Irish mythology, led by Finn Mac Cumhaill</note>. With equal emphasis and in total agreement, the angels answered him: “Holy cleric, those ancient warriors tell no more than a third of their stories because of forgetfulness and a lack of memory. However, let these stories—such as they are—be written by you on the writing tablets of poets and in the words of *ollaves* <note>term: ollaves; high-ranking learned men or master poets in ancient Ireland</note>; for it will be a pastime for the companies and nobles of future times to listen to these stories.” Having said this, the angels departed.

Messengers were then dispatched from Patrick to fetch Caeilte. He, along with the nine companions who made up his group, was brought to the saint. Their names were these: Failbhe son of Flann; Eoghan Red-weapon, the son of the king of Ulidia <note>modern: Ulster</note>; Flann, son of Fergus, king of Kinelconnell; Conall the Slaughterer, son of Angus, king of Connacht; Scannlan, son of Ailell, king of Ossory; Baedan, son of Garb, king of Corcaguiney; Luaimnech Linn, son of the king of the *Erna* of Munster; Failbhe and Uainchenn, the sons of the king of Dalaradia from the north; and Fulartach, son of Finghin, king of the *tuatha* <note>term: tuatha; territories or tribal lands</note> of Bregia and of Meath.

Patrick said: “Do you know why you have been brought to speak with me?” 

“In truth, we do not know,” they answered. 

“So that you should show obedience and conform to the gospel of the King of Heaven and Earth: the True and most Glorious God.” Then and there, the water of Christ’s Baptism was sprinkled on them by Patrick, as a preparation for the baptism and conversion of all Ireland.

Then, with his right hand, Caeilte reached across himself to the rim of his shield and gave Patrick a ridged mass of gold taken from there, which contained one hundred and fifty ounces. This was a fee for the baptism of the nine men with him. He said: “That was the last wage given to me by Finn, the chief; and Patrick, you shall have it for the sake of my soul’s health and the soul of my commander.” The size of this mass of gold reached from Patrick’s middle fingertip to the highest point of his shoulder, while in width and thickness it measured a man’s cubit <note>A cubit is an ancient measurement roughly the length of a forearm</note>. This gold was then bestowed upon the *Táilchenn*’s <note>term: Táilchenn; "Adze-head," an archaic Irish nickname for Saint Patrick, referring to his tonsure</note> canonical hand-bells, on psalters, and on missals <note>A missal is a book containing the texts for the celebration of the Mass</note>.

Patrick said again: “It is well, Caeilte; what was the best...”

<margin>✓</margin>
"...hunting that the Fianna <note>The Fianna were a legendary band of semi-independent warriors in early Ireland</note> ever had, whether in Ireland or in Scotland?" "The hunting of Arran." Patrick enquired: "Where is that land?" "Between Scotland and Pictland <note>The land of the Picts in northern Scotland</note>: on the first day of the *trogan*-month (which now is called *Lughnasadh* <note>original: lughnasadh</note>, that is, 'Lammas-tide' <note>A harvest festival held on August 1st</note>), we, to the number of the Fianna's three battalions, used to travel there and have our fill of hunting until the time when the cuckoo would call from the tree-tops in Ireland. More melodious than any music was it to listen to the voices of the birds as they rose from the waves and from the island's coastline; there were one hundred and fifty separate flocks that encircled her, dressed in the bright brilliance of all colors: such as blue, and green, and azure, and yellow." Here Caeilte spoke a poem:

> "Arran of the many stags—the sea beats against her very shoulders! An island where whole companies were fed—and with ridges where blue spears are stained red in battle! Skittish deer are on her peaks, soft blackberries on her waving heather; there is cool water in her rivers, and acorns upon her reddish oaks! Greyhounds were there, and beagles; blueberries and sloes <note>The small, sour fruit of the blackthorn</note> of the dark blackthorn; houses were built with their backs against her woods, and the deer grazed scattered by her oak thickets! A crimson crop grew on her rocks, and faultless grass in all her glades; over her crags, which provided friendly refuge, there was leaping and fawns were skipping! Her level ground was smooth—her wild swines were fat; her fields were cheerful (this is a story that can be believed), her nuts hung on the boughs of the forest hazels, and long ships sailed past her! Their condition was truly pleasant when the fair weather set in: trout lie under the banks of her rivers; the seagulls circling her grand cliffs answer one another—at every season, Arran is delightful!"

<margin>✓</margin>
"Victory and blessing be upon you, Caeilte!" said Patrick: "from now on, your stories and you yourself are dear to us."

Immediately after this, Patrick saw a fort, a beautiful dwelling, and asked: "Caeilte, what is that town?" "That is the proudest town I was ever in, whether in Ireland or in Scotland." "Who lived there?" "The three sons of Lughaid Menn, son of Angus—that is, the King of Ireland's three sons: *Ruidhe*, *Fiacha*, and *Eochaid* were their names." "How did they acquire such great wealth?"

"It happened once upon a time that they came to speak with their father at the *Mound of the Druids* <note>original: fert na ndruadh</note>, northwest of Tara <note>The ancient seat of the High Kings of Ireland</note>. 'Where do you come from, young men?' he asked. They answered: 'From the *House of Women's Mourning* <note>original: echlais banghuba</note> to the south, from the house of our nurse and our guardian.' 'My boys, what brings you...'"

<header>The Colloquy</header>

"In motion?" asked the king again. "To crave a country from you, a domain." For a space the king was silent, and then said: "No father of mine conferred on me either country or domain, but my own luck and dazzling achievement. Lands, therefore, I will not bestow on you, but win lands for yourselves." 

Thereupon they rose with the readiness of one man and took their way to the green of the *brugh* <note>original: *brugh*; a large mound or prehistoric passage tomb, often considered a dwelling of the fairy folk</note> upon the Boyne where, with no one else in their company, they sat down. Ruidhe said: "What is your plan tonight?" 

His brothers rejoined: "Our project is to fast against the *Tuatha Dé Danann* <note>original: *Tuatha Dé Danann*; the 'People of the Goddess Danu,' a race of supernatural beings in Irish mythology</note>, aiming thus to win from them good fortune in the shape of a country, of a domain, of lands, and to have vast riches." 

Nor had they been long there when they noticed a cheery-looking young man of a peaceful demeanor coming toward them. He salutes the King of Ireland's sons; they answer him in the same manner. "Young man, where are you from? From where do you come?" 

"Out of yonder *brugh* chequered with many lights close by you here." 

"What name do you carry?" 

"I am the *Daghda*'s <note>The Daghda was a father-figure and king of the Tuatha Dé Danann</note> son, *Bodhb Derg*; and it was revealed to the *Tuatha Dé Danann* that you would come to fast here tonight for lands and for great fortune. But come with me, lads." 

Simultaneously they rose and entered into the *brugh*; supper was served to them, but they did not eat it. Bodhb inquired of them why it was that they took no food. 

"Because the King of Ireland, our father, denied us territory and lands. Now, there are in Ireland but two tribes that are equal: the sons of Milesius <note>The mythical ancestors of the modern Irish people</note> and the *Tuatha Dé Danann*; we have now come to the second of these two options."

Then the *Tuatha Dé Danann* went into council. The one in that council who was most noble in rank and most influential was *Midhir* Yellow-mane, son of the Daghda, who said: "Provide those men yonder with three wives, since it is from wives that either fortune or misfortune is derived." 

Whereupon they were given *Midhir*'s three daughters: Doirenn, Aife, and Aillbhe. Midhir said: "Say, Bodhb, what gifts shall be given them?" 

Bodhb said: "I will declare it. We are three times fifty sons of kings in this *sídh* <note>original: *sídh*; a fairy mound or the spirit world itself</note>; from every king's son among us, let them be given one hundred and fifty ounces of red gold, while from me they shall have, in addition, one hundred and fifty suits of clothing of many various colors." 

Aedh, son of *Aedh na nabusach* from *Cnoc Ardmulla* out in—

Regarding these, one needs only to fill the vat with pure water, and it will turn it into drinkable mead with the power to intoxicate; but into the horn, put bitter brine from the deep sea, and it shall instantly turn into wine."

"A gift from me to them," said Bodhb Derg: "a good minstrel that I have named *Fer-tuinne mac Trogain*. Even if saws were being used nearby, or if there were women in the sharpest pains of childbirth, or brave men wounded early in the day, they would nevertheless fall asleep to the fitful melody he makes. Yet, he is not a more effective minstrel to the house where he is physically present than he is to that whole country's inhabitants in general, for they all may hear him just as well." For three days and nights, they remained in the <term>sídh</term><note>A fairy mound or hollow hill.</note>.

Angus told them to take away three apple trees from *Fidh Omna* (that is, "Oak-wood"): one in full bloom, another shedding its blossoms, and another covered with ripe fruit. Then they went to the <term>dún</term><note>A fort or royal residence.</note>, where they lived for one hundred and fifty years, until those kings disappeared; for by virtue of marriage alliances, they returned again to the Tuatha Dé Danann, and from that time forward they have remained there. A din, and oh, a three-fold din! The sound of tramping rising from that fort’s green, the uproar of racing, and the lowing of cattle. Three noises, and oh, three noises indeed! The sound of its swine, thick with fat and excellent; the buzz of the crowd upon the palace lawn; and indoors, the hilarity of revellers with a clamor born of drinking mead. Fruit crops in three stages, and oh, crops in three stages indeed, that used to hang there upon the boughs! One tree shedding its leaves, one tree in bloom, and yet another laden with ripe fruit. 

It was three sons that Lughaid left (though their great deeds have passed away): Ruide, the son of the spacious-landed Lughaid; Eochaid; and the manly Fiacha. I will testify for Eochaid that he never took a single step in flight; he was never without his customary music, nor was he ever for any time without the drinking of ale <note>meaning that banquets were constant in his mansion</note>. I will testify for Fiacha (though the fame of his raids is now obscured): he never uttered an excessive word, and in his time, none excelled more in valor. I will testify for Ruide, to whom those three aforementioned things <note>young men, horses, and hounds</note> flowed in great plenty: he never denied anything to any man, nor did he ever ask anything of any man. There were thirty chieftains, thirty leaders, and thirty champions fit for a king; while the strength of his hundred-fold host was three times three thousand."

"Caeilte," said Patrick, "success and a blessing to you! All of this is a recreation of spirit and mind to us."

They had not been there long when they saw a young man approaching them from the south, traveling as straight as could be, who made a brave appearance. He wore a crimson mantle fastened with a gold brooch <note>original: fibula</note>, and next to his skin was a shirt of yellow silk. He also brought a double armful of round, yellow-headed nuts and beautiful golden-yellow apples, which he placed on the ground in front of Patrick. 

Patrick asked, "Where do you bring this fruit from, lad?" 

He answered, "From the lush-branched Feeguile." 

"What is your name?" 

"I am Falartach, son of Fergus." 

"What is your rightful heritage?" 

"The rule over the tribes <note>original: tuatha</note> of Bregia and Meath, and over the Decies of Tara, is my right; but instead of enjoying it, I am a freebooter and an outlaw." 

"Against whom do you commit these raids?" 

"Against my own brother: Becan, son of Fergus." 

"Your rights shall be restored to you shortly," said Patrick. 

"Holy cleric, give me a definite time." 

"It shall be within this very year; but where did you get the fruit?"

"Truly, I know where it was brought from," Caeilte said. "From the Wood of the Son of Treun <note>original: ros mic Triuin</note> beyond Feeguile. It was a hunting preserve belonging to a trusted warrior of Finn mac Cumall: the lusty and heroic son of Lugh." 

Patrick said, "It is well; that is where a trusted member of my own community <note>original: familia</note> dwells, Oesan..."

namely, the king of Scotland's son, who is also a chaplain to me." "That place," Caeilte went on, "was a hunting preserve for the Fianna; and whenever a scarcity of game befell them in both Ireland and Scotland, in *Ros Mic Triuin* <note>modern: New Ross, County Wexford</note> they always found enough hunting to last for three days and three nights":—

->Caeilte sang:<-

> "As *Cluain Chesáin* <note>original: 'the meadow of Chesán'</note> it was known far and wide, a place where the son of Lughach would go; but with the coming of the *Táilchenn* <note>term: 'Adze-head,' a traditional Irish name for Saint Patrick, referring to his tonsure</note> its name became *Ros Mic Triuin*. Though psalms are now sung in alternate verses by the clergy in *Cluain Chesáin*, I have seen that gentian-covered meadow entirely filled with red deer at play. Though there is reading over the pool now, there was a time when *Cluain Chesáin* held no church; instead, it was a land of apple trees, a place for swimming in the streams, and a residence for collecting tribute. The favorable prophecy has come to pass, and the 'Adze-heads' have made their home in *Cluain Chesáin*: Finn the generous, the giver of rings and bracelets, had predicted that it would become a sanctuary for saints and angels. Many a time, we and our hounds took turns chasing the young and gallant deer, while our warriors and their beagles hunted at will throughout the region around the fair meadow. In truth, I once had sixty queens at the same time, and I provided for them all, for I was a very clever charmer."

Patrick asked: "What time of day is it now?" Benignus said: "It is nearly night." "Has our supper been brought to us yet?" the saint inquired. Benignus answered: "No, it has not." Fulartach, the son of Fergus, then said: "Holy Patrick, I can direct you to a town where you shall have supper and provisions tonight." "What place is that?" "At Becan's house, my own brother's home, in the territories of Bregia and Meath."

Some clerics went ahead of Patrick to the house of Becan, who was so wealthy that he owned thirty herds of milk cows; yet he refused to give them any food. Benignus and the clergy returned and told the story to Patrick, who said: "I decree that by tomorrow, not a single one of the cattle or the people this fellow owns shall remain alive." This came true, as Patrick said <note>original: 'ut dixit Patricius'</note>:—

> "Becan here and Becan there: let his fasts be few; as long as the sun travels clockwise, let Becan bring no joy to his people."

Then the earth swallowed up Becan along with his people—all his wealth, both animal and human, at once—and Fulartach, son of Fergus, said: "Holy cleric, this night's lodging and entertainment—"

"The agreement I offer you: twenty-nine cows which I have used until now to support my soldiers <note>original: "kerne"; Gaelic foot soldiers</note> while they were marauding as outlaws." Patrick said: "I grant the chief's power to you from midday tomorrow, and to your descendants after you, as long as you do not oppose the Church." Thus was Becan buried in the earth, and Patrick committed the governance of the area to Fulartach.

Next, Patrick asked Caeilte how many brothers Finn had, and he answered: "He had two brothers: Fithal and Dithran.

-> "On this point regarding the three sons of Cumall, our historians <note>original: "antiquaries"</note> are unclear, but I can clarify it: they were Finn, Dithran of the feasts, and Fithal of the bards." <-

"Whose son was Mac Lugach—the one I asked you about last night?" Patrick asked. Caeilte answered: "For another person that would be a difficult question, but not for me. He was the son of Finn's son, Daire Derg.

"As soon as the boy was born, he was placed in Finn's arms <note>original: "bosom"</note>. Finn then placed him in the care of Duban's daughter, Muing-finn. She was Finn's wife, and she had raised eight hundred members of the Fianna <note>The Fianna were semi-independent bands of warriors in Irish mythology.</note> who now carry shields and weapons. She nurtured the boy until he was twelve years old. Then she gave him a full set of arms and armor, and he traveled until he reached *Carraic Conluain* and the mountain of *Smól mac Edlecair*, which today is called *Sliabh Bladhma* <note>modern: Slieve Bloom Mountains</note>, where Finn and the Fianna were located. 

He entered the presence of Finn the chief, who gave him a very kind welcome. The lad pledged his service and loyalty, shook hands with Finn, and remained with the Fianna for a year. However, during that time he showed great laziness. Under that youngster's leadership, no more than nine of the Fianna had managed to kill even a boar or a deer. In addition to this, he used to beat both his hunting dogs and his servants.

"Then the Fianna traveled to *Ros in Fhéinneda* <note>meaning: 'The Warrior's Point'</note> on the edge of the great *Loch Léin* in the south <note>modern: Killarney</note>. When the three battalions of the Fianna had arrived there, they brought a complaint against Mac Lugach before Finn, saying: 'Choose now whether you wish to keep us, or Mac Lugach alone.'

"Then Mac Lugach was brought to speak with Finn, who asked him: 'Now then, Mac Lugach, what harm have you done...'"

# The Colloquy

<margin>Finn's advice to Mac Lugach</margin>
"...done to the Fianna <note>original: "Fianna"; the legendary band of warriors led by Finn mac Cumhaill</note>, seeing that one and all they have a spite against you?" 

"I affirm upon my word," he said, "that I do not know their reason; unless indeed it is because they are averse to my practice of athletic feats and spear-casting among them."

To Mac Lugach then the chief <note>Finn mac Cumhaill</note> gave counsel, and his counsel had great virtue in it, and remained lastingly with Mac Lugach; and Finn said:

"Mac Lugach! If armed service is your design, be quiet in a great man’s household, but be formidable in the rugged mountain pass. Do not beat your hound without a fault of its own; do not bring a charge against your wife until you ascertain her guilt; in battle, do not meddle with a buffoon, for, O Mac Lugach, he is but a fool. Do not censure anyone if he is of grave repute; do not stand up to take part in a brawl; neither have anything at all to do with either a madman or a wicked one. 

Show two-thirds of your gentleness to women and to those who creep on the floor <note>i.e., little children</note>, likewise to men of art who compose the poems <note>original: "duans"</note>; and do not be violent toward the common people. With your close friends, and those who are of your counsel, do not hasten to be the first into bed; shun perverse alliances and all that is prohibited; do not yield your reverence to everyone. 

Do not utter swaggering speech, nor say that you will not provide what is right; for it is a shameful thing to speak too stiffly unless it is feasible to carry out your words. So long as you exist in the universe, do not forsake your lord; neither for gold nor for any other valuable in the earthly world abandon your guarantee <note>original: "guarantee"; referring to one who has placed himself under your protection</note>. 

To a chief, do not utter strenuous criticism of his people; for it is not a gentleman’s occupation to abuse a great lord’s people to their chief. Do not be a continually tattling tale-bearer, nor a false one; do not be loquacious, nor rashly censorious; no matter how many chivalrous qualities you possess, do not let the assemblies <note>original: "Irachts"; tribal gatherings or districts</note> be hostilely inclined toward you. 

Do not be a frequenter of the drinking-house, nor given to mocking an elderly man; the conduct you hear recommended, that is what is appropriate <note>original: "τὸ πρέπον"</note>: do not meddle with a man of mean estate. Do not make a habit of refusing to share your food, and do not have any penurious person as a close friend; do not force yourself upon a chief, nor give a chief lord occasion to speak ill of you. Stick to your clothing, hold fast to your armor, until the stern fight with its glittering weapons is well ended; never renounce your luck, yet follow after gentleness, Mac Lugach!"

"Success and benediction!" said Patrick. "It is a good story that you have told us there; and where is Brogan the scribe?" 

Brogan answered, "Here, holy Cleric." 

"Let that tale be written by you"; and Brogan performed the task on the spot.

Then Patrick questioned Caeilte: "Did you have musicians among the Fianna?" 

And he answered, "We did indeed, the finest musician that was in either Ireland or Scotland." 

"What was his name?" 

"*Cnú dheireoil*" <note>meaning "diminutive nut"</note>. 

"Where was he found?" 

"Between the Harps of Cliu <note>original: "crota cliach"</note> and the Mound of the Fair-Haired Women <note>original: "sídh na mban fionn"; anglicized as Slievenaman</note>..."

The trouble with him was this: the other musicians of the *Tuatha Dé Danann* <note>original: "tuath dé Danann"; the mythological race of god-like beings in Irish folklore</note> had grown jealous of him.

"On the day in question, Finn went to Slievenaman <note>original: "Sliabh na mBan"; the Mountain of the Women in County Tipperary</note> with the intention of hunting, and there he sat upon a certain turf-built grave. The chief, looking around, saw a tiny man close to him upon the green mound who played and performed upon his harp. He had long, light-yellow hair reaching down to his very loins. As soon as he noticed Finn, he came to him; and since the chief was the first man he had encountered since emerging from the *sidhe* <note>original: "sídh"; a fairy mound or the spirit world</note>, he laid his hand in Finn's. > "It was a dwarf that stalwart Finn obtained; such was the excellence of his memory that he learned by heart everything he happened to hear in both the east and the west. *Cnú Deireoil* <note>literally "Tiny Nut"</note> was the man's name; he was not unknown in Ireland. Beloved was the wee urchin who was so expert of speech, whose nickname was *Cnú Deireoil*. I will tell you how Finn found the dwarf: it was a fortunate child that was found then, for he was the only son of Lugh mac Eithlenn <note>A major deity in Irish mythology associated with skill and light</note>.

# The Colloquy

The chief <note>Finn mac Cumhaill</note> said that he would bestow both gold and silver on anyone in Ireland who could discover such a thing: a woman who was his dwarf’s counterpart. *Scí mac Eoghain*—a warrior with the nature of a lion—spoke up: "I will name a place (and my story is full of good fortune) where there is one who shall match him exactly." 

"Take my blessing, and return to your home, O son of Eoghan from Munster! But first, for the sake of friendship, tell us the country where such a person is reported to be." 

"O Finn, the hardy and triumphant, make your way to *Tech Duinn* <note>original: 'Donn's House'; a legendary dwelling of the dead or a fairy mound in Munster</note> in Munster. There is a woman there who will fit your purpose, whose name is *Bláthnait* <note>meaning 'Little Flower'</note>." 

In all haste then, we and the chief of the Fianna—who was skilled in the use of all bladed weapons—took our journey to "Donn’s House" to seek the woman; a journey that greatly raised our spirits. We found Bláthnait within the *sídh* <note>original: 'sídh'; a fairy mound or hollow hill</note> and truly brought her away. Then, in the great house yonder, Bláthnait and *Cnú Deireoil* <note>meaning 'Little Nut'; Finn's beloved dwarf musician</note> slept as man and wife. Every man of us among the Fianna gave an ounce of gold as a dowry for the blameless woman who was given to the dwarf. 

The man’s stature was four fists high, I say, and his smooth white harp was three fists; the wife was taller than the husband, and they made a lovely, white-handed couple! The wife possessed all the mysteries of the embroidering art, with the skill to work both silver and gold. The man’s gift—and it was a stupendous one—was to delight the crowds of the whole world at once with his minstrelsy. Among the Fianna, there was not a queen, a leader, nor a wise chief who did not give their love and various precious gifts to the couple, who were so childlike in size. 

Whenever hard, foul weather came upon the Fianna, Finn—a man of kingly mind—would keep them both under his mantle: Bláthnait and the little man. When good fortune was coming to the Fianna, Bláthnait would wisely reveal it; and when evil awaited them, the dwarf would not hide it from them. There is no melody on Earth that a man’s soul might desire that did not seem insignificant in the banquet hall compared to the music *Cnú Deireoil* used to make. These were the three best windfalls that Finn, the most generous chief of the Fianna, ever received: his faultless deerhounds, Bran and Sceolan, and Bláthnait together with the dwarf.

After this, it was not long before they saw seven tall young men coming toward them. Patrick asked, "Where do you come from, young men, and who are you?" 

"We have come from *Eoghan Lethderg*, son of Angus and king of both provinces of Munster, to fetch you, holy Patrick." 

The saint said, "We will indeed go there; for wherever gifts and support may be found, it is a duty to accept them." 

"And what shall we nine warriors do?" asked Caeilte. 

"You have a welcome to stay with me for a month, a quarter, and a year," Patrick replied.

Then Patrick set out, and the path he took was into Fee-guile; into Drumcree, which is now called "Kildare"; across the stream <note>original: 'sruithlinn'</note> in Durrow, and over the river Barrow; and over the causeway <note>original: 'tóchar'</note>...

Then his tent was unfurled over Patrick; the king of Munster came with the chief men of his people and laid his head in Patrick's bosom <note>A gesture of submission and seeking protection</note>, and made obeisance to him. For a week the saint remained there: raising the dead, healing those who had diseases and infirmities, and relieving every other affliction besides.

Patrick was granted the right to decide his own reward; after which Eoghan went his way to *Rosach na Rígh*: to his own stronghold, and the nobles of Munster sought their own various forts and good towns.

Patrick said: "Well now, Caeilte, why was the name *Fionntulach* [meaning 'white hill'] given to this eminence on which we stand?" 

"I will tell you the truth of it," answered Caeilte: "it was from here that we, the three battalions of the Fianna, marched to fight the Battle of Ventry. Our spears had been brought to us here, along with charmed willow-wands <term>withies</term> for our spear-shafts. Finn surveyed the hill around him and said: 'The hill is white <note>likely referring to the white appearance of the peeled willow sticks</note>; what better name then could it have than *Fionntulach*?'"

We used to gain some eminence in a level land, and there we would have beautiful blackberries, haws of the hawthorn, and nuts from the hazels of Kintyre. We ate tender twigs of the thorny bramble-bush and sprigs of the beneficial gentian; and every Beltane <note>The Gaelic May Day festival marking the beginning of summer.</note> we used to consume both the smooth shoots and the heads of the watercress. Birds from trackless oaken woods would find their way into the Fianna's <note>The legendary band of warriors led by Finn mac Cumhaill.</note> cooking pit; we had multi-colored squirrels from Berramain and variegated nests from mountain pinnacles. 

We caught rapid salmon out of *Linnmhuine*, eels from the noble Shannon, woodcocks from Fidhrinn, and otters out of the hidden places of the River Deel. We took fish of the briny sea from the coasts of Buie and Beare; *medhbán* <note>A type of edible seaweed or sea-produce.</note> from bright *Fáide*, and *duilesc* <note>Dulse, a red edible seaweed.</note> from the coves of Cape Clear. To swim the lake-forming *Loingsech* was a frequent habit of Mac Lugach; upon your far side, O hill, we used to gather as a great host. I and the renowned Ossian used to embark in currachs <note>Traditional Irish boats made of a wooden frame covered in hide or canvas.</note>; as I frequented its waves and its neighboring hills, I endured the hardships of the green sea.

"From this spot also, as I mentioned before, we marched to fight the Battle of Ventry. As we did so, we saw a young man of Finn's people approaching us from another direction: the valiant and hundred-slaying Cael, grandson of Nemhnainn. 

'Where have you come from, Cael?' Finn asked. 

'Out of the perilous *brugh* <note>A fairy mound or palace.</note> to the north.' 

'What did you seek there?' 

'To speak with Muirenn, daughter of Derg, who was my own nurse.' 

'What was the reason for that?' 

'It was because of a fairy sweetheart and a splendid match revealed to me in a dream: *Créidhe*, daughter of Cairbre, surnamed "Whiteskin," the King of Kerry.' 

Finn said, 'Do you know, Cael, that of all the women in Ireland, she is the greatest deceiver? There are few costly things she has not coaxed away to her own mansion and grand dwelling.' 

Cael replied, 'And do you know the condition she requires of all who would woo her?' 

'I know it,' Finn answered. 'She will accept no one unless he has enough artistic or poetic skill to compose a *duan* <note>A poem or song.</note> for her, giving a full description of her *cuachs* <note>Drinking bowls or quaichs.</note>, her horns, her cups, her *ians* <note>Large vessels or vats.</note>, and all her other fine containers, along with a description of her various vast palaces.' 

'I have all of that ready,' Cael said, 'given to me by Muirenn, daughter of Derg, my nurse.'

"Then, for the time being, we set aside the battle. We traveled over regions of hills, rocks, and *tulachs* <note>Small hills or mounds.</note> until we came to Loch Cuire in the west of Ireland. We reached the door of the *sídh* <note>A fairy dwelling.</note>, and with the shafts of our long, gold-socketed spears, we performed the *dórd fiansa* <note>The "warrior's buzz" or "cry of the Fianna," a rhythmic, low-toned chant.</note>. Yellow-haired girls of marriageable age appeared on the balconies of the bowers; and Créidhe, accom—

Accompanied by one hundred and fifty women, she came out to speak with us. The Fian-chief <note>The leader of the Fianna, likely Finn mac Cumhaill</note> said to her, "We have come to choose you and to woo you." The lady asked who it was that sought to court her. "It is Cael the valiant," he replied, "the slayer of hundreds, grandson of Nemhnann and son of the King of Leinster in the east!" She said, "We have heard reports of him, although we have never seen him. But does he have my *duan* <note>original: *duan*; a poem or song of praise</note> for me?" Cael answered, "I do," then rose and sang his poem:

> "I have a journey in hand on a Friday—if I go then, I am a true guest—to Credhe’s mansion against the mountain’s breast in the northeast; the effort is no small one. It is appointed for me to go there to Credhe, at the Paps of Anann <note>Two mountains in County Kerry, Ireland, named for the goddess Anu</note>; and there I must remain, facing many challenges, for four days and half a week. 
>
> Pleasant is the house in which she lives: filled with men, boys, and women; with both magicians and minstrels; with both cup-bearer and door-keeper; with both the horse-keeper who never shirked his duty and the steward who distributes the meat. The command over all of them belongs to fair Credhe of the yellow hair. 
>
> What with the coverlets and the down bedding, my lot in her *dún* <note>original: *dún*; a fortified residence or castle</note> will be a pleasant one. It has been heard of old that, should Credhe only wish it, my journey would be a lucky one for me. 
>
> She has a bowl from which the juice of berries flows, which she uses to darken her eyebrows. She has crystal vats of fermenting grains, exquisite cups, and goblets. The color of her castle is like that of lime; coverlets and rushes for the beds abound there. Silk is found among them, and many a blue mantle; among them are red gold and polished drinking-horns. 
>
> Her bower by the lake of *Cuire* is made of silver and yellow gold. Its ridged thatch is laid without any defect, made of the crimson-red wings of ruddy birds. The two green-hued door-posts which you see—their door has no deformity. Silver taken as spoil from the slain (it is of ancient renown) was the beam that provided its lintel. 
>
> Credhe’s chair upon your left as you enter becomes more and more delightful the longer one looks at it; it has an overlay of gold from the Alps <note>original: *Elpa*; likely referring to the Alps or a distant, exotic source of gold</note> and stands at the foot of her delicate bed. A glittering bed is laid out that overlooks the chair; it was made by Tuile in the east, out of yellow gold and precious stones. 
>
> There is yet another bed on your right hand, wrought unerringly of gold and silver, with tent-like curtains that look like the foxglove’s flower, running upon slender copper rods. As for the household in her house, their lives have fallen in pleasant places above all others; their mantles are neither faded nor worn thin, and their thick hair is curly and fair in color. 
>
> Even wounded men losing heavy streams of blood would fall asleep to the warbling of the fairy birds on the radiant eaves of her bower. If I should have reason to be grateful to this woman—to Credhe, for whom the cuckoo calls—her praises shall live on in even more numerous songs, if she only rewards the loving service done for her in composing this. 
>
> If it pleases the daughter of Cairbre, she will not force me to wait or postpone my suit; but may she rather say to me here and now: 'Your journey is most welcome to me.' There are a hundred feet in Credhe’s house from one corner..."

...until you reach another; and the noble door is twenty fully measured feet in width. Her roof is thatched with the wings of blue and yellow birds; her front parapet stands by a well made of crystal and carbuncle gems. Around every bed there are four posts of gold and silver cunningly joined together; in the head of each post is a crystal gem: they make for a pleasant sight. There is a vat of princely bronze from which runs the juice of merry malt <note>original: "juice of merry malt" refers to ale or beer</note>; over the vat stands an apple tree with a multitude of heavy fruits. When Credhe’s horn is filled with the vat’s potent mead, four apples fall into the horn at once with great precision. Those four attendants mentioned above set about serving the mead: to the four people sitting there, they hand a drink and an apple each. She who owns all these things in their entirety—namely Credhe from the triple-pinnacled <term>tulachs</term> <note>Irish: hills or mounds</note>—has excelled all the women of Ireland by a spear-cast’s length. Here is a poem for her—no poorly formed bride-gift—no <term>epithalamium</term> <note>A song or poem in honor of a bride and bridegroom</note> rashly or carelessly made! Here on the spot, I present it to the lovely Credhe; may my journey have been a welcome one in her eyes!”

Then that couple was bedded, and the <term>Fianna</term> <note>The legendary bands of warriors led by Finn mac Cumhaill</note> remained there for seven days. They spent the time drinking and in total enjoyment, lacking nothing in the way of food, liquor, or any good thing, were it not for one care that oppressed Finn: the presence of the <term>allmarachs</term> <note>Irish: foreigners or invaders</note> at Ventry. Then the woman presented to each warrior individually a special and sufficient suit of battle-armor, and we took our leave of each other.

“Let the woman come with us,” Finn said, “so that we may learn which of us shall meet with good or ill fortune in this current business.” The woman brought a vast number of cattle with her to provide for the sick and wounded; and it was she who fed them all with dairy products and new milk for as long as the battle was fought. It was also in her house that the invalids and the sick of the Fianna stayed. And just as the woman surpassed the women of the Fianna in the giving of jewels and treasure, so too did her husband, Cael, outstrip the three battalions of the Fianna in valor and skill at arms during that battle. 

Truly, a great calamity occurred on the last day of the battle: namely, the drowning of Cael. There were other creatures of the animal kind that had lives of equal length to his—that is, they perished at the same time. When he drowned, the outer swell of the sea washed him ashore. The women and the nobles of the Fianna came to find him; they raised him up and carried him to the southern shore (that is, to the south of Ventry), so that the shore has been called <term>trágh Chaeil</term> or “Cael’s Strand” ever since, and his burial place <term>fert Chaeil</term> or “Cael’s Grave.”

# The Colloquy

“The woman came and stretched herself by his side; she raised a loud, wailing cry and mourned greatly: ‘Why should I not,’ she said, ‘die of grief for my mate, when even the restless wild creatures die of sorrow for him!’ Then Credhe said:

> “The harbor roars, and oh, the harbor roars over the rushing current of the *Point of the Two Ships* <note>original: rinn dá bharc</note>! The drowning of the warrior of the *Lake of the Two Dogs* <note>original: loch dá chonn</note> is what the wave laments as it strikes the shore. Melodious is the crane, and oh, melodious is the crane in the marshlands of the *Ridge of the Two Strong Men* <note>original: druim dá thrén</note>! It is she who cannot keep her brood alive: the fox <note>literally: "the wild dog of two colors"</note> is intent upon her nestlings. A sorrowful note, and oh, a sorrowful note, is that which the thrush in Drumqueen emits! But no more cheerful is the wail that the blackbird makes in Letterlee. A sorrowful sound, and oh, a sorrowful sound, is that which the deer utters in Drumdaleish! Dead lies the doe of the *Ridge of Silenn* <note>original: druim silenn</note>; the mighty stag bellows after her.
>
> Bitter suffering to me, and oh, suffering bitter, is the hero’s death—the death of him who used to lie beside me! That the son of the woman from the *Oak-grove of the Two Thickets* <note>original: doire dá dhos</note> should now have only a bundle of reeds beneath his head! Bitter suffering to me is Cael, and oh, Cael is a bitter suffering, for he lies by my side in the form of a dead man! That the wave should have swept over his white body—that is what has driven me to distraction, so great was his beauty. A dismal roar, and oh, a dismal roar, is that which the shore’s surf makes upon the beach! Seeing that the sea has drowned that handsome, noble man, it is a grief to me that Cael ever sought to face it.
>
> A sorrowful booming, and oh, a boom of sorrow, is that which the wave makes upon the northern beach! It beats against the polished rock, lamenting for Cael now that he is gone. A sorrowful fight, and oh, a fight of sorrow, is that which the wave wages with the southern shore! As for me, my time has come to an end; it is plain to see that my beauty is withered by this grief. A sorrowful melody, and oh, a melody of sorrow, is that which the heavy surge of Tullachleish emits! As for me: the calamity that has fallen upon me has shattered me; for me, happiness exists no more. Since the son of Crimthann is now drowned, there is no one left in existence for me to love; many a chief fell by his hand, and in battle, his shield never cried out in retreat!”

“Then the young woman stretched herself out by Cael’s side and died of grief because he was gone. They were both buried there in the same grave; and it was I myself who raised the stone that stands over their resting place, which is why it is called ‘the tomb of Cael and of Credhe.’”

“Success and blessings to you, Caeilte!” Patrick said. “It is a good story you have told. Where is Brogan the scribe?” “I am here,” Brogan replied. “Let everything that Caeilte has spoken be written down by you.” And so it was written down.

They were not there long before they saw a strong, impressive body of men approaching them, surrounded by a defensive wall of...

# The Colloquy

<margin>A description of the warriors' equipment.</margin>
They arrived with shields locked together, carrying at their shoulders a virtual forest of lofty spears with gold sockets. Some of them entered the tent where Patrick was staying. Their leader laid his head in the saint's lap while the others knelt before him in respect. Patrick asked, "Who are you, young man?" He answered, "I am Bran, son of Derg, King of Munster." Patrick continued, "Why have you come here?" "It is the art and discipline of the Fianna <note>original: "Fianry"; the lifestyle and military training of the ancient Irish warrior bands</note> that I am eager to learn, holy cleric. I have heard that in your company is a warrior of Finn’s people, and I desire to study the *war-cry of the Fianna* <note>original: "dord fiansa"; a rhythmic, low-toned musical chant or war-cry used by the Fianna</note> with him."

"Caeilte, my soul, you hear that," said Patrick. "I hear it," Caeilte replied. "Now then, Bran, how do you usually manage a hunt?" Bran answered, "We surround some hill <note>original: "tulach"</note>, cairn, or mountain woods rising from a plain, and we pursue the game there for the entire day. Sometimes we kill a deer; other times he escapes us." In Patrick’s presence, Caeilte wept then, tearfully and in such sadness that his very breast and chest were wet.

Then Patrick and Caeilte, along with their entire company, went up to *Cenn Febhrat* <note>a mountain pass in the Ballyhoura Mountains</note> of Slieveriach. The layout of the particular spot they reached was this: there were three glens around the mountain and a lake between them called *Loch Bó* ("Lake of the Cow"). The mountain itself was named *Osmetal*. 

Caeilte said: "West of the lake is *Cnoc na Haeire*, and *Finninis* is the name of the easternmost hill. But the name of this hill is *Cnoc Máine*. Here lived a famous, cunning stag called *Liath na dTrí mBenn*, or 'The Grey One of the Three Antlers.' For twenty-seven years, he had eluded the Fianna, both men and deerhounds alike. Eventually, a warrior of the Fianna killed him—and I am that warrior."

Caeilte rose then. He stationed his people to the east, west, south, and north of the lake. Patrick sat down, which is why that place in *Cenn Febhrat* of Slieveriach is called *Suidhe Pátraic*, or 'Patrick’s Seat.' Then Caeilte raised his waving signal for the chase and for the hunt in the manner of the Fianna. He let out three mighty and terrifying whoops. Because of this, there was not a single free-roaming stag nearby—whether on the plain, the moor, the mountain, or in the woods—that did not come running at headlong speed. To cool themselves after their race, they all plunged into the water.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Colloquy of the Ancients, where the Fenian hero Caeilte travels with Saint Patrick, blending Irish mythology with Christian conversion stories.</meta>

# The Colloquy

Before the hunters' faces, the animals fled into the great Lake of the Cow <note>original: *loch bó*</note>. At that rushing noise and mighty resonance, horror, fear, and apprehension seized the men. I speak of the wild stags, the frenzied roe deer, and the heavy-sided boars; they were so exhausted by the length of the chase and the distress of their breathing that they nearly all perished on the spot. The huntsmen spread themselves around the lake, and not a single beast from the quarry escaped alive. They divided the fruits of the chase, with eight hundred head of game to be apportioned. Benignus <note>A disciple of Saint Patrick</note> said, "Let a tithe <note>A tenth part</note> of the hunt be given to us." But Bran mac Derg was not at all pleased to share what had fallen to his own portion—that is, the proceeds of the hunt he had started and his men had carried out.

At this, an inward disorder <note>likely a digestive ailment or flux</note> seized the son of the King of Munster, who cried out, "Holy cleric, lay your hand on this!" Caeilte said, "By my word, he shall not go to help you until you pay the fee." Bran asked, "What fee?" Caeilte replied, "Since the ailment is in your stomach, let the belly of every cow, every swine, and every sheep slaughtered in your country be yielded by you to Patrick for the Church’s use forever." Bran said, "I will grant that, and my son shall do so after me." From that time forward, this became a continuous practice throughout all Ireland. Then Patrick placed his hand on Bran mac Derg’s stomach, and he was healed instantly.

"We must be going now," Caeilte said. Patrick asked, "And which way is that?" Caeilte replied, "I remember, saintly Patrick, that for fear of the *Tuatha Dé Danann* <note>The 'People of the Goddess Danu,' a supernatural race in Irish mythology</note>, no crowd or host dared to sit upon these three mounds <note>original: *tulachs*</note>."

->Caeilte sang:<-

> "Three mounds I bear in mind that do not age or fade away, over which the 'Grey One of Three Antlers' used to run from one border to the other. I also remember three churches that were once the strongholds of a good lord; in those days, no bell was heard within them, but rather the 'wizard's knot' <note>A reference to druidic or pagan magic</note> surrounded them. *Caeilte* is indeed my name; I have been a captain of the truthful *Fianna* <note>The legendary band of warriors led by Finn Mac Cumhaill</note>. When we had to cross the glen, we never halted. When Finn the Fian-chief was alive, he would not endure the sound of a flighty young buck with sprouting horns belling over his spacious camp. I and Flann, son of Failbhe, used to 'redden' <note>To draw blood or wound</note> many heroes of the men of Leinster; I affirm on my conscience that I remember many a battle."

# The Colloquy

And so the company, laden with the spoils of the hunt, departed.

With a look that Caeilte cast around the mountain on his left hand, he saw a fort—a beautiful town. He said, "On my conscience, we never knew a fort to exist over there; let us go toward the town."

They made their way to the dwelling accordingly, but it was a wonder to them that they saw neither a crowd nor a throng there. Instead, they found only nine female slaves and three male slaves. They entered a private chamber apart from the rest of the town, where they found two women weeping and mourning. Here, Caeilte and his men were fed and cared for; their traveling and wayfaring gear was taken from them, and Caeilte asked the women what fort this might be. 

"It belongs to the two sons of the Chief of Fermoy," they replied. "Their names are Lochan and Eoghan." 

"And why are you so gloomy and melancholy?" 

"We have good cause," they said. "We are two sisters married to two brothers. Our husbands have gone tonight to bring home other wives. Therefore, our time in this fort lasts only until our husbands return with their new brides."

With a glance that Caeilte threw around him into the inner part of the fort, he noticed a huge mass of stone. This stone had once belonged to Senach mac Maeilchró, a trusted warrior and one of Finn mac Cumall’s <note>Finn mac Cumall: The legendary leader of the Fianna; Caeilte’s former commander.</note> original people. This stone was significant because all the wages Finn had ever given to Senach—one hundred and fifty ounces of gold, one hundred and fifty ounces of silver, and one hundred and fifty ounces of white bronze <note>original: "findruine"; a precious alloy of copper and tin or silver used in ancient Irish metalwork.</note>—were sealed up tightly beneath it.

Caeilte then said to the women, "If I were to help and relieve you, and bring your husbands back to you, what payment would you give me?" 

They replied, "If we had anything in the whole world that would please you, we would give it to you." 

"Truly, you have such a thing," Caeilte said. "That vast lump of stone at the far side of the fort." 

"Alas that you should say so!" they cried. "For it took the multitude of the entire country to move it to where it sits, and setting it properly took all their effort. Yet you seem to think you can manage it alone!" 

"It is I who will be proven wrong," said Caeilte, "if I am not able to do it." 

"Then it is granted from us to you, and with our blessing," said the women.

<summary>Caeilte discovers a hidden fort where two sisters are about to be displaced by their husbands' new wives; he strikes a bargain to help them in exchange for a stone hiding a legendary Fenian treasure.</summary>

Then he came out of the town and took back his right hand's fill of special fairy herbs. These were known to him as having been used by the queens and noble ladies of the Fianna <note>The legendary band of warriors led by Finn Mac Cumhaill</note>. These he gave to the women, who washed in a bath made of those herbs. This compelled their own husbands to love them again, so much so that the new wives whom the men had brought home were dismissed and sent away. The great stone was handed over to Caeilte, and he said:

->“O stone of *the pass of the ford* <note>original: *belach átha*</note>...”<-

Caeilte remained in that place and was well cared for and served. Early the next morning he rose and gave the flagstone a wrench toward him, pulling it out of the earth. They traveled along until they reached the "fair hill" <note>original: *finntulach*</note>, which today is called Ardpatrick, where Patrick was staying. The saint questioned him: “Where were you last night, Caeilte?” and Caeilte told him the story from beginning to end.

They had not been there long when they noticed seven men approaching them. Patrick said: “Where have you come from, young men?” 

“Out of the province of Connacht to the north.” 

“What has set you in motion?” 

“We come from the nobles of Connacht to fetch you, holy cleric, to convert us—both men and women—to your Gospel.” 

Patrick then said: “It is not right for the Church to delay in spreading the word.”

Patrick and his followers set out and traveled from the south: through mid-Munster, past the "estuary of the Ulstermen" <note>original: *luimnech uladh*</note>, into the "wood of the litters" <note>original: *fidh na gcuan*</note> which is now called Cratloe; into the "mountain of the king's death" <note>original: *sliabh aidhid in rígh*</note>, into Slieve Aughty <note>original: *sliabh Echtge*</note>, or ‘the mountain of Echtge,’ daughter of Nuada Silver-arm; by the "post of Cepán in Aughty" <note>original: *cuaille Chepáin in Echtge*</note>, the place where Cepán mac Morna fell; past the "lake of the short-horn cow" <note>original: *loch na bó girre*</note> which is called Lough Graney <note>original: *loch Gréine*</note>, or ‘the lake of Grian,’ daughter of Finn; into the "speckled country" <note>original: *brecthír*</note>, which at this time is called Hy-Many <note>original: *tír Máine*</note>, that is, ‘the land of the O’Kellys’; past the "lake of the windy pool" <note>original: *loch linnghaeth*</note> which is called Lough Croine. 

There, Muiredach the Great, son of Finnachta and King of Connacht, was waiting for Patrick. The saint's tent was spread over himself and his clerics. The chiefs of the province of Connacht then arrived, made obeisance to Patrick, and bowed their heads to him in submission.

As for the saint, he came out of the tent and sat on a burial mound made of sods. Caeilte came with him and said: “It was here, holy Patrick, that Oscar <note>The son of Oisín and grandson of Finn Mac Cumhaill, known as the greatest warrior of the Fianna</note> fought his first battle.” 

Patrick asked: “What was the cause of it?”

> “Rise up, Oscar! Let it be known that you are of the true stock. Formidable as is the stature of the good men arrayed against you, nevertheless, relieve us of a hundred of their heroes! From the armed warrior who wounds you, win his shield and win his spears; win his mail—may it serve you—and let them not boast of taking your trophies! It is a great event for me, in the presence of witnesses, to devote my boy to battle at only nine years old! > “Oscar’s victorious first deeds were: the towering, haughty King of Ulster slain; the King of Leinster, without any doubt, and Connacht’s hardy king likewise. Aedh Donn, son of Fergus Finn—Ulster’s king with the deadly spear-point—Oscar killed at the same time by force of shield and hard sword. Baedan, son of Fernarb the Virulent, whom Leinster had as an impetuous king—though his daring was great—Oscar killed at that same moment. Oscar’s own fellow-student <note>original: "condisciple"</note>, who was handsome, kingly, gentle, and prudent—Linne, son of Lighne, a man of great deeds—Oscar slew by mistake. Niamh of the many-colored garments came to view the battle: the rout of the battle burst toward her, and the steadfast queen was killed. Patrick, you who possess the truth, I tell you that in this matter Oscar’s royal fury was prodigious, and his first exploits were not small.”

“Success and a blessing to you, Caeilte!” Patrick cried. “And where is Brogan? Let that tale be written down by you, so that it may provide entertainment for the leaders of the world’s final ages.” And Brogan wrote it down.

# The Colloquy

"Whose grave is this on the hill upon which we stand?" Patrick asked. "That is easily told," Caeilte answered: "It belongs to a warrior of the <term>Fianna</term><note>The legendary band of semi-independent warriors led by Finn Mac Cumhaill</note> of Ireland who met his death there: Airnelach, son of Admallan, the king of Leinster's son. A poet came here with a *poem*<note>original: "duan"</note> composed for him, and Airnelach said, 'It is well, poet; grant me a short grace period until I have my jewels and my treasures with me to reward you.' The poet replied, 'Truly, by my word, I will not; if I am not paid immediately, I will lampoon and satirize you this very day.' When the warrior heard that, he laid his face to the earth and never lifted his head again until he died of shame. The green-surfaced *mound*<note>original: "tulach"</note> was closed over him, his memorial stone was raised, and it is against that very stone that your back is resting now, holy Patrick." The saint said, "May he receive Heaven and release from torment from me, as a reward for his great sense of honor." In that very hour, the warrior's soul came out of pain and, in the form of a white dove, sat upon the pillar-stone above Patrick.

Patrick then inquired, "And who, Caeilte, is buried in the southern end of this mound?" "Salbhuide, son of Feidhlecar, King of Munster, who perished there while hunting a fairy deer. His party consisted of thirty deer-hounds, thirty servants, and thirty warriors, all of whom died with him; the mound was walled up over them." <note>original: "Ut dixit Caeilte" — As Caeilte said:</note>

> "In this southern end lies Salbhuide's son, a friend to the poets; fifty *clusters*<note>original: "conghlanns" — likely a measurement or specific grouping of rings/links</note> of white silver were not considered a small treasure for him."

Benignus<note>A disciple and successor of Saint Patrick</note> said, "We would very much like to see these precious things." "You shall have exactly that," Caeilte said. He opened the grave, which contained a quantity of rings and bracelets reaching the full depth of a spear-shaft.
<margin>✓</margin>
Benignus spoke again: "To the man from a moment ago, you granted Heaven for the sake of his honor; now, because of these valuables revealed to us, grant Heaven to this other warrior who owned them." Patrick said, "It shall be granted."

Then Patrick asked Caeilte, "What was it that brought the Fianna, as great as you all were, to nothing?" He answered, "The two battles we fought last: specifically the Battle of Gowra and the Battle of Ollarba. We marched three battalions strong to fight the Battle of Inverollarba, and except for six hundred of us, no one survived. Until that point, Finn's spirit had never complained about the losses of the Fianna in any battle or skirmish. But this time, he took notice of the loss of such great chiefs..."

“Success and benediction, Caeilte!” said Patrick.

Then Cainen, son of Failbhe, son of Fergus, son of Eoghan More, questioned Caeilte: “Where was Olioll Olom, son of Mogh Nuadat, slain?” and he answered that: “On the summit of *Sliabh Claire* <note>modern: Ballyhoura Mountains</note> to the southward he died, of an apoplexy brought on by grief; and Sabia, daughter of Conn, died in Tara of sorrow for Maccon, her well-beloved son.” Thus spoke Caeilte.

> “*Ath Tuisil* <note>original: áth tuisil</note> is the ford’s name; to all men this is a sign of the true cause: it was a fall that Connla of *Cnoc Den* caused worthy Fiacha Muillethan to make.”

At *Dun ar Sleibh* or ‘Fort on the Mountain’ he had a fort built, a good town, which was situated so that in its midst was a sparkling and translucent lake-well. Around the spring he had a great and royal house made; but immediately at its brink three huge pillar-stones were planted. There—with his head to the eastward and between the three columns of stone—the king’s bed was set, while out of a *cuach* <note>term: a shallow drinking cup or bowl</note> or else a bowl, a confidential warrior of his people splashed water on his head continually. There too he died, and in that fort was laid in a subterranean excavation—

To return to Muiredach son of Finnachta, the King of Connacht: he had a beloved son named Aedh. At this time, a hurling match <note>original: "goaling match"</note> was announced by the young men of Connacht. During the game, Aedh won six goals by himself. Afterward, as he sat down, a sudden fit of grave and fatal sickness took him, and he died there. This news was brought to his people and to his mother, Aeife, the daughter of the King of Ulster. The women of the province raised a great cry of woe because of the youth's death. His mother requested that he be laid in the care of the *Táilchenn* <note>A title for Saint Patrick, meaning "Adze-head," referring to his tonsure</note>—specifically in the care of him to whom God had granted all Ireland and the power to benefit everyone within it. But the King of Connacht said, "Such an action would be most reprehensible in my sight, unless it were also acceptable to the Saint himself."

Then, a message was sent to fetch Patrick from the tent where the King of Connacht and his attendants were staying. The dead youth was also there, covered with a fringed mantle and wearing a soft crimson hood. The boy's mother, his three school companions, and his sister all said they would die of grief for him. When Saint Patrick heard this, he felt compassion, and his heart went out to them.

A basin of pale gold filled with water was brought to the cleric. He blessed the water, and it was poured into an exquisite *cuach* <note>a traditional shallow drinking bowl or cup</note> of fine silver. The holy cleric went to the youth, raised the soft crimson hood, and poured three drops of the water into Aedh’s mouth. At the third drop, Aedh rose up healthy and whole; he drew his hand across his face and got out of bed. At this, the entire gathering was joyful and in good spirits, and they believed in God. They submitted themselves to Patrick <note>original: "laid their heads in Patrick's breast"</note> and granted him authority over them all, from the greatest to the least. They stayed there throughout that night. The next morning, they left the town and traveled together into *Gannmagh*, which is now called *Magh Finn*; then to *Tóchar an Bhanchuire*, which men now call *Tóchar Finn*; keeping to the right of *Ros na*...

# The Colloquy

<margin>The Wood of Fratricide</margin>
*Fingaile* <note>original: *fingaile*; meaning "kin-slaying" or "fratricide"</note>, which is now named *Ros Comáin* or "Roscommon." The reason it was once called *Ros na Fingaile* <note>The Wood of the Kin-slaying</note> was because the nine sons of Uar mac Idhas slew each other there. They passed *Ráth Ghlais*, which men now call *Ráth Brénainn* <note>Brendan’s Fort</note>. There, the King of Connacht’s tent was pitched. Patrick and Caeilte came and sat on a sodded mound that overlooked the outer limit of the fort. The King of Connacht and all his company joined them, sitting down beside Patrick and Caeilte.

Then Muiredach mac Finnachta questioned Caeilte: "Why is the name *Ráth Ghlais* <note>The Fort of Glas</note> applied to this place?" 

"I will tell you," Caeilte said. "It was Glas, son of Drecan, King of Lochlann <note>The Gaelic term for Scandinavia, particularly Norway</note>, who came with a force of twenty-five battalions to win the royal power of Ireland. The place where they arrived was the stone fort of *Damh Dilenn*, now called *Dún Rosarach*. At that particular time, Finn mac Cumall was in *Almha* <note>The Hill of Allen</note> in Leinster." 

Here, Muiredach asked Caeilte: "Why was that place named *Almha*?" 

Caeilte replied: "There was a warrior of the *Tuatha Dé Danann* <note>The "People of the Goddess Danu," a supernatural race in Irish mythology</note> who lived in the teeming, glittering *Brugh* <note>Newgrange/The Boyne mound</note>; his name was Bracan. He had a daughter who was a virgin named *Almha*. Cumall, son of Trénmór, took her as his wife. She died while bearing him a son, and this green-surfaced hill was closed over her. It is named after her now; whereas until then, it had been called *Tulach na Faircsena*, meaning 'the Look-out Hill.' 

Alternatively, it is said that *Almha* was the name of the man who owned it in the time of Nemed <note>A legendary early colonizer of Ireland</note>. Or yet again, it is said that Nuada the Magician built a fort and stronghold there, and from that fortress he produced an *almha*—meaning a 'herd of cattle'—from which the name *Almha* is derived." 

And Caeilte said:

> "Leinster’s *Almha*—the fortress of the Fianna—the town which the 
> most bountiful Finn made his resort: here follows, according to every 
> historian, the origin of the name. *Almha* was the name of the man 
> who possessed it with vigor and fame in Nemed’s time; upon that 
> green hill yonder, he died of a sudden and immediate plague. 

> There was a warrior of the Firbolgs <note>An ancient race of people who inhabited Ireland before the Tuatha Dé Danann</note> 
> who was no fool—Iuchna was the warrior’s name. Both east and west, 
> the fort was full of his cattle and his herds. His droves, driven by 
> thirst, went to a spring to drink water; so urgent was their thirst 
> that they all fought and left their horns behind them. From these 
> horns of the cattle (some white, some flecked with other colors) 
> which they left around the cold spring—from that, I say, we now 
> have the place-name *Adarca Bó Adbal Iuchna*, or 'The Horns of 
> Iuchna’s Mighty Cattle.' 

> Lofty Iuchna had five daughters: he was a warrior skilled, cheerful, 
> yet fierce when necessary; from..."

He provides multiple mythological explanations for the name Almha, involving the Tuatha Dé Danann, the Firbolgs, and ancient herds of cattle.</summary>

# The Colloquy

<margin>The origin of the name Almha.</margin>
It was from her that all the countries they occupied extended far and wide in the course of time. *Carmann* lived in *Carmann's* rough land, where bards stayed for a season; *Trega's* wife lived in his powerful house. . . . . . Liffey's golden-hued plain was the share of that skilled, fair, and tall young woman (as I believe this is no false tradition), and the fifth daughter was *Almha*, who was seated here. *Nuada* the wizard <note>original: "druid"</note> was a harsh man; he built a strong, high *fort* <note>original: "dún"</note> in *Almha*, using bright crystal for the stockade of his spacious fortress. The fort was pure white all over, as if it had been coated with all the lime in Ireland. It was named after the *almha* or "herd" that he brought from his mansion; from that, I say, the name of *Almha* sticks to it.

"You have told that tale well, Caeilte," said Muiredach son of Finnachta.

Caeilte resumed: "Where we were then—at the point where you interrupted me—was in this same *Almha* of Leinster. News of that invading fleet reached us there. The one who brought the news was *Spré aithinne*, or 'Firebrand-spark,' the daughter of *Mughna mucraesach* and the King of Ireland's female messenger <note>original: "she-runner"</note>. Finn summoned his own messenger to gather and muster the Fianna <note>The legendary band of semi-independent warriors</note> of both Ireland and Scotland. King Cormac, the grandson of Conn, gathered the tribes of Tara, the bands of Bregia, and the great general army of all Ireland. They came here to this place, twenty-five battalions strong. Lots were cast between the Fianna and the settled people to determine who should engage the *foreigners* <note>original: "allmarachs" or "over-sea men"</note>; it fell to the Fianna to open the battle. 

A fight was fought every day for a week. Fifteen hundred foreigners and Irishmen <note>original: "éirennachs"</note> were killed, and then the main battle took place. In that battle, Glas fell at the hands of Finn son of Cumall, and his seven sons were killed by the Fianna. We marched with Finn to that battle one hundred and fifty warriors strong, and each one of us killed fifty fighting men. Three of us from the Fianna entered the tent where Glas son of Drecan was; there we found nine columns of gold, the smallest of which was as thick as a yoke for three oxen. We hid these in this red moor north of the circular fort <note>original: "rath"</note>, and here Glas son of Drecan was buried. Therefore, this fort is called *Ráth Ghlais* <note>The Fort of Glas</note>."

Patrick exclaimed: "Victory and a blessing to you, Caeilte! That is a good story you have told us. Brogan, let this be written down." And Brogan wrote it. They stayed there for that night and rose early the next morning. They traveled to the *Arena of Fergus's Chariot* <note>original: "roe carpait Fergusa"</note>.

Which at this time is called *Iomaire Meic Chonrach*, that is, "Mac Conrach’s Ridge"; touching *Cnoc na Rígh*, that is, "Hill of the Kings," now named *Uarán nGaradh* or "Garadh’s Spring-well," where they halted and pitched camp. He blessed that rare hill with the beautiful sides and said: "This shall be the eighteenth burial ground that I shall hold most dear in Ireland." "The most unfortunate thing—the great objection to it—is that it has no water in its vicinity," said the King of Connacht.

Then Patrick rose and drew near to a jutting rock which he perceived just on the outskirts of the spot where they were. He thrust his staff into it so that it struck the ground and the bedrock underlying it; whereupon three jets of clear water burst out of the rock. Benignus <note>Saint Benignus of Armagh, a disciple of Patrick</note> cried: "Endow the well, holy Patrick!" 

"Protection for a certain time to everyone who shall drink its water," said Patrick. "Also, by the grace of the Creator, all of Ireland’s wells shall fail in the world’s final days, and all Ireland shall be comforted by this one well. Furthermore, if this water is administered three times to any man, there is no distress afflicting him that it will not relieve."

"Tell us a story, Caeilte," said Patrick. 

"I will tell you a story of a time when the Fianna <note>original: *Fianna*; the legendary band of warriors led by Finn mac Cumhaill</note> of Ireland, both man and dog, nearly perished upon this very hill where you now sit. It happened like this: Guaire Goll and Flaithes were Finn’s two bearers of the chess-board. A warrior came to this hill <note>original: *tulach*</note> to play a match against Guaire: Finn Bane, son of Bresal, King of Leinster. 

Guaire Goll said: 'I will play with you for a stake.' 

Finn Bane asked: 'What stake?' 

'Three ounces of gold from each of us.'

Finn Bane raised his hand and gave Guaire such a blow with his fist...

"At the end of twenty years, we came to *Coill Choiméta*—that is, the 'Wood of Safe Keeping'—in the land of the *Úi Tairrsigh* of Leinster, which is now called 'Drumcree.' The Fianna went out to hunt and left behind a warrior of their number to protect the women. His name was *Garadh mac Mórna*. His condition was this: the greater part of his life was behind him, and all his kinsmen had been slain. The women said to him, 'Come now, Garadh, do you have a mind to play chess with us?' 'By no means,' he answered. 'What does this mean?' the women asked again. Garadh began: 'One day when we were at *Tulach na Rígh*, or "the Hill of Kings," and at *Loch an Éin*, or "the Bird’s Loch," in the province of Connacht—' and so he told them the story which you have just heard, holy Patrick. One of the women then said, 'The very reason Garadh was left behind with us was to make a fire for us and to play chess with us, because he has lost his vigor and his skill at spear-throwing, and because he is in the state of old age.' But Garadh said, 'By my word, this is the talk of hostile women; no matter how long I remained in fellowship with the Fianna, they would never be true friends to me.'
