(As is common in an ancient legend) a situation arose in which nearly all of them were slaughtered because of a quarrel born from a certain match. Guaire, Finn’s *gilla* <note>original: gilla; a servant, attendant, or youth</note>, and Bresal’s son, Finn Bane, held a skillful match at the chess-board, from which a contention sprang. As a player, Finn Bane was better than Guaire from *Glas Bemann*; Finn Bane won four games, and Guaire won only a single one. 

Great anger now grew in Guaire against Bresal’s green-mantled son; he said evil things to him in earnest, all because of his opponent's fair and honorable play. Finn Bane’s shame was very great, and he quickly lifted his hand; thus, from Bresal’s winning son, a fist landed on Guaire’s mouth. 

The splendid Fianna <note>The legendary band of warriors led by Finn mac Cumhaill</note> rose up—the generous, the famous, the all-valorous. It was a vexation to them to have the Chief’s servant stricken for such a paltry cause. They rose up, I say, with one accord: Finn Bane’s Fianna and Finn mac Cumhaill’s; Caeilte’s Fianna and Conan’s, Ossian’s and Ferdoman’s. 

Then it was that Finn himself said: "See outside, my stalwart, powerful son, why the Fianna’s anger kindles—what may be the cause of their uprising." 

[But here a man of Finn’s comes in and cries:] "Guaire your servant, O Finn, a young man who was the bearer of your chess-board: it is for no valid cause that his slaughter by the son of Bresal has been carried out!"

"Let the son of Bresal be seized," said Finn, "and let no ransom-gift be accepted in his place; neither let Ossian, Dermot, nor Caeilte act as a protection for him in this matter."

Ossian spoke then: "By your hand, O glorious Finn—by your nobility and by your honor—the son of Bresal shall not be slain because he happened to be involved in a brawl. Father, O son of Cumhaill, stand fast by your wisdom! It is straight judgment that befits a prince, not blustering words of menace. If it were we who lacked self-restraint, it is from you that our correction should come. Submit your finger to your tooth of knowledge <note>A reference to Finn's supernatural ability to gain wisdom by sucking his thumb</note>; do not pass a rash judgment based on one-sided evidence. Let us take Faelchú, Fercrom’s son and the servant of heavy-haired Finn Bane; if Finn Bane has indeed slain Guaire, then let Faelchú be killed by your own hand."

We came away from the son of Cumhaill (the man of tender honor) to find Finn Bane; and so we brought Bresal’s son to the Chief of Ireland’s Fianna. The first Finn—Finn mac Cumhaill of *Almha*—then questioned the other Finn: why it was that he assaulted Guaire, who was now gone and passed away.

Finn Bane answered: "Finn! Guaire your servant, a fellow who carried your chess-board, came at early morning and defied me to play a single game. I then won four games against Guaire, son of Beobertach; but because this was an irritation to him—and through his anger—he 'scalded' me; he shouted vituperation at me. Because he inflicted such stiff insults on me in the presence of all Ireland’s Fianna—I was no servant, I was no *laech* <note>original: laech; a warrior</note>, and when the pinch came I was no *óglaech* <note>original: óglaech; a young warrior or soldier</note>—I lifted up my straight right arm (making no mistake about it) and dealt a fist across his mouth. I tell nothing but a truthful tale."

"A blessing on the arm that gave it to him," Ossian surely said. "Finn of the chiefs, the slaying of your servant is found to have been not without cause. Unless you readily forgive the blow, Fian-chief, it shall be compensated to you: you shall have a *screpal* <note>original: screpal; an ancient Irish unit of weight or currency, often a silver or gold coin</note> of gold from each man of us. It would be wrongful now if you persisted in refusing to listen to us. But if this proposal pleases you, perhaps it will serve to check your servants in their ill-behavior: Guaire, Coman, and active Saltran, who make a practice of railing at all Ireland’s Fianna. Guaire reviled Finn Bane; Coman has upbraided Glas; and more preposterous than anything that can be told is how the flippant Solam punished Ferdoman. If Finn Bane, son of Bresal from *Ráth Chró*, to this servant he..."

"...have given a blow. 'O Chief, owner of many polished drinking-horns, give a blow yourself to Mac Bresal’s servant <note>original: *gilla*; a servant, youth, or attendant</note>!' Patrick said: "Success and blessings to you, Caeilte! This is grand lore and knowledge you have spoken to us."

Then the whole company rose and moved on to the burial mound of Fraech, son of Feradach (known as *Carnfree* <note>original: *carn Fraeich*</note>), and Patrick climbed up onto the high ground. To which Caeilte answered: "The Fian-chief knew it, for he was a magician, a seer, and a prince. We all also understood there was a God because of a deadly event we witnessed one night." "And what was that event?" Patrick asked. 

"Cormac, son of Art, the King of Ireland, had a great household. There were ten score <note>200</note> sons of kings—every one of them the son of a king and a queen—and they used to stay at the 'Wood of the Kings' <note>original: *ros na rígh*</note> northeast of the 'Ford of the Boyne' <note>original: *áth na Bóinne*</note>." "What wood <note>original: *ros*</note> is that?" Patrick asked. "The 'Wood of Loss' <note>original: *Ros cailledh*; Caeilte explains there are a thousand of every kind of tree there</note>, and there these youths had a vast and royal mansion. However, their food was always served and brought to them from Tara. One night, they were there after feasting and enjoying themselves; their beds were prepared, and they stayed for the night.

"But when the chief steward of Tara (his name was Binne) came in the morning to speak with the King of Ireland’s son in the hostel <note>original: *bruidhen*</note>, the house was opened before him, and he found them all dead. From this, we understood that the True and most Glorious God existed: the One who has dominion and power over us all." Caeilte then said:—

# The Colloquy

> "Town of the kings—*ros Temrach* <note>original: 'Tara's wood'</note>—it is there that many a time a great household resided. Upon its slopes with their smooth turf, throngs of men and herds of horses gathered in great numbers. Two hundred <note>original: 'Ten score'</note> such stately sons of kings made up that honorable household; an equal number of women were there to provide for them. Thus, O noble and pure Patrick, this was no long-drawn destruction; for all together and at once they passed away—that entire company that lived in the one town."

> "Those two hundred men, and as many women, were buried in that *tulach* <note>term: a hill or mound</note>, and therefore from that time to the present its name is *cnoc an áir* or 'the hill of slaughter.' As for the wood in which they had lived, the earth swallowed up the entire *ros* <note>term: wood or promontory</note> before the people's very eyes; and by this miracle we came to understand the King of Heaven and of Earth."

> "Victory and blessings to you, *Caeilte*!" cried Patrick.

Then *Caeilte* said: "Holy Patrick, my soul, I believe that tomorrow it is time for me to depart." "And why do you go?" "To seek out the hills, bluffs, and mountains of every place where my comrades, my foster-brothers, and the Fian-chief <note>Refers to Fionn mac Cumhaill, leader of the Fianna</note> were with me; for I am weary of staying in one place." There they remained that night. The next day they all rose, and *Caeilte* laid his head in Patrick's bosom. The Saint said: "I promise you, that in whatever place God shall lay His hand upon you—whether indoors or outdoors—Heaven is granted to you."

Then Muiredach mac Finnachta, king of Connacht, went his way to exercise his royal rule and government. Patrick also went his way: to sow faith and piety, to banish devils and wizards out of Ireland, to raise up saints and the righteous, and to erect crosses, station-stones, and altars. He went also to overthrow idols and goblin-images, and the whole art of sorcery.

As for *Caeilte*: he traveled northwards to the wide plain of *lorg an Daghda* <note>original: 'the plains of Boyle'</note>; across *coirrléim na féinne* <note>original: 'the leap of the Fenians'</note>, which at this time is called *eas meic Néra* or 'the waterfall of Nera's son'; northwards still into *sliabh Seghsa* <note>original: 'the Curlieu mountains'</note>; into *berna na gcét* <note>original: 'the gap of the hundreds'</note>, now called *céis Chorainn* or 'Keshcorann,' and out upon the level lands of Corann.

They carried the venison with them and brought it along to *Eas Meic Modairn* or "the waterfall of Modarn’s son," now called *Eas Dara* or "Ballysodare"; into *Críoch an Chosnama*, which is called *Críoch Chairbre* or "the barony of Carbery"; past the *Rinn* or "point" of Ebha, daughter of Geibtine mac Morna—the place where a tidal wave drowned her. They skirted *Druim Derg*, now called *Druim Cliabh* or "Drumcliff," and *Áth an Chomraic* or "the fighting ford," now called *Áth an Daimh Ghlais* or "the grey stag’s ford." 

From there they held on to *Lecht na Muice* or "the swine-grave," where once the wild pig killed Duibhne’s grandson Dermot <note>Dermot mac Daimhne, a famous hero of the Fianna known for his tragic death during a boar hunt</note>; and to the top of the hill where *Leaba Dhiarmata*, "Dermot’s bed," is located. There Caeilte laid his weapons on the ground and lay down himself on his dear comrade’s grave and place of rest. He wept copious and very lamentable tears, so that both his breast and chest were wet, and he said: "Alas that my companion is gone from me!" 

<margin>✓</margin>
From midday until the end of the day’s waning they lingered there. "Friends," he said, "woe is me! With grief for Dermot and for his children, I could be content never to depart from this place where they recline!" Failbhe asked then: "How now, did Dermot have sons?" 

"He did indeed, and here are their names:

"The names of brown-haired Dermot’s sons by the daughter of Conn’s grandson Cormac: Finnchad, and Illann, and Uath; Selbach, Sercach, and Iruath.

"That 'grey stag’s ford' of which we spoke a while ago—it was there that Caeilte *Coscair Rígh* <note>original: 'Caeilte of the King's Trophies'</note> fought with Dithramach, son of Eoghan’s son the *Scál* <note>original: *Scál* or 'The Phantom/Giant'</note>, who was king of Munster and half-brother <gloss>that is, mother's son</gloss> to Finn." And Caeilte said:

"In the presence of the great and goodly host, they hardily fought a duel. In their wrath, they tore up the very trees upon the path over the grey stag’s ford. It was Caeilte who hurled his spear first, such was the perfection of his fine weapon-play; but no sooner had he released it from his hand than a well-aimed javelin struck him. He sheared the right hand and the left foot from the vehement Dithramach; but it was Dithramach's own head that stern Caeilte left upon the north side of Drumcliff."

From there they proceeded to *Coill na mBuidhen* or "wood of the companies," now called *Coill Muadnatan* or "Muadnait’s wood"; over the peak of Muiredach’s son Gulban, or "Benbulbin"; to *Garbhros* or "rough-grove," now *Daire na Damhraidhe*, "the deer-herd’s oak-grove." There they made a spacious hunting booth for cooking; they roofed it in with sedge that was green at the top and pale towards the bottom...

the roots, securing it with ties over all, and there the roasting and boiling <note>original: "brandering and seething"</note> of their meat is carried out by them. One of the men asked, "Is there water near us?" Caeilte answered, "Surely there is—Ossian's well." "It is a dark night," said the others. "It is not dark to me," said Caeilte, "for in Ireland's five great provinces there is not a spot where a <term>cuachful</term> <note>The amount held by a cuach, a traditional Irish cup or bowl.</note> can be obtained, whether from rock or river, but that I am at home there by both day and night." 

In one hand, therefore, he took a silver <term>cuach</term> <note>A shallow, two-handled drinking bowl.</note>, and in the other his thick-shafted, solid-socketed spears, and walked straight to a well. He heard the sound of water being lapped and troubled, and discovered a long-sided boar drinking there. He placed his finger into the thong of his well-balanced, riveted spear and threw a cast at the swine which killed it; then, with his <term>cuach</term> still in his hand, he carried the boar away upon his back.

<margin>✓</margin> They spent that night there, and on the morrow they traveled across the falls of Assaroe <note>A famous waterfall on the River Erne at Ballyshannon.</note> to the <term>sídh</term> <note>A fairy mound or subterranean dwelling of the Tuatha Dé Danann.</note> of Aedh at Assaroe. Upon their arrival, they noticed a young man waiting for them on the green-clad <term>tulach</term> <note>A hill or mound.</note>. He was wrapped in a fringed crimson mantle, fastened high on his breast with a silver brooch. He carried a white shield decorated with interlaced creatures in red gold. His hair was rolled into a ball at the back and covered with a golden <term>cuach</term> <note>Here referring to a bowl-shaped hair ornament.</note>. With a long chain of antique silver, he held two hunting hounds in a leash; he also bore mighty, heavy weapons that glinted blue. 

When Caeilte reached him, the young man gave him three loving and warm kisses and sat down beside him on a mound. "Warrior, who are you?" Caeilte asked. "I am Derg Dianscothach, son of Eoghan from the <term>tuatha</term> <note>Tribes or people.</note> of Usnach abroad, and I was your own foster-brother." "And how is your life with your mother's people: the <term>Tuatha Dé Danann</term> <note>The 'People of the Goddess Danu,' a supernatural race in Irish mythology.</note> in the <term>sídh</term> of Aedh?" 

The young man answered, "We lack for nothing in terms of food or clothing, and yet: Ligairne Licon, Semenn the Priest, and Beg—who was the <term>gilla</term> <note>A servant or attendant.</note> to the young horses—those three had the hardest lives of anyone in the Fianna <note>The legendary band of warriors led by Fionn mac Cumhaill.</note>, yet I would rather live their life than the one I lead in the <term>sídh</term>." 

"Though you hunt alone today," Caeilte said, "I have seen you escorted by a great company at the <term>comar na dtrí nuiscedh</term> <note>original: "the meeting of the three waters."</note> in the south, where the Suir, the Nore, and the Barrow rivers meet: fifteen hundred young men, fifteen hundred <term>gillas</term>, and fifteen hundred women." # The Colloquy

"Derg, your hunting party consists of only a few; you are separated from your Fianna <note>The Fianna were semi-independent warrior bands in Irish mythology, led by Finn mac Cumhaill.</note>, the companions of your chase. But are you well-versed in the various violent deaths they suffered?" 

"I am well-versed in all the places where they fell," Derg replied. "For though my gentle hound and I now dwell in the *sidh* <note>original: *sidh*; a fairy mound or the spirit world</note>, my mind is still fixed on the Fianna. There was never a time or place—whether east, north, south, or west—where my time passed more quickly than among them, no matter how few their number."

"Derg, my soul, it is well. Which of the Fianna lies in this sod-built burial mound where we now stand?" 

"It was you and I who buried him," said Derg. "It is only right, therefore, that I should know it." Then he said:

> "Cuinnscleo the servant, son of *Ainnscleo* . . ."

"Derg, against whom or what was that desperate and distressing race run?" 

"Against the black horse owned by *Dil mac dá creaca*," Derg answered:

> "A black horse *Dil mac dá creaca* had; in all the sports held at the rock overlooking *Loch Gur* <note>original: *loch Guir*; located at the Hill of Doon over Loch Gur in County Limerick</note>, he swept away all three prizes of the meeting."

"Caeilte," said Derg, "in whose house were we on the night in question?" 

"In the house of Cahir, son of Ailell. Having issued an invitation, he personally escorted Finn and the Fianna there. We stayed in Cahir’s house for three days and three nights, during which our company lacked nothing in meat, drink, or any kind of hospitality." 

"Did we give him anything in return?" Derg continued. 

"Finn gave him three hundred cows, as many mantles, and three hundred ounces of gold," Caeilte answered; and he said:

> "Three hundred cattle, three hundred mantles, and three hundred swords of solid temper, Finn gave to Cahir, son of Ailill, as payment for his hospitality."

Derg questioned him again: "Who was it that actually gave the horse to Finn? Was it *Dil mac dá creaca*, or was it Cahir, son of Ailill?" 

"It was Fiacha, called *Muillethan* or 'Broad-crown,' the son of Eoghan More," Caeilte answered, and said:

> "'Take this headlong black horse,' said Fiacha to the chief of the Fianna. 'Here also is my renowned sword, and I give another horse for your charioteer.' Finn went to the strand above *Berramhan* to test the black horse; and three times I outran him, for I was swifter than any mortal thing.

"The horse ran to the westernmost end of the strand and died there from the exhaustion of the gallop; for this reason, the strand <note>original: *tráigh*; meaning a beach or strand</note>..."

# The Colloquy

<margin>The Black Horse's Strand</margin>
"*An eich dhuibh*", or "the black horse’s strand," is the name of that shore which until now had been called *tráigh Bherramhain* or "the strand of Berramhan."

Caeilte said again: "It is the end of the day now; for the beautiful, shining clouds of day are gone, and the night’s dark shades have come to us." <insert>✓</insert>

Then, in order to tell Ilbhrec of Assaroe and Aedh *minbhrec* <note>Aedh of the small freckles</note>, son of the Daghda <note>The "Good God" and chief deity of the Tuatha Dé Danann</note>, everything about Caeilte, Derg *dianscothach* <note>Derg of the swift speech</note> went into the *sídh* <note>term: sídh; an Irish supernatural dwelling or fairy mound</note>. He recounted their entire conversation from the moment Caeilte first approached him until that very hour. "He must be brought into the *sídh*," they said, "for we have heard of his honor and of his prowess." Derg went to fetch him and brought Caeilte and his people back; inside the *sídh*, they were seated in their rightful and appropriate places. 

At that time, there was a great war between Lir of *sídh Finnachaidh* and Ilbhrec of Assaroe. A bird with an iron beak and a tail of fire used to come and perch at a golden window in the *sídh*. Every evening, it would shake itself until it knocked every sword from its pillow, every shield from its peg, and every spear from its rack, bringing them all down upon the heads of the people in the *sídh*. The residents used to hurl missiles at the bird, but every shot would instead strike the head of one of their own boys, women, or foster-children. 

On the night of Caeilte’s arrival, while their banquet hall was being prepared, the same bird arrived and caused the same destructive mischief. The people of the *sídh* began throwing at it, but they could not do anything to stop it. Caeilte asked, "How long has the bird been acting this way?" Derg answered, "For a year now, ever since we and the people of the other *sídh* went to war."

Then Caeilte reached his hand inside the rim of his shield and pulled out a copper rod. He threw it at the bird, knocking it down so that it fell onto the floor of the *sídh*. "Did anyone ever throw better than that?" asked Ilbhrec. Aedh *minbhrec* of Assaroe asked, "Was there anyone among the Fianna <note>term: Fianna; the legendary band of warriors led by Fionn mac Cumhaill</note> who was as good at throwing as you?" 

"I give my word," Caeilte answered, "that no one among them had any right to brag over another; for every man of them possessed a full and sufficient measure of martial strength and marksmanship, and I was the same." <insert>✓</insert>

# The Colloquy

At this, Ilbhrec reached up his hand and took down a sharp javelin with shining edges from his rack, which he placed in Caeilte’s hand, saying: “Caeilte, my soul, examine this spear and tell me which of the <term>Fianna</term><note>The Fianna were semi-independent bands of warriors in early Ireland, led most famously by Finn mac Cumall.</note> owned it.” Caeilte removed the protective shoe and the wraps from the javelin; there in its socket were thirty rivets of Arabian gold. . . . “That is the spear of Fiacha mac Congha,” Caeilte said, “the very weapon by which Finn, son of Cumall, first acquired the chief command of Ireland’s Fianna. It was originally brought out of the green-grassed <term>sidh</term><note>original: *sidh*; a fairy mound or dwelling of the supernatural Tuatha Dé Danann.</note> of Finnachadh.

“For it was <term>Aillén mac Midhna</term> of the <term>Tuatha Dé Danann</term><note>The 'People of the Goddess Danu,' a supernatural race in Irish mythology.</note> who used to come to Tara from the fairy mound of Finnachadh in the north. He would arrive carrying a musical <term>timpán</term><note>A stringed instrument, often translated as a lyre or lute.</note> in his hand, and whenever anyone heard its music, they would immediately fall into a deep sleep. Then, while everyone was thus lulled, Aillen would emit a blast of fire from his mouth. He came every year on the solemn day of <term>Samhain</term><note>The festival marking the beginning of winter (November 1st), a time when the veil between the physical and supernatural worlds was thinnest.</note>, played his instrument, and everyone would fall asleep to that fairy music. With his breath, he would blow up the flame and so, for a period of twenty-three years, he burned down Tara and all its property every single year.

“That was the same period when the Battle of Cnucha was fought, in which Cumall, son of Trenmor, was killed. He left behind a pregnant wife: Muirenn Smooth-hair, the daughter of Teigue mac Nuadat.

“With Cumall dead, the leadership of the Fianna was handed over to Goll mac Morna, who held it for ten years. In the meantime, a son had been born to Cumall, who was Finn; until he was ten years old, he was forced to live as a marauder and an outlaw. In his tenth year, the Feast of Tara was held by the king, Conn <term>of the Hundred Battles</term><note>original: *cédchathach*</note>. As all the people of Ireland drank and enjoyed themselves in the great <term>Mead-Hall</term><note>original: *Midchuart*; the great banqueting hall at the royal seat of Tara.</note>, they did not notice anything until a youth of striking and varied appearance arrived among them. He sat down in the presence of Conn of the Hundred Battles and Goll mac Morna, surrounded by the nobles of Ireland. Note that one of the sacred laws of the Feast of Tara was that for six weeks—the entire time men were occupied with the festival—no one was allowed to pursue a feud or a revenge-killing. The King of Ireland looked at—

“His horn of state was brought to the king then, and he put it into the lad’s hand. He inquired of him: ‘Whose boy is this?’ ‘I am Finn mac Cumall, son to the warrior that formerly had the command in chief of the Fianna <note>The Fianna were a legendary band of semi-independent warriors</note> and, King of Ireland, I am come to procure my friendship with you [that is, to be reconciled with you and to enter your service].’ Conn said: ‘Boy, you are a friend’s son and son of a man of trust.’ Then the lad rose and, toward the King of Ireland, made a pact of service and of fealty. Conn took him by one hand, placed him at the shoulder of [next to] Art mac Conn, and for a space and season they devoted themselves to drinking and to enjoying themselves.

“Then, with a smooth and polished drinking-horn that was in his hand, the King of Ireland stood up and said: ‘If, men of Ireland, I might find among you one that until the point of daybreak tomorrow should preserve Tara <note>The ancient seat of the High Kings of Ireland</note> so that she be not burnt by Aillen mac Midhna, I would bestow on him his rightful heritage, whether that be much or little.’ To this the men of Ireland listened mute and silent, however; for they knew that at the plaintive fairy strain and at the subtle, sweet-voiced notes produced by the wondrous elfin man that yearly used to burn Tara, even women in the pangs of labor and warriors gashed with wounds would fall to sleep.

“Finn rose now and said to the King of Ireland: ‘Who will go security on your behalf and be sureties to me for the fulfillment of this?’ Conn answered: ‘The provincial kings of Ireland, and Cithruadh with his magicians.’ They all of them enter into the bond, and Finn takes in hand to safeguard Tara with all her substance until tomorrow’s daybreak. Now in the King of Ireland’s retinue was one that had been a young man of trust to Finn’s father, Cumall: Fiacha mac Congha. ‘Listen now, my lad,’ he said, ‘suppose that I furnished you a certain spear of deadly property, and with which no crooked cast was ever made, what reward would you give me?’ ‘What fee do you demand of me?’ ‘Whatever prosperous result your right hand wins at any time, one-third of it to be mine; a third part moreover of your innermost confidence and private counsel [that is, I shall be one of your three most private counselors].’ ‘It shall pass for

"You shall have it," Finn said, and upon his word, he took the obligation upon himself. Then Fiacha instructed him: "Whenever you hear the fairy melody—the sweet-stringed *timpan* <note>original: "timpán"; an ancient Irish stringed instrument similar to a lyre or lute</note> and the sweet-breathing pipe—strip the casing from the javelin's head and press the weapon against your forehead or another part of your body. The horrific effect of the deadly weapon will prevent sleep from falling upon you."

Then, in the presence of all Ireland, Finn rose to guard Tara <note>The ancient seat of the High Kings of Ireland</note>. Unknown to the sons of Morna or anyone else in the mansion of Tara, the son of Congha gave him a shield and spear, and Finn made a complete circuit around the site. He was not long there before he heard a plaintive strain of music. He held the flat of the spear-head, with its dire energy, against his forehead. Aillen began to play his *timpan* until, as was his custom, he had lulled everyone else to sleep. Then, to consume Tara, he emitted a blast of fire from his mouth. But Finn opposed this with the crimson, fringed mantle he wore, so that instead of spreading horizontally to fulfill its mission, the flame fell straight down through the air. It carried the fourfold mantle twenty-six spans deep into the earth. Because of this, "Fire Hill" (*Ard na Teinedh*) is the name of that hill, and "Mantle Glen" (*Glenn an Bhruit*) is the name of the adjacent glen.

When Aillen, son of Midhna, realized that his magical trick was completely baffled, he returned toward the fairy mound of *Sídh Finn-achaidh* on the summit of *Sliabh Fuaid*. Finn followed him there. As Aillen passed through the door of the fairy mound, Finn put his finger into the spear's thong and delivered a well-calculated, successful throw. The spear entered the upper part of Aillen's back and, in the form of a great lump of black blood, drove his heart out through his mouth. Finn beheaded him and carried the head back to Tara, where he fixed it upon a pole of dark significance. It remained there until the sun rose high over the heights and river-mouths of the land. Then Aillen’s mother came to him and, after giving way to great grief, went to seek a healer for him:

> "A lamentable case, O most admirable woman-physician: by Fiacha son of Congha's spear—by the fatal mantle and by the pointed javelin—Aillen son of Midhna is slain! Alas, Aillen has fallen! Three jets have spurted from him: here is his heart's blood, along with the marrow of his back. Alas, Aillen has fallen! Fairy chief of *Benn Boirche*: now the numbing mists of death have come upon him—O *Boirche*, O woman-physician, it is a lamentable case! Alas, but he was joyous, and alas, but he was blithe, was Aillen son of..."

"Midhna of *Sliabh Fuaid* <note>modern: Slieve Fuad, in County Armagh</note>! Nine times he burnt up Tara, and to gain high fame was his constant endeavor."

"Then with their king, all Ireland came upon Tara's green where Finn was, and he said: 'King, you see the head of that man who used to burn Tara; his pipe also, his *timpan* <note>an ancient Irish stringed instrument</note>, and all his music. I believe, therefore, that Tara with all her goods is saved.'"

"At this, the place of assembly was filled by them, and a course of action was proposed. The plan finally adopted was to confer the chief command of the *Fianna* <note>the legendary bands of warriors led by Finn</note> of Ireland upon Finn. 'Now then, my soul, Goll mac Morna,' said Conn of the Hundred Battles, 'what is your choice: whether to leave Ireland, or to lay your hand in Finn's <note>an act of submission and alliance</note>?' Goll answered: 'I pledge my word that I will lay my hand in Finn's rather than take the alternative.'"

"By this time, the charms used to procure luck and a successful outcome had worked, and the chiefs of the *Fianna*, rising, struck their hands in Finn's. But first of all, Goll mac Morna struck his, so that the others of the *Fianna* would feel less shame in doing so. Finn continued in that command until he died; and where he met his death was at *Aill an Bhruic*, or 'the Badger's Cliff,' in *Luachair Degaidh* <note>modern: Slieve Lougher, County Kerry</note>. Now, the spear you put into my hand, Ilbhrec—with that spear was that beneficial deed done for Ireland. By its means, also, Finn always had his good fortune, and the spear's constant original name was *Birgha*, or "Spit-spear.'" Ilbhrec said: "Keep the spear with you, Caeilte, until we learn whether Lir will come to avenge his bird upon us."

Now their horns and their cups were raised, and they feasted and enjoyed recreation of mind and spirit. Ilbhrec said: "Now then, Caeilte, my soul, to whom will you assign command of the battle, should Lir come to avenge his bird on us?" 

"To the one to whom Finn used to commit his battle's chief command: to Derg *Dianscothach* <note>meaning 'Derg of the swift speech' or 'fierce flowers'</note> over there." 

Those of the *sídh* <note>the fairy mound or its inhabitants</note> questioned: "Do you take this upon yourself, Derg?" 

He replied: "I do, with its pleasure and with its pain." 

Thus they passed that night; and in the morning, it was not long before they heard the blowing of horns, the rumbling of chariots, the clashing of shields, and the general uproar of a great host that approached and surrounded the *sídh*. From the mound, some were dispatched to spy out how many they were; and it turned out that they were three valorous—

# The Colloquy

The battalions were of equal size. Aedh *Minbhrec* <note>Aedh "of the delicate freckles"</note> said: "It is a sore vexation to me, what will be done now: that we must violently perish and die, and our fairy palace <note>original: *brugh*</note> will be possessed by Lir of *Sidh Finnachaidh* <note>The Fairy Mound of the White Field</note>." 

But Caeilte said: "Do you not know, Aedh, that the mighty wild boar often escapes from both hounds and wolves? And do you not know that when the stag at bay is roused to a last desperate charge, he likewise escapes scot-free from the deer-hounds? Who is the man, in a one-on-one fight, whom you deem most formidable in the battle?" 

"The man who excels in prowess above all the People of the Goddess Danu <note>original: *Tuatha Dé Danann*, the supernatural race of Irish mythology</note>: Lir of *Sidh Finnachaidh*," they answered. 

Caeilte went on: "In every battle I have ever undertaken, I have committed to meeting the best champion present hand-to-hand; I will not allow that tradition to fail today." 

"What other single combat do you promise us, Derg?" they asked. "Whose encounter, after the first, do you hold to be the most difficult?" 

"The encounter with Donn and Dubh," they answered. 

Derg said: "I will handle them both." 

The forces of the fairy mound <note>original: *sidh*</note> now came out to face the battle. From early sunrise until midday, each side pelted the other with easily-thrown darts, small spit-like javelins, broad blue-headed spears, and great stones. Caeilte and Lir of *Sidh Finnachaidh* clashed aggressively and bloodily; in the end, Lir fell by Caeilte’s hand. 

Then that pair of good warriors, Dubh and Donn—the two sons of Eirrge *Anghlonnach* <note>Eirrge "the Exploited" or "the Mighty"</note>—consulted on how to maintain the battle. They ordered the fight this way: Dubh was in the front of the troop <note>original: *phalanx*</note>, and Donn was to provide a vigilant defense in the rear. Derg *Dianscothach* <note>Derg "of the Vehement Utterance"</note> noticed this move. He put his forefinger into his spear's thong and made a lucky cast at the closer man. The spear broke his spine in two and penetrated deep into the body of the man behind him, so that they both died from the single throw. 

Ilbhrec said then:

> "By Caeilte’s hand Lir has fallen: a deed well-deserving of a song of victory <note>original: *pœan*</note>; by Eoghan's son Derg, and with a single throw, Dubh and Donn have fallen. The battle, having gone against Lir and his great host, has withered away toward the north; except for only three who were skilled enough to find their way out, not one of them has escaped the field."

After victoriously looting the enemy and celebrating their triumph, they re-entered the fairy mound. From that time forward, they held forceful possession...

Caeilte said: “Here is your spear for you, Ilbhrec.” “It is not fitting for you to say that to me,” Ilbhrec answered, “for even if Lir had possessed no other weapons but that spear to give as spoils, it is to you it should have fallen, seeing that you are its true and rightful heir.” After this, they remained in the fairy mound for three days and three nights.

“Now then, Caeilte, my soul,” said Ilbhrec of Assaroe, “where was it that Finn actually came to believe in the true faith, or did he ever do so?” Caeilte answered: “He did indeed.” “But where? And what was the cause of his doing so?” “It was on the *secret ridge* <note>original: druim diamhair</note>, which men now call the *ridge of two birds* <note>original: druim dá én</note>, upon the Shannon. The origin of his belief was the clearing of the name of Bodb’s daughter, Finnin. It was said she had killed her own husband, Conan, whereas in truth Conan and Ferdoman had slain each other. The Fianna then arrived at the *wood of birds* <note>original: fidh énaigh</note>, which at this time is named the *secret ridge* as mentioned before. A bowl of pale gold was brought to Finn; he washed his white hands, splashed the clear water over his face, and placed his thumb under his tooth of knowledge <note>A ritual act Finn Mac Cumhaill performed to gain prophetic insight</note>. The truth was revealed to him and the false hidden from his sight. It was shown to him that in the world’s later time, both the gift-bestowing *Adze-Head* <note>original: Táilchenn; a prophetic name for Saint Patrick, referring to his tonsure</note> would come, and Kieran, the *son of the carpenter* <note>original: mac an tsaoir</note>, would found a house <note>Clonmacnoise</note> that would influence half of all Ireland.” Then Caeilte spoke:

->“Beloved is the church . . .<-

“There, knowledge of that conflict in which Conan and Ferdoman fell came to us; there it was that Finn made this act of faith, and by it gained Heaven:

> “Woe to the Fian-warrior who heard the news when we came to the *swim of two birds* <note>original: snámh dá én</note>: the slaughter of bald Conan from the plain, and Ferdoman’s slaughter too. *Secret ridge*, O *secret ridge*, was this spot’s name until the time of the Fianna; *bird ridge* has been its name ever since, because of the fowling Finn and the Fianna did there. By the good will of Him who is Lord of all the clans, an illustrious child shall be born there: a worthy son of Heaven’s King, whom angels are awaiting. Kieran the pure he shall be; he it is who shall be born in the royal fort. He likewise shall claim half of Ireland—the son of the carpenter from Murthemny. Those who dare to become plunderers of his church shall suffer a sudden death by the reddened points of spears: torment and miserable execution, and the lowest depth of Hell. I, even I, tell you now—the prophecy is true for me—I believe in the Father, in the Son,

and in the Holy Spirit, all in One. I believe that the King of the Kingdom of Heaven exists—whose inhabitants are better than any other tribe. The King who has granted me a respite <note>original: "respite [to this hour in which I believe]"</note> will not suffer me to fall into eternal woe.”

After this, they remained in the *sídh* <note>A fairy mound or supernatural dwelling.</note> for another six weeks. Caeilte said, “It is time for us to depart, for we have been here inside for a good while now.” 

“God’s blessing <note>original: "benison"</note> on you, and that of the people here within,” said the folk of the *sídh*. “Even if you desired to stay with us forever, you would be welcome to do so.” 

Ilbhrec said, “Since you are intent on going, here are nine gorgeous sets of clothing for you, including rich mantles; also nine shields, nine spears, and nine long swords with hilts and guards of gold; and nine hounds besides for the pleasant chase.” 

They took leave of each other. Those departing left a blessing and carried away gratitude. As wearying as the battle had been, it was even more painful for Derg *dianscothach* <note>Derg "of the nimble speech."</note> to part from his own companion and fellow-student <note>original: "condisciple"</note>; for since the day he was separated from Finn and all the Fianna <note>The legendary band of warriors led by Finn mac Cumhaill.</note>, he had not found a day sadder than this one.

With those nine warriors of his, Caeilte took his way and visited *Sliabh Cuire*, *Sliabh Cairbre*, and *Sliabh Céide* to the northward, and *Cathair Dhaimh Dheirg*, or the “Red Stag’s Fort.” Soon they noticed a brilliant, gaily-colored pair awaiting them upon a burial mound <note>original: "cairn"</note>: a handsome young man with a lady of his own age beside him. He asked Caeilte for news, and Caeilte told him his story: 

“I am of Finn mac Cumhaill’s people, and Caeilte mac Ronan is my name. But by what name are you known, warrior?” 

“Eoghan the Princely Hospitaller <note>A "hospitaller" (biatach) was a person of high status responsible for providing food and lodging to travelers.</note> is my name. I am of the former people of Cormac’s son, Cairbre Lifechair. Becnait the She-Hospitaller is this lady’s name. She and I are of the same age, and we have both completed ten-score years <note>200 years.</note>.” 

Caeilte inquired, “Did you not once have enormous wealth, young man?” 

“I did indeed,” he answered. “For from Mac Modharn’s Assaroe northward to *Cnoc an Fhomorach*, or ‘the Pirate’s Hill’ (which is now called Torry Island in northern Ireland), there were no districts where I did not have a herd of milk cows for every second or third townland.”

“A thieving monster, a most hideous pirate, and a ‘son of misfortune’ whom Finn once ruined <note>meaning: utterly defeated</note>. He has wasted seven entire *triucha céts* <note>An ancient Irish land division, roughly equivalent to a barony or a population group of 3,000.</note> until there...”

There is no one left to take land or estate; and these being thus utterly exhausted, he has turned everything into a desert. Me too, in truth, he has diminished and harried, down to the last twenty-seven milch herds <note>original: "milch herds"; cattle kept for milking</note> of my substance that I still possess.” Caeilte asked: “Where does this man stay?” “A strong, impregnable rock that is to the north of us here, right on the spacious bay—that is his post. Even though he is only one of three in his company, he still carries off a ship’s full cargo of booty and captives; for he is himself a match for four hundred men, his hound for three hundred, and his daughter for three hundred more; neither can any man hurt them.” Caeilte asked: “At what point does he enter the bay?” “Why, right across from the town on the north-west.” There Caeilte and his companions stayed for that night, and in all respects were served and tended.

Early the next morning, Caeilte rose alone. He took his sword, shield, and spear, and made his way to the impregnable rock beside the bay. Here he waited for a time and then saw a *curach* <note>original: "curach"; a traditional Irish boat made of a wicker frame covered with hides</note> with three in it: a shag-haired dog of a dirty grey that wore a rude iron chain around its neck; in the *curach’s* bow was a great lump of a wench, bald and dark-skinned, who from a distance loomed like some jutting point of rock and held a substantial spit-spear <note>original: "spit-spear"; a heavy, sharp-pointed weapon resembling a roasting spit</note> in her hand; while in the rear sat the hulking carle <note>original: "carle"; a fellow of low birth or a churlish, large man</note>. They reached the beach near Caeilte, and as they approached, a certain repugnance and fear affected him. The massive man said to his daughter: “Loose the hound and set him at yonder tall man standing all alone, so that before the dog begins his expedition and excursion, he may feed his fill on him.” 

The daughter loosed the animal. At this, Caeilte felt a loathing and a timidity which had never touched him before, whether in battle or in single combat, and he said: 

> “My Creator and my *Táilchenn* <note>original: "Táilchenn"; a term meaning 'Adze-head,' a nickname for Saint Patrick referring to his tonsure</note> both I call upon to oppose the three of you!” 

Then, with a small copper dart that he had, he delivered a most careful throw at the hound, in such a way that one end of the spiked dart stuck in its upper palate and the other in its lower, pinning its mouth shut. Then it fell out of the *curach*, and in the end, it perished in the depths of the sea. With their intent set on Caeilte, the other two came ashore and fought with him boldly and hard-heartedly. The great man now fought more intensely and pressed him home; with three cuts he made three pieces of him (the third being his head). Eoghan and Caeilte’s people came, recognized those heads, and gratefully acknowledged the deed. Feeble and strengthless, Caeilte sat down, and upon him fell dimness and stupor-clouds <note>a poetic description of fainting or losing consciousness</note>. Balsamic herbs were applied to him, and for a fortnight he was under medical care; by which means he was made a smooth, whole man without a scar.

Caeilte said: “We must depart tomorrow, and we leave a blessing with you.” Next day, accordingly, they bade Eoghan farewell, and from there they came away to *Tulach na gcét* or “the Hill of Hundreds,” now called *Tulach dá ech* or “Two Horse Hill”; northward to *Cuillios na fénne* or “the Fianna’s Rear-fort”; to *Currach na míolchon* or “the Greyhound Plain,” called *Currach cuan* or “Plain of Wolves”; northward still to *Both chnó* or “the Nut Bothy,” where once the poet appeared to Lugh Long-arm son of Ethlenn <note>Lugh Lamfada, a major deity and hero of the Tuatha Dé Danann</note>, and where Columkill son of Felim <note>Saint Columba, one of the three patron saints of Ireland</note> was born; northward to *Daire Guill* or “Goll’s Oakwood” where, as they emerged from the grove’s edge, they saw a young man with his back leaning against a massive pillar-stone. He wore a fringed mantle with a brooch <note>original: fibula</note> of gold upon the breast, and under that a tunic of soft silk; he held two wolf-dogs in his hand, and in front of him was a pack of beagles. Caeilte greeted the young man, who returned the salutation and inquired: “To whom do you belong?” Caeilte answered: “Our chief and lord lives no more; I mean Finn son of Cumall.”

Then the young man wept copious and very lamentable tears so that his breast and chest were wet, and he asked: “Who then are you yourself, warrior?” “I will declare myself to you,” said Caeilte: “I am Donn, son of Aedh, son of Garadh mac Morna.” “Your father was a good man,” said Caeilte; and he recited:

> “He was the disdainful one of lasting fame—the Fian-warrior of genuine audacity; he was the productive branch of good repute: one to sweep up the whole world’s valuables.”

"It was Dubhdithre then, the chief of the Fianna of Ossory <note>A kingdom in ancient Ireland, located in modern County Kilkenny and western County Laois</note>, who had been slain by your grandfather, Garadh mac Morna. The people rose to make him welcome; Caeilte, for his part, sat down on a cairn <note>A mound of stones built as a memorial or landmark, often over a grave.</note> in front of the *dún* <note>original: *dún*; a fortified residence or fort.</note>, and the crowd sat around him.

Conall questioned Caeilte: “Why was this cairn named the *Cairn of Garbhdaire?*” Caeilte answered this query, for it was he who knew the history: “A trusted warrior of Finn mac Cumall was here, named Garbhdaire mac Angus, son of the king of Munster in the south. As he hunted one day, he killed one hundred and fifty stags, as many does, and as many boars. The people of this country and land saw him; they set upon him and violently robbed him of his game and the spoils of his chase, though he slew three hundred of their fighting men. The inhabitants closed in around him and turned him into ‘an apple on spear-points’ <note>A metaphor for being pierced by many weapons from all sides.</note>, thus killing him. But we, the three battalions of the Fianna, arrived to avenge him; we laid waste to the entire country and killed its three kings. The rest of the inhabitants fled for safety to the islands:

> “By the race of the spacious Eoghan, Garbhdaire was slain upon the shore; fifty warriors we slaughtered here in vengeance for Garbhdaire.

Caeilte and his people were shown to a private and quiet house apart from the rest, and there they were well served. They remained there throughout that night, and the next morning they went to Garbhdaire’s cairn. It was excavated, and the chain of Lugh mac Eithlenn was found. In which the largest man of the assembly found room while sitting. Conall said, “By my soul, Caeilte, it is a huge head!” “It was huge, and he who wore it was good as well,” Caeilte answered. Then Conall mac Neill questioned Caeilte, saying, “Right out before us in the sea is an island, and on it a fort; within this is a colossal sepulchre <note>original: "sepulchre" - a tomb or burial chamber</note> whose origin we do not know.” At hearing this, Caeilte wept. The tomb was one hundred and forty feet long and twenty-eight feet wide by Conall’s measurement. “Sabia, daughter of Conn of the Hundred Battles; *Eithne the Noble*, daughter of Cahir the Great; Cormac’s daughter *Aillbhe*, called *of the freckled cheek* <note>original: "gruaidbhrec" - meaning red and white or dappled complexion</note>; and the woman of this grave: *Berrach*, called *the Freckled*, daughter of Cas Cuailgne, King of Ulster in the north, and Finn mac Cumall’s well-beloved wife. Now, if any one of those women possessed more goodness than the others, it was she. In her mansion, a guest could stay from the first Monday of <term>Samhain</term> <note>original: "samhain-tide" - the festival marking the beginning of winter, around November 1st</note> until the first Monday of spring, and then have the choice whether to depart or to stay on from then after. Any man who could not find enough weapons or clothing elsewhere would receive more than enough of both from her.” Conall asked, “And what was the cause of her death?” “I have the story for you,” [Caeilte replied].

# The Colloquy

Caeilte said: "It was the father and mother of Goll mac Morna <note>Goll mac Morna was a famous leader of the Connacht faction of the Fianna and often a rival to Finn</note> who brought her up; they had no foster-child other than her. Finn requested her hand from her father, who said, however, that he would not give her to Finn unless it was with Goll mac Morna's consent. Finn then requested his foster-child from Goll, who answered: 'There are conditions upon which I would grant her: that she never be dismissed for all time; that she be your third wife; and that she shall never be refused anything she may request of you.' Finn said: 'It shall all be granted.' 'Who shall be our guarantors?' asked Goll. 'Take your choice of them,' Finn replied. Finally, as trustees for her, Finn appointed his own three foster-sons: Daighre, Garadh, and Conan.

She remained with Finn and bore him three sons: Faelan, Aedh the Small, and Uillenn called *Red-edge*. Finn kept her as a loving wife until the time her foster-brothers, the Clan Morna <note>original: *clanna Mórna*</note>, became plunderers and outlaws against Finn, their number being three thousand warriors." Regarding this, Caeilte recited a poem:

> "Ten hundred and twenty hundred there—that was the size of proud Clan Morna's rank and file; besides these, the count of their chiefs and chieftains was fifteen hundred.

> "The sons of Morna went off to the 'Oak-wood of Bulls' <note>original: *daire tarbdha*</note> in the province of Connacht. There, the three battalions of the Fianna <note>The Fianna were the legendary bands of warriors led by Finn mac Cumhaill</note> caught them before they had risen from their camp, and fifteen of their reliable and well-armed men fell in the wood. But then that mighty man of valor, Goll mac Morna, arrived and covered their retreat; once he took this task upon himself, we were unable to do them even the slightest harm. 

The Clan Morna then determined that they would not hesitate to kill anyone who was attached to Finn and the Fianna by bonds of friendship. The one who gave them this counsel was Conan the Bare <note>original: *Conan mael*</note>, son of Morna; for he was a breeder of quarrels among followers and a malicious mischief-maker in every army and host. The sons of Morna came to this green-grassed meadow, where they considered what they should do to Berrach Brec, their own foster-child. They decided to offer her a condition: she was to bring away all her jewels and other valuables and forsake Finn, and then she would never need to live in fear of the Clan Morna. When this was conveyed to her—

She cried: "Alas! Is it to injure me you would, foster-brethren of my heart?" "Truly it is," they answered; but the woman said: "By no means will I, to please you, forsake my spouse, my first husband and my gentle love!"

The sons of Morna <note>A rival clan to Fionn mac Cumhaill's followers</note> in their entire battle-formation came to the town in which she was; they surrounded it, each putting his hand into his fellow’s, and from every direction <note>original: "airt"</note> of the four, they set it on fire. The queen came out of the town, having with her thirty women as companions; but from the balcony of the *dún* <term>a fortified residence or fort</term>, Art mac Morna noticed her step onto the white beach and head for her galley. He put his finger into the spear’s thong and hurled it at her. The lady heard the javelin’s whistling sound and turned her face toward the missile; it landed full in her chest, in her very bosom, and broke her spine in two; thus she died. Afterward, when her own people had plundered the *dún*, she was carried up from the shore and laid in this grave. Thus spoke Caeilte, and he recited:

> "Berrach Brec, O Berrach Brec, daughter of Cas Cuailgne, whom I loved: she was a queen of yellow hair, a wife right worthy of a good man. Upon the seashore she was slain: a deed that surely was not right; her *dún* was set on fire: that was a lawless deed with evil intent. Three hundred shields there were within her house, three hundred sets of chessmen and three hundred boards; three hundred beakers for drinking, decorated with red gold. Never had she refused the prayer of any; her physical form was excellent, as was her wisdom: there in the very place where her venerated grave is, which men call 'Berrach’s tomb.'"

"Under you here then," he continued, "is the woman whose grave this is and whose story you have heard."

After this, Caeilte rose and skirted the town in a northerly direction, with everyone following him. He laid his hand upon a huge stone that projected somewhat from the side of the dwelling and said, "Men, take hold of one end of the stone and leave me the other." The whole company tried, but they could do nothing against it. Caeilte said: "Where is Donn mac Aedh mac Garadh?" "Here am I," he answered. "Go and face me, for you are the son of a hero and a battle-champion; and should I find treasure beneath the stone, I would give you a third of it." Both came and gave the stone a powerful wrench, dragging at it determinedly and with great strength in such a way that they landed it fairly on the ground, on the earth’s surface. "Success...

But of the precious things, give me nothing except only the *craebghlasach* <note>original: *craebghlasach*; literally "the green-branched one," the name of Finn’s sword</note>—the sword from Finn’s thigh—and the *escra* <note>original: *escra*; a drinking vessel</note> or goblet of his hand, so that I may present them to Patrick. For in their ornaments and engravings are thrice fifty ounces of gold, as many of silver, and three times fifty crystalline gems.” All three went into the cave as instructed and brought out a load each: one of each kind of treasure. The whole crowd also entered the recess and carried off their fill of the same, so that among them all, there was not a family of nine that was not amply stocked with silver and gold.

At this point, his chariot came to Conall, and he said, “Get into the chariot, Caeilte.” “I have need of it,” answered Caeilte, “for I am weary from the assembly.” They mounted the chariot, and Conall urged his horses westward with the goad to *tráigh chonbhice* <note>original: *tráigh chonbhice*; Conbeg’s Strand</note>, or ‘Conbeg’s strand,’ where he asked: “Why is this shore called by that name, Caeilte?” 

“It is easily told,” was the reply. “It was a favorite deer-hound that Finn had, and in all Ireland there was no stag that, once the hound was slipped after it, could find cover before the dog would head him off and run him back right to the Fianna’s main pack and their attendants. No other hound but he ever slept in the same bed with Finn. It was here that Goll mac Morna drowned him; here also a tidal wave washed him ashore, and so he lies under that green cairn you see bordering the beach.” Then Caeilte spoke:—

> “Piteous to me was Conbeg’s cruel death! Conbeg of perfect symmetry: in the wake of wild pig or of deer, I have never seen one more expert of foot! A pain to me was Conbeg’s cruel death! Conbeg of the hoarse, deep bark: at the swift killing of the buck, I have never seen one more expert of foot! A pang to me was Conbeg’s cruel death! Conbeg drifting on the high green seas: his cruel fate gave rise to conflict; his death lacked nothing of sorrow!”

That night they came to *dún na mbarc* <note>The Fort of the Ships</note>, and the next day Conall said: “Hard by us here is a ridge (*druim Náir* or ‘Nar’s ridge’ is its name), and in it is a wild pig against which both...

# The Colloquy

"Hounds and men are powerless." "I have seen the day," Caeilte answered, "that I was a hunter; but where is Donn mac Morna?" "Here," cried Donn. "Take then your weapons, so that we—as many as are left of the Fianna—may proceed to hunt the wild pig." They went up onto the ridge, and there they saw the boar with nine tusks growing from each of its jaws. At the sight of the colossal hounds and men, the beast screamed, and in its presence, a certain horror and fear overtook them. "Leave it between me and the swine," said Donn, "for whether I live or die, it is all the same!" Caeilte said: "You claim a hero's privilege." Donn therefore addressed himself to the boar; but as the creature charged him, Caeilte dealt it a spear-thrust from one shoulder to the other, and in this way, it perished by their hands. Until Conall's group came to fetch the boar, they could not move him from the spot; but then he was brought into the presence of Conall, who said: "It is a huge swine." "True," said Caeilte: "this is the *<term>muc shlángha</term>* <note>original: "prophylactic pig," likely meaning a pig of healing or a legendary beast of great significance</note>, and it was over just such another creature that the war and feud between the clan-Morna and the clan-Baeiscne first began."

They were not there long before they saw seven men coming toward them. "Where do you come from, young men?" asked Conall, son of Niall. "We have come from Patrick, son of Calpurn; from Ossian, son of Finn; and from Dermot, son of Cerbhall, to fetch you and Caeilte." The latter said: "After my hunting, I am indeed too weak to go there today; but you, Conall, go and take these presents with you: for Patrick, the goblet that belonged to Finn; the *<term>craebghlasach</term>* <note>original: "green-branched," the name of Finn's sword</note>—Finn's sword—for Cerbhall's son Dermot, King of Ireland; and for the same king (seeing as this is the 'prophylactic swine'), take the boar which has just been killed, so that all may see it and the king may divide it among everyone, both high and low." Even so, the whole thing was carried out: first of all, the sword was put into the hand of Donn, son of Aedh, son of Garadh, son of Morna, with Caeilte saying: "Until the time you reach the King of Ireland, both the profit and the peril of the sword rest on you, young man!" Conall himself took the *<term>escra</term>* <note>original: "drinking cup" or "goblet"</note> for Patrick, the slaves carried the pig, and they traveled as far as *<term>cnoc uachtair Erca</term>* <note>original: "the upper hill of Erc"</note>, which at this time is called Usnach. When they arrived, Patrick was there on the summit of Usnach, with Dermot, son of Cerbhall, on his right...

# The Colloquy

On his right hand sat Ossian son of Finn, and on his left sat Muiredach mac Finnachta, king of Connacht. Beside him sat Eochaid *Leithderg* <note>original: "leithderg" meaning "Red-side"</note>, king of Leinster, and next to him was Eoghan *Derg* <note>original: "derg" meaning "Red"</note> mac Angus, king of both provinces of Munster. These men sat in a circle so that Eoghan touched the right hand of the king of Ireland.

Then Conall mac Neill came forward, laid his head in Patrick's bosom, and knelt before him in reverence. Dermot the king said, "Come here, Conall," but he answered, "I would rather stay in Patrick's presence to serve him, so that just as he is here on Earth, he shall also be my superior in Heaven." Patrick answered, "I grant royal power to you, and decree that thirty kings of your lineage shall reign. Furthermore, I grant you my metropolitan city and my abbacy, and you shall enjoy whatever I receive from the five great provinces of Ireland."

Conall placed a gold *escra* <note>term: a drinking cup or flagon</note> into Patrick's hand and said, "It is your own friend, Caeilte son of Ronan, who has given you this gift." "By my word, he is a friend indeed," Patrick said, and he passed the *escra* into the hand of the king of Ireland. The king examined it for a long time and then said, "We have never seen a more excellent or precious object than this *escra*. Ossian, look at it closely and tell us who it might have belonged to." 

"It belonged to my own father, Finn mac Cumall," Ossian replied. "He gave it to one of his wives, Berrach Brec, daughter of Cas Cuailgne, whom the sons of Morna killed. I am certain," he continued, "that whoever found this also found the second-best treasure in all of Ireland or Scotland. Where then is the *Craebhghlasach* <note>term: "The Grey-leafed," the name of Finn's famous sword</note>, Finn's sword?" 

"I have it here for the king of Ireland," answered Conall. "You have recognized it well. Go, Donn, and deliver it to the king of Ireland, for Caeilte has assigned it to him." Donn placed the sword in Ossian's hand, and as he did so, it was seen that the weapon's hilt perfectly filled Donn's own grip. At this, Ossian said, "It is a wonder to me that the sword fills your grasp, for it has never filled the grip of any man except those of the Clan Baeiscne or the Clan Morna." 

"What is your descent, young man?" asked the king of Ireland. 

"I am Donn, son of Aedh, son of Garadh, son of Morna." 

"By my word, your father and grandfather were good men," said Ossian. "Now, deliver the sword into the king of Ireland's hand." 

"What is the fee for the sword, king of Ireland?" Donn asked.

# The Colloquy

<margin>✓</margin> Donn asked, “What reward do you seek?” 

“The leadership of the Fianna <note>original: "Fian-chiefry"; the command of the elite warrior bands of Ireland</note> of Ireland, just as my grandfather’s brother Goll mac Morna held it.” 

“If Ossian and Caeilte grant permission, it shall be yours,” the king replied. 

“Yes, we do,” Ossian consented, “for my permission is the same as Caeilte’s. This office is natural to Donn, as seven chiefs of his lineage have held the high leadership of the Fianna in both Ireland and Scotland.” 

“I confer it upon you in this way,” said the king: “You shall receive no tax or tribute of gold or silver, such as was paid to every royal Fian-chief before you. Instead, the sole right to the hunt and the game of Ireland shall be yours.” 

Then Donn took pledges and sureties for the office. For twenty-seven years, he held the high leadership of the Fianna in Ireland and Scotland, until the time when Dubh, son of Dolar, slew him in the battle of *Cuire* over in Scotland.

Lastly, the boar was brought before the King of Ireland. <margin>✓</margin> “There,” said Conall, “is the pig which Caeilte and Donn have killed. Caeilte presents it to you to distribute among the men of Ireland, believing that for a portion of this medicinal swine <note>original: "prophylactic swine"; a magical animal whose meat was believed to have healing or protective powers</note> to be given to them would serve as a preservation for their health.” 

The king divided the boar among the twenty-five battalions that all Ireland had mustered at the Hill of Uisneach. Because of this, they were all made joyful and cured of their sadness. This was the last medicinal swine ever distributed among the men of Ireland.

Then Conall More mac Neill said to the King of Ireland: “What decree are you pleased to make for Caeilte if he comes to seek you?” 

“He is to have the rations of a thousand warriors,” the king answered. “Also, one hundred and sixty cows are to be put into a fenced grass field, and their milk and produce served nightly to him and to his fellow-student <note>original: "condisciple"</note> Ossian before they go to sleep.” There they all remained for that night until the following morning.

To return to Caeilte: as we have mentioned, Conall’s horses were harnessed for him and his chariot was made ready. He traveled over the summit of *Sliabh Fuaid* <note>the Fews Mountains in County Armagh</note>; past the *Rowan-tree of Fair Women*, which is now called the *Rowan-tree of the Two-Ox Meadow*; and past the northward side of the *Height of the Prowess-Feat*, now named the *Throwing-place of Murthemny*. This was the site where, during the Great Cattle Raid of Cooley <note>original: "táin bó Cuailgne"; the central epic of the Ulster Cycle</note>,

Everyone cried out: "There is none!" But Ossian heard this and said: "Bring me a <term>sithal</term> <note>original: *sithal*; a bucket or large vessel</note> so that I may go in search of water." "Take a <term>gilla</term> <note>original: *gilla*; a servant or lad</note> with you," said Dermot. Ossian answered: "Neither servant nor <term>óglaech</term> <note>original: *óglaech*; a young warrior or attendant</note> shall come with me."

Ossian went out, but he kept his face turned backward as he walked to ensure that no one in the Irish camp was watching him. In this manner, he reached the well of Usnach, called *an fhinnlescach* or "the white-rimmed." From the time the Battle of Gowra was fought until that very day, no man in all Ireland had ever found it. He came to the well’s gravelly brink and saw eight beautiful salmon within it, shimmering in their various colors; the place was so secluded and difficult to reach that the fish had nothing to fear there. He pulled eight sprigs of watercress and eight of brooklime. He dipped the vessel into the pool and scooped up the eight salmon, alive and splashing wildly. Then, with the sprigs of cress and brooklime floating in the water, he returned to Usnach and set the vessel before the King of Ireland. Everyone was amazed at the sight—the stalk of each sprig alone reached up to Dermot’s knee. "These must be divided into two portions," the King said: "one half for Patrick, and the other for ourselves."

The Saint answered: "Not so, since your group is more numerous. Instead, let them be divided into three parts, and one-third given to the Church, for that is her rightful share." This was done, and Patrick said: "It is well, King of Ireland. But never let those two <note>Caeilte and Ossian</note> deprive you of your place in Heaven." Dermot asked: "What is the meaning of that, holy Patrick?" "It refers to the great intensity with which you focus your thoughts on them <note>Patrick warns the King not to be so captivated by the old pagan legends that he neglects his Christian soul</note>."

Regarding Caeilte again: he traveled as far as the *brugh* <note>original: *brugh*; a fairy dwelling or mansion</note> of *Aengus mac an Daghda* to the north; across *Féic* on the bright-streaming River Boyne; keeping the hill of *Tlachtga* <note>The Hill of Ward, a sacred site of festival fires</note> to his right hand, and the hill of *Taillte* <note>Telltown, named for the foster-mother of the god Lugh</note> daughter of *Mac ú Móir* to his left. He then ascended by *Ród na Carpat*, or "the Road of Chariots," to the summit of Usnach <note>Uisneach, the traditional center of Ireland</note>, where the men of Ireland were gathered. Caeilte dismounted in the assembly and went to where Patrick was; he bowed to him and laid his head in his bosom. An elderly warrior (now a member of Patrick’s religious community), Muchua son of Lonan, rose before him. "It is well, Caeilte, my soul," said Patrick, "tell us who Muchua is." Then Caeilte recited:

> "Muchua: son of Lonan of the tunics, son of Senach (at whom we will direct no spear-thrust), son of Angus of the iron-grey horse-stud, son of... son of Blath *Brecdhorn* or 'Freckle-fist,' son of Aedhan, grandson of Fergus, son of Cinaeth, son of Fiacha... son of Eoghan’s son Muiredach."

Muchua said: "What can I do but remember you in all the eight canonical hours <note>The scheduled times for daily prayer in the Church</note> of the Church!"

<margin>at evening</margin>
"Come up here, Caeilte," cried King Dermot, "and sit at my shoulder!" "I am not a man for a king’s shoulder, but one for a king’s presence," he answered. "For I am only the son of a simple man of war, and he who is currently at your shoulder is better than I." "I pledge my word," said Ossian, "that never in all Ireland did a woman of your generation bear a son who could justly call himself a better man than you!"

Then the men of Ireland welcomed Caeilte, and the king gave him a triple welcome; Caeilte gave Ossian three kisses and sat down beside him. Ossian had a handful of watercress and brooklime in his hand, and he shared it with Caeilte. "This is cress and brooklime from the *flescach* <note>original: *flescach*; likely referring to a specific stream or wooded area</note>," said Caeilte, "and did you have any fish with it?" "I caught eight salmon," Ossian answered, "and we two have the eighth salmon of them." Caeilte said: "By my word, there was never a portion of food in the hand of any woman or man that I would prefer over yours."

Caeilte then reached into the rim of his shield and threw the chain of Lugh <note>Lugh Lamfada, a major deity and king of the Tuatha Dé Danann</note> son of Eithliu onto the ground before them. Ossian said: "Caeilte, it was in Garbhdaire’s cairn <note>A stone burial mound</note> that you found that chain." "It certainly was," he answered, and he gave it to the King of Ireland. There were twenty-five battle-units mustered in that assembly, and this chain was long enough to go around them all; supposing...

eight hundred warriors to fit within it, and it was to be locked on the first man; to open it was not possible until that first man should be freed.

The king said: "It is well, Caeilte—there were a good four who were in Ireland at the same time: Cormac mac Art, Finn, Cairbre Lifechair, and Ossian." "Cormac was a fine warrior, and Finn's excellence was known to all"; and Caeilte uttered:

> "Had his own son and his enemy come to stand for a legal verdict: it was one of his virtues that he would not have pronounced a lying judgment between them."

"Caeilte," said Dermot, "was Cormac better than Finn, and was Cairbre better than Ossian?"

"By the King who is over me <note>referring to God</note>, Cormac was not better than Finn; nor was the far-famed Ossian inferior to Cairbre Lifechair."

Eochaid Lethderg, King of Leinster, inquired of Caeilte: "What reason did Finn and the Fianna <note>The Fianna were a legendary band of semi-independent warrior-hunters</note> have that, among every other monster you banished out of Ireland, you did not kill the reptile that we have in the glen of *Ros Enaigh*?" 

Caeilte replied: "Their reason was that the creature is the fourth part of Mesgedhra's brain, which the earth swallowed there and converted into a monstrous worm. Now, it was not fated that we should slay it until the *Táilchenn* <note>term: "Adze-head," a nickname for Saint Patrick, referring to his tonsure</note> should arrive. It is a disciple of his *community* <note>original: "familia," referring to a monastic household</note> who, at the end of time, shall bind it with a single rush-stem; and in this bond it shall remain until the Day of Judgment." 

"To what end, then, did the Fianna come to have themselves and their hounds slain by the reptile in that lake?" 

"A fairy sweetheart that Finn had—whom he renounced because of the many different shapes she assumed (for there was no animal whose form she would not take)—did this. One day the Fianna came upon the burial mound overhanging that lake, and a deer swam out onto the water. But the *beast* <note>original: "piast," a Gaelic term for a monster or water-serpent</note> rose up at us and killed a hundred of our hounds and a hundred of our men. I asked Finn whether it was by our hands that the creature was destined to fall; if so, we would fight it and avenge our people. Finn placed his thumb under his tooth of knowledge <note>A legendary ritual where Finn mac Cumhaill gained prophetic insight by chewing his thumb</note>; a true prophecy was revealed to him, and he declared:

> "Glen of *Ros Enaigh* (this will come true for me), the bell's voice shall yet sound there sweetly and perpetually; though it should carry nothing but the roe deer, yet its precious virtues were many..."
